PREFACE. 


——too—— 


Durine the time I was travelling in the interior of 
British Guiana, apart from my official work I noted . 
down each evening any incidents that occurred during 
the day. 

In this volume I have set down those incidents in 
the order in which they took place, together with 
the routes: followed, and so present them to the 
reader in the hope that they may be found worthy 
of perusal. | 

I have confined myself exclusively to a relation 
of the above, not touching upon that part of my 
work relating to the Physical Features and Descrip- 
tive Geology of the Colony, the reports upon which 
have already been published by the order of the 
Lords Commissioners of Her Majesty’s Treasury. 

The accompanying map is based upon that of 
Schomburgk, to which has been added sketch sur- 
veys of the Potaro, Siparuni, Burro-burro, New and 


vi PREFACE. 


Mazaruni rivers, the points on the latter having 
been determined by observations for latitude and 
longitude. 

The course of my journeys is marked on the map 
by a blue line, so that the reader will have no 
diffculty in tracing it out. 


LONDON, June, 1876.

CANOE AND CAMP LIFE 


IN 


BRITISH GUIANA. 


CHAPTER I. 
TO THE CUYUNI RIVER. 


Guiana — Demerara — The Interior — Embark Indian Crews — Ascend 
Estuary of the Essequebo — Bartika Grove — River People — Penal 
Settlement. 


Wirnin the Torrid Zone, and forming the northern 
portion of South America, lies the wonderful country 
of Guiana, now divided into five portions, known 
respectively as Venezuelan, British, Dutch, French, 
and Brazilian Guiana. 

British Guiana—the only possession of Great 
Britain on the whole South American continent— 
is better known to the world at large as “ Demerara,” 
from its capital (Georgetown), being situated at the 
mouth of a river of that name. 

The civilized and cultivated portion of the colony 
lies only along a narrow strip of sea-coast. Behind 
.this are swamps, then wooded rising ground, and 
finally mountains and savannas stretching southwards, 
all lying ina state of nature, the haunts of wild animals 
and various Indian tribes. This portion, between the 

B 


2 TO THE CUYUNI RIVER. Canar. I. 


rear of the sugar estates and the confines of the colony, 
is known as the “ Interior”; and, with the exception 
of a few settlements on the banks of the lower Berbice, 
Demerara, and Essequebo rivers, it remains to-day in 
the same state as in the time of Raleigh. 

Whilst engaged as geologist on the Government 
geological survey of the West Indies, it fell to my lot 
to visit and explore much of this interior region ; and 
in these pages I have attempted to recount the inci- 
dents that occurred during those explorations. 

The first journey, performed by my colleague, Mr. 
Sawkins, and myself, had been over the north-western 
portion of the colony, towards the mouth of the 
Orinooko, on the Pomeroon, Waini, Barama, and 
Barima rivers. As the only mode of travelling, when 
the narrow fringe of cultivated land is left, is by 
water, and occasionally by Indian paths, we had jour- 
neyed in our own boats upon the above-named rivers, 
furnished with crews of civilized Arowaak, Warrow 
and Ackawoise Indians, by Mr. McClintock, of the 
Pomeroon. Thinking that these Indians would serve 
as boat crews on the large rivers of the interior, we 
had again applied to Mr. McClintock to re-engage 
them, and were now about to proceed down the coast 
in a hired sloop to meet them at Anna Regina sugar 
estate—a place to the north-westward of the mouth of 
the Essequebo river. 

On the 14th of August, 1868, as the sun was 
dipping behind the long distant band of courida trees, 
which line the low western shore of the mouth of the 
Demerara, the hawsers of the sloop “Swift” were 
cast off from Kingston Wharf, and, close-hauled on a 


Cuar. I. RUN AGROUND. 3 


fresh north-east trade breeze, she glided out of the 
river’s mouth, past the old fort, to the sea. Gaining 
a good offing the sheets were eased out, and away the 
old craft went on a north-westerly course, soon leaving 
the lights of Georgetown far behind. After a pipe or 
two I felt the drowsy effects of the soft sea breeze, 
and stretching myself on the after hatch-cover pro- 
cured a sort of broken sleep. 

Just before daybreak the captain—a negro—in- 
formed me that in his opinion we were quite up to, if 
not past, our place of call; and as there were no 
landmarks on the dim coast-line to show him his 
whereabouts, he would run in shore a bit. He was 
preparing to do so when the sloop struck heavily on a 
sand-bank, bumped along, and then became stationary. 
When daylight came we found that we were not within 
ten miles of “ Anna Regina,” and that as the tide was 
running out rapidly, the sloop would remain a hopeless 
fixture till it rose again. We took a boat and pulled 
along near the coast until we arrived opposite the long 
wooden pier or stelling of Anna Regina estate, from the 
end of which we were separated by a most uninviting 
stretch of mud flats. There we sat in a broiling sun 
for two hours, until the tide crept slowly up over the 
great glistening stretch of gray sea bottom, and floated 
our boat landwards, foot by foot. On gaining the 
shore we found the seventeen Indians and a Portu- 
guese, hired by Mr. McClintock, waiting for us. 
When the tide rose sufficiently high to float the sloop 
she got under way, and in the afternoon came up to 
the pier. We embarked the Indians and left for the 
meuth of the Essequebo, arriving at Spring Garden 

R 2 


4 TO THE CUYUNI RIVER. Cuap. I. 


at midnight. During the small hours of the morning 
I awoke, feeling feverish; and landing at daylight 
went to the house of my friend, the late Mr. G. 
Bascom, then proprietor of Spring Garden sugar estate. 
My attack of fever lasted two days and prevented my 
accompanying Mr. Sawkins on a journey up the 
Supinam creek. Three of our Indians also suffered 
from a similar attack of fever and influenza. 

On the 24th we left Spring Garden with our four 
boats. Two of these were tent-hoats, and one a long, 
narrow, dug-out canoe. Paddling along near the 
muddy tree-clothed margin of the river we wended our 
way in a southerly direction up the broad estuary of 
Essequebo; and at nightfall camped in the forest on 
the edge of a sandy bay, hanging our hammocks to trees 
for the night. In this land of palms, within seven de- 
grees of the equator, the thermometer out of the sun’s 
rays seldom falls below 73° or rises above 85°; so that 
near the sea level one can sleep in a hammock in the 
open air without any inconvenience, except when the 
dew is particularly heavy or a rain storm comes on. 
A light canvas awning, spread on a ridge pole over 
the hammock, is a sufficient protection against these 
evils. When a person goes on a long journey through 
the wilds of a tropical country, he must take every 
reasonable precaution to protect his health, for there 
are times when a drenching rain will bring on a sharp 
attack of fever. 

We spent two days upon a small tributary called 
Groote creek, and then proceeded on our journey up 
the Essequebo, reaching Bartika Grove at noon on 
the 28th. | 


Cuar. I. BARTIKA GROVE. ð 


Bartika Grove is a small village, situated on a low 
level point of land facing the north, at the junction of 
the Mazaruni river with the Essequebo, at a distance 
of some 45 miles from the mouth of the latter. It has 
one grass-grown street along the river’s edge, shaded 
with mango trees, a good church, a few wooden houses, 
and a lot of thatched huts. The inhabitants of this 
place and the neighbourhood are chiefly a mixed race, 
the descendants of the progeny of Indians, negroes, 
and the early Dutch settlers. They are short in 
stature, dark-skinned as a rule, strongly built, and 
very hardy. Amongst the cataracts of the rivers, 
where the navigation is extremely difficult and dan- 
gerous, these men are quite at home, and display great 
skill and courage in guiding boats safely up and down 
them. They have small settlements, here and there, 
along the west bank of the tidal portion of the Esse- 
quebo, westwardly along the south bank of the Maza- 
runi to the head of the tide, and along its west bank 
above its junction with the Cuyuni river. They are 
an exclusive set of people, and gain a livelihood by 
working timber punts for the wood-cutting establish- 
ments in the neighbourhood, or as sailors in the small 
craft carrying granite from the quarries at Palmer's 
Point and the Penal Settlement to Georgetown. 

On landing we went to the house of Mr. Grant, the 
magistrate of the district, who assisted us in procuring 
the services of Peterson and other river men, to act as 
steersmen and paddlers up the Cuyuni river, whither 
we were then bound. After leaving the Grove we 
paddled along in shore for about a mile and a half 
against the strong tide setting down the Mazaruni, and 


6 TO THE CUYUNI RIVER, Casar. I. 


then crossed over to the Penal Settlement. This 
establishment is situated on rising ground on the 
north side of the Mazaruni, and with its neatly-kept 
buildings and rows of mango and other trees, has a 
very pretty appearance from the river. It is a prison 
for all those convicts of the colony whose term of 
confinement is of long duration. 

The Visiting Commissioner’s house having been 
placed at our disposal by the Government Secretary 
before leaving (Georgetown, we landed with the 
perinission of the superintendent, my esteemed and 
lamented friend, the late Captain Kerr, and took up 
our quarters in it for the night. From its wide 
verandah in front there is a fine view looking east 
down the Mazaruni and across the broad Essequebo, 
in which the wide expanse of water is relieved by a 
cluster of small islands. 

Most of our Indians would not remain at the Penal 
Settlement, where there were lots of sheds for them to 
hang their hammocks, but going across to the other 
side of the river, camped in the forest. One of them 
was still very ill with fever, and the doctor of the 
prison wished him to be taken to the hospital, where 
he could be properly nursed, but nothing would 
induce him to go. These Indians are of such a timid 
nature, and have so great a love of freedom, that the 
very name of prison sounds terrible in their ears. 

For the next three days we were completely in the 
hands of the river men, with old Peterson at their 
head. They condemned the long canoe as unfit for 
traversing cataracts, and our Indians as useless, from 
their want of knowledge of the kind of work before us. 


Cuar. I. PURCHASE BOAT. 7 


We agreed to bow to their decision regarding the 
canoe, but adhered to our determination to keep the 
Indians and give them a trial at any rate. Being in 
a fix, therefore, about a boat to replace the canoe, we 
were induced to buy the only one for sale in the 
district. After the purchase was made it was put in 
the hands of the Settlement boat-builder to caulk, and 
was found to require extensive repairs. As it would 
take some days to perform this work, we left a crew to 
bring on the boat and join us at the ‘‘Gold Mine,” 
some thirty miles up the Cuyuni. 


8 ON THE CUYUNL : Cuar. Il. 


CHAPTER II. 
ON THE CUYUNI. 


Leave Penal Settlement — First Rapids — River Scenery — Shooting Fish 
— Iguana — The Gold Mine — Lowlow Fish — Meet Indians — Wood- 
skins — Birds — Weynamou — Send back Boat — Manufacture of 
Cassava — Labba — Indian Pepper-pot — Banaboos — Shooting Cata- 
racts — Camp at Puruni Path — Sudden death of an old Indian — 
Indian dying from Secret Poisoning. 


Wir our three boats we left the Settlement on the 
morning of the Ist of September. Each boat was 
now furnished with a bowman and steersman, who, 
with our Indian paddlers, made a total of twenty 
men. Proceeding along the north side of the river, 
we soon entered the mouth of the Cuyuni—a fine 
river, some three-quarters of a mile in width—and 
passing some islands during the forenoon reached the 
first rapids. There, of course, the influence of the 
tide was no longer felt, and the skill of both bowmen 
and steersmen was brought into play. Each was 
provided with a large paddle with a blade some three 
feet long, and a handle of equal length. The bowman 
stood in the bow, and using his paddle like a lever, 
kept the boats prow. up stream at a sharp angle, 
whilst the steersman held as it were against him on 
the opposite side of the boat at the stern. The 
paddlers gave way, beginning a short distance below 
the foot of the rapid on one side of the channel with a 


Cuar. IL ASCEND RAPIDS. 9 


strong but slow stroke, getting quicker and quicker as 
they proceeded, and as the boat mounted up it was 
carried sideways across to the opposite edge. When 
the rapid was small the boat was then above it; but 
when wide the’ bow was deftly turned so as to catch 
the force of the current on the other side, and the 
boat propelled across diagonally to the opposite shore 
of the channel. As the boat got above the strong 
grasp of the rush of water to the level on top she 
bounded forward, when the paddlers changed the 
stroke at once to a few long pulls, and then rested 
awhile. When the rapid was very strong this mode 
of procedure did not succeed, and the boat fell back 
in spite of every effort. Then the men drew her to 
the rocks on the side of the channel and got out the 
tow-rope—a 3-inch tarred hemp—and hauled her up 
in shallow water, some men holding on alongside to 
guide her. 

Where the river flows smoothly it is deep, and if 
without large islands is in one broad channel; but 
where its passage is obstructed by bars of rock, 
forming rapids or cataracts, it widens out amongst 
rocks, rocky islets, and shallows, to an immense width. 
Of the various channels into which it then splits there 
is always one large or main one, and this is the one 
usually followed by the boatmen when the river is 
low. On the other. hand, when it is in flood, the 
smaller channels are sought. © These channels are 
usually full of pointed rocks, some above, and some 
just beneath the surface, so that it requires great skill 
on the part of the steersman to evade them. 

We had paddled up two rapids and hauled up three, 


10 ON THE CUYUNI. ' Cuar. H. 


when Mr. Sawkins found that his crew of Arowaaks 
were not sufficiently acquainted with the work to 
wade and swim in strong currents, and that it was 
dangerous to proceed with them. Putting the boats’ 
heads down stream we ran the rapids we had ascended 
to their foot, and there held a consultation as to our 
next move, which resulted in my going back to the 
Settlement that afternoon and returning next day with 
six river men in place of an equal number of the 
Indians whom we sent off to their homes. The river 
men worked splendidly in the cataracts, swimming, 
diving, and wading in the strong currents from rock 
to rock, while leading out the tow-ropes and hauling 
the boats up. During our journey up this river we 
encountered in many parts a succession of rapids and 
cataracts. At the latter the contents of the boats had 
sometimes to be carried across, and the boats them- 
selves hauled over the rocks on the thick covering of 
aquatic plants. The most formidable cataract we en- 
countered was the “Style Fall,” which is about eight 
feet in height, and is produced by the river flowing 
over a bar of whitish granite. 

The scenery on the river is pretty, but no views 
of any extent are disclosed. The chief features ob- 
servable are intervals of comparatively still water 
lying between large, low, irregularly-shaped rocky 
areas, cut up by wide channels of foaming water, 
where the river rushes over granite or gneiss obstruc- 
tions. Each level patch is higher than the last by 
a few inches when a rapid intervenes, and by two or 
three feet where a cataract occurs. On the rocky 
islands where soil has accumulated are clusters of low

Cuar. I. AQUATIC PLANTS. 11 


trees of various sorts, the stems and large branches 
of which are covered with various parasitic plants, 
orchids, and wild pines. The forest trees on the main 
banks of the river are of different kinds and larger 
growths than those on the rocky islets, being chiefly 
moras (Mora excelsa). In cracks in the rocks the 
stunted water guava (Psidium fluviatile) sends its 
roots and thrives; whilst on the rocks under water 
where the current runs strongest is a luxuriant growth 
of various sorts of aquatic plants (Lacis sps.). These 
plants bear very pretty flowers at this season of 
the year as soon as they are left uncovered by the 
subsiding of the waters after the rainy season, but 
still kept moist by the wash of the water’s edge. One 
small-leaved species has a little white star-shaped 
flower, on a short delicate stem, which has a slight 
perfume, and proves an attraction to numerous species 
of wild bees. Another kind with large coarse crisp 
leaves, growing amongst the cataracts on the upper 
part of the river, presents a most beautiful sight when 
in flower. It has tall flower stems over a foot in 
height, of a greenish colour, and, though thick, of a 
plumose form. The flowers of a delicate pink colour 
burst out along the edges of the upper portion of the 
stalk from half-way up to the top. It sends up its 
flower stalks when the water covers its leaves to a 
depth of three or four inches, so that there are large 
shallow water areas thickly studded with these 
beautiful flowers. | 
When the sun is high numerous butterflies, chiefly 
of various shades of yellow and orange, flit about over 
the open spaces amongst the cataracts, or sit in clusters 


12 ON THE CUYUNI. Cuar. Il. 


near the water’s edge. The beautiful azure blue butter- 
fly (Morphos) is also seen, but its favourite haunts 
are the narrow side streams. 

Our men frequently shot deep-bodied, silvery-scaled 
fish, called cartabac (Tetragonopterus latus), and 
another somewhat similar fish, called pacu (Myletes 
pacu). The male of the latter has a large deep body 
of a dull goldfish colour, while the female is of a dull 
bluish brown. Their flesh is firm and of a peculiarly 
pleasant nutty flavour. They have even rows of 
teeth shaped like the incisors of mammals. These fish 
browse upon the aquatic plants in the swift running 
water, and are easily shot, when feeding near the 
surface, by those skilled in the use of the bow and 
arrow. The bows that our men used for this purpose 
had been procured from the Indians of the interior. 
They were very long and straight, and made of hard 
red wood. The shafts of the arrows were made of 
long straight reeds—the flower stalks of the wild cane 
—tightly bound to a short piece of extremely tough 
wood inserted at one end, called a “shimara-sherie ” 
or * wirecash.” The end of the latter fits into an iron 
arrow-head, to which it is attached by a piece of cord 
in such a way, that when the point pierces the fish 
the barbed head comes off the arrow end, but still 
remains attached to it by the cord. This allows the 
shaft freer play, so that during the struggles of the fish 
it is not broken. Sometimes a light cotton cord of 
about fifteen feet in length is made fast to the wire- 
cash, one end being attached to the left hand of the 
fisherman. When the fish is within a short distance of 
the man the latter lets fly the arrow, and aiming a few 


Cuar. II. IGUANAS. 13 


inches below the fish, to allow for refraction, strikes it in 
the body. The fish, pierced by the barbed point which 
remains in it, being now in direct communication with 
the man’s hand by means of the cord, is easily landed. 

On our way we landed near an isolated hill called 
Woka, and walking along a level tract in the forest 
to its foot, climbed to its summit. Under a large 
ferruginous rock on the hill-side one of the Caribs 
pointed out the sleeping place of a jaguar, and down 
in the mud on the river’s edge we saw the tracks of 
this animal. 

We were frequently amused with the manner in 
which the iguanas (Iguana tuberculata), alarmed by 
the noise produced by the boat’s paddles, threw them- 
selves from the overhanging branches of trees into the 
river, many coming down broadside on the water. 
These reptiles, when full grown, attain a length of 
between three and four feet, including the tail, which 
is long and pointed. Their flesh resembles that of a 
chicken in flavour and quality, and is very good when 
properly cooked. They are exceedingly numerous on 
this river, where they dig long underground chambers 
in the sand-banks at the ends of islands, in which they 
deposit their eggs. Near the foot of a cataract, where 
there was a high beach, our men dug up the sand in 
order to trace out some of these chambers for eggs, 
and succeeded in capturing four iguanas, which they 
dragged out by their tails and then seized by 
the back of the neck and secured. They had to be 
pretty quick, for the iguana can turn round suddenly 
and give a fearful bite. An iguana must be drawn 
from its hole with rapidity, for, if it has time to think, 


14 ON THE CUYUNI. Caar.. II. 


it lets go its tail at the base, leaving it in the hands 
of the would-be capturer, and thus escapes. 

One of these reptiles, captured in its burrow, when 
killed and cut up for cooking, was found to contain 
ten eggs of an ellipsoidal form, shell-less, and midway 
in size between a pigeon’s and hen’s egg. These are 
good eating when boiled for about five minutes and 
then allowed to get quite cold. They then require 
some manipulation. A hole is made in one end of 
the skin, and the albuminous part, which never coagu- 
lates, is squeezed out; then the skin is stripped off, 
and the semi-hardened yelk, of the consistency of butter, 
is eaten with salt. Our men and the Indians had a 
most cruel way of preventing captured iguanas from 
‘escaping, by slitting down the sides of two fore and two 
hind toes, on opposite feet, and passing the toes between 
the bone and sinew in such a manner that they could 
not be disengaged by the struggles of the poor animal. 
The manner in which they were slaughtered, viz. by 
inserting a hard pointed stick up one nostril into the 
brain, was also a very cruel proceeding. 

At noon on the 5th we reached the abandoned 
“gold mine” of the Demerara Gold Mining Com- 
pany. We found the place all overgrown with tall 
shrubs and vines, and a few outbuildings in a dilapi- 
dated condition. The dwelling-house, however, was 
in pretty fair order, and after the men had cut a path 
to it through the shrubs, we took up our quarters 
there. The place is rather prettily situated on high 
ground on the south side of the river, opposite a long, 
wide island of some ten miles in length called Suwa- 
raima. We cut a path to the old stamping mill and 


Cuap. II. GOLD MINE. 15 


adit mouth, and, after inspecting the former, took 
lamps and entered the latter, following it to its termi- 
nation. On the low hill-top, above the adit, are some 
large blocks of quartz, in parts of which minute lines 
and specks of gold can be plainly seen; and on the 
banks of a small stream, at a distance of one mile to 
the southward, there are the remains of some old 
alluvial diggings. On washing the sand and gravel 
at the latter spot we obtained a few minute grains of 
gold. Some six years before the date of our visit this 
place was taken possession of by a company, formed 
in Georgetown, and worked for about three years, but 
not paying, it was abandoned. 

Our men one morning set two strong lines with 
large hooks attached to wooden buoys in the river 
opposite the house, and succeeded in capturing two 
large fish. One, called a lowlow, measured seven feet 
in length, and when cut up gave enough food to last 
our men for two or three days. We had part of its 
flesh salted and part dried on a low wooden staging 
over a fire. This latter process is called Barbacuing, 
and meat or fish subjected to this treatment every 
night will keep for over a week without decaying, but 
not without losing most of its flavour. The other, 
called a fresh-water gilbacker or porawarima, was three 
feet four inches in length. Both these fish were fur- 
nished with two long feelers situated on the top of the 
fore part of the head near the nostrils. Their eyes 
are small, and it is evident that they feel their way 
along muddy river bottoms with these long tentacles. 
The body and head of the lowlow (Hypophthalmus 
Dawaila) is of a bluish-gray colour, while the gilbacker 


16 ON THE CUYUNI. Czar. II. 


(Phractocephalus hemiliopterus) has a red tail and 
bluish back with white under parts, the two colours 
meeting in an abrupt uneven line along the sides. 

Numbers of small bats had taken up their abode 
in the casings of the windows and the roof of the 
dwelling-house, and kept up an incessant rustle and 
chirping cry all day and night. They never all went 
out at a time at night in search of food, but a respect- 
able number was always left at home to squabble and 
squeak. Upon a shelf in one corner of the room was 
a gourd, with a long neck and small aperture, which 
a colony of small black wasps had taken possession of 
and converted into a home. One of these insects 
mounted guard at the aperture, and looked quite 
comical with its head out of the hole, prepared to 
defend it against all comers. Small ants were in 
abundance, and overran the whole place, getting into 
our eatables; while a colony of large black ants had 
taken up their abode in an old gun-case, in which they 
nursed their eggs and larvæ. They sallied forth in /* 
great numbers, and made themselves so disagreeable 
that the gun-case, ants, and larve had to be thrown 
out of the window. One evening we were visited by 
a number of little brown tree-frogs, which climbed up 
the walls and hopped about the floor. 

I enjoyed sitting in the balcony of the Gold Mine 
house until a late hour, smoking my pipe and listening 
to the various night cries of bird and batrachian life. 
There were owls and hawks, whose calls now and 
then broke on the night with startling effect, and the 
maam, whose loud whistles, dying away into a series of 
short notes, had a weird and melancholy sound. The 


Crap. II. NIGHT CRIES. 17 


mosquito hawks, also, would at intervals whistle out 
their “ Who are you” and “ Chuck Will’s widow” ina 
plain and anxiously inquiring manner. But most per- 
sistent of all in row-producing qualities were the tree- 
frogs. One kind uttered a most forlorn cry, not unlike 
the bellowing of a calf that has lost its mother, and 
quite as loud. These fellows, fortunately, are not 
numerous, or only roar at intervals from their homes 
in the tree-tops in the forest depths. Another wretched 
kind, called burra-hararoo, on the contrary, is very 
numerous; one specimen, I believe, living in every 
hollow tree along the river’s edges in this and most 
parts of the colony. They keep up an incessant 
bellow, sounding like “kronk-kronk,” from dusk till 
daylight, with a pertinacity worthy of a better cause. 
When camped at a short distance from the river’s 
edge, the “ kronking ” sounds beat somewhat in unison, 
and resemble the clang of distant machinery. At first 
this noise was neither conducive to sleep nor very 
soothing to my temper when trying to procure my 
forty winks; but after a time I became so used to it 
that I was hardly aware of its existence. The burra- 
bararoo is an ordinary-sized frog, of a pale-drab colour, 
with a knob at the end of each toe. In the daytime 
it is the most used-up, sleepy-looking creature imagi- 
nable, lying in a quiescent state, with tightly-closed 
eyes. Tickle one with a twig just enough to rouse it 
from sleep, and it slowly opens its eyes, disclosing two 
great, bright, protuberant eyeballs, staring at you 
with a most startled and astonished gaze. It then 
quickly closes them again, the light being too much 
for it, and relapses into slumber. On the last evening 
c 


18 ON THE CUYUNI, Cuap. IT. 


of our stay, the shrubbery around us was lit up by 
numbers of fireflies. Their sudden appearance would 
lead one to suppose that a flight of these insects had 
just arrived, or that a lot had just emerged from the 
pupa state. 

Late in the afternoon of our fifth day at the mine, 
the boat which we were expecting arrived, and we 
continued our journey. This boat, like our two tent- 
boats, was made of a large tree, which, after having 
been hollowed out, was spread amidships, and had its 
sides raised by planks. The bow and stern were a 
good deal higher than the midship portion ; the bottom 
was curved in the form of a crescent. This style of 
construction renders them safe conveyances in shooting 
cataracts and rapids, for in so doing, when the boat 
takes a plunge down a steep dip, it cannot bury the 
bow under water, but rises buoyantly. Our tent-boats 
had a space amidships covered with a painted canvas 
awning, supported on iron stanchions, and furnished 
with canvas curtains that could be let down at pleasure. 
All the boats were laden with provisions, consisting of 
rice, flour, sugar, biscuits, salt fish, and salt pork for 
the men; and rice, tinned meats, &c., for ourselves. 

On arriving at the foot of cataract Payacu, we had 
to cross the river to find a suitable place to ascend. 
In doing this my boat got caught in the current, and 
swept down stream with great rapidity for a short 
distance, shipping water over its sides. At the foot 
of a cataract there is always a turbulent mass of 
surging and eddying water mixed with large waves, 
which tail off to a point in still water below. These 
eddying masses are dome-shaped, and are caused by 


Cuar. II. INDIAN TRAVELLERS. 19 


water that has passed over the bottom of the cataract 
rising to the surface. On the outer edges of this 
rushing mass, there is a strong current setting back 
to the rocks on both sides of the cataract. In 
small channels the men took advantage of this back 
current when guiding the boats to the rocks on either 
side, preparatory to getting out the tow-rope and 
hauling up. 

Near Payacu we met five Ackawoise Indians, in 
two bark canoes coming down stream. One of our 
men who could speak their language held a conversa- 
tion with them, by which we learned that they were on 
their way from the Yuruari river, in Venezuela, to 
Bartika Grove, with articles of native manufacture, 
such as hammocks, a sort of arrowroot, &c. Besides 
these they had fifteen parrots,.‘tied by the legs to 
arched sticks. They looked very picturesque in their 
native costume, which consisted only of a narrow strip 
of calico passed over a cord tied round the body above 
the hips, in such a manner that part hung down in 
front like an apron six inches wide, and part hung 
behind like a single attenuated coat-tail. This article 
of dress was called a “lap” by our men. These 
Indians were true redskins in colour,. plump in form, 
and wore their coarse black hair of great length. 
Above the Copang river we met another party of 
Indians in a canoe or woodskin coming down river, 
who, as soon as they saw us, all, with one exception, 
landed swiftly on the bank, and disappeared amongst 
the trees. We soon came up to the spot, and whilst 
my men were inquiring of the remaining man the 
whereabouts of the next village, I happened to glance 

c 2 


20 ON THE CUYUNI. Cuar, IT. 


upwards to the trees close by, and to my astonishment 
beheld an Indian damsel—one of the late occupants of 
the canoe—perched amongst the foliage some ten feet 
from the ground, where she had evidently gone to 
observe, but not to be observed. 

The bark canoes used by Indians are called “ wood- 
skins ” by the Creoles, and are made of one piece of 
bark, stripped from a tree called the purple-heart 
(Copaifera publiflora). The bark of the locust tree 
or simiri (Hymenca courbaril) is also sometimes used. 
This bark is from one-eighth to one-fourth of an inch 
in thickness, and very heavy, being of greater specific 
gravity than water; so that in the event of the canoes 
shipping water to any extent, it sinks immediately, 
leaving its occupants to find their way ashore. 

They are ticklish things to travel in at first, and to 
stand up in one, until acquainted with its freaks, is a 
dangerous experiment, ending in an undignified exit 
over one side, the canoe shooting away in the opposite 
direction. i 

To make one of these woodskins, a large purple- 
heart tree is cut down, and the bark of the requisite 
length taken off. A wedge-shaped piece is then cut 
out of the trough-shaped bark, from the top down- 
wards, at a distance of three feet or so from both ends 
on each side. The ends are then raised till the edges 
of the cuts meet, when holes are pierced on either 
hand, at a distance of six inches from the cut, and 
numbers of turns of a strong. withe or liana, called 
Mamurie, passed through them and made fast in a 
neat manner to a small round stick placed along the 
inside. Two strong pieces of wood fastened across at 


Czar, II. WOODSKINS. 21 


the splits prevent the sides from closing in. The 
ends are then trimmed down level with the sides, and 
a ticklish but serviceable little craft is turned out. 
The seats are made of curved pieces of the same bark, 
and are very low. The whole process of making one 
of these woodskins, including the drying of the bark, 
occupies a space of three weeks. In this period must, 
I think, be included some loss of time from laziness on 
the part of the Indian canoe builder. Woodskins vary 
in size, but usually are from fifteen to twenty-five feet 
in length, and an ordinary-sized one will carry three 
or four people with their hammocks and provisions. 

The absence of visible mammal life was very 
striking in this region, though there could be no 
doubt that the forest bordering the river abounded in 
game both large and small, such as tapir, deer, wild 
hog, labba, accourie, &c., for their tracks were numer- 
ous. The sound of the paddles against the sides of 
the boat, and the talking of the men, would no doubt 
scare them, and cause those near the river’s edge to 
seek the depths of the forest. 

By day we saw two or three species of kingfisher, 
two kinds of small swallows, hawks, the red-headed 
vulture, and the curi-curi or river ibis; whilst in the 
morning and evening scattered bands of screeching 
parrots crossed the river, going to and returning from 
their feeding grounds. The river ibis (Jdis infuscata) 
gets its name of curi-curi from its cry, which resembles 
that word repeated a few times. When they utter 
their cries in the evening, the natives say it is a sure 
sign that rain will fall during the night. They are 
not quite so large as the beautiful scarlet ibis in- 


22 ON THE CUYUNI. Cmar. IL 


habiting the coast region, and are of a dull olive-green 
colour, with light-green bills and legs. They drill 
holes in the mud-banks with their long bills for 
worms, feeding after the manner of snipe. 

From a long straight reach of the river we obtained 
a distant view of a range of hills called Waikuie, and 
near its end, on the south bank, arrived at the landing 
of an Indian village called Weynamou. 

Taking Peterson with me I started from the Wey- 
namou landing and walked to the village, which 
consisted of four or five palm-thatched houses with . 
open sides, situated in a clearing, in which dwelt 
some Ackawoise Indians. 

I was struck with the manner in which the men 
of the place wore their hair, allowing it to grow 
long and fall far below their shoulders, giving them a 
very feminine appearance. The head man had his 
hair bound round at the end with cord in such a 
manner that it stuck out at a right angle behind. 
The old fellow’s corporation projected in front quite 
as far as his pigtail did behind, and gave him a most 
ridiculous appearance. Neither men nor women had 
eyebrows, having, according to Indian custom, plucked 
them out. Their upper lips and both sides of the 
mouth were tattooed with blue curved lines.. The 
dress of the women was exceedingly simple, consist- 
ing only of a small cotton and bead-work apron, made 
fast to a cotton cord round the body above the hips. 
The apron, called a ‘“‘queyou,” is manufactured by the 
wearer in a most ingenious manner, each bead being 
slipped on the cotton thread in its proper place as it 
is being woven. The patterns of these dresses are 


Cuap. II. AFTERNOON STORMS. 23 


marked out with red, blue, and white beads, and they 
generally have an ornamented border. 

Finding that we could carry all the remaining 
provisions in three boats, we sent the “ Cataboo ” and 
her crew of six men back to the Settlement, and con- 
tinued our journey up river. 

On the afternoon that we reached Wakupang 
cataract we had a heavy rain storm, accompanied by 
thunder and lightning, which lasted for two hours; 
and on nearly every remaining afternoon of our stay 
on this river we had similar heavy rainfalls. The 
nights and forenoons were fine, but in the afternoons 
great masses of dark clouds spread gradually over the 
sky, covering it, as it were, with an inky pall. Out 
of the dark clouds came flashes of lightning followed 
by heavy peals of thunder, and then came a strong 
breeze bearing with it a storm of pelting rain. This 
would last for an hour, and by that time the storm 
had passed over to another tract of country, leaving a 
slight drizzling rain behind. Then the sky would be- 
come clear overhead, and the sun set in most magni- 
ficent golden and crimson clouds. These rains saturated 
the ground with its covering of dead leaves, making 
our forest camping places damp and noisome; while 
the drip from the leaves overhead lasted for a consi- 
derable time after the shower had passed away. 

Our supply of farinaceous food had by the time we 
reached the mouth of the Appa river decreased consi- 
derably in quantity, and it became necessary to pur- 
chase all we could get, in the shape of cassava bread, 
from the Indians living in the neighbourhood. There- 
fore, when we arrived at a landing place a little 


24 ON THE CUYUNI. Cuar. II. 


beyond the Marupa river, we proceeded to the village 
to induce the people to prepare some for us. On 
arriving at the place we found only two temporary 
huts, situated in an old plantation, partially overgrown 
with shrubs, vines, and weeds, and occupied by one 
man, four women, and five children. This man had 
come from his place higher up river to gather the re- 
maining cassava roots (Janipha Manihot) in this old 
plantation. His wife spoke English, having been 
brought up at Bartika Grove, and wore clothing in 
the shape of a loose cotton gown. She said that her 
husband’s name was Edward and her’s Caroline. 
Edward was in a most delightful state of extreme sub- 
jection to her, and appeared to consult her in every- 
thing. One pleasing feature in the British Guiana 
Indians is that, as a rule, they treat their women well, 
evidently looking upon them as equals, and not making 
slaves of them as is the custom of many other wild 
tribes. These people promised to make us some cassava 
bread, for which we had to remain a day, the process 
of manufacture of that article being a rather slow 
one. The Indians take the roots of the cassava, peel 
them, and then grate them on boards called Tumaries, 
into which small sharp angular pieces of felstone have 
been inserted. After this, they place the grated mass 
in a long cylindrical basket-work tube, called Tenge or 
Matapi, by which all the poisonous juice of the cassava 
is got rid of, leaving a white coarse meal behind. 
This meal is sifted through a basket-work sieve, then 
spread upon a large flat stone or iron pan placed over a 
fire, and baked. Only half a minute is taken to bake 
a cake two feet in diameter and a quarter of an inch 


Cuar. II. LABBA HUNTING. 25 


thick—the usual size of cassava bread of Indian manu- 
facture. The heat has the power of making each grain 
adhere to those around it, thus forming a firm and 
exceedingly nutritious cake. Eaten just after it is 
cooked—when still warm—it is palatable, but, if kept 
till the following day, it becomes tough and almost 
tasteless. On the contrary, if dried for a few hours in 
the sun upon the same day that it is cooked, it becomes 
hard and crisp, has a sweet nutty flavour, and will 
keep for months. 

During our stay Edward went with one of our men 
to procure some game for us, and returned with three 
fine labba (Celogenis paca). He had two dogs of 
true Indian breed, most unlikely-looking black-and- 
tan curs, and, though larger than bull-terriers, ap- 
peared to have some of that breed in them. One was 
a weak creature with plenty of prominent ribs, and a 
white film over each eye nearly obstructing its sight. 
This film is frequently seen on the eyes of hunting 
dogs, and is produced by injuries received from blows 
of twigs and vines, when rushing headlong through 
the undergrowth on the track of game. Indians take 
great pains in training their dogs, and have some 
taught to follow deer, others labba, and some only to 
hunt the tapir. Other dogs, whose education has been 
partly neglected, will follow the track of the first 
animal they come across, and this, in nine cases out 
of ten, turns out to be a small rodent, about the size of 
a rabbit, called the accourie (Dasyprocta aguti). The 
flesh of this rodent is not very highly esteemed when 
better is to be had, especially as it is never in good 
condition. Our men bad a saying, that the accouries 


26 ON THE CUYUNI. Cuar. II. 


are fat the same year that the bamboo blossoms, an 
occurrence which, they say, takes place about once in 
forty years. The flesh of the labba is considered the 
most delicate of all bush animals, and is therefore 
much sought after. This animal is also a rodent 
about the size of a hare, but with a stouter body and 
rat-like head. It is nocturnal in its habits, spending 
the day in its burrow under tree roots near the edges 
of rivers. These sleeping chambers are not very long, 
and have two entrances. The huntsman rams a thin 
pole in at one end, which startles the occupant, 
causing it to fly precipitately from the other, like 
a ball from a cannon, closely followed by the dog. 
The labba, when close pressed, takes to the water, 
and whilst swimming is hunted down by the Indian 
sportsman in his woodskin. Sometimes the animal 
escapes by diving, and hiding amongst fallen brush- 
wood on the river’s edge, but more frequently falls a 
victim to the arrow of the Indian. 

The people of the place brought out to our camp 
the cassava they had made, together with some plan- 
tains and a bottle of crab oil, all of which I paid for 
with articles of barter from our store of “ negotiz,”’ 
consisting of beads, calico, knives, pins, fish-hooks, 
powder, shot, &c. The crab oil, which is made from 
the seeds of the crabwood tree (Carapa Guianensis), I 
used for the boat lamps, in which it burnt well, giving 
a good bright light. They also brought out their 
hammocks and provisions, packed in basket-work 
affairs, called Urutehs or Cartoweries, and covered 
with plantain leaves to keep off the rain; and an- 
nounced their intention of travelling in our company 


Cuar. II. CASSAREEP. 27 


to their village. Their provisions consisted of cassava 
bread, barbacued fish and game, and the everlasting . 
pepper-pot. The Indian woman who heads a house- 
hold has charge of the earthenware pepper-pot, the 
contents of which consist of red peppers boiled with 
cassareep and water into a sort of fiery soup. Into 
this they dip their cassava bread before eating it, and 
thus obtain a highly-flavoured relish. Sometimes fish 
or game is boiled with the peppers. Cassareep is 
made from the poisonous juice of the cassava root, 
boiled down to the consistence of treacle, and has the 
property of preserving animal substances boiled in it 
for a length of time, besides giving them a very 
pleasant flavour. Indian pepper-pot would not be 
bad if those fiery hot peppers were not put in with so 
heavy a hand. 

After camping one night two of the men went a 
short distance up river to shoot fish, and soon re- 
turned, bringing two Indians with them, whom they 
had found with a party encamped on a sand-beach. 
They were of the Ackawoise tribe, and one spoke 
a little English and Dutch. This man had a piece of 
reed passed through a hole in the cartilage of his 
nose, and two similar pieces through holes in the lobe 
of each ear, the ends of which rested on the ends of 
the nose piece. With these ornaments, and stripes 
of red paint on his face, he looked, by the light of the 
camp fires, a smart sort of a savage. I asked him to 
go with us as guide to the Puruni, to which he readily 
assented. He asked for salt fish and “ cavendish 
tobacco,” and soon after returned with his companion 


to their encampment. 


28 ON THE CUYUNI. "Guar. IL. 


That night a small variety of termites or wood-ants 
took possession of everything that we had brought on 
shore, and in the morning I found my hat swarming 
with them. They usually live in large round nests, 
which they build of a brown earthy material in the 
forks of tree branches, or on the sides of tree stems. 
From these they build covered ways leading in all 
directions, and bore chambers through dead wood and 
dry tree branches. At night they sally forth in great 
bands, seeking what they can devour, and, if they get 
into a canister of provisions, build their nasty covered 
ways all over its contents. 

When we continued our journey in the morning, we 
stopped for a short time at the Indian camp. This was 
composed of two small temporary huts, called Banaboos, 
made of poles stuck deeply in the sand, with palm- 
thatched roofs; the poles were firmly lashed together 
with lianas or “-bush-ropes,” made of the pliant woody 
stems of climbing vines, seen at every turn in the 
forest, twining round the stems of trees or hanging 
in fantastic loops from their branches. Some of these 
are brittle and cannot be tied in knots, whilst others 
are very pliant, and when twisted till their bark 
cracks, will not break when knotted. To the best 
kind of bush-rope the name of mamurie has been 
given. 

There were eight Indians at the camp, amongst 
whom was my friend of the night before, who, with 
a boy, was ready to accompany us. But how different 
he appeared in the daylight to what he did in the dim 
firelight the previous night. His naked body was now 
seen to be covered with patches of a skin disease very 


Cuap. II, DESCEND CATARACTS. 29 


, common amongst Indian tribes, which gave him an 
exceedingly mangy appearance. 

We reached Otomong creek in the forenoon of the 
24th of September, and after exploring Otomong bill, 
commenced our return journey. The water of the 
shaded creek was so cool, in comparison with that of 
the Cuyuni, that I tried the respective temperatures 
of each, and found the former to be 78° Fahr. and the 
latter 88°, the temperature of the air in the shade 
being 85° and in the sun 110°. The cool nights at 
this time of year do not lower the temperature of the 
water more than two or three degrees, so that in 
the early morning the air is much cooler than the 
water, and one is not the least refreshed by a bath 
in the river at that time. 

On approaching the head of the first cataract in our 
descent, the bowman and steersman both stood up, and 
gazing intently down the long slope of foaming water, 
agreed upon a joint course of action. The word was 
then passed to “ give way,” and the boat bounded for- 
ward under the strong, steady strokes of the six 
paddlers. It was an exciting moment to me as the 
boat, impelled by the paddlers and the rush of water, 
dipped over the smooth brink of the cataract and flew 
along the short slope of water where it was commencing 
to foam; then was half submerged in a set of short, 
high waves, each of which gave it a heavy blow that 
shook it from stem to stern; and finally was shot out 
of the great surging body of water into the still pool 
below. 

Whilst shooting a cataract the bowman keeps a 
sharp look-out for rocks, and guides the bow accord- 


30 ON THE CUYUNI. Cuar. II. 


ingly, skilfully aided by the steersman, who can tell 
by the slightest movement of the bowman’s paddle 
which way he is required to work his own, so as to 
give the former every assistance and at the same time 
keep full control over the boat. The large channels 
are generally free from sunken rocks, but the great 
dip that the boat takes in descending causes its bow 
to be buried under water for an instant, and the great 
danger to be apprehended is in shipping water. In 
the narrow channels, where the water is shallower, 
there is danger of running upon sunken rocks. Their 
slope is much longer than in the larger ones, and often 
at the foot of one the boat rushes headlong to the top 
of another, and away down it at great speed, the bow- 
man and steersman working hard to keep the boat 
under command, and the paddlers pulling like fiends 
with strong, quick strokes. 

When we met with a cataract too steep for the boats 
to run in this way, they were lowered down in shallow 
water by the tow-rope, two or three men wading 
alongside, the others holding on by the rope and 
lowering slowly. 

We passed a place one afternoon where the river 
was studded with high granite rocks, two of which rose 
ten feet or so above the -level of the highest floods ; 
their upper portions covered with a white lichen. Our 
guide, Edward, knowing of their existence and of some 
superstition connected with them, turned his head 
away and would not look at them. Eruma, one of 
our Caribs, took some tobacco, and dipping it into the 
water leant back and squeezed the juice into his eyes, 
and as soon as the tears thus produced had subsided 


oe 


Cuar. H. ` DEATH OF INDIAN. 31 


he calmly gazed upon the rocks. By this procedure 
he proved himself to be a peaiman or sorcerer, as no 
Indian, not having gone through the severe ordeal 
entitling him to be such, could have gazed on these 
rocks without imagining that some great evil would 
befall him. nay & 

When we reached the path end leading to the 
Puruni we landed and formed a camp. I went down 
to the village of Cicimong to get some cassava bread 
that the Indians there had promised to make for us, 
and found the only occupants of the place to consist of 
an old man, a lot of women, and one young man. The 
old man was very feeble, and when I entered his house 
was reclining in his hammock. He arose from it im- 
mediately after my entrance and went to a basket 
containing his treasures, such as iron arrow-heads, 
balls of cotton, &c., and began to fumble amongst 
its contents. I left him thus employed, and went 
out to one of the other houses to pay for the cassava, 
when one of the men came and told me that the old 
man had just died. He said that the poor creature 
had got back into his hammock, stretched himself out, 
turned his head on one side, and with a deep sigh 
breathed his last. 

On learning that the old man was dead, only one of 
the women showed the least concern, and she merely 
went to look at him, quickly returning with tears in 
her eyes. The others went on with their various 
occupations as if nothing had happened, seeming 
quite unconscious of the fact that a spirit had passed 
from amongst them to its long rest. Two of them 
were engaged in the disgusting operation of chewing 


32 ON THE CUYUNI. Caap. II. 


up toasted cassava bread, and spitting it into a large 
pot simmering on a low fire, the first step in the 
production of their great national beverage called 
Paiworie. In the pot was some cassava juice diluted 
with water. They boil this mixture for about twelve 
hours, and then put it in earthen jars, where vinous 
fermentation takes place. As soon as that ceases it is 
ready for use. 

The young Indian above mentioned was worn away 
to a mere skeleton, the effects, he said, of some poison 
secretly administered by an enemy. His eyes were 
large and preternaturally bright, and it cut one to the 
heart to see a fine young fellow like that lying patiently 
awaiting his early doom at the hand of the most cowardly 
of all murderers, the Kanaima or secret poisoner. 

Returning to our camp, we made preparations for 
our journey overland to the head of a branch of the 
Puruni, called Cartoonie, where we were told we 
should find Indians to take us in woodskins down 
that river to the Puruni, and on down the latter river 
to the Mazaruni. 

Each man made a basket of green palm leaves, in 
which he placed and lashed his load. This basket of 
peculiar construction is open at the top and back, and 
is called a “ waiarie.” It is strapped on the man’s 
back by three bands made of the under bark of a tree. 
Two of these bands are tied on in such a way that the 
man’s arms can be passed through them, which causes 
the weight of the load to rest on the shoulders; this 
means of support is chiefly used when halting. The 
true support is the third band, which is fastened to the 
top of each upper corner of the flat back of the basket, 


Crap. Il. WAIARIES. 33 


and passes across the carrier’s forehead. With the 
pack thus suspended, fitting flat to the back, a prac- 
tised hand bends well forward, and goes along through 
the bush-path at an astonishingly quick pace. These 
waiaries, being made of green leaves, soon wither and 
break up. Indians, as a rule, all possess good, strong 
load-baskets of a similar shape to waiaries, called 
cartoweries, made of cane-work strengthened with 
pieces of wood, which last for months. 


34 DOWN THE PURUNI. Cuar. IlI. 


CHAPTER III. 


DOWN THE PURUNI. 


March out of Camp — Indian Path — Ticks — Camp in Forest — Tricked 
by Guide— Fleas and Jiggers in unoccupied House — Mogoose — 
Wind Storm — Start down Cartoonie in Canoes — Otters — Get 
jammed under Log — Humming-bird Nests — Reach Puruni River — 
Maam — Stung by Ants — Kill a Capybara — Scorpions — Reach the 
Mazaruni. 


On Sunday, the 27th of September, we commenced 
our march through the gloomy forest, along a narrow 
Indian path, with the mangy Indian, whose name was 
Joseph, for our guide. We certainly were a motley 
crew as we filed off one by one out of camp, twenty- 
five in number, leaving only two men behind to take 
care of the boats. There was the guide in a blue- 
striped shirt, his only article of clothing; there were 
two Indians, whom we had hired, dressed in nature’s 
garb, barring the “lap”; and there were our men 
in white singlets, osnaburg trousers, felt hats, and 
bare feet. As for Mr. Sawkins and myself, we were 
dressed in the lightest costume. 

Our way lay over a slightly undulating country, 
traversed by small brooks and medium-sized streams, 
and covered with a forest of the highest possible 
growth. The enormous spreading tops of the giant 
trees, meeting together above, completely excluded 
the direct rays of the sun, leaving only a subdued 


Cumar. III. PRIMEVAL FOREST. 35 


light around us amongst. their stems. Beneath the 
large trees were others of smaller growth, the progeny 
of the former, growing slowly up to treehood, and 
struggling hard in the deep shade for existence. Then 
beneath all this there frequently came a third growth 
of still younger trees, ranging from six inches to six feet 
in height, placed close together. The tree stems and 
branches were clothed with a profusion of parasitic 
plants, mosses, and ferns; while huge snake-like forms 
of vines and creepers encircled them, or hung gracefully 
in festoons from their tops. No grass grew in the 
deep shade of the forest, but a thin covering of 
decayed leaves and rotten tree brauches strewed the 
soil. At distances of every fifty yards or so lay the 
stem of a tree, decayed and moss-grown, ranging in 
diameter from one foot to four feet, or more. In hurry- 
ing along we had to be continually climbing over one 
of these huge trunks and stepping upon slippery tree- 
roots, which made our walk a tiresome proceeding. 
There was a great sameness in the restricted forest 
views, one glade being like another, and fifty yards of 
the path like every other fifty yards. Where there 
was no undergrowth the walking was not so bad, but 
where it prevailed to any extent it grew so close on 
either hand that we were brushed by its branches as 
we passed along, gathering a few spiders, ants, and 
caterpillars on our necks every now and then, and, 
what was still worse, a tick or two at intervals. Of 
all the horrible insects in the forest that tap the 
human claret, the ticks (Izodid@) are, without doubt, 
-the most loathsome. They are of all sizes, from a — 
pin’s point to a shirt button, and all bite most 
D 2 


86 DOWN THE PURUNI. Cumar. III. 


viciously. A small kind, called “ Bête rouge” by 
the Creoles, is very troublesome; it is the counter- 
part of the English harvest bug. 

On the first night of our journey we camped on the 
banks of a little stream, in the cool water of which I 
took my usual evening bath, had dinner, and a pipe 
or two before turning into my hammock for the night. 
The men hung their hammocks in a group about 
twenty yards from ours, and being weary after their 
day’s tramp did not talk much, so that soon after 
8 P.M, quietness fell upon the camp. Intense darkness 
reigned aretind, for the camp fires soon burnt out, and 
‘then’ I fully realized the boundless extent of my bed- 
room as I peered round into the thick blackness before 
falling into a deep sleep: 

Awaking at daybreak next morning, I jumped out 
of my hammock, finding all the camp astir, the men 
letting loose hammocks, packing up loads, &c., and 
the cooks boiling coffee for us and hot water for the 
men. The meal for the men at this time of day 
consisted of two ounces of sugar, with which they 
sweetened their hot water, and a piece of cassava 
bread or a biscuit each. 

Every man being ready, the guide went to the 
front, and the march was resumed. On, on, we went 
along the winding path, brushing against dew-laden 
undergrowth, over fallen trees, across little streams 
with a hop, and over large ones on slippery logs, 
upon which we could with difficulty balance ourselves. 
In three hours after leaving camp we marched out of 
the forest into a small clearing, in which an Indian 
house, the home of Edward and Caroline, was situated. 


Caap. III. TRICKED BY GUIDE. 37 


They had come home the day before, and we now 
found the latter busy with her household duties, while 
the former reclined gracefully in his hammock. Off 
went the men’s loads, and down we all squatted to 
rest and dry ourselves in the sun. Here we regaled 
ourselves upon a small forest deer, called Wiriebiserie 
(Cervus humilis), which Edward had shot. This 
animal is of a reddish-fawn colour, with white spotted 
flanks, and has two short, straight horns. 

The path from the Cuyuni to this place was well 
beaten and distinct, but beyond this it was the faintest 
trail imaginable. We prevailed upon Edward, after 
much persuasion, to come and show us the track. 
Away we all went again, and plodded on for two or 
three hours, coming to a small stream, at which 
Edward stopped and informed us in a most positive 
manner that it was the last place at which we could 
get water that night, and that we would bave to camp. 
It being out of the question to camp anywhere except 
in the vicinity of such a necessary article as water, 
we did so, though it was at such an early hour. 
Edward then disappeared from our midst, and we 
never saw him again. Next day, when we continued 
our journey and passed over stream after stream, we 
saw how thoropghly we had been “ sold ” by that wily 
savage. ‘The fact was that he did not like to refuse 
to guide us, and therefore came ; but, wishing to get 
back to his house that same night, he concocted the 
story of the scarcity of water to induce us to camp 
early, and thus give him time to retrace his steps 
before night set in. 

With Joseph as guide we found our way with diffi- 


eo 


38 DOWN THE PURUNI. Cuap. IIL. 


culty along the faint trail till we crossed a large 
stream — the Cartoonie— where we struck a well- 
beaten path, and soon after came into a large clearing 
of many acres in extent, in the middle of which was 
a small village of three houses. In these were seven- 
teen Indians of the Ackawoise tribe, who seemed 
rather astounded at the sudden advent of our party 
amongst them. 

As the village houses were small we did not take 
up our quarters in them, but camped on the edge of a 
stream in the forest, on the path beyond. Henry 
Cornelius, one of our bowmen, acted as interpreter, 
and explained to the Indians that we wanted them to 
take us down to the Mazaruni river, to which they 
assented, promising to go to the next place to procure 
woodskins for the journey. 

On the following morning we sent back most of the 
men to the boats to take them down to the Penal 
Settlement for a supply of stores, with instructions to 
rejoin us at a village called Cowenamou, on the 
Mazaruni river. Our three Caribs accompanied them 
to the boats for the purpose of bringing back three 
loads of stores. The morning had broken dull and 
cloudy, and rain fell steadily all day ; we were there- 
fore confined to the restricted areas ugder our small 
canvas awnings, where we spent a dull and gloomy 
day. The dull stillness of our camp after the de- 
parture of our band of men, broken only by the cease- 
less drip of rain upon the sodden ground, made our 
situation anything but cheerful. Our party of re- 
tainers now consisted of only five individuals, viz. 
Vincente, a Portuguese; Cameron, a mulatto; Ben, an 


Cuar. III. JIGGERS. 39 


Arowaak; Pedro, a Spanish Arowaak; and Joseph, an 
Ackawoise. When I turned out next morning, finding 
the rain still falling, I made up my mind to go up 
to the village and “pig” with the savages—anything 
would be better than remaining in that dismal swamp. 
Just then Ben returned from a voyage of discovery 
along the path leading away from our camp, with a 
joyful expression of countenance betokening good news. 
“ Why, sir,” he said, “ there is a good house not far 
off, and it taint got no one in it.” I immediately 
went with him to see it, and, plodding through mud 
and water, climbed a slight eminence to a large open 
shed, situated in the midst of a clearing. I halted 
close to it, and proposed that he should take a look at 
the floor for insects. He walked boldly in, hesitated, 
looked down at his bare fect, and exclaiming, “ Jig- 
gers and dog fleas full da here,” fled precipitately. I 
may here mention that the jigger or chigoe (Pullex 
penetrans) is a small flea that burrows under the toe- 
nails, where it forms an egg-bag almost the size of a 
small pea, which has to be extracted with a needle, 
an operation giving a considerable amount of pain. 

I remarked to Ben in a casual way, “Surely the 
natives have some way of getting rid of these insects; 
just think the matter over in your mind, and don’t 
lose sight of the fact that it depends upon you whether 
we sleep in the moist swamp to-night or under this 
good roof.” He proposed two ways of performing the 
operation, one was to “bun them,” as he called it, 
and the other was to sweep them out. I seized upon 
both ideas; we would burn them first, and sweep out 
their roasted carcasses afterwards. 


40 DOWN THE PURUNI. Czar. III. 


Fortunately there was a heap of dry palm leaves in 
the house, of which a few bunches were made into 
brooms, and the rest scattered over the floor. If any- 
one had been looking at us from a short distance 
when performing this operation, he would have come 
to the conclusion that we were both demented, from 
the way in which we skipped about, darting out every 
now and then to brush off our enemies from our 
clothes. Then we set fire to the leaves, and had the 
whole place covered with flames a foot in height—a 
grand and satisfactory though short-lived flare up. 
At this stage of the proceedings we were reinforced 
by Pedro and Vincente, who on the subsiding of the 
flames swept out the residuary matter, composed of 
carcasses, ashes, and dust. After the crusade was 
over, a few hundreds of the enemy were left, but 
these we did not mind. It is a curious and sad fact, 
as I have always found to my cost, that when Indians 
leave their houses for a time the dust on the floors 
becomes infested with jiggers and fleas. The former 
come from the eggs dropped from the jigger egg-bags 
in the toes of both Indians and their dogs, and the 
latter from eggs dropped by the fleas of the dogs. 

We then had all our effects taken to the cleansed 
house, and were in complete possession of our new 
abode. By midday the rain had all passed away, 
and the sun shone brightly. In the cassava clearing 
I routed a troop of gray monkeys (Cebus apella?), and 
soon after saw some more in a grove of trumpet trees 
(Cecropia peltata), close to the house, where there 
also was a lot of large toucans (Rhampastos toco), and 
a beautiful fire-bird (Phenicircus carnifex). 


Caap. IiI. TROPICAL MORNINGS. 41 


The following morning was foggy from six to seven 
o'clock; but there was a fresh, invigorating feeling in 
the air which made one feel cheerful, and ready for 
any amount of work. Soon after the fog vanished, 
and the sun came out. As he rose higher and higher 
the inclination for bodily exertion became feebler, and 
disappeared entirely by the time he had gained the 
meridian. Those early mornings in the tropics were 
delightful, and the remembrance of them makes me 
sigh for them as for “the touch of a vanished hand,’ 
Numbers of small birds visited the low cotton (Gos- 
sypium) and papaw (Carica Papaya) trees plauted in 
the vicinity of the house, in the early morning, and 
were very tame. Amongst these were cashew birds, 
blue sackies, tompitchers (Saltator sp.), and qua- 
quaras (Thamnophilus sp.), all insignificant-looking, 
but very lively birds. The two last mentioned whistle 
loudly, producing sounds from which they have de- 
rived their names. 

At noon the two Indians, who had gone on to Car- 
toonie creek, returned, and said that they had pro- 
cured woodskins for us. They brought an old man 
with them, who was the owner of the house we oc- 
cupied, and who, now appearing in the character of 
visitor to those so coolly inhabiting his domicile, was 
made welcome accordingly. That evening they com- 
menced a “grand paiworie” at the village, drinking 
their national beverage, and dancing all night long. 
Next morning they visited us in a body, all looking 
more or less seedy from their night’s debauch. Their 
bodies were daubed with red paint made from the 
Arnotto seeds (Bixa orellana), and dyed with pat- 


42 DOWN THE PURUNI. Cuar. III. 


terns in black, produced by the juice of the fruit of 
the lana tree (Genipa Americana). Nearly all wore 
anklets and armlets of white or blue beads, besides 
other ornaments, such as necklaces of peccary and 
labba teeth. They were exceedingly honest, and 
though they handled and examined everything they 
could lay hands on amongst our baggage, they re- 
stored each article to its place again. One man, 
named Mogoose, who was to be our guide down the 
Puruni—an intelligent-looking fellow, with an open, 
smiling countenance—got hold of a piece of news- 
paper from amongst our things, and pointed out to me 
the letters S, O, and E, which he named correctly. He 
then repeated the letters of the alphabet, omitting, 
however, a letter here and there, for which he substi- 
tuted one of his own. He became a great nuisance, 
inducing me to read aloud a line of the newspaper, 
which he repeated after me. Like a parrot, he could 
pronounce the words without having the remotest idea 
of their meaning. Joseph informed me that Mogoose, 
when a boy, had spent some time at Bartika Grove, 
and had then gone to school. 

Late that afternoon our Caribs returned with their 
loads, and brought the intelligence that our men had 
started on their voyage down the Cuyuni. 

After a short walk next day, over a swampy tract 
of country, we reached a new clearing, planted with 
young cassava, and, going on a short distance, came 
to the creek where our woodskins were moored. 
There we camped on a slight rising ground, and a 
little before dusk were visited by a storm of wind and 
rain, with thunder and lightning. The wind blew so 


Cumar. IlI. WIND STORM. 43 


violently that numbers of large trees in the neighbour- 
hood came crashing to the ground, while one flash of 
lightning struck a tree close by. It was utterly im- 
possible, in the midst of such din and danger, to 
remain under my awning, in case a tree should come 
upon it like a deadfall. I therefore stood out in the 
storm, with my servant, watching the swaying trees 
around us, ready to bolt should any near give signs 
of coming down. An old dead tree close, by leant in 
the direction of my awning, and had it come down 
would have demolished my nightly shelter; but, not 
having leaves to catch the wind, it fortunately with- 
stood the fury of the blast. 

Next morning the whole party, with stores and 
baggage, were stowed away in five .good-sized wood- 
skins, four of which contained our party, while the 
fifth carried two men with their wives and children, 
who appeared to be only going to accompany us to 
the next village. My canoe was manned by one of 
our Caribs, named Yackarawa, who sat near the bow, 
and Ben, who sat aft, and both paddled and steered. 
The Cartoonie, being a narrow stream, large trees had 
fallen across it in many places, obstructing the passage 
of the woodskins. These large dead trees are called 
“ Tacoobas,” and are the curse of narrow rivers, Some 
lay across from bank to bank, and left room for us by 
stooping to pass beneath, while others lying half sub- 
merged formed perfect bars across the stream. Over 
these we had to haul the canoes, after landing their 
loads on the bank, or on the log itself. Thus our pro- 
gress was greatly retarded, and became tedious and 
monotonous to a degree as the day wore on. 


44 DOWN THE PURUNI. Casar. ILI. 


We passed a red cliff and hill on one side of the 
river, where Mogoose inforined us there was a colony of 
wild dogs. Ben told me that they were of small size, 
with drooping ears like a lapdog’s, and of a brown 
colour. They live in burrows in the ground, and bark 
like dogs. As I did not see them I cannot speak posi- 
tively as to their identity, but believe them to be the 
South American wild dog (Canis Azarae). Late that 
afternoon we reached the landing of a place called 
Ositiquah, where we camped for the night, our guides 
going to the village to sleep. Some of our men went 
there also, and, on returning next day, told me that 
the people had kept a kind of wake during the night 
over an old woman who had just died, and about mid- 
night they all had a pepper-pot feed. They came out to 
camp, accompanied by eighteen villagers, who brought 
provisions for sale. The Indians who had accompanied 
us, with the exception of Mogoose and another man, 
remained at Ositiquah; and after we had paid those 
to whom the woodskins belonged for their hire we 
continued our journey. 

We frequently came up with parties of otters 
(Pteronura Sanbachi), composed of from five to eight 
individuals, which tried to dispute the passage of the 
river with us. They used to approach the canoes, and, 
all popping their heads out of water together, would snort 
and snarl as if trying to intimidate us. Seeing that our 
advance was not to be checked, they took long dives 
away, and quickly disappeared beyond a bend in the 
river, to be seen no more. They were of a brownish 
colour, with a patch of white under the throat. I 
could not resist the temptation of having a shot at one 


Cuar. III. PERILOUS POSITION. 45 


occasionally, but never could tell whether it took 
effect, for, as they always disappeared beneath the 
surface at the instant of the report, it was difficult to 
say whether they had dived at the flash or were killed 
and sank to the bottom. 

On one occasion, as I was eagerly endeavouring to 
get a shot at one of these animals, we did not observe 
a log lying across the river, only about two feet above 
the surface of the water, to which the canoe was borne 
by the force of the current, till we were close to it. I 
had just time to deposit my gun in the bottom of the 
canoe, and duck my head as we went under it, before 
either of my men had time to check our way. The 
log was lower than it looked, and as my seat was 
higher than that of the Carib in the bow, he just 
passed under while it caught me on the back of the 
neck, as I leant forward with both hands on the 
gunwale on either side, giving me a heavy blow and 
bringing the woodskin to a dead stand. There I was 
jammed hard and fast, unable even to move my hands 
from the sides of the canoe, over which the water 
poured, threatening to sink us. In this helpless state 
the thought flushed through my mind that nothing 
but the sinking of the canoe could extricate me, and 
though by this I should lose all my worldly goods, yet 
anything was preferable to the nightmare sort of con- 
dition I was in. Bent down as I was, I could not 
make my voice heard as I tried to shout to Ben to 
shove the woodskin back. He, meanwhile, had left 
his seat and was doing his best by pushing the end of 
his paddle, with all his might, against the log. His 
endeavours, facilitated by the settling down of the 


46 DOWN THE PURUNL Car. II. 


canoe, which eased the pressure of my back against 
the log, were eminently successful, and we were 
happily extricated from our perilous position. We set 
to work to bail out the water as rapidly as possible, 
being just on the point of sinking; and when this 
had been accomplished, and the pain in my back 
had subsided, we enjoyed a hearty laugh over our 
mishap. 

On our third day’s journey we landed at a hilly 
range abutting on the river, and ascended it to a 
height of 800 feet above the sea. The foliage of the 
magnificent trees, with which it was crowned, ob- 
structed our view of the surrounding country; but 
through gaps here and there we obtained glimpses of 
high ranges, looking of a pale-blue colour, in the 
distance to the westward. Upon the ground, beneath 
a large tree, we obtained some small-sized fallen fruit, 
which had an acid flavour, was of a yellow colour, 
and somewhat resembled the sappodilla (Achras) in 
structure. The Indians showed us the milk tree 
(Tabernemontana utilis), from an incision in the bark 
of which we obtained a good flow of thick, white, 
creamy sap, of a rich nutty flavour, but a little of it 
went a long way. ‘The tree was large, its stem per- 
fectly cylindrical in form, without any root buttresses, 
and its bark of a dull, blackish colour. 

Small singing birds were very numerous along the 
edges of the river, which trolled out their songs late of 
an afternoon, before seeking their perches for the 
night. Humming-birds, of four or five varieties, were 
frequently met with ; some were exceedingly bold and 
pugnacious, following us for short distances and poising 


Cap. IlI. TIGER-BIRDS. 47 


themselves close above our heads for a few seconds 
at a time, as if resenting our intrusion upon their 
domain. On two places, where a wild fig (Clusia 
alba) had grown upon the branch of a dead tree, which 
rose above water in mid-stream, I saw the beautiful 
little nest of the king humming-bird ( Trochilus pella). 
They were made of the yellow silky substance which 
surrounds the seeds in the pod of the silk cotton tree 
(Bombaz ceiba). We also saw a small kind of grebe 
(Heliornis Surinamensis), ducklars (Plotius anhinga), 
curicuries, tiger-birds, and two species of kingfisher. 
There were two kinds of tiger-birds ( Tigrisoma tigri- 
num and T. Brasiliense), one of a light brownish-yellow 
colour, with black bars across each feather, and the 
other of a dark brown. They utter their call, which 
somewhat resembles that of the jaguar, early in the 
morning and late in the evening, and thus have 
obtained the name of tiger-bird. 

In the evening of our fourth day's journey in wood- 
skins, we left the turbid water of the Cartoonie and 
entered upon the clear, black water of the Puruni, 
immediately above a fall called Pyuca. Next 
morning our stores were carried to the foot of the fall, 
and the woodskins dragged over the rocks a distance 
of some sixty yards. This fall is not high, but very 
picturesque, as the water is precipitated almost per- 
pendicularly over a mass of jointed greenstone. 
On looking back, after leaving it, I observed two 
Indians bringing a woodskin over in great haste. 
They soon caught up to us, and turned out to be a 
Cartoonie Indian and his wife whom we had left at 
Ositiquah. They hada very light load in their canoe, 


48 DOWN THE PURUNI. Car. III. 


and had come along more rapidly than we had. On 
joining us they produced a large gourd containing 
paiworie, and distributed a few calabashes of that 
horrid liquid amongst the men, who swallowed it with 
great gusto. Our guide, Joseph, was transferred to 
the newly arrived Indian’s canoe, and in the afternoon 
had a few high words with its occupants. Rising up 
suddenly, the owner seized Joseph by the shoulders 
and gave him a good shaking. The latter, though 
much ruffled, did not resent the insult, and about an 
hour afterwards both were apparently as good friends 
as ever, drinking paiworie out of the same calabash. 
We arrived at the landing of a recently deserted 
village late that afternoon and camped. On approach- 
ing the place I heard a curious loud purring sound, 
which seemed to come from the bottom of the canoe, 
and, on inquiring the cause, was told by Ben that it 
was produced by a fish which congregated in numbers 
.in very deep parts of the river. 

At another village landing we purchased a large 
Maam (Trachypelmus suberistatus) from some Indians; 
the sides of the head appeared to be marked with 
vermilion pigment, but upon close examination I found 
that this colouring was produced by clusters of that 
minute insect pest, the bete rouge. At this place I 
observed a tall, straight sapling, with a regularly 
arranged style of branches in one or two sets near its 
top. Taking a machette or-cutlass, I gave the tree a 
blow, with the intention of cutting it down, and was in 
the act of raising my hand to deliver another, when I 
received two or three sharp stings on the back of the 
neck, which felt like sparks of fire, and produced such 


Cuap. III. WATER-HAAS. 49 ` 


a demoralizing effect upon my nerves that I dropped 
the cutlass and fled. I hastily raised my hand to my 
neck and seized two or three long-bodied, amber- 
coloured ants, which had been dislodged from the tree 
by the jar produced by the blow and had fallen upon 
me. These ants always inhabit this kind of tree, 
living at the base of its leaf stalks. The pain did not 
entirely leave me for over an hour after, and made 
me feel exceedingly ruffled and wroth. 

One afternoon, as we were paddling quietly along, 
three water-haas (Hydrocherus capybara) leaped from 
a low bank into the river close to us and disappeared 
under water. We pulled hastily to the spot and 
awaited their re-appearance. One came up to breathe 
close to Mogoose’s canoe, and his companion fired at 
its head—the only part visible—but without effect. 
The next time it came to the surface close to a low 
mud-bank, upon which it landed, whilst we were all
