looking out in quite another direction. We saw it 
making off at a heavy lumbering gallop for the bush, 
just in time for me to get a shot at it with my trusty 
breechloader. The charge of BB-shot lodged in its 
shoulder, breaking the upper bone of one fore-leg. It 
fell with its head towards the river, and, floundering 
about a little, took to the water again, but in such a 
thoroughly exhausted condition that it was captured 
and killed by the Indians with little trouble. It was 
an exceedingly large specimen of its kind and very 
fat. The men ate portions of it with evident relish. 
I tasted a piece, and thought it was about the rankest 
particle of food that ever passed my lips. 

That evening, whilst camping, Vincente was stung 

F 


50 DOWN THE PURUNI. Cuar. III. 


in the foot by a black scorpion, and suffered a good 
deal of pain. He came to me immediately after, 
trembling from head to foot, as if suffering from ague, 
and his face was quite bloodless. I gave hima strong 
dose of ammonia, which afforded him almost instan- 
taneous relief. There are two kinds of bush scorpions 
to be found under the dead leaves and decayed logs in 
the forest. One kind is very large and black, with 
two short clavate claws, shaped like those of a lobster ; 
its sting is dangerous, producing fever, vomiting, and 
intense pain. The other is a small, almost harmless 
thing, of a pale drab colour, with long pointed claws. 
Its sting is not more painful than that of a wasp. 

I had a small tin case, shaped like a sandwich-box, 
with a layer of cork in the bottom, in which I pinned 
any curious insects met with. One night our camp 
was not far from the large earth mounds of the cusbi 
ant (C&codoma cephalotes), and a band of these creatures 
got into the insect case, deposited their loads of earth, 
and then set to work to destroy all my small but 
choice collection in the most heartless manner. They 
were unable to carry off the insects, after cutting them 
up, owing to the curved shape of the sides of the box 
preventing their egress, and were thus neatly trapped. 

On October the 13th we passed over a portion of 
river where it flowed through a hilly tract of country, 
and obtained some fine views of wooded hills, varying 
in height from 500 to 800 feet. We also ran down 
some small rapids, which was rather dangerous work 
in our frail canoes. The river occasionally received 
the waters of. a small tributary here and there, and 
gradually widened out to 60 yards. Its water lost its 


Crap. III. LUKENANIES, 51 


black cast as we proceeded, and at last became quite 
turbid, flowing in an exceedingly tortuous course. 
Next morning we arrived at the landing of a village 
called Wahmatta, where Mogoose and friends, after 
providing us with a man and boy as guides to Cowen- 
amou, were paid for their faithful services, and set out 
on their homeward journey, whilst we continued ours 
towards the Mazaruni river. Passing a few small 
cataracts next day, we arrived at the mouth of the 
Puruni, and soon found ourselves upon the black 
water of the Mazaruni, in a part studded with rocks 
and islands, and of great width. Turning the bows 
of the canoes up stream, we paddled steadily along 
against the current, and in the afternoon reached the 
landing place of Cowenamou, where we camped for 
the night, our Wahmatta guides going to the village 
to apprise the natives of our arrival. 

On our way up river, one of the men shot two sun- 
fish or Lukenanies (Geophagus ocellaris), each about 
two feet in length. They were of a greenish-yellow 
colour, and have a black spot encircled with a golden- 
yellow rim upon each side of the body, near the base 
of the tail. The males and females of this fish go in 
pairs, and lie in shallow pools amongst the rocks, 
directly over the spot where they have deposited their 
eggs, for the purpose of guarding them from the 
attacks of numerous small fish, The female watches 
over her brood after they are hatched, like a hen over 
her chickens, until they are strong enough to take care 
of themselves. 


52 UP THE MAZARUNI. Cuar. IV. 


CHAPTER IV. 
UP THE MAZARUNI. 


Cowenamou — Tale of Poisoning — Indians poison Pool for Fish — Our 
Boats arrive — Continue Journey up Mazaruni — Ascend Issano Creek 
— Mysterious Sound — Sponges — Sororieng — Red Howlers — Oura- 
napai — Indian Dances — Attacked by Warracaba Tigers — From 
Carabung to the Upper Mazaruni — Proceed in Canoes to Apiopai — 
Starvation — Collect Provisions — Return to Carabung — Adventure 
with Bushmasters — Regain our Boats — Ascend Mazaruni to Peaimah 
— The Didi — Descend Mazaruni. 


On the day succeeding our arrival at the village 
landing, a lot of the villagers came out to see us, and 
I accompanied them back to Cowenamou, which was 
about a mile and a half from the river. Our way lay 
along a well-beaten path, which led up a gradual 
ascent toa level tract 200 feet above the sea. Situated 
in a large opening, ankle deep in white sand, stood 
four houses, constituting the village and forming the 
homes of twenty-two people. The floors of the houses 
were of the same deep sand which, from being mixed 
for years with the ashes of wood fires, had become the 
colour of moulders’ loam. The children, from rolling 
and tumbling about in this mixture, had partaken 
somewhat of its hue, whilst the lean curs, which as 
usual on such occasions charged us in a body, were in 
a similar grimy condition. 

Our arrival at a village was always announced by 
the barking of dogs and cackling of fowls, the latter 


Cuar. IV. DOGS AND FOWLS. 53 


usually fleeing at once into the thickets at the sight of 
a white face and clothed form; and the former soon 
being induced to scatter indiscriminately by a charge 
upon them by some members of our party. Many of 
these dogs are savage, but all have a wholesome dread 
of sticks, from receiving continual thumps from their 
owners. They become very mangy-looking brutes, 
by lying on the caustic wood ashes of dead fires, and 
getting singed by crowding too close to hammock fires 
during the cool nights. The good hunting dogs, as a 
rule, are prevented from taking part in these injurious 
practices, by being tied on top of small raised stagings. 
The hunting dogs are fed regularly, but the useless 
curs live on scraps, bones, and air. Some of the curs 
follow the women to the cassava fields when they go 
to dig that root, and are looked upon as a protection 
from wild animals, for should a jaguar pounce down 
upon the party, he will be quite satisfied with seizing 
and carrying off a dog, giving the women time to 
escape. 

It seems to be a passion with Indians to keep fowls 
and curs which are quite useless to them. They never 
eat their fowls by any chance, and were it not for the 
depredations of vampire bats and small tiger-cats they 
would increase rapidly. I often felt thankful for the 
custom, superstitious or otherwise, that precluded 
Indians from consuming their own fowls, but which 
did not prevent their selling them to me. 

Most of the men at the village were reclining in 
their hammocks, smoking their long home-made bark- 
wrapped cheroots; and the women were all engaged in 
household duties, such as boiling pepper-pot, making 


54 UP THE MAZARUNI, Cuar. 1V. 


cassava bread, and spinning cotton. The last-men- 
tioned operation is performed by hand, the cotton 
being attached to the end of a small stick passed 
through a circular disc of bone, which they roll 
between the palm of the hand and the outside of the 
thigh. The cotton is obtained from the cotton trees 
(Gossypium sp.) which they plant around their houses. 
Pepper, cotton, and papaw trees, together with gourd- 
vines, are always seen in closer proximity to Indian 
habitations. l 

There was an Ackawoise youth from Fort Island, 
in the mouth of the Essequebo, at this village, who 
spoke English, whom I engaged as interpreter to our 
expedition. He told me that when he was a boy his 
father had been killed by people of his own tribe at 
their village on the Upper Mazaruni, and that his 
mother had taken him and two sisters down to Fort 
Island. There she died, and he was adopted by a 
Creole named Adams, who brought him up and taught 
him to read and write a little, and had him baptized 
under the name of William Abrahams. Not long 
before the period of which I write, he had learned 
from some friends that his elder brother had been 
killed at the village of Cabunie, and he had come up 
to take away the sons of that brother. The Indian 
who had murdered his father was an Ackawoise, who 
afterwards became head man of a small settlement on 
the Essequebo, called Macedonia, not far below the 
Penal Settlement, where he was known as Captain 
John. Living in dread of the law of blood atonement 
which makes it incumbent on a son to avenge a 
father’s death, Captain John had left the Mazaruni 


Cuar. IV. ISLAND CAMP. 55 


and settled at Macedonia, watching his chance, mean- 
while, to plot against the sons of the man whose life 
he had taken. Through his agency the eldest had 
now been put out of the way in a most barbarous 
manner. According to what William had gleaned 
from the murdered man’s wife, it appeared that he had 
been induced to join a fishing party, and then had 
been set upon by a number of men, who, with a refine- 
ment of cruelty practised only by savages, had forced 
his limbs out of joint, rubbed his body over with a 
white powder made from a species of wild tannier, and 
then pulled them into their sockets again. He 
managed to reach home with great difficulty, and take 
to his hammock, where he was seized with vomiting 
and died in a few hours. 

After returning to our camp I went in a canoe 
with Ben in search of a good spot, on an island point, 
commanding a view of the main channel of the river, 
to camp upon, so that our boats could not miss seeing 
us on coming up. We were successful in our search, 
finding a lovely spot, with rocky and sandy pro- 
menades around it, together with bathing pools innu- 
merable. A small cataract extended from the head 
of this island to another near by, and at its foot, in a 
shallow, we saw a broken woodskin and the remnants 
of earthen pots, telling plainly that when the river 
was high a party of Indians had been upset while 
attempting to shoot the cataracts, losing their canoe, 
with all their things, and perhaps their lives also. 

We broke up our camp at the landing, and moved 
over to the island, where we formed a new one, 
most delightfully situated in a cool and airy position. 


56 UP THE MAZARUNI. Canar. IV. 


There we took up our abode, awaiting the arrival of 
boats and men, then nearly due. 

The weather at this period was beautifully fine; a 
bright blue sky, crossed now and then by a few white 
fleecy clouds, and almost continuous sunshine, made 
the whole face of nature look smiling. In these 
latitudes one’s spirits always rise with the sunshine, 
and sink with clouds and rain. A gentle breeze came 
up the river, about the time of morning when the 
heat began to make itself felt, and stirred the leaves 
overhead all day as it swept over our encampment, 
bringing with it a grateful and refreshing coolness. 

I made excursions amongst the rocks of the neigh- 
bourhood, journeying in a woodskin propelled by Ben 
and Yackarawa, who at the same time plied their 
bows and arrows against fish, and so kept our larder 
supplied. 

In these regions night closes in about half-past six 
o'clock during the shorter days of the year, and at 
a little before seven o'clock on the longest day. 
After dining late, I was wont to stroll up to the 
rocks on the upper end of our island, and there smoke 
a pipe or two, looking up the broad expanse of river 
westward and watching the beautiful colours of the 
sunset clouds fade gradually away. As their re- 
flections were withdrawn, a sombre dark colour over- 
spread the placid surface of the water, interrupted here 
and there by light bands, marking the places where 
its surface was broken by rapids. Such a scene had 
its effect upon the mind, bringing up various thoughts, 
and a longing to press on over those dark waters 
to the unknown regions beyond. 


Cuar. IV. POISONING POOL. 57 


I set out at an early hour one morning, with Ben, 
Eruma, and Yackarawa, in a woodskin, for a place 
where the Cowenamou Indians were going to poison 
a pool so as to obtain its fish. After about two hours’ 
hard paddling we arrived at a large cataract, called 
Cartowerie, and, taking our canoe into smooth water 
above, found the Indians, eleven in number, busily 
engaged in beating bundles of a soft yellow root 
with sticks. These Haiarie roots were each about 
two inches in diameter, and of a light yellow colour, 
Containing a yellow creamy juice, having a dis- 
agreeable, raw smell. Each bundle was about a foot 
in diameter and two feet in length. When thoroughly 
pounded into pulp, they were thrown into canoes, in 
which a little water had been previously placed, and 
then the juice was wrung from them. The enclosure 
to be acted upon was of an irregular shape, occupying 
about two acres of river, and formed by dams of rock, 
built into the spaces between rocky areas and small 
islands. In building this the Indians had left two 
large gaps open, one being where the greatest body 
of water ran in and the other where it flowed out. 
When we arrived they had closed these gaps with a 
wattle arrangement, so that all chance of escape for the 
fish was cut off. Three canoes, containing the juice of 
six bundles of Haiarie, were then taken to the upper 
end of the enclosure, aud the subtle poison discharged 
from them. It was borne down by the slight current, 
and mingled rapidly with the pure dark water. Most 
of the Indians then got into their canoes and ‘pushed 
out, bow and arrows in hand, into the middle of the 
enclosure, while the remainder, with my men, also 


58 UP THE MAZARUNL Cuar. IV. 


furnished with the same weapons, stood upon the 
rocks at the edge. In ten minutes’ time numbers 
of small fish came to the surface, and swam uneasily 
about, trying to rise above water; these soon were 
floating about quite dead. After an interval of five 
minutes more, a single pacu showed its back fin, and 
also tried to raise its head above water. Au instant 
more, and the whole place seemed alive with large 
fish, Pacu and Cartabac, all struggling and flapping 
at the surface or whirling round and round. Many 
tried to force themselves out of the water up the 
sloping surface of the rocks, and two were successful 
in this, dying on the strand. From the excited 
manner in which they struggled, it seemed to me as 
if the poison had an intoxicating effect upon them. 
It might have been that the contact of the poison with 
their gills had produced a feeling of suffocation—hence 
their endeavours to escape from their native element. 

It was a most exciting scene for a time, as the 
Indians shot arrow after arrow into the bewildered 
dying fish, and hauled them ashore or into the canoes. 
In about an hour the murderous work was over, and 
150 fine Pacu and Cartabac were lying dead upon 
the rocks around the pool, the victims of Indian 
prowess and poison. During the whole proceeding 
I stood on the rocks at the upper end of the pool and 
had a fine view of the scene, the finest part of which 
was to see the naked savage, in all his glory, drawing 
his bow with strength and ease and letting fly his 
arrows with unerring aim. 

How curious it is that this poison, which kills the 
fish, should not have a deleterious effect upon its flesh. 


Cuar. lV. ARRIVAL OF BOATS. 59 


That it has not, is proved by the fact that fish pro- 
cured by its aid are as good and wholesome food as 
when merely shot with the arrow or caught with hook 
and line. The process of cooking probably drives 
out or destroys the bad properties of the poison, 
as it does with the yam, which is said to be injurious 
if eaten uncooked. Boiling down the poisonous 
cassava juice likewise renders it innocuous. It may 
be, however, that this poison acts in some peculiar 
way without entering the tissues of the flesh. 

On the morning of October 21st our boats arrived, 
having been nine days in coming up from the Penal 
Settlement. An Ackawoise, named Simon, came up 
in his canoe as guide, and about ten other Indians, 
met on the way, came along with them. Amongst 
these was an Indian, rejoicing in the name of Jimbo, 
who, with his two wives and three children, was 
going to a place up river. 

Two of our Pomeroon Arowaaks, the three Caribs, 
and Joseph, left us at this stage of the journey. 
Eruma (one of the Caribs), who seemed to have great 
influence over all Indians with whom he came in 
contact, had completely subjugated poor Joseph, who 
responded to his beck and call like a dog, and was now 
going to accompany him to the Pomeroon. We gave 
them two of our Puruni woodskins, together with a 
supply of provisions, and on the following morning 
they started on their journey down river, in company 
with Simon. At the same time, we set out on the 
continuation of our journey up the Mazaruni, accom- 
panied by some Indians in their own canoes. In one 
was Jimbo and family, and in the others were five 


60 UP THE MAZARUNI. Cuar. IV. 


Indian men from the Upper Mazaruni, who had been 
staying at Cowenamou. 

During our stay on the island, Mr. Sawkins and I 
agreed to extend our journey, if possible, to the moun- 
tain of Roraima. We had obtained all the inform- 
ation we could, through William, from the Indians 
from that part of the country, and learned that we 
could not follow the Mazaruni round to the mouth of 
the Cako river, owing to many great falls, by which 
its navigation was closed ; but would have to cross by 
land from the Carabung creek to the Upper Mazaruni. 
It would therefore be necessary, on our way up river, to 
be on the look out for villages where we could obtain 
provisions to replenish our stock, and where we could 
hire Indians as carriers and guides for our land journey. 

Whilst breakfasting, one morning, a dog, which one 
of our men named Schomburgk had brought up with 
him, was sent into the forest to hunt, and soon after 
came out in full chase of a labba, which took to the 
water to escape from the dog, and thus fell a prey to 
the arrows of the men. This dog had been bought by 
Schomburgk from an Indian at Weyuamou, on the 
Cuyuni, and had become exceedingly attached to him. 
It was a symmetrically formed animal, of about the size 
of a bull-terrier, and in colour of a pure, spotless white. 
Its ears stood erect, and gave it a most intelligent, 
watchful look, while its tail, which had been docked 
to a most judicious length, was carried in a jaunty 
manner. It went by the name of Chickerine,; which 
is the Ackawoise word for that horrid toe pest, the 
jigger. I took a fancy to this little dog and 
purchased it. 


Cuap. IV. _A BUCK GUN. 61 


On the second day, after leaving Cowenamon, we 
cleared all the islands and came to a portion of the 
river lying W.S.W. and E.N.E., where a fine view was 
opened up, disclosing, in the far distance, the northern 
escarpment of the sandstone mountains lying to the 
south of the Mazaruni, from which two high bluffs 
jutted out. We soon after arrived at the mouth of the 
Issano river, a small tributary on the south, which I 
ascended in our woodskin, manned by William and 
Cornelius, in the company of Jimbo and family. 
Jimbo’s first wife had a child of four or five years of 
age, who appeared to be slightly idiotic, and was 
partially blind. A little before dusk Jimbo informed 
me that we were approaching the landing of the village 
of Peoaquah, and then proceeded to load a new gun he 
had brought from the Demerara. This was of the sort 
sold in Georgetown by the name of “ buck gun,’— 
“ buck ” being the Creole name for “Indian.” It had 
a long single barrel of cheap Birmingham manufacture. 
Jimbo discharged three shots, to let the village people 
know that he was coming; but in the excitement of 
the moment, and exhilarated by the thoughts of a 
triumphal entry into his native place, he had put in 
too heavy a charge. At the third shot, which sounded 
remarkably loud, he gave a cry of surprise, which 
made me ask him what was the matter. He handed 
me the gun with this laconic but forcible remark: 
“T gun no good, him bust.” Sure enough it had 
burst near the breech, and a large piece of the barrel 
had been blown completely away—fortunately into 


We reached the landing at dark and walked to the 


62 UP THE MAZARUNI. Cuar. IV. 


village, a distance of two miles, along a forest path, 
lighted on our way by a small boat lamp, without the 
aid of which I must have broken my neck over one of 
the numerous fallen trees. Making arrangements for 
a supply of cassava to be prepared for us, I returned 
next day to our camp at Issano mouth, where we had 
to remain for two days. 

As we were on the point of leaving the landing to 
descend the Issano, we were all startled by a heavy 
booming sound, resembling the distant discharge of a 
heavy piece. of artillery. The sun shone brightly at 
the time, and not a cloud was to be seen in the sky., 
On making inquiries I learned from the Indians that 
these sounds were frequently heard at this place, and 
are supposed to have their origin in the mountains 
to the south. It is difficult to account for this loud 
and mysterious sound, unless we suppose it to be 
produced by a fall of rock from the sandstone moun- 
tains, the northern escarpment of which shows a vast 
front of bold precipices. 

The rocky surfaces of a point jutting out at our 
camp near the Issano mouth were covered with a 
growth of fresh-water sponge. On the portions un- 
covered by water the sponge was dead, and formed a 
dirty, blackish plant-like covering, with hard silicious 
spicules sticking up of one-eighth of an inch in height; 
while on the submerged portions the live sponge was 
covered with a gelatinous coating of the sponge 
animalcule itself. To the dead stems on the river’s 
banks, and also to low trees growing on gravel beaches 
which for a part of the year are submerged, curious 
globular, hard, brown sponges are also attached, many 
feet above the surface of the water. 


Crap. IV. SORORIENG INDIANS. 63 


Whilst proceeding up a long southerly reach of the 
river near Teboco cataract, a magnificent view was 
disclosed of a high and distant table-topped mountain, 
shaped somewhat like the roof of a house with sloping 
gable ends, with a lofty conical peak of double its 
height rising behind. A Mr. Hillhouse, who ascended 
this river many years ago, called this Raleigh’s Peak, 
but its Indian name is Illuie. We passed a large river, 
called Semang, coming in on the south, and rounding 
the bluff point of the Karanang hills on our right, came 
to the cataract of Teboco. After hauling up this cataract 
we passed a lot of rocks and small islands, and paddled 
along open stretches of river with both banks in view, 
lined with magnificent mora trees. Our course then 
lay in a westerly direction, over a portion of the river 
half a mile in width, where, owing to the lowness of 
the water, numerous sand shallows made their 
appearance. 

When we reached the landing place of a village 
called Sororieng, we found the Indians who had left 
Cowenamou with us had already arrived, and, with 
forty-seven others, had collected there to receive us. 
Many youths of this party had perched themselves 
like monkeys on tree branches, in order, I suppose, to 
obtain a bird’s-eye view of the Parangheries (sea 
people), as they call white men—from their having 
come from the sea and landed on the coast. Most of 
the men and women were got up in full dress, consist- 
ing of patterns dyed in black over their naked bodies, 
and red paint on their faces and feet. Bead ornaments, 
consisting of armlets and anklets, were on them in 
abundance. ‘These are made of white beads run on 
a string, and rolled evenly round the ankle and wrist 


64 ; UP THE MAZARUNI. Cuap. IV. 


in a broad band. Some of the men had headdresses 
made of parrot and macaw feathers, tastefully arranged 
on coronets of basket-work, held on the head by a 
string under the chin. Others had a short string of 
beads, terminating in a bell-shaped piece of bone 
passed through a hole pierced through the lower lip 
and hanging down to the chin, while all had blue 
tattoo patterns at the corners of their mouths. Their 
red skins were of a fine healthy tinge, and all were 
well made and in good condition. Every man and 
nearly every youth had a dirty greenish pellet of a 
leafy-looking substance held between his lips, which 
he rolled about every now and then. A dark-greenish 
juice oozed from it, staining the lips and sometimes 
trickling down from the corners of the mouth. The 
presence of the ball caused their lips to separate and 
protrude, producing an effect on their countenances 
which did not render them more beautiful than nature 
had intended. On questioning William, I learned that 
these pellets are made of green tobacco leaves, mixed 
with the saline ashes of an aquatic plant called houyah. 
Every male Indian met with on our journey from 
this to the Upper Mazaruni had his houyah pellet 
between his lips. Our Indian friends appeared to me 
to be all more or less under the influence of paiworie. 

One tall man, with bristling hair and large rolling 
eyes, whose body was bedaubed with red paint, made 
a long and remarkably loud speech in Ackawoise, 
giving force to his remarks with gesticulations and 
wavings of a huge stick. No one paid the slightest 
attention to him until he became tiresome, when he 
was with great difficulty suppressed. 


Crap. IV. . HOWLING MONKEYS. 65 


Some of the Indian children had a small red howling 
monkey (Mycetes seniculus), which could not have 
been more than a few weeks old. This little thing, 
when touched, uttered a few deep grunts, containing 
the elemental sounds of the howl. 

At early morning, at dusk, and through the night, at 
all our camping places, we were accustomed to hear 
the howlers serenading. To my mind the sounds pro- 
duced by these monkeys more nearly resembled a roar 
than a howl, and when sufficiently far off are not 
unpleasant to the ear. When heard from a distance 
of half a mile or so, they seem to begin with low 
notes, swelling gradually into louder and longer ones 
till they merge into a prolonged roar, which dies 
gradually away with a mournful cadence. When not 
more than one or two hundred yards away, and con- 
sequently plainly heard, they commence with a series 
of short howls, which break off with grunts, and, at 
every repetition, become longer and longer till their 
voices have got fairly in tune, when they give their 
final roar, which dies so gradually away. Then, after 
an instant’s silence, a few deep grunts are given, as if 
the remains of the compressed air in their throat 
drums were being got rid of. Listening carefully to 
the performance, one can detect a voice at a much 
higher key than the others, especially in the dying- 
away portion. The Indians say this is made by a 
dwarf monkey of the same family which accompanies 
every troop. I was of the opinion that it was the 
voice of a female howler, but the Indians, who are 
very careful observers, said it was not. With their 
black negro-like faces, and long red beards covering 

F 


66 UP THE MAZARUNI. Canar. IV. 


the bony howling apparatus below their throats, they 
are curious-looking creatures. 

After agreeing with eight Sororieng men to accom- 
pany us in their woodskins, to act as carriers across 
the country to the Upper Mazaruni, we continued our 
journey. . 

Passing along, day after day, we could see the edge 
of the great table-land on our left, rising to a height of 
over 2000 feet above the intervening forest-covered 
plain, with pinkish and gray precipices here and 
there, and huge wooded bluffs at intervals. From 
the end of one of these rose a curious pinnacle of 
rock, to which the Indians have given the name of 
“Caribisi.” They have a tradition that years ago a 
Carib ascended the slope of the bluff to hunt and, 
for reasons not satisfactorily explained, was suddenly 
turned to stone. Not being of soluble materials, like 
the pillar resulting from the transformation of Lot’s 
wife, his stony remains have successfully withstood the 
destructive forces of atmospheric agencies. It did not 
require this melancholy tradition to give a charm to 
these grand mountain escarpments, with their deep 
glens and mysterious valleys, their frowning pre- 
cipices, castellated tops, and tree-clothed slopes, for 
they looked weird enough without any such adjunct. 
These mountains are known by the general name of 
Merumé, but to striking portions of different parts the 
Indians have attached other names. 

From two reaches of the river a most curious light- 
coloured sugar-loaf hill can be seen to the northward, 
rising: high above the surrounding forest-covered 
country in the rear of the village of Sororieng. 


Cuar. IV. OURANA-PAI. 67 


Though distant, it can be made out to be a mass of 
bare rock. The natives call it Sororieng, the meaning 
of which in English is “ Swallow-nest.” 

One afternoon we arrived at a village on the north 
bank, called Ouranapai, and landing, walked up to the 
houses situated on a raised level tract of sandy ground. 
Approaching a large circular edifice, walled in with 
bark, an Indian dressed in civilized costume of shirt and 
trousers, with a red handkerchief tied round his head, 
came to meet us, followed by some twenty women and 
children, whom he drew up in line as we passed. He 
addressed us in English, and bade us enter the house. 
Our men greeted him at once as an old acquaintance, 
named Robert, who, they told me, had been brought 
up at the Grove Mission, where he had been taught to 
read and write. He had disappeared from that locality 
some years ago and had not been seen since. He was 
now playing the part of schoolmaster and missionary, 
on his own account, to this tribe, and these women and 
children were his scholars. He was, in fact, a sort of 
chief amongst these people, ordering their goings out 
and comings in. His circular schoolhouse had log seats 
for the accommodation of his pupils in one part, and 
rude hand-looms, on which some women were making 
hammocks, in the other. The men of the place had 
been sent to hunt and fish, and did not return till the 
evening. 

We had a palaver with Robert on the subject of 
provisions and carriers, and he promised to do any- 
thing in his power for us if we would remain a day or 
two at his place. We returned to the landing at 
which I camped, Mr. Sawkins crossing over to the 

F 2 


68 UP THE MAZARUNI. Cuar. IY. 


other side of the river. About midnight I was awoke 
by a party of our men, who had an Indian in custody, 
his hands being tied behind his back with hammock 
ropes. They said that they had seen him go on board 
one of our boats, and had watched him fill a calabash 
with rum from their demijohn, but had pounced upon 
him before he had time to drink it. Our men were 
very fond of rum, and we had brought a few gallons 
for their use, a glass of which was served out to each 
man of an evening, after a day’s work amongst cata- 
racts or in rain. This Indian had therefore, in their 
eyes, committed a dreadful crime when he attempted 
to pilfer from their small store of this precious stimu- 
lant. They begged me to pronounce sentence upon 
him at once, as he was in a highly excited state. I 
told them to take him bound as he was to the village 
and there cast him loose, and threaten that if he 
returned during the night they would be prepared to 
pepper him with shot. 

On the second evening of our stay, I went to the 
village to see the dancing which had been got up in 
honour of our presence, and, seated on a log of wood, 
witnessed a series of dances in the moonlight, on the 
large open sandy area in front of the chief’s residence. 
At first our men danced amongst the savages, to the 
music of their own fiddle and an Indian drum, but 
their clothed forms spoilt the wild effect of the scene, 
and I was glad to see one commenced composed 
entirely of Indians, thirty-three in number. They had 
no instrumental music, but chanted a simple and wild- 
sounding refrain, a fitter accompaniment to a funeral 
ceremony. The whole performance consisted of their 


Casar. IV. INDIAN DANCES. 69 


walking round in a circle, with a measured tread, and 
with their hands on each other’s shoulders, preceded 
by a sort of drum-major armed with a hollow bamboo 
staf, which he held vertically, and beat time with on 
the ground. To this bamboo were attached tassels 
made of seeds, the kernels of which rattled in their 
casings, and the whole lot rattled against the hollow 
bamboo. The drum-major’s legs were bent at the 
knees, his body arched, and his voice loud and sepul- 
chral. Round and round they marched, each bringing 
one foot heavily on the. ground at every third or fifth 
blow of the bamboo, singing all the while. Then they 
varied the performance by breaking into two lines, 
which advanced and then retired backwards, with the 
same measured tread. After each set was over and 
broken up, they all set up a series of discordant shouts, 
followed by much hooting, and then paiworie was 
handed round and drunk ad lib. The third dance was 
rather lively, being a sort of whirling-dervish affair, 
and more nearly resembling what one would expect 
from savages just returned from the war-path laden 
with scalps. They stood up in bands of five or six 
linked together, the men with their arms around the 
women’s shoulders, and advanced and retired with 
short running steps, giving a simultaneous stamp at 
the termination of the advance, and another at the end 
of the retreat. After five or six of these rushes, every 
band whirled round and round as if on a pivot, and 
then as quickly resumed the former measure. Many 
of the dancers wore feather hats, were daubed with 
red paint, and had various ornameuts hanging about 
them. The whole scene, lit up by the clear light of 


70 UP THE MAZARUNI. Cuap. IV. 


a nearly full tropical moon, was a curious one, and I 
enjoyed it immensely for a time; but when it became 
monotonous I retired to our camp and turned in. 

During the day small parties of Indians kept con- 
stantly arriving at a landing a short distance above 
our camp, and passed through to the village. My 
canvas awning was close to the path leading from this 
landing, and its three side cords stretched across it. 
That night some more parties arrived at midnight, 
and awoke me by tripping over the tent-cords, and 
nearly bringing the whole edifice to the ground. That 
was bad enough in itself, but their curs, seeing my 
white tent gleaming in the moonlight, became so scared 
that they durst not pass it, and so remained about 
fifty yards off on the path, barking and howling to an 
alarming extent. Abandoned by its owners, and a 
prey to feelings of fright, one would, once in a while, 
in a fit of desperation, make a rush to get past. The 
tent-rope, taking it across the chest, would stop its 
headway, and the rebound would throw it backwards, 
when with fearful yells it would fly back to its fright- 
stricken companions. At last I had quite a collection 
of dogs on the path, whose infernal yelpings roused the 
whole camp, and no sleep could be obtained. These 
cowardly brutes, fearing hidden dangers in the forest 
more terrible than the tent, would not take to the 
bush, and so gain the village. Feeling that something 
had to be done to free us from this terrible plague, I 
got up and charged them with much shouting, happily 
dispersing the whole band. 

All the Indians—ninety-two in number—turned 
out on the rocks at the landing to see us depart on 


Cuar. IV. INDIAN EXODUS. 71 


the following day, and watch our ascent of a rapid 
above the landing. Standing, sitting, and squatting 
on the rocks, in groups, these red-skinned individuals 
formed a very picturesque and interesting sight. That 
forenoon we met seventy-three Indians in woodskins, 
coming down river to Ouranapai, thirteen of whom, 
belonging to a place called Apeng, turned back with 
us. Our party then consisted of forty-four men, 
twenty-three of our own, and eighteen Indians, the 
latter occupying quite a fleet of woodskins. 

It was difficult to find out the meaning of this sort 
of exodus of Indians from the Upper Mazaruni, and in 
answer to inquiries ou the subject we obtained a 
variety of replies. One was, that they were going 
down to the Grove; another, that a hostile tribe from 
the south was going to make a raid upon them, and 
they were fleeing for safety. My private opinion was 
that the head man at Ouranapai was enticing them to 
his place, in order that they might form a large settle- 
ment there under his guidance. 

At noon on Monday, November 2nd, we entered the 
mouth of the Carabung, a branch river on the south, 
and there camped, as it was our intention to ascend it 
in woodskins, owing to its water at the time being too 
shallow to float our boats. My tent was erected on 
the east bank of the river, on rising ground; most of 
the men, with all the Indians, slung their hammocks 
to poles stuck in the beach below; while Mr. Sawkins 
had his boat made fast at a large sand-beach on the 
western side. In the evening I was busy writing 
letters, to go down to the Penal Settlement by the 
boat we intended to send back from this point, when 


72 UP THE MAZARUNI. Cuar. 1V. 


my attention was attracted by our two dogs, which 
were tied up, barking furiously, followed by a great 
stir in camp. Then some voices proclaimed loudly, 
“ The tigers are coming!” and one man called to me 
to come down as quickly as possible to the boats, and 
bring my gun. Thinking at the moment that a couple 
of jaguars had been heard near by, I seized my gun 
and made a rush down the slope to the camp, jumping 
down the low bank, eager to get a shot at one, when, 
to my surprise, I found the beach deserted. Where 
some twenty Indians had been camped, there was now 
not even a hammock left ; all had suddenly and com- 
pletely vanished, leaving only two or three smouldering 
fires and their hammock poles. My men had all taken 
to the boat, and „had it afloat, with its bow barely 
grounded, in readiness to shove off. They greeted me 
with cries of “ Quick, sir, quick; the Warracaba tigers 
are coming!” There was quite a flutter of relief 
amongst them when the boat was pushed off into mid- 
stream, where they all began to talk excitedly over 
our escape. The dogs still gave tongue, and were 
even more excited than the men, the hair on their 
backs standing erect as they sniffed the air in the 
direction of our camp. I eagerly inquired what were 
Warracaba tigers, and was hastily informed that they 
were small and exceedingly ferocious tigers, that they 
hunted in packs, and were not frightened by camp 
fires or anything except the barking of dogs. To 
water they have a special aversion, and will never 
cross a stream which is too wide for them to jump. 

In crossing the narrow river to the sand-beach op- 
posite, we passed two or three of the Indians’ wood- 


Cnap. IV. WARRACABA TIGERS. 73 


skins, with one or two people in them, and on landing 
found the remainder of those who had stampeded 
walking about and talking on the beach. The moon 
was just rising over the tree tops which lined the 
side of the river we had quitted, throwing that part 
into deep shade. Immediately after we landed I ran 
a little way up the beach, along the water’s edge, 
with some of the men, listening to hear something of 
our enemies, for up to that time they had been to me 
both inaudible and invisible. At the same time I be- 
lieved that some terrible animals had nearly pounced 
upon us, otherwise the Indians would never have 
acted as they had done. As we stopped a shrill 
scream rent the night air, proceeding from the oppo- 
site side of the river, not 200 yards above our camp, 
and, waking up echoes through the forest, died away 
as suddenly as it rose. This was answered by another 
cry coming from the depths of the forest, the interval 
between them being filled by low growls and trumpet- 
ing sounds, which smote most disagreeably on the ear. 
Although I knew that I was perfectly safe from any 
attack from these animals, whatever their nature, 
having the river between us, yet I felt a sort of 
creeping sensation of horror pass through me at the 
first shrill cry I heard. Gradually the cries became 
fainter and fainter, as the band retired from our 
vicinity, till they utterly died away. Seeing nothing 
of them, and only hearing their diabolical screams, I 
pictured them in my mind as a withering scourge- 
sweeping through the forest. 

Walking on to the upper end of the beach, we met 
one of our Indians coming back, who said that he had 


74 UP THE MAZARUNI. Cuar. IV. 


been the first to land and run up the beach, when he 
had seen five of the tigers come out to the water’s 
edge, and after walking a short distance along it 
turn into the forest again. 

The call of these animals resembles that of the 
Warracaba or Trumpet-bird (Psophia crepitans), and 
hence they have obtained the name of Warracaba 
tigers. The Ackawoise Indians call them “ Y’agam- 
isheri,” and say that they vary in size as well as in 
colour. As many as a hundred are said to have been 
seen in one pack. I can form no idea as to what 
family of the carnivora they belong, but from their 
hunting in packs imagine they must be a sort of wolf. 
Their cat-like screams, on the other hand, would lead 
to the supposition that they belonged to the Felide. 
They are said to frequent the mountains, but when 
pressed by hunger during the dry seasons, at which 
time their march is not impeded by streams, they 
descend to the lowlands, and scour them in search of 
food. Woe be to the animal on whose track they once 
get if it be far from some friendly stream. 

My men were of an opinion that had not the bark- 
ing of the dogs turned the pack they would have been 
down upon us before we could have had time to take 
to our boats, and situated as I was, some little distance 
in the forest, I should have been the first victim to 
their rapacity. They must have come out a hundred 
yards above our camp, and to this, as much as any- 
thing, we owed our escape. 

After spending about an hour on the sand-beach, 
we recrossed the river to our camp, and, taking my 
hammock from its isolated position, I hung it to poles 


Cuar. IV. WARRACABA TIGERS. 75 


on the beach. Not one of the Indians returned, but 
all remained on the opposite side of the river. We all 
turned in, and had been some time asleep when we 
were aroused again by some of the men who had kept 
watch, and who heard the Warracabas coming again. 
We all bundled into the boat, and put out into the 
stream till the animals had passed on, apparently 
along the same route as before. One man said that 
the pack had evidently lost their way, were bewil- 
dered, and had doubled on their own line of march. 
After giving the brutes half an hour’s good start we 
returned to our camp, and were uot again disturbed 
that night. 

Peterson told me that, a few years previous to the 
time of which I am writing, a band of these tigers 
visited the back of the settlements near Cataboo, at 
the mouth of the Cuyuni, where he then lived, and 
that he and all his neighbours at once left the place 
and crossed over to the settlements on the east bank 
of the Mazaruni and elsewhere, where they remained, 
leaving the tigers in undisputed possession of their 
lands. Wishing to return to their homes, they met 
together, and agreed to form a party armed with guns, 
to reconnoitre the surrounding country to see whether 
the tigers had left the vicinity. Thirty of them met 
on a Sunday, and agreed to start on this expedition 
next day, fully armed and equipped, appointing a 
certain rendezvous. When the time arrived for them 
to start, only three put in an appearance ; the others, 
thinking that discretion was the better part of valour, 
stayed away. These three put off the journey for an 
indefinite period ; and it was fully three months before 


76 UP THE MAZARUNI. Cuar. IV. 


they finally made up their minds to return to their 
homes. 

The following morning saw the main portion of our 
party, consisting of Mr. Sawkins and myself, twelve 
of our men, and sixteen Indians, proceeding in six 
woodskins up the Carabung river. We left Captains 
Peterson and Cornelius and three men in charge of our 
two boats at the mouth of the Carabung, with orders 
for one boat to go down to Apeng for provisions 
during the time we were away. Our woodskins were 
all of good size, holding four people each; while one 
was particularly large, being of great length and over 
four feet in width, carrying ten Indians. The river 
became so shallow at a short distance from its mouth, 
where it widened out and ran swiftly, that the men in 
many places had to get out and tow the canoes over 
great sand flats, where there was just sufficient water 
to float them. 

The banks were low, and the trees growing upon 
them were of small growth, of kinds peculiar to 
swampy regions, such as wallabas (Zperna sp.), cork- 
wood, &c. The great mora trees, which lined the ` 
main banks of the Mazaruni, did not extend up this 
creek. 7 

Our course was first in a westerly, and then 
southerly, direction; and along the reaches running 
due south fine views were obtained of the eastern end 
of a huge flat-topped mountain, called Waterbarru, 
with perpendicular sides of about 2000 feet in height, 
near which was a high, irregularly conical peak. 

I took my dog Chickerine in the woodskin with me, 
and being tied in the bottom of the canoe, it had no 


Cuap. IV. LOSE CHICKERINE. 77 


opportunity of seeing in which direction we were 
going. When night came on we camped on a fine 
sand-beach, and, after our previous night’s experience 
with Warracaba tigers, hung our hammocks to poles 
stuck in the sand close to the water’s edge. 

One pleasing thing in regard to the Mazaruni and 
its branches is, that there are no caymen alligators, 
and but few water-boas, so that one need not fear an 
attack, by night, from the water. There is one small 
species of alligator (Champsa vallifrons), however, 
which is a timid creature, measuring only four feet 
in length. 

As soon as we landed Chickerine ran into the 
forest, barking loudly, which induced some of the men 
to follow, thinking it had got on the track of game. 
They returned after a time, stating that they could not 
see anything of the dog, and imagined that it had got 
on the track of a tiger, in which case it stood a poor 
chance of its life. It had not returned by the time 
we started on our journey next morning, so I gave it 
up as lost for ever. 

The mornings at this season were mol and cloudy ; 
but at noon the clouds dispersed, and the sun shone 
with its accustomed fierceness. 

We passed on our way close under Waterbarru 
‘mountain, one of the jutting spurs of the great sand- 
stone mountains. Groups of trees grew upon it in clus- 
ters where they could gain a footing in little slopes, but 
the greater portion of its sides was composed of bare, 
grayish and pinkish horizontal beds of sandstone. 
We now had left the plain and had entered the gorge 
cut in the mountains by the Carabung river, winding 


78 UP THE MAZARUNI. Crap. IV. 


about in a narrow valley. Soon after we had high 
mountains on our right, which, closing in as we pro- 
ceeded, narrowed the alluvial tract till it ended at 
the foot of a very picturesque fall called Macrebah. 
Just below this the Carabung was joined by the 
Seroun river, and at the point of land at their junc- 
tion was the landing of the path leading across to the 
Upper Mazaruni. Opposite this was a fine sand-beach, 
on which we camped, and about ten o'clock that night, 
as I was taking a stroll in the bright moonlight, I 
nearly stumbled upon a small tiger which was drink- 
ing at the river's edge. On seeing it I immediately 
stood still, and it walked leisurely across the beach 
into the forest, passing within ten feet of me. We had 
our woodskins sunk in quiet pools, with their painters 
of bush-rope made fast to the roots of trees growing 
under water, so as to escape the vigilant eyes of 
Indians who might be in want of such articles. 

We commenced our arduous journey by a climb of 
963 feet along the sloping side of the mountains 
forming the eastern side of the Seroun valley. As the 
path did not lead straight up the mountain at first, 
but up and down, over spurs, we had to make a long 
journey before reaching the top of the table-land, and 
had some steep ascents to perform. On the way we 
passed a very large isolated rock of diorite, which had 
formed part of one of the great layers of this rock, 
horizontally bedded in the sandstone, upon which 
were lying the bruised remains of a small tree branch, 
with many more around its base. These were offer- 
ings left by Indian travellers at the shrine of the spirit 
of this rock, who believe that if they did not perform 


Cuar. IV. OVERLAND JOURNEY. 79 


the rite of breaking off a green bow and beating it on 
the rock, evil would most assuredly befall them. The 
top of the mountain was an almost level plateau, 
covered with a very different vegetation from that of 
the plain below, the trees being small, and chiefly of 
a kind called Bartabally, whilst many small shrubs 
growing there were quite new to me. This vegeta- 
tion is due to the poor nature of the very thin soil 
covering the sandstone. Our route lay south, along a 
rough path leading over the almost level table-land, 
and across many small streams, till 2 p.m., when we 
ascended a small escarpment of a greenstone layer, 
covered with deep red soil, where a taller and better 
growth of trees flourished. We met eight Indians on 
their way out to the Carabung, accompanied by a set 
of large and ferocious dogs, which were restrained 
with difficulty by their owners from attacking us. 
They wore Ita palm sandals, showing that they came 
from the savannas, probably from near Roraima. 

Late in the afternoon we camped on the borders 
of a small stream of dark water, at a height of 1480 
feet above the sea, having started in the morning 
from a level of 175 feet. On camping our Indians 
saw signs of rain, and all put screens of palm leaves 
over their hammocks, which they had hung in close 
proximity together, forming a party by themselves, 
each three having a fire amongst them. Their weather 
prognostications proved correct, for about midnight 
down came shower after shower till daylight. Mr. 
Sawkins, who camped at a short distance from me, 
told me next morning that he was visited during the 
night by two tigers, one coming close to his tent. 


80 UP THE MAZARUNI. Crap. IV. 


The walking next day was exceedingly fatiguing, 
owing to the path being crossed by tree roots, inter- 
laced together, resting on the sandstone. The rain of 
the previous night had rendered them slippery, and 
they bruised the soles of the bare feet of our men. 
Owing to some of our party being unable to proceed 
farther that day we camped at 2 p.m. There was little 
bird and no quadruped life visible, but grasshoppers _ 
and crickets kept up a constant chirping in the trees 
overhead. A cicada now and then broke the sur- 
rounding stillness with its loud whistle, which some- 
what resembled that of a railway train. 

Next day our journey lay across various water- 
courses, some dry, others containing water, till noon, 
when we crossed a large creek called Stenaparu. 
There we met six Indians, who told us we could not 
reach any water till late in the evening, and would 
have to descend a steep mountain side to get to it. 
We therefore pressed on and ascended rapidly for 
many miles, having in two places to climb escarpments 
of over 400 feet each. Gaining a plateau 2590 feet 
above the sea, we traversed it for some distance, 
coming out at 4.30 p.m. on the edge of what appeared 
to be a precipice. There the path led down at an 
angle of 60°, and long bush-ropes for the first hundred 
feet had been tied to the trees to assist in the ascent 
and descent of the place. The rest of the way was 
not so steep, but in places we had to grasp saplings 
and tree roots to assist in lowering ourselves down. 
We gained a level place at the foot, 645 feet below the 
edge of the precipice, where there was a fine stream, at 
which we stopped for the night. 


Crap, 1V. UPPER MAZARUNI. 81 


Next day Mr. Sawkins, who was not well, became 
very much fatigued, and had to camp at an early 
hour, keeping eight men withhim. As there was only 
enough rice and cassava left for the dinner of those 
men, I was forced to press on with the remaining 
twenty, and reached the Upper Mazaruni river late 
in the afternoon. Where we left the Mazaruni it was 
flowing in an easterly direction at a height of 150 feet 
above the sea, while here it ran west at an altitude of 
1300 feet, and was broad and sluggish. Serving out 
some of the few biscuits I had to ny men, they made 
a sort of a dinner with them and salt fish, whilst the 
Indians dined off small bits of cassava bread that they 
had put by from time to time in parts of their packs, 
in anticipation of our running short. To the slowness 
of our march was to be attributed our shortness of 
provisions, for had we got through in three days, as we 
expected, we should have been able, by waiting, to get 
supplies at villages on this part of the river. The 
Roraima Indians went immediately and raised their 
three small woodskins which they had hidden on their 
way down, and six of us went on in them as rapidly 
as possible to reach a village not far off up river. By 
ten o’clock on the following day we passed a cassava 
plantation and Indian house, with no living soul near 
it, and soon reached another village not far back from 
the rivers edge, called Apiopai. Here there were 
seven or eight squalid-looking people, most of whom 
were down with fever and unable to prepare cassava 
bread for us. From them we obtained some sugar- 
cane and green papaw fruit, and I made an agreement, 
through the interpreter, with an Indian woman to pay 

G 


82 UP THE MAZARUNI. Cuap. IV. 


for part of a field of cassava, and set our men to dig 
and make bread from it. Returning to our camp 
at the Carabung path termination with three more 
woodskins, I packed off all our men at sundown for the 
first village, to make cassava. Mr. Sawkins had 
arrived at camp that forenoon with his men. 

We did not feel very comfortable that night, not 
being quite sure of amassing sufficient necessaries of 
life to enable us to return. The most disappointing 
part to me was the probable failure of our onward 
journey, and that we should have to return without 
reaching Roraima. Our party was too large, and 
could not be sustained in so thinly populated a coun- 
try; but I had still some hopes of getting on. These 
gradually died out, as day after day we struggled for 
an existence, our large party consuming the miser- 
able pittance of food that could be ‘collected at the 
surrounding villages. To make matters worse, the 
people withdrew from their bargain of selling us part 
of a field of cassava, and threatened to put a Kanaima, 
or secret murderer, upon our men for having touched 
it, which frightened them greatly. The Apiopai 
people just kept us supplied with enough provisions 
to give all hands two half-allowance meals per day, 
and when we had all moved to a camp at their 
landing we spent four wretched days. We had scouts 
out beating up food at villages, and men out shooting 
and fishing. They shot nothing, and caught fish of 
about the size of sprats. 

I used to go out on the river to shake off the 
gloomy thoughts engendered in our dull camp, and 
on my return once was greeted with pitiful cries of 


Crap. IV. STARVATION. 83 


“ Wambay, wambay!” from the Indians, signifying 
“I am hungry, I am hungry.” The fever did not 
abate at the village while we were there, and one 
morning a poor woman died of it. 

Paddling along one morning on the river, I observed 
a curious physical phenomenon. The surface of the 
water was like glass, and the sun, shining brightly at 
about 10° above the horizon, caused the shadows of 
the canoe and our bodies to be thrown on the water. 
From it they were reflected upon the wall of foliage 
on the bank at a height of about twenty feet up and 
twenty yards from us, producing a greatly enlarged 
and double image, the under one being inverted. 

Of an evening in camp I used to overhear the 
conversation of our men; and it struck me as curious 
that it always was about good meals of which they 
had partaken, and places where food was plenty. No 
doubt the empty state of their stomachs suggested 
such topics of conversation. 

On our fifth day of semi-starvation some Indians 
arrived with a lot of unbaked cassava-meal, which was 
capable of being converted into very good food by 
boiling into a sort of pap. There was enough of it to 
furnish food for a portion of the party, so that on the 
following morning Mr. Sawkins and eight men started 
on their return overland to the boats. One old Indian 
woman, who came with the party who brought the 
meal, was very kind to the men, and supplied each - 
with a taste of pepper-pot and cassava, ‘and a drink 
of paiworie, as a present. 

On the following day more supplies of food arrived, 
and I started off with the remaining fourteen men. 

G 2 


84 UP THE MAZARUNI. Cuar. IV. 


Arriving in woodskins at the path end we walked as 
fast as possible, starting at daylight in the morning, 
and walking till dusk, and in this way reached the 
landing on the Carabung in two days and two hours 
from the time of our leaving the Mazaruni. Whilst 
camped the first night on the path our Indians were 
aroused by sounds, which they said were produced by 
the Kanaima, and slept little, expecting an attack 
every moment; but nothing came of it. On the way 
through we stopped to cook breakfast at thé foot of 
the precipice before mentioned ; and as I sat upon a 
log to rest with an Indian beside me, I was startled 
by his uttering a cry of alarm as he sprung up and 
fled, pointing behind me. I turned hastily round, 
and, looking in the direction indicated, saw a large 
snake coming swiftly towards me, not more than ten 
yards off, with its forked tongue darting in and out. 
I seized my gun, and, taking aim at its head, fired 
just as it was passing over a rock. The instant the 
smoke from the charge cleared away I saw, to my 
astonishment, what I took to be the same snake still 
coming on; I therefore fired again, and then saw 
nothing more of the reptile. I turned towards our 
cooking place, and was astonished to find that it was 
deserted, every man having fled. Calling out that all 
danger was over, they came back, dropping in one by 
one. Walking up to the stone over which I had seen 
the snakes passing, I found two large bushmasters 
(Lachesis rhombeata), side by side, quite dead, their 
heads riddled with shot. One was 6 feet 9 inches, 
and the other 6 feet 4 inches in length, and both had 
two very long poison-fangs in their upper jaws. My 


Cuar. IV. RETURN TO CARABUNG. 85 


men told me that these snakes are greatly dreaded, 
as they will follow and overtake a man with ease, 
and there is no way of escaping from them unless 
armed. 

We met ten Arecuna Indians on their way through, 
who were fine strong-looking people, of a lighter 
colour than the Ackawoise. They are the most war- 
like of the Guiana Indians, and are consequently 
dreaded and feared by all other tribes. 

When we reached the Carabung, after our forced 
march, every one of the party was dead beat, and 
my servant, Cameron, performed the descent of the 
mountain to the landing by sliding and tumbling 
down, his legs having refused to perform the duties 
‘for which they were intended by nature. At the 
landing I found a note in pencil, stuck on a stick, 
written by Mr. Sawkins, stating “that he and party 
had arrived there at 1 p.m. that day,” just two hours 
before us, “and had gone on down stream.” We 
raised our sunken woodskins, and then camped. 

Late in the afternoon on the following day we 
arrived at our old camp at the mouth of the Carabung, 
rendered memorable by the night attack of Warracaba 
tigers, and were disappointed at not finding our boats 
there. On the way down we landed at an old deserted 
Village, and gathered a lot of ripe cashew fruit 
(Anacardium occidentale), which was very acceptable ; 
and that night, when encamped on a gravel beach on 
the Mazaruni, we finished the last particle of food in 
our possession. We hung our hammocks in the top 
of an old dead Mora tree which had fallen on the 
beach, and put up the awning amongst its branches, 


86 UP THE MAZARUNI. Cuar. 1V. 


Rain came on, of course, to add to other evils, accom- 
panied by strong gusts of wind, which nearly blew 
away our shelter. 

Next day we met our two boats coming up the 
river from Apeng, where Mr. Sawkins had found 
them. I was surprised to see my dog Chickerine 
with Peterson, having long since believed it dead— 
from the day it left us on the Carabung. Peterson 
informed me that on the morning following our de- 
parture from the boats he observed the dog coming 
out of the forest on the opposite side of the river, and, 
going across, took it over. Since then it had become 
much attached to him. It exhibited an extraordinary 
amount of sagacity in performing this journey back 
to the boats, and one would be inclined to put its 
success down to pure reasoning powers, for not having 
a chance of knowing in which direction it was being 
carried, when in the bottom of the canoe, it must have 
been guided by observing the direction of the flow of 
the stream. It was exceedingly timid, and would 
hardly approach the brink of the river to drink, yet, 
on this occasion, it must have swam many small 
branch streams, and travelled the whole night. 

After paying off our Indian carriers, we made a 
journey farther up the Mazaruni to the foot of a fall 
called Peaimah, where the river debouches from the 
sandstone mountains into the plain. Flowing from a 
gorge in the mountains in a northerly direction, it 
turns quickly to the eastward, and so continues 
winding about the plain all the way to its junction 
with the Essequebo. The Peaimah fall is some fifty feet 
in height, but not perpendicular, the water pouring 


Cmar. IV. THE DIDI. 87 


down a rugged incline plane 200 yards in length over 
horizontal beds of coarse conglomerate. Above it is a 
pool where the river bends round to the west, and, 
being backed by high and almost perpendicular sand- 
stone cliffs, forms a striking scene. In two places 
over these cliffs fell long white columns of water. 
Whilst camped below it, we were visited by a number 
of Indians who lived below the falls, many of whom, 
especially the females, had tattooed cheeks marked in 
dark-blue cross-barred lines, which completely spoilt 
any good looks they originally possessed. The fine 
weather, from the time of our crossing the Carabung 
mountains, had broken up, and though the afternoons 
were clear and intolerably hot, yet the forenoons were 
dull and cloudy and the nights rainy; evidently the 
short rainy season had set in. 

On the 24th of November we commenced. our 
return journey to the Penal Settlement, and, assisted 
by the strong current of the now swelling river, came 
along rapidly, performing in one day a distance that it 
had taken us two to accomplish in going up. 

The first night after leaving Peaimah we heard a 
long, loud, and most melancholy whistle, proceeding, 
from the direction of the depths of the forest, at which 
some of the men exclaimed, in an awed tone of voice, 
“The Didi.” Two or three times the whistle was 
repeated, sounding like that made by a human being, 
beginning in a high key and dying slowly and 
gradually away in a low one. There were conflicting 
opinions amongst the men regarding the origin of these 
sounds. Some said they proceeded from the wild 
hairy man, or “Didi,” of the Indians; others that they 


88 UP THE MAZARUNI. Cuar. IV. 


were produced by a large and poisonous snake which 
lives in one tree from its youth up, where it attains a 
great size, living upon birds which are so unfortunate 
as to alight near it, and thus become victims to its 
powers of fascination. The “Didi” is said by the 
Indians to be a short, thick set, and powerful wild 
man, whose body is covered with hair, and who lives 
in the forest. A belief in the existence of this 
fabulous creature is universal over the whole of 
British, Venezuelan, and Brazilian Guiana. On the 
Demerara river, some years after this, I met a half- 
bred woodcutter, who related an encounter that he 
had with two Didi—a male and female—in which he 
successfully resisted their attacks with his axe. In 
the fray, he stated that he was a good deal scratched. 
His story requires to be taken with a very large grain 
of salt. 

I shot a forest deer near the Carabung, standing on 
the river’s briuk, which, though it saw us, did not trust 
its eyesight, but deemed us harmless, and so fell an 
easy prey to my breechloader. 

We stopped an hour or two at Ouranapai on our 
way down, and found it greatly changed for the better, 
many new houses having sprung up. These, of course, 
were merely thatched roofs, supported on posts, the 
rafters, &c., being lashed on with bush-ropes. <A 
house of this sort can be erected in a few days, but 
the time all depends on the number of Indians em- 
ployed. The chief edifice had also been enlarged, and 
I saw over two hundred people in the village. Tame 
trumpet-birds, parrots, and small macaws overran the 
whole place. 


Cuar. IV. ALLIGATOR AND BOA. 89 


We spent a day encamped on an island near the 
Issano mouth, while Mr. Sawkins sent a canoe up to 
Peoaquah village to get some Indians to assist in 
paddling his boat, deeming it short handed. We were 
now approaching the most dangerous cataracts of the 
river, which all lie between Turisie and Waramambo, 
near the head of the tidal portion, one called Itukie, 
near the Puruni mouth, being the worst of all. The 
men who went up in the canoe brought down our old 
friend Jimbo and another man. They told me that on 
their way they encountered a large water-boa, coiled 
round an alligator, floating on the surface of the 
stream. They shot an arrow into the neck of the boa, 
which caused it slowly to unwind its coils from the 
alligator, and then disappear beneath the water. As 
soon as the latter found itself free it kicked up its heels 
and dived likewise, seemingly not much hurt, owing, 
I suppose, to its not having been long subjected to 
the crushing power of the snake. 
The remainder of our journey to the Settlement was 
not enlivened by many incidents. The most im- 
portant occurrences to me were the passage of the 
Turisie cataract, where my boat struck sunken rocks ; 
and running down the Itukie cataract, where there 
was a steep descent and fearful waves at its foot, in 
which my boat was nearly swamped, and all hands 
got a thorough drenching. 
_ We might be said to have flown down the cataracts, 
so rapidly did we pass them, only being checked for a 
short time at one called Arumatope, where we had to 
lower down after unloading; and at another called 
Muttosie, where the boats were lowered down a 


90 UP THE MAZARUNI. Cuar. IV: 


long narrow side channel, then navigable for large 
boats owing to the height of the river from recent 
rains. 

On Thursday, the 3rd of December, 1868, we 
reached the Penal Settlement, and took up our 
residence once more in the Visiting Commissioner’s 
house, glad to get into civilized quarters under a 
friendly roof again.
