IMMEDIATELY after our arrival at the Penal Settle- 
ment, Mr. Sawkins went down to Georgetown, whilst 
I set to work to get things in readiness for a trip to 
Roraima mountain, intending to ascend the Essequebo 
and Rupununi rivers, and then make a walking 
journey to it across the Pacaraima mountains. The 
repairing of boats, and sending to Georgetown for 
stores, caused a delay of two weeks’ duration, which 
was most pleasantly spent with my good friends the 
officials of the Penal Settlement. 

The days were cloudy and dull, and as I looked out 
over the broad waters of the Essequebo to the south- 
ward, in the direction of my proposed journey, where 
distant forest and leaden sky met beyond the pale 
gray waters, I thought gloomily of my lonely voyage 
about to be undertaken on the eve of Christmas 
times. 

At last, everything being ready, I departed on my 


92 UP THE ESSEQUEBO TO RORAIMA. Cap, V. 


voyage, my party consisting of twenty-two boat hands, 
in three provision-laden boats. In passing Bartika 
Grove, and paddling up the Essequebo, numbers of 
the inhabitants of that place, some of whom had sons 
or brothers amongst my crew, turned out to see us 
pass, waving kindly adieus. 

Glancing due north, down the wide stretch of 
smooth river, a water horizon met the eye; but 
looking due south up stream, the sombre forest lining 
both banks met in a thin, low, dark line in front. 
When the tide was with us, we paddled out some 
distance from the shore; but when it turned against 
us, we crept close in to the low, muddy bank. Here 
and there, where the land was high, a thatched house 
or two nestled in small clearings not far from the high 
wall of forest behind. The fine broad-leaved plan- 
tains (Musa paradisaica), growing close to these 
habitations, and the curious Moco-mocos (Caladium 
arborescens), flourishing on the mud- banks at the 
water's edge, together with a graceful palm tree here 
and there, relieved the somewhat monotonous cha- 
racter of the river-side foliage. 

When night overtook us we camped in a swampy 
place, where, unfortunately for me, there was a tree 
called the “bloodwood,” which was selected as a 
ridge pole for my awning. Whilst sleeping peace- 
fully, copious drops of its blood-red gum fell upon my 
devoted head, and when I awoke next morning I 
found my hair in a most disagreeably matted con- 
dition. 

We met two large Indian boats, with curved 
thatched tents and high prows and stems, quite a 


Crap. V., CARIBS. 93 


contrast to the frail woodskins used by the Mazaruni 
Indians. They contained a number of Caribs, one of 
whom spoke English fluently, and informed me that 
they came from the mouth of the Rupununi river, 
and were on their way to Georgetown with ham- 
mocks, &c., for sale. This man had been brought up 
by Mr. Bernau, the missionary, who at one time 
resided at Bartika Grove Mission. He and the other 
Caribs acted as traders for many tribes of the interior, 
by purchasing their articles of manufacture, such as 
cotton hammocks, cassava graters, strainers, &c., 
and, making annual visits to Georgetown, exchanged 
them for salt, knives, “axes, cutlasses, &c. Some of 
these Caribs showed signs of a mixture of negro 
blood, and originally came from Oreala, on the Coren- 
tyne river, to the Rupununi, at the time when Sir 
Robert Schomburgk was travelling in the colony. 
Passing two wood-cutting establishments, one called 
Mora, on the west bank, and the other Morabally, on 
the east—places where the far-famed greenheart (ec- 
tandra Rodai) timber is procured—we reached the 
head of the tide at the foot of the first rapid, named 
Aretaka, where the rivers surface was broken by 
ripples and lines of foam, whilst granite rocks and 
islands studded its surface, dividing it into numerous 
channels. Soon after we hauled up numerous cata- 
racts and rapids at a slow rate. Finding the river 
low, we kept up the main channel, and passed up 
Tabinetta, Marihi, and Abouyab-mallali cataracts, 
coming to a Carib village on an island, on which a 
drinking and marriage festival was being held. All 
the people were painted and ornamented. One man, 


94 UP THE ESSEQUEBO TO RORAIMA. Czaar. V. 


the bridegroom, resembled a clown in features and “get 
up,” and was said to be a Brazilian Indian. There 
was also a Madras Coolie amongst them, whose time 
of indenture having expired, was living with Indians. 
Two old women marched about from guest to guest 
with calabashes of paiworie, chanting as they offered 
the drink. 

Our boats being heavily laden, our progress was so 
slow that a party of Macusi Indians, amongst whom 
was the chief of that tribe, passed us at Itaballi, the 
last cataract of this set, on their way to their homes 
on the savannas. They were mostly middle-aged 
men of good stature, with s¢vere countenances and 
dark-red skins, darkened apparently by exposure to 
the sun in tbe open country in which they live. 

On our fourth day out we passed the last cataract 
of this set, and came upon open water, with a few 
large islands in mid river, where both banks of gray 
clay, lined with a magnificent growth of enormous 
Mora trees, could here and there be seen at one time. 
Soon after we cleared these islands, and had uninter- 
rupted views of long vistas of river. 

At the mouth of the Paruacabra we saw a cayman 
(Champsa nigra) for the first time. It is a singular 
fact that on the Cuyuni and Mazaruni—tributaries of 
this river—none of these reptiles are to be seen. It 
would appear that they do not, care to pass below the 
cataracts at the head of the tide; but for what reason 
it is impossible to say, for the water there is quite 
fresh, the brackish water never coming more than a 
short distance into the mouth of the Essequebo, and 
then only in the very driest seasons. Again, the 


Cuap. V. GULLS AND TURTLES. 95 


Demerara river, lying between the Essequebo and 
Berbice rivers, has not a single cayman in it, while 
the upper part of the Berbice is swarming with them. 

In some places on the wide, open portion of the 
river, curious long trap dykes rise above the surface 
of the water. Our general course was a little to the 
westward of south, passing on our way a tree-clothed 
mountain on the east bank, called Arrisaro. There 
were some large sand-beaches in this and other parts 
of the river, on which two species of grayish-white 
gulls have their nests—mere holes scratched in the 
sand. They fly and wheel about over the river, 
and will swoop down at anyone walking on the sand 
near their eggs or young. The scissor-bill gulls, or 
“sea-dogs” (Rhynchops melanurus), also are fre- 
quently seen flying swiftly along in small parties, 
with their sharp, flat beaks dipping in the water. 
Their cry resembles somewhat the bark of a dog, 
hence they have received the name of “sea-dogs.” 
On these sand-beaches we sometimes came on the 
nests of turtles, and often obtained as many as one 
bundred and thirty eggs from one nest. There are two 
common kinds of fresh-water turtle, one of large size, 
called Sachapana (Podocnemis expansa), the other a 
small one, called Taracai (Emys tracaza). The latter 
lays ellipsoidal eggs, with shells, whilst the former 
lays large spherical eggs, without shells. The yelk, 
the only part of the egg that is eaten, has a pleasant 
but oily flavour. 

Before reaching the mouth of the Potaro—a large 
black-water tributary on the west—we saw some dis- 
tant high mountains on the right hand, where the 


96 UP THE ESS8EQUEBO TO RORAIMA. Cuar. V. 


river made a great bend to the westward. Not far 
beyond the Potaro, where the river sweeps back to 
its original course, we came to the cataract of Wara- 
puta, at the foot of which we encamped .on a white 
sand beach, and spent Christmas Day. There, in the 
easterly channel, on large blocks of porphyritic green- 
stone, are inscribed rude markings and unintelligible 
characters, formed by the hands of men of prehistoric 
times. On Christmas morning I visited the spot, and 
spent a portion of the day in copying the inscriptions. 

There was an indescribable charm about these 
rocks, situated in the midst of the rush of mighty 
waters, marked with their cabalistic signs, the work of 
a vanished race. No one that sees them can doubt for 
a moment that every sign has a meaning, or was made 
to serve some important purpose of the sculptors. It 
may be that they point to a time when men of some 
extinct race had arrived at that stage of human ex- 
istence when mysterious feelings of reverence for the 
seen and unseen find a place in their minds, filling 
them with longings for worship of any sort, and 
that to satisfy these cravings they set-up their own 
idols in the form of rude carvings on rocks. This will 
explain how the figures of men, the sun, &c., came to 
be formed, but not the scrollwork and inscriptions, 
which I believe were intended to record some striking 
events in the history of these ancient people. 

The Indians residing in the districts where these 
records are found are a quiet, docile race, who have 
not arrived at the stage mentioned above, nor worship 
either idol or unseen deity ; and though they acknow- 
ledge the existence of one great and good spirit, never 


Cuar. V. INDIAN PICTURE-WRITING. 97 


appeal to him for aid in time of trouble. Their 
doctrine is, that being good he does not afflict them, 
but that all their ills proceed from the workings of a 
number of bad spirits. These ills, they believe, can be 
nullified or cured by their sorcerers or Peaimans, who 
call to their assistance a host of good spirits. This is 
their only religion, and its fits come on only during 
seasons of illness. 

They can offer no explanation of the meaning of 
the picture writings, nor conjecture by whom they 
were made; and one would suppose that had they 
been the direct descendants of the picture-writing 
people they would have had some tradition con- 
cerning them. One man, however, in the Pacaraima 
mountains, who showed me some of these writings, 
said that the Great Spirit, or Mackunaima, had made 
them. 

Not far beyond Waraputa we passed a singular 
pillar of granite, called Paiworie cayra, some fifteen feet 
in height, standing on a pedestal of the same rock. It 
is formed of very hard granite, which has resisted the 
disintegrating powers of the weather and the denuding 
forces of the river, whilst the surrounding mass has 
succumbed to them. 

Some three miles beyond this rock we came to a set 
of steep cataracts, called respectively Haiawah, Haba- 
cuhaha, Owspart, and Saya. On the west bank of the 
river some high wooded mountains with deep glens 
can be seen, not far from these falls. They are part 
of the escarpment of the greenstone and sandstone 
mountains, which come round from the south of the 
Mazaruni river. Farther on, above Itaboo cataract, 

H 


98 UP THE ESSEQUEBO TO RORAIMA. Cnap. V. 


we passed the continuation of these mountains, abut- 
ting on the river and rising above it to a height of 
about 1100 feet. On the face of their most southerly 
point is a high pillar of greenstone rock, of a most 
singular form, which, from its similarity in shape to 
an earthen water-jar, has obtained the Indian appella- 
tion of Comuti. 

Between these falls I observed, for the first time, 
that numerous pacu came to the surface, bringing 
their heads and back fins out of the water, after the 
manner of a porpoise, and dipping instantly again. 
The men said that, on hearing the noise made by our 
boats, these fish came to the surface in this manner to 
see what was disturbing their pools. My men shot 
numbers of them, keeping our larder well supplied 
with good fresh food. After quitting the last fall my 
only regret was that no more pacu were to be obtained. 

About this time our nights were rendered burden- 
some by the attacks of countless sandflies, which fed 
viciously on our blood, drawing it from the region of 
the wrists, neck, and ankles. 

After leaving the Tarquarie mountain we passed 
two cataracts, and had a long stretch of open river, 
unbroken by a single rapid until we arrived at 
Ouropocarie cataract, where we had to portage the 
stores. There we camped and spent New Year’s Eve 
on a sand-beach, where the wind blew a drizzling rain 
on me all night, and the men saw the old year out 
and the new year in, dancing and firing guns at 
intervals to commemorate the event. 

The next falls, a few miles above Ouropocarie, are 
called the Carricurie, or gold falls, where, on the west 


Cmar. V. POISONOUS BAMBOO. 99 


bank, is a small village of Macusi Indians, whose 
houses are like large haystacks, thatched from the 
apex of the conical roof to the ground. 

We spent January 3rd encamped on a sand-beach 
opposite the end of a high mountain on the east, called 
Maccari, where the men set a hook for a cayman, 
which they succeeded in catching and destroying. 
Next day we passed Shani-pishani cataracts and the 
granite rocks of Acramucra, where there is another 
Macusi house upon an island, and arrived at smooth, 
open river beyond the Rappu Rapids. The islands in 
these rapids and a river near by are so called from 
the existence of a peculiar species of tall and graceful 
bamboo which flourishes there, not being found farther 
north. Pieces of the stem of this bamboo are dried 
and used by the Indians as arrow-heads, which are 
said to possess similar properties to the far-famed 
Woralli poison. They split up the stem, and dry the 
pieces over a fire, and then shape them into lance- 
heads, which they fasten on the ends of arrows. Wild 
animals wounded by these arrows are at once com- 
pletely paralyzed, and in that condition easily de- 
spatched. This bamboo is tall, growing singly, and 
not in clumps, from a mass of matted roots, like the 
common bamboo. 

I landed at the mouth of Aruan inlet, and there got 
my legs covered with large ticks, which bit very 
severely, producing small sores, which became very 
troublesome on my walking journey afterwards. From 
the Rappu Rapids the river comes from the south-west, 
so that the north-east trade blowing along it was fair, 
and we hoisted the awnings as sails. The boats thus 

H 2 


100 UP THE ESSEQUEBO TO RORAIMA. Crap. V. 


propelled moved steadily, but not very rapidly, up 
stream, and we passed the mouth of the Rupununi, a 
large tributary, 200 miles from the mouth of the 
Essequebo, at 4 p.m. on the 6th of January. We 
landed at the Carib village of Apoterie, on the west 
bank, soon after, and there found some twenty Indians, 
families of the Caribs we met the day we left the 
Grove. 

The water of the Essequebo, above the mouth of 
the Rupununi, was of a dark colour by reflected light, 
almost black in fact, whilst below that river, after 
receiving a large volume of whitish Rupununi water, 
it lost much of its dark tint. At their junction, 
before mingling, the contrast in colour between the 
two waters is very great. 

The band of Mora trees lining both banks of the 
Essequebo continued along it as far up as we went, 
but did not grow on the banks of the Rupununi up 
to Pirara landing. Quite a different sort of forest 
growth begins there, formed of lower and more varied 
descriptions of trees, of species capable of resisting 
long periods of drought. One of these, the sapwood 
of which is white, and the heart bright red, has the 
somewhat singular name of Itickiebourabally. The 
Mora grows only on a moist soil along the borders 
of the river, forming a band on both sides of varying 
widths, according to the breadth of the band of 
flooded country during the rainy season. In places 
it is often not more than 100 yards in width. Up 
many small side streams the Mora has marched to 
their sources, while along others it has not attempted 
to spread. By their solid-looking tops, composed 


Cuar. V. CAPTURE CAYMAN. 101 


- of massive dark green leaves, and their great height, 
they attract attention and excite admiration; and 
when portions of their tops are sprouting new leaves, 
which are of a light liver colour at first, turning red, 
and then green, a forest of them presents many varied 
tints. Each branch throws out shoots and gets new 
leaves at all times of the year, independent of other 
portions of the same tree. — 

Next morning we returned to and commenced 
our voyage up the Rupununi, the largest tributary 
of the Essequebo in this part. It is wide and 
shallow, with many sand-beaches at its bends, high, 
gray clay banks, and yellowish-white water. Shortly 
after entering it a fine view of the high, wooded, 
distant mountain of Macarapan is obtained, which, seen 
in the afternoon through the clear atmosphere, is of 
a dark blue colour. In the mornings and forenoons 
the weather was dull and cloudy, and large masses 
of mist hung about the mountain top. A large river, 
called the Rewa or Quitaro, with dark water, was 
passed on our second day on the Rupununi. 

We spent Sunday, the 10th, on a sand-beach near 
the mouth of Taraqua inlet, where the men caught a 
cayman, eleven feet in length, with a strong hook and 
cod-line. The baited hook was placed on a small 
piece of wood, and allowed to float on the surface 
of the stream, where the cayman seized it. As the 
men were hauling the reptile ashore, before settling its 
hash with an axe, it floundered and splashed a good 
deal, attracting the attention and exciting the ire 
of its companion, which swam to within five yards 
of the beach, where it prudently remained, its eyes 


102 UP THE ESSEQUEBO TO RORAIMA. Cuar. V. 


shining in the starlight like live coals of fire. An 
extraordinary incident occurred that night which is 
worth relating here. One of the men left some food 
in a tin pan under his hammock, along with two iron 
spoons, and in the morning, pan, food, and all had 
disappeared. The only trace of the thief was the 
footprints of a cayman in the sand leading from the 
water to the hammock, and back to the river again. 
Two and a half months afterwards, in passing this 
sand-beach, we landed, when one of the men, recol- 
lecting the episode of the tin pan, went down the 
beach, which was much enlarged by the subsidence of 
the river, in search of it, and on the lower end, under 
the branches of overhanging trees, found the pan, much 
dented by the teethmarks of the cayman, but with the 
two spoons still in it. The singular part of it is, how 
on earth did the cayman manage to carry off the pan 
without upsetting the spoons? 

When we camped on the Saturday night, black- 
headed turkey buzzards (Cathartes foetens) arrived, 
one by one, until nearly 100 were collected in the 
neighbouring tree-tops. There they sat out the 
following day and night, patiently waiting till we 
left camp, to get any scraps and bones that might 
remain behind. Their patience was well rewarded, 
for no sooner had we pushed off than the lot pounced 
down on the sand, and made a rush at the decapitated 
cayman, over which they fought, hissed, and jostled 
each other, tearing away at its neck and soon making 
a hole big enough for one to get its body in. Some 
of these unclean birds hopped round the camp fires 
and fought over scraps and bones of fish. 


Cuar. V, RUPUNUNI SAVANNA. 103 


On arriving at the first place where the savanna 
comes to the edge of the river on the north side, 
I landed, and climbing up the clay bank, gazed with 
delight upon the fine, open, grass-covered country 
to the north-west. Stretched out before me was an 
open rolling prairie, carpeted with short green grass, 
and dotted sparingly here and there with small trees, 
while the watercourses traversing it were lined with 
bands of green foliage. Amongst the grass were 
some pretty flowers and shrubs, a small yellow crocus 
and a beautiful large twin-flowered lily being most 
couspicuous. This latter plant had simply a leafless 
green flower-stalk, about eighteen inches high, from the 
top of which two white flowers with yellow centres 
bent out at right angles to the stem. The view was 
backed to the north-west by grass-covered mountains, 
looking in the distance like hillocks placed one behind 
the other, stretching far away till they became of a 
faint blue in the distance. To the north-east was 
the great isolated mountain of Makarapan, rising like 
a giant from the plain. 

Continuing our journey, the high gray banks hid 
the view, and in many parts on the north bank a 
band of trees grew along the river’s edge, while the 
south side was all wooded. When we arrived at 
the mouth of a creek called Watama, from which 
a path led to two Macusi villages on the savanna, 
we landed and walked to one for the purpose of 
obtaining an interpreter for the Macusi language, 
who was known by my men to live there. After 
an hour’s walking over the hard gravelly savanna 
we reached a small village, consisting of five conical 


104 UP THE ESSEQUEBO TO RORAIMA. Cuapr. V. 


thatched houses, where I obtained a guide and went 
on to Annai mountain four miles off. From the top 
of the first range, 700 feet in height, which was 
covered with coarse dry grass, I could see the savanna 
extending far to the westward, while all the moun- 
tains to the north were covered with dense forest. 
At the village I engaged a Macusi, called Henry, 
to come with us as interpreter, he being able to 
speak English a little, and Dutch patois fluently, from 
having lived at the Grove for a considerable period. 

In recrossing the savanna to the boats, we started a 
large flock of savanna plover ( Vanellas Cayennensis), 
a kind of pewit, identical in form and size with the 
English green plover. Their flight is the same, and 
they wheeled over the plain in great numbers. Their 
colour, however, is of a slaty cast, and they have a 
red iris, with a red spur on the wing at the end of the 
portion supporting the secondary feathers. Benjamin 
shot two large black savanna ibises, which were flying 
about, uttering most melancholy béll-like crics. ‘They 
were about the size of large fowls, having red bills 
and legs. The sides of their heads and under parts of 
their throats were bare of feathers, while the skin 
on the former portions was red, that of the throat was 
orange. They are called Tarar, and ure never sought 
after as food by the Indians. The savanna pewit also 
is never eaten by Indians, as they say that partaking 
of its flesh produces deafness. 

Large birds, called sowewies or negrocopes ( Tan- 
talus loculator), were numerous in this portion of the 
country, and are seen on the sand-beaches in the river. 
Their heads and necks are bare of feathers, being 


Cuar. V. BIRDS. 105 


covered with a hard, black skin, divided by furrows 
into plates. They are white, with black wings, and 
are frequently seen soaring high in the heavens in 
circles, mounting higher and higher till they appear 
like mere specks. Common gray cranes (Ardea cocoi) 
are very numerous, and one frequently meets with the 
great Jabiru ( Mycteria Americana), with its long, stout, 
upcurved beak, black bare head, and scarlet-banded 
neck. When wounded and brought to bay, it inflates 
the skin of the scarlet-coloured portion like a great 
bladder. It stands fully five feet in height, and walks 
with a slow and stately tread. 

Flights of a small kind of brownish pigeon were 
very common, arriving of an evening amongst the 
trees on the river’s banks, and there finding a roosting 
place. Small flocks of Muscovy ducks (Anas moschata) 
were seen every day, and also at times the pretty 
little Wicissi duck (Dendrocygna viduata). The latter 
generally fly by night, when their presence is known 
by their whistle. 

My men frequently shot a fish called the Aruana 
(Osteoglossum arowanna), which was capital eating. 
It is a sword-shaped fish, of about two feet six inches 
in length, covered with large iridescent scales. Its 
mouth turns up at an angle of some 45° to the axis of 
the body, and it generally swims near the surface of 
the water, where it jumps at passing flies. This fish 
is not found on the Essequebo river below the Rappu 
Rapids, as far as I could learn, and is not known to 
exist on the Mazaruni or Cuyuni rivers. I have since 
seen the same fish on the Purus and Jurua rivers, 
southern tributaries of the Amazon. 


106 UP THE ESSEQUEBO TO RORAIMA. Canar. V. 


One day’s journey from Watama brought us to the 
end of the general westerly course pursued in ascend- 
ing the Rupununi, and from thence on in following up 
its windings we travelled in a southerly direction for 
two days, when we arrived at Pirara landing, the 
termination of our journey by water. At the entrance 
of a little inlet, called Wai-ipukari, we formed a camp, 
and set to work to make waiaries and pack up loads 
of provisions for our walking journey to Roraima. 
I sent the interpreter to Carenacru, the village where 
Passico, the Macusi chief, lived, to ask for Indians as 
guides and carriers to Roraima. 

Our camping place was pretty, but dull, its stillness 
being occasionally most disagreeably broken by the 
heavy splash of tbe huge arrowpaima fish (Suidas. 
gigas) in the inlet, or the gulping bellow of the 
cayman, of which reptile there were great numbers in 
this part. At first the latter were very tame, from 
never being molested by the Indians, but after getting 
some heavy blows from stones, thrown now and then 
by our men, they kept off a bit. When one was 
struck it became greatly enraged and swam hastily 
about, inflating its body with air until it floated high 
in the water, when it would raise its head and tail 
simultaneously, giving a great snort. 

The arrowpaimas had a trick, on coming to the 
surface, of bringing their tails flat down on the water, 
with a blow that sounded like the report of a pistol, 
and, as they practised it chiefly at night, it had a most 
startling effect. My men shot one of these fish with 
arrows, which was some five or six feet in length, with 
long head, and large greenish scales riinmed with red. 


Cuar. V. VISITED BY MACUSIS. 107 


In this part a band of trees grew along the river’s 
edge, and concealed a large pond at the head of the 
inlet, which was covered with the beautiful Victoria 
Regia lily, whose leaves completely covered the water. 
Water-fowl, chiefly the spurwing (Parra jacana), 
inhabited the pool, running about on the Victoria 
leaves in search of flies, &c., and uttering their curious 
startled cry when approached. 

Growing on the branches of the surrounding trees, 
amongst numerous other parasites, were two or three 
kinds of orchids, the loveliest of which was the 
Cattleya superba, then in full bloom. 

On the second morning of our stay at the landing, 
loud and wild shouts proceeded from the pathway 
leading through the belt of wood from the river to the 
savanna, making the forest ring again; and soon after 
a band of painted, feather-hatted warriors of the 
Macusi tribe marched into camp, armed with guns 
and bows and arrows, headed by old Passico, their 
chief. Squatting on the ground round me, they lis- 
tened to the palaver I held with Passico, through the 
interpreter, regarding arrangements for men to go 
with me to Roraima. The chief difficulties lay in 
getting guides who knew the path all the way, and in 
overcoming the reluctance of the Macusis to go into 
the country of the Arecuna Indians. A fine strapping 
young Indian was at last pointed out as the only one 
who had ever been to Roraima, and the statement 
was made that he and his brother would be our 
guides. With regard to the fear of the Arecuna 
Indians Passico made light of it, as he was not to 
be one of the party, and settled the question by 


108 UP THE ESSEQUEBO TO RORAIMA. Cuar. V. 


pointing out each man that was to go, in a regal sort 
of way. 

This I thought was put on, and would have little 
effect, but, to my surprise, each man cheerfully re- 
sponded to the call, answering with the usual “ wah,” 
which stands for “all right,” with Indians. Before 
the palaver was over more shouts were heard, and in 
came another band of Macusis, men and boys, headed 
by a bone-flute player. This kind of flute, in common 
use amongst Mecusis, is made of the thigh bone of the 
jaguar, and has only three holes, but produces good 
Indian music. Closely following on this party were a 
number of women and children, making a total of over 
fifty people. The whole lot spent the day talking, 
laughing, and examining our kits, evidently enjoying 
themselves; and at night all but those who were told 
off to accompany us left by the way they came. They 
all wore sandals, made of the base of the leaf stalk of 
the Itah palm (Mauritia flexuosa), tied to the foot in a 
most ingenious manner by cord made of the outer part 
of the young leaf of the same tree. 

The surface of the higher parts of the savanna being 
covered with sharp pellets of iron oxide, renders it 
necessary for barefooted travellers to have some pro- 
tection for the soles of their feet in traversing them. 
My men had to be provided with these sandals, and, 
owing to their soon wearing out, had to get a new 
pair each, every third day, as we marched along, the 
Indians generally making them when we stopped to 
break fast. 

After sending our surplus stores down river some 
five or six miles to a village called Karinambo, to be

Cuar. V. THE SAVANNA. 109 


taken care of by Indians there, we moored two of our 
boats in the inlet and sent the other back to the 
Penal Settlement. ! 

I then started on my journey inland with thirteen 
of my own men, the interpreter, and thirteen Macusis, 
all carrying loads of about thirty pounds weight each. 
In ten minutes after leaving camp we emerged from 
the band of bush upon the high rolling open savanna, 
along which one could see the plain stretching away 
to the westward for many miles. Small trees dotted 
the surface of the arid soil here and there of species 
peculiar to such localities. None were more than 
between twenty and thirty feet in height, and many 
of them not more than ten. There was one with large 
leaves and white blossoms like a magnolia, another 
like a myrtle, and a third—the Curatella—with large 
leaves having their under surfaces as rough as sand- 
paper. All had rough-fluted bark stems, and were of 
soft, brittle wood. Being so scattered, they did not 
afford the least particle of shade against the rays of 
the burning sun. 

North and south of this great rolling plain were 
two masses of high mountains, the one being part of 
the Pacaraima range, and the other the lofty Canuku 
chain. The Pacaraimas, being only some sixteen 
miles off, their yellowish dry grass covering, with 
small patches of forest clustering here and there, 
could be distinctly seen; while the Canukus, being 
twenty-four miles away, presented an aspect of a 
dark-blue colour. 

I felt my pulse bound as I stepped along this 
glorious plain with a sense of boundless freedom, 


110 UP THE ESSEQUEBO TO RORAIMA. Curar. V. 


engendered by the nature of the scene, after our late 
cooped-up river journey, hemmed in by tall forests 
and high clay banks. We took a southerly course, and 
soon reached a small village called Aringwong, at 
which Passico was waiting for me. On we went 
towards the Canuku mountains, marching in single 
file along the well-beaten path, and after crossing a 
great bog, half-knee deep in black mud and water, 
came to Passico’s village, called Carenacru. This 
village consisted of twenty large circular haystack- 
like houses, situated on high ground on the savanna, 
containing a population of about two hundred souls. 
A good mud-walled house was assigned to us for the 
night and following day, where we took up our 
quarters. The next day was spent in making 
arrangements for the journey. Two men were sup- 
posed to have gone on, as I wished to apprise the 
people of the various villages in our line of march 
of our coming, in order that they might prepare pro- 
visions for us; but as we never heard of them again 
at any village through which we passed, we concluded 
that they never went. One man was told off as hunts- 
man to the party, and was provided with a gun and 
ammunition, but carried no load saving his own ham- 
mock. 

During our stay at the place, parties of villagers 
would stalk quietly into our house, examine every- 
thing, talk a good deal, and then retire, to be replaced 
by another party. Five or six little boys kept con- 
tinually about us, and made themselves generally 
useful in bringing water and wood to the cooks. 
Passico paid me frequent visits, and also conducted 


Cuap. V. LEAVE CARENACRU. 111 


me round the village from house to house, where many 
objects of interest were to be seen. 

On Friday, January the 22nd, we made our final 
start, passing out of the village in single file to the 
number of thirty-seven individuals, midst much talk- 
ing, laughing, shouting, and dog barking. Our course 
at first lay in a west and west-north-west direction, 
over the rolling savanna, passing to the southward of 
the old village of Pirara, and Lake Amucu; across 
the Pirara river, and over the level, clayey plain of 
the Jreng. This plain, being lower than the savanna, 
is all flooded in rainy seasons. It has very few stunted 
trees upon it, while the higher portions of the savanna 
have, in some few places, large groves of trees, which 
are called islands, in which the Indians of the region 
cut down patches and plant their cassava. 

The Pirara river was low at the time, the water 
lying in holes surrounded with growths of moco-moco, 
a sort of alder, and some prickly soweri palms (Astro- 
caryum jauari). In one of these we saw a manatee 
(Manatus Americanus), or river-cow, and a large 
wooden dam across the river, made by the Indians 
for the purpose of capturing these animals at certain 
seasons. 

On the following morning we reached the Ireng 
river, a broad, black-water stream bordered by trees, 
and running south to the Rio Branco, across which we 
were ferried in a leaky dug-out canoe, four or five at a 
time. On reaching some water-holes in a small river 
beyond, from which the westerly direction of our 
journey was to be changed for a more northerly one, 
we were compelled to camp on account of there being 


112 UP THE ESSEQUEBO TO RORAIMA. Crap. V. 


no more water obtainable under six or seven hours’ 
good walking. There, in a snug camp, we spent our 
third day out, a Sunday, principally to rest the men, 
who were footsore and weary from their two days’ 
walk over gravelly paths with heavy loads, under a 
merciless sun, the rays of which beat down with 
fervent heat at every lull in the fitful north-east 
wind. Without that breeze, which blew in spurts 
night and day, to cool us, I do not know how we 
could have stood the heat. The ground was baked 
like brick, and it was difficult to understand how the 
thin blades of grass retained any green colour in them. 
They grew thinly in scattered clumps and bunches, 
and made a poor show. 

A species of termite builds high conical houses of 
gray clay over the savannas and savanna mountains, 
some of which are twelve feet in height; and these 
are usually built in clusters, looking in the far distance 
like miniature Indian villages. Under many of them 
are the holes of a small species of armadillo (Dasypus 
12-cinctus), while some have been partially under- 
mined, or altogether upset, by the great ant-bear 
(Myrmecophaga jubata). 

Whilst resting that day, the huntsman and two 
other Indians shot two Muscovy ducks, two savanna 
deer, and a small Abouyah tiger. This tiger’s body was 
four feet long, of a light yellow colour, with black cir- 
cular spots. The Indians and my men caught numbers 
of small fish, called Houries (Erythrinus unitaeniatus), 
with hook and line, in the shaded water pools near our 
camp. These fish were from six inches to one foot in 
length, with large mouths, and were very good eating. 


Cuar. V. CROSSING SAVANNA. 113 


Continuing our journey due north over the savanna, 
we plodded on from 6 a.m. until 1 p.m. over the arid 
plain, without meeting with a drop of water to quench 
our thirst, and suffered greatly. Then we arrived at a 
beantiful lake of clear water, the very sight of which, 
with its miniature waves beating on its white sand 
beach, revived us. A ridge of white sand, on its 
leeward edge, was dotted with large, wild cashew 
trees, from which we obtained a plentiful supply of 
that sweet but astringent fruit. On the way we 
killed a rattlesnake (Crotalus horridus), which was 
crossing the path; it had seven rattles. 

Leaving the lake of Culucurana, we advanced 
towards the Pacaraima mountains, which showed a 
beautiful picture of highly-coloured peaks and ridges 
of yellow, purple, and blue colours. The atmosphere 
being remarkably clear, without the usual haziness it 
so often possesses, all details in form and colouring 
were strikingly apparent, aud the fine cobalt blue of 
the distant hills very remarkable. Late in the even- 
ing we arrived at the foot of some outlying ridges, 
and were forced to camp at a water-hole, in which the 
water was so mixed with white clay that it exactly 
resembled milk in appearance. This adulterated liquid 
we were forced to drink and use for culinary purposes, 
nothing purer being obtainable. We caught a glimpse 
of some Indians fleeing from us in the distance, where- 
upon our Indians shouted loudly after them, which 
only increased their fright. 

Crossing a low pass in the mountains, we came 
upon the valley of the Urumara river, and, traversing 


it westward, passed through two small Macusi villages, 
I 


114 UP THE ESSEQUEBO TO RORAIMA. Czar. V. 


and arrived at a third, called Enamouta, where a great 
dance and paiworie-drinking festival were going on. 
On the hard sun-baked clay, in front of the village 
houses, thirty Macusis were dancing to the sound of 
drums and bone flutes, in a most solemn manner, 
whilst paiworie was being handed round. We marched 
up and rested awhile under the open shed, called the 
strangers’ house, with which all these villages are 
furnished, and, having attracted the attention of a 
small crowd not engaged in dancing, offered to buy up 
all the eatables in the village, as we were getting 
hard up for food. All the people, being in an excited 
condition, merely jeered at us at first, but, seeing what 
we offered in exchange, were struck with admiration, 
and soon glutted the market with every old scrap of 
food they could gather. Those who got calico in 
exchange for cassava, wound it round their bodies, 
and danced the whole night thus enrolled. Some had 
guns, and danced with them loaded, discharging a 
blank shot every now and then in close proximity to 
the heads of others. One man, who foamed consi- 
derably at the mouth, had a stout sapling in his hand, 
which he now and then offered to a bystander, and 
holding out one leg, received a few good whacks, 
which seemed to satisfy him for awhile, for he then 
joined the dancers. Emerging from the circle again, 
he gave his stick to the first person he saw, and once 
gave it to me, but I would have nothing to do with 
him, at which he was much cast down. Besides the 
usual red paint and black dye on their bodies, these 
people had marked themselves with a white felspathic 
clay found in the neighbourhood. 


Cuar. V. INDIAN FESTIVAL. 115 


After our arrival, a lot of Indians of a branch tribe 
of the Macusis, called Tasoulemas, came from another 
village to join the dance, equipped most picturesquely 
in headdresses, tippets, and short skirts, made of the 
young pale-yellow leaf of the Itah palm. Many of 
these were provided with long wooden tubes, painted 
with rings of red and black, and with flat carved ends 
of wood, into which they blew, producing a sort of 
horn-like sound. To the music of drums, bone flutes, 
and these wooden horns, they all danced, chanting all 
the while the endless accompaniment of “ Hi-yáb- 
Hi-yah-Hi-y4h-Ha-a-a-a,” from the time of our arrival 
until we left next morning. I had to go and hang my 
hammock for the night to trees, some distance off on 
the savanna, to get away from the horrid row of the 
festival. 

When the sun rose next morning, it disclosed a 
draggled set of debauchees, weary, stale-looking, and 
dirty, bedaubed with paint and dust, and in striking 
contrast with the cool, fresh, sparkling appearance of 
the mountain-locked valley of the Urumara, with its 
balmy, refreshing morning air. During the night 
some ancient feud between the two tribes that had 
lain dormant, cropped up again, and nearly produced 
a hand-to-hand fight, but happily was settled by gesti- 
culations and words. 

In crossing from the valley of the Urumara river 
to the next valley beyond, the path followed the dry 
bed of a mountain torrent, shaded by trees amongst 
huge rocks. Not a drop of water was to be seen 
until the very top of the pass was reached, where the 
head of the stream, a deep pool of limpid water, was 

I 2 


116 UP THE ESSEQUEBO TO RORAIMA. Cuar. V. 


curiously situated. Descending from this pass, we 
entered a large valley, where yellow withered grass 
and great masses of rock, tinted with a purple lichen, 
a hard sun-baked soil full of quartz blocks, and an 
entire absence of water, combined to give it a most 
sterile aspect. The great masses of quartz porphyry 
in these mountains assumed most curious forms, 
sticking up in clusters like old ruined walls, or rose 
in irregular obelisks. 

A pass divided this from another valley, where we 
found pools of water in the rocky bed of a river, 
called the Conewyutelli. In this part were numbers 
of gray flycatchers, with black heads and exceedingly 
long tail-feathers. 

During this portion of our journey we met with 
water only twice a day, the path making detours to 
meet the beds of streams; so that during the intervals 
between 6 A.M. and noon, and between noon and 
5 P.M., we plodded on in a thirst-stricken condition. 
Frequently, on arrival at a water-hole at noon, I would 
find, to my intense disgust, many of the Indians of 
our party, who had led the way and arrived a few 
minutes before me, all sitting in the water, cooling 
themselves, tugether with a dog or two. At first I 
gave vent to my feelings in very strong language, 
but, as it was not understood and had no good effect, 
I resigned myself to my fate. Once or twice we 
found heaps of pounded haiarie on the rocks bordering 
the pools, showing that Indians had poisoned them to 
procure fish, and thereby rendered their water un- 
drinkable. The scarcity of water was owing to the 
dry season being then at its height. During the rainy 


Canar. V. GLIMPSE OF RORAIMA. 117 


season many of these valleys and small rivers would 
be almost impassable from the quantity of water in 
them. At night there was not a particle of dew, 
although the sky was cloudless. The strong north- 
east trade wind was our chief comfort, and though in 
some sheltered valleys it was wanting, yet in others, 
and on the ridges, it made the air delightfully cool. 

Although we crossed many Indian paths going in 
various directions, we did not meet a human being 
from the time of leaving Enamouta until we reached a 
place called Cumparuyamo—a period of three days. 
Leaving this village, the path led up a steep ridge, 
2117 feet above the sea level, from the top of which I 
obtained a most glorious and finely-coloured view of 
mountains and valleys beyond; and what added 
greatly to the charm of tbe scene, was the glimpse 
afforded of the faint blue outlines of Roraima moun- 
tain, far away in the north-west. To the east, one 
looked down on a small river winding its way through 
a level bottom to the Ireng ; while to the west a long 
valley stretched away to the Cotinga river, behind 
which came masses of irregularly-shaped mountains. 
Far away northward were dark, heavy-looking moun- 
tains, evidently covered with forests. Lying before 
me, across the valley, was the southern escarpment of 
the sandstone mountains, whose northern faces we had 
seen from the Mazaruni. 

Not far from the foot of the escarpment above 
alluded to we passed through a village and over the 
Saca-outa river, continuing our journey to the Mara- 
kang, a small stream with fine clear water, at which 
we spent Sunday, January 3lst. That day I ascended 


118 UP THE ESSEQUEBO TO RORAIMA. Cuar. V- 


an outlying mountain of quartz porphyry, crested with 
sandstone, accompanied by a few of my men, who 
amused themselves by starting large blocks of sand- 
stone from its top, and watching them roll down the 
mountain side into the valley below, where, with a 
noise like a report of a cannon, they were broken into 
a thousand fragments against the hard porphyry 
masses. This outlier is called Cumararing; its top 
is 2600 feet above the sea, whilst the level of our 
camp below was 1335 feet. I spent the latter part of 
the day in making brads out of small fish-hooks, to 
secure the soles of my boots, which were on the point 
of coming off, owing to the nails having dropped out 
one by one. 

Ascending the escarpment of the sandstone moun- 
tains, we kept along its edge, and crossing many 
valleys, descended the greenstone layer, upon which 
it rests, to a large village on the Karakanang river. 
As our party marched in Indian file into one end of 
the place, nearly all the inhabitants fled from the 
other. One man who remained came forward to greet 
me, and proved to be one of the Indians who was one 
of our carriers from the Carabung creek to the Upper 
Mazaruni. He informed the interpreter that he had 
returned to this place, his home, by a path across the 
Marumé mountains. When he went and told the other 
Indians they all returned to their houscs, and furnished 
us with supplies. 

Each of the five large houses, of which the place 
was composed, had a large store of Indian corn placed 
on poles across the tie-beams in its roof. In large 
wooden mortars, sunk in the floors of the houses, the 


Cuar. V. SCARCITY OF FOOD. 119 


Indians pounded this corn into meal with long wooden 
poles, used as pestles. From the meal the women 
prepared a sour drink, and also a nutritious pap. 

The inhabitants of this village, though similar in 
appearance to the Macusis, are a branch tribe like the 
Tasoulamas, and are called Karakanang-outas. They 
said that many of their people had gone to Roraima to 
see an Indian sorcerer there, who had the power of 
making himself invisible at will. 

Our store of salt fish by this time was very low, 
and our huntsman seldom procured anything for us 
save two or three pigeons or parrots per day. 

Leaving Karakanang, we again ascended the sand- 
stone escarpment to its crest, at a height of 2800 feet 
above the sea, and passed over a remarkably sterile 
tract of country, covered with dry grass and blocks of 
jasper, where scarcely a stunted tree was to be seen. 

When we reached a small village on the Pepee 
river we were disappointed at not being able to pro- 
cure any provisions, and, as our stock was so low, I 
saw that it was impossible to go on with such a large 
party. Dividing, therefore, the remaining provisions, 
I sent most of the men back to Karakanang village, 
where there was plenty of corn to live on, to await 
my return; and with three Macusis, Ben, and William, 
I continued my journey. William had by this time 
completely mastered the Macusi language, from its 
being so similar to his own, and was able to act as 
interpreter. 

We had not gone many miles before the guide lost 
the path, and we all scattered to look for it. In 
doing so I walked almost on the top of a sleeping 


120 UP THE ESSEQUEBO TO RORAIMA. Cuar. V. 


ant-bear ( Myrmecophaga jubata), which, springing up, 
sat on its hind legs, and grasped at me with its huge 
fore claws. I sprang quickly to one side, and thus 
escaped. Thinking that it was good eating, I shot it, 
but the Indians said that it was not wholesome food, 
although, from the great interest they took in seeing 
it killed, I thought it was. These large ant-eaters are 
very dangerous customers, and have been known to 
kill men. William told me that an Indian, living 
near Roraima, was hunting in the forest to the north 
of that mountain with some others, armed with his 
long blow-pipe. In returning home, considerably in 
advance of the rest of his party, it is supposed that he 
saw a young ant-eater, and, taking it up in his arms, 
was carrying it home, when its mother gave chase, 
overtook, and killed him; for, when his companions 
came up, they found him lying dead on his face in the 
embrace of the ant-bear, one of its large claws having 
entered his heart. In the struggle he had managed 
to stick his knife behind his back into the animal, 
which bled to death, but not till after the poor fellow 
had succumbed to its terrible hug. It was evident 
that he had only heard the ant-eater coming when it 
was close upon him, and in turning round to look his 
blow-pipe got caught across the path in front of him ; 
then, as he turned to run, it formed a bar to his pro- 
gress, and he fell over it as the animal seized him. 
So firmly had the animal grappled him, that to 
separate it from the corpse the Indians had to cut off 
its fore legs. 

After little loss of time the guide found his way, 
and we reached the Cotinga river just in time to wade 
to a small island, on which a few trees grew, to get 


Cuar. V. SOLITARY HOUSE. 121 


a place to hang our hammocks for the night. We 
crossed the Cotinga next day at the head of a large 
fall, some fifty yards in width, called Orindouie, the 
water not reaching much above our knees. It was 
with the greatest difficulty that I could keep my 
footing, the smooth, flat rocks on the bottom being 
covered with a gelatinous sponge, which rendered 
them exceedingly slippery. Passing through a belt 
of wood beyond we came to an Indian house of small 
size, but inhabited by twenty-four individuals. It 
put me in mind of a bee-hive well stocked with bees. 
There an old Ackawoise joined us as guide, and on 
we went through a partially wooded country, rising 
gradually from the valley of the Cotinga to a great 
open, undulating plateau, spreading southward at an 
elevation of 3500 feet above the sea. We kept on 
in a north-west direction, reaching near nightfall a 
solitary house belonging to our new guide, and there 
we spent the night. 

There was a strange charm about this place; 
situated on a great lonely elevated land, not far from 
a few scattered groves, and exposed to the strong 
winds, which came in gusts from over the dark forests 
to the northward, whistling mournfully through the 
cracks and crannies of the old mud-walled palm- 
thatched house. To the north-west, rising to a height 
of some 2000 feet above the level of the place, stood 
the great Waetipu (Sun mountain), formed of hori- 
zontal beds of sandstone, the alternate hard and soft 
layers of which produced most singular terraces on its 
sides, while near it stood two most curious conical peaks. 

The owner of the house made the door fast at dark 
with a palm mat and large poles, and we turned in, 


122 UP THE ESSEQUEBO TO RORAIMA. Cear. V. 


each Indian having a small fire placed under his 
hammock to keep his naked skin warm, the night 
being quite chilly. 

Long before daybreak on the following morning, 
lighted by the moon and the morning star, we were 
on our way once more. The morning air was deli- 
ciously cool, and, thinly clad as I was, it almost made 
me shiver. 

Early in the forenoon we rounded the end of 
Waetipu, and a glorious view of Roraima mountain 
burst upon us, with the bright sun’s rays lighting up 
its curious details. Marching directly towards it, 
over ridge and hollow, where small streams were 
numerous, we halted for a time on a ridge at the edge 
of an extensive valley, in which ran the Arapu river. 
Nestling in the valley at our feet was a small village, 
called Ipelemouta. From the opposite side of the 
valley a number of ridges intervened between it and 
the foot of Roraima. Turn in any direction I would 
most wonderful scenery was presented to my view, 
from the great pink precipiced Roraima in the north- 
west, looking like a huge fortification surrounded by a 
gigantic glacis, to the great undulating plain stretching 
southward as far as the eye could reach, where at the 
horizon land melted into sky. 

A great layer of white fleecy cloud hung over 
Roraima, obscuring its top, which, driven by the wind, 
seldom cleared it before another cloud, following in its 
wake, enveloped its farther end. 

Descending to the village we found it abandoned 
temporarily by its owners, members of the Arecuna 
tribe, and took up our abode in it.

Cuar. V. BEARDED BIRD. 123 


In traversing the country between Waetipu and 
Ipelemouta, we stopped in the forenoon to rest and 
feed, when we were startled by a most singular pro- 
longed cry, which made my Indians spring to their 
feet, listen intently, and then talk earnestly with 
bated breath. As there was something so human in 
the sound I wished to go and see what it was, but the 
Indians would not stir a foot to accompany me, and 
fearing I should lose myself I gave up the idea. The 
Indians, ever ready with an improbable explanation, 
said that the sound must have proceeded from some 
Arecuna, who, having killed one of his own people, 
had been turned into a wild animal. 

In the small groves bordering the Arapu river 
were numbers of birds of a kind allied to the bell-bird 
(Casmorhynchus carunculatus), whose singular call 
attracted my attention. They utter a repetition of 
one metallic sounding note, not unlike that produced 
by repeated blows of a hammer on an anvil, with 
great rapidity. A specimen of this bird which I 
procured was submitted to Dr. Sclater, who identified 
it as the Casmorhynchus variegatus. The peculiarity 
of this bird consists in the extraordinary long black 
leather-like filaments hanging in a fringe from the 
sides of its large mouth and throat. Its body is 
white, head brown, and wings black. These birds are 
very numerous from the Cotinga river to Roraima, 
but east of that river only one or two individuals were 
met with. 

On Sunday, February 7th, I took the guide and 
another Indian, and crossing to the foot of Roraima, 
ascended its sloping portion to a height of 5100 feet 


124 UP THE ESSEQUEBO TO RORAIMA. Cuar. V. 


above the level of the sea. Between the highest point 
I reached and the foot of the great perpendicular 
portion, which towered high above us, was a band of 
thick forest. Looking up at the great wall of rock, 
2000 feet in height, I could see that a forest covered 
its top, and that in places on its sides, where small 
trees or shrubs could gain a hold with their roots, 
there they clung. The great beds of white, pink, and 
red sandstone, of which it is composed, are interbedded 
with layers of red shale, the whole resting upon a 
great bed of diorite. 

No one can view this wonderful mountain and 
its surrounding similarly-shaped neighbours without 
feeling convinced that they stood at one time as 
islands in the ocean, but at what period of the earth’s 
history it is difficult to- say. If any mammals then 
lived upon them, when the sea washed the bases of 
their cliffs, the descendants of those mammals may 
exist there still, for all communication with their tops 
and the surrounding country has ever since been 
effectually cut off by their perpendicular sides. It is 
interesting to speculate upon what sort of animals 
might be found there, but as a speculation it must 
remain; for unless one ascended the mountain by 
means of a balloon the question could never be 
settled. The length of Roraima is about eight or 
twelve miles, Kukenam is perhaps more, and Ille- 
beapeur still more extensive, while all have perfectly 
level tops. The area of the surface of Roraima moun- 
tain must be considerable, for Sir R. Schomburgk, 
who visited its southern end, to the westward of the 
point to which I ascended, describes some beautiful 


Cuar. V. RORAIMA. 125 


waterfalls as leaping from its sides, forming the 
drainage of part of its top; when viewing it from 
a mountain on the Upper Mazaruni, in 1872, at a 
distance of thirty miles, I distinctly saw an enormous 
waterfall, on its north-east side, of very considerable 
width and extraordinary height. 

One might spend a month in the district, and not 
exhaust the wonderful treasures of scenery to be found 
there. 

As night closed in, the great masses of fleecy clouds 
that enveloped the upper thousand feet of Roraima 
seemed to sink lower and lower, until they rested on 
the sloping glacis. Then the temperature fell rapidly, 
from 79° at sunset to 59° at daybreak. With the 
rising sun the clouds lifted to their usual level again. 

On the day following my journey to the foot of the 
precipice, I was compelled to turn my back upon this 
wonderful place, owing to our stock of provisions 
being insufficient to sustain us much longer, and the 
uncertainty regarding the welfare of the men I had 
sent back to Karakanang. There was, however, some 
satisfaction in the knowledge of having accomplished 
the chief object of my journey, and I therefore set 
out on my return with a certain amount of light- 
heartedness. 

Soon after leaving Ipelemouta we came upon two 
small yapocks (Chironectes variegatus) on the path, 
and gave chase to them. They ran swiftly, but we 
managed to gain on them, and nearly overtook them 
as they reached a small water-hole near a stream. 
The Indians managed to kill one with stones, while 
the other made for the stream, closely pursued by the 


126 UP THE ESSEQUEBO TO RORAIMA. Czar. V. 


Indian guide and myself. I could have shot it had 
not the old Indian guide skipped along close on its 
heels, until it reached the water and disappeared. 

Early in the forenoon we rounded the end of Wae- 
tipu, and I took my last near look of Roraima, with 
its attendant fleecy clouds. On arriving at the Indian 
place near the Cotinga, called Weynamou, at dusk of 
that day, the women, as usual, placed pepper-pot and 
cassava on a mat in front of the door of their houses 
for us. Feeling ravenously hungry I partook largely 
of the compound placed before me, which, though 
covered with deer’s hairs, had an excellent flavour. 
Dipping a piece of cassava into it, I felt it touch a 
hard substance, which I unfortunately hoisted to the 
surface, when, to my horror, I found that it was a 
mass of deer’s entrails. I immediately arose and left 
the spot. I found upon inquiry that this delicious 
compound was made of deer’s blood and entrails. 

I had my hammock hung to poles outside the house, 
and was just going to sleep when one of the most 
diabolical noises ever heard sounded in the house. 
It resembled a mixture of sound produced by the 
striking of an alarm clock and the cackling of a fowl, 
and I felt sure proceeded from some raving maniac of 
an Indian; but, to my surprise, learned that it was 
produced by a large tame owl. 

Two men of the place went on with us to Kara- 
kanang, one to carry provisions we had bought, and 
the other to take the load of William, who had 
sprained his ankle, and walked with great difficulty. 

On arriving at that village I found my nien had not 
suffered from their diet of corn, upon which alone they 


Cuar. V. FEVER. 127 


had subsisted during my absence, never having been 
able to find any game to shoot, or fish to catch. 

My shoes being much worn by constant tramping 
over rough gravelly and rocky paths, had got sadly 
out of shape and injured one toe, which gave me such 
pain that my mode of walking was reduced to a limp; 
so that, instead of keeping my piace in the van as 
hitherto, I formed one of the rear-guard of stragglers. 
I had a touch of fever the day I spent at Karakanang, 
and the following day, whilst on the march, was again 
seized with a sharp attack of that malady, accom- 
panied by vomiting, which threw me completely on 
my beam-ends. I had to lie in my hammock between 
two low scrubby savanna trees, which afforded hardly 
any shelter from the sun’s rays, for some five hours, 
until I felt well enough to proceed. About half of 
the party of Indian carriers being in advance when I 
was obliged to stop, had gone on, and carried most of 
my things with them. Some of those rerhaining with 
us employed themselves during the day in catching 
numbers of the large species of termite, which build 
low mounds of earth, and, after roasting, ate them 
with evident relish. They captured these insects by 
inserting a dry grass stalk into their nests, to which 
the termites adhered by their mandibles, and were 
drawn out in hundreds. 

Early next morning we started off, and after three 
hours’ march came up with the advanced members of 
our party, who had camped near a small stream, in 
the bed of which were large areas of flat sandstone 
rocks, with hollows in them. Some of these hollows 
were filled with clotted blood, which, upon inquiry, I 


128 UP THE ESSEQUEBO TO RORAIMA. Cuar. V. 


learned had been drawn from the arms and legs of the 
Indians. When Indians suffer from any pains or 
stiffness in their limbs, caused by fatigue or other- 
wise, they procure a sharp flinty stone, or piece of 
broken glass, with which they score the affected part 
in long parallel cuts, just piercing the skin, and allow- 
ing the blood to ooze out in little bead-like drops. 
By scraping down the cuts with a knife’s edge the 
beads of blood' are removed and more blood oozes 
out; but on letting the beads remain they coagulate 
and prevent any further flow. In this way they can 
draw just as much blood as they like from the part. 

When we reached Cumparuyamo village our supply 
of provisions was nearly exhausted, and with the little 
we had left I sent eighteen Indians on to Enamouta, 
whilst I remained there one day with the rest till 
enough cassava was made to give us half allowance. 
Whilst there some people belonging to the place killed 
an alligator in a water-hole near by, which must have 
come up into the mountains from the Cotinga river. 

Resuming our march, we reached Enamouta village 
in two days. On the second day a light misty rain 
fell, which made the air cool, and was the first regular 
rain we experienced on the whole of our journey 
amongst these mountains. 

On our way along, near the Curiewakie river, a 
splendid antlered deer sprang up about 50 yards in 
front of me, dashing up a steep and rocky hill-side, 
rising some 300 feet above us. The huntsman tried 
to cutit off, by taking the hill in a slanting direc- 
tion ; but, though he ran gamely, the deer beat him. 
When the deer arrived near the top it could not go 


Cuar. V. INDIAN CURIOSITY. f 129 


at more than a walk, so thoroughly had the run up 
hill exhausted it. In the Unamara river valley we 
started two deer, one of which was followed by a 
large hound belonging to some Enamouta Indians 
who accompanied us. It was a splendid sight to see 
the manner in which the deer drew away from its 
pursuer. The dog, not being urged on by the cries 
of its master, and seeing that no one took much 
interest in the chase, gradually drew off. 

I remained a day at Enamouta, a victim to the in- 
quisitive inhabitants of that place, while food was 
being prepared for us. These people never left me 
entirely alone, but usually squatted round me in 
relays, watching my every movement. I heartily 
wished them miles away; but, as I was a guest of 
theirs, refrained from getting the interpreter to tell 
them to clear out. At this stage of my journey my 
foot had got well, and the fever-—touches of which 
followed the first attack—had now left. It is a ~ 
curious but well-known fact in this country that a 
slight injury to any part of the body will invariably 
bring on short attacks of fever. 

In the early morning, about 4 a.m., the Indians in 
these mountains, as well as those on the savannas, 
become wakeful, and talk a good deal, some of them 
singing a most tuneless, dirge-like song. At Ena- 
mouta they added the noise of a drum to the per- 
formance, and at daylight they all issued from their 
houses simultaneously, greeting the morn with cries 
and loud shouts. 

Early in the morning of the day on which we left 


Enamouta, we cleared the mountains, and were once 
K 


130 UP THE ESSEQUEBO TO RORAIMA. Cegar. V. 


more back on the great level savanna. By 4 P.M. we 
reached the Lake of Culucurana, and camped under 
the large cashew trees. From this place we went 
on to the spot at which we had spent a day on our 
outward-bound journey, after crossing the Ireng, and 
taking a bee-line across country, made a forced march, 
which nearly proved fatal to one of the party, a 
young man named D’Este, who, being footsore, fell 
behind, and thus lost his way. Fortunately some 
Indians, who did not leave the lake for two or three 
hours after us, came upon him, after he had given 
himself up for lost, and had sat down in a completely 
bewildered state. 

During this day's walk I witnessed a most beautiful 
mirage to the eastward, which resembled a large 
expanse of water, on the edges of which I could 
imagine I saw the actual movement of waves. 
Mirages are very common on the savannas, causing 
distant lines of trees to look as if raised up from the 
ground. In the afternoon all hands were busily en- 
gaged catching fish in the pools which had proved so 
prolific before, and a large number of houries were 
obtained. We seemed to have come suddenly upon a 
land of plenty, for some of the men shot Wicissi ducks 
and pigeons. My dog Chickerine and the hound fre- 
quently amused themselves in catching small arma- 
dillos and carrying them in their mouths, 

Crossing the Ireng river we reached a place on the 
Pirara river, where Indians usually camped, and there 
stopped for a night. Our huntsman, who had been so 
unsuccessful during the whole journey, had become 


Cuar, V. INDIAN CURIOSITY. 131 


much nettled at his want of luck, and after we crossed 
the Ireng he left our line of march, stating that as he 
knew this part of the country he would procure a deer 
that day. At dusk he joined us in camp with two of 
those animals that he had shot, one a large buck, with 
velvet-covered antlers, and the other a young doe, 
both of which he carried on his shoulders. The doe 
he had killed at midday, and he had therefore been 
lugging it about for six hours. Not a particle of these 
animals was wasted, even the legs, feet, and entrails 
being boiled into pepper-pot by the Indians. 

Half a day’s march from this, in a light drizzly 
rain, brought us to Carenacru village, where I was 
welcomed by old Passico, who was arrayed in two 
shirts (one over the other) that I had given him. 
The Macusis left me in comparative quietude that day, 
but I paid for it on the next, when they kept visiting 
me in numbers, begging to see my sketch-book, of 
which the Indians who had been with me had told 
them. They were greatly pleased with it, and 
shouted with delight on recognizing the animal forms 
that I had attempted to delineate therein. Amongst 
those who requested to see this book was a bevy of 
young damsels, some of whom were very pretty. It 
took them some time to muster up courage to make 
the request, but as they became interested in the 
sketches their shyness wore off, and they clustered 
around me, as I turned from one picture to another, 
expressing their delight in a most natural and enjoy- 
able manner. 

Having paid off my Indians in negotie I bid them

132 UP THE ESSEQUEBO TO RORAIMA. Cuar. V. 


adieu, and on February 25th, with my own men, went 
on to Pirara landing, where I found our boats as we 
had left them. 

On the way down, at Aringwong village, I saw a 
tame savanna powie (Craz Urumutum), which differed 
considerably from the common powie (Crax alector), 
but was of nearly the same size. It had a red beak, 
and chestnut feathers in the part where the common 
powie is white. 

During the night spent at the landing we were 
attacked most unmercifully by mosquitoes. I men- 
tion the fact because, though a prey to almost every 
other kind of insect pest, we had not during all our 
journeyings, from August to this period, met with 
night or day mosquitoes in sufficient force to be at 
all troublesome. In the forest a very large mosquito, 
with a steel blue body, was frequently met with, that 
had the curious and, I may add, disagreeable habit of 
making incessant attacks on the end of one’s nose; 
but as only one at a time came on, and its bill gave a 
sharp prick, it was never able to effect a lodgment 
for the length of time requisite to enable it to get 
even a fair drink of blood.
