CHAPTER VI. 
TO PIRARA AND THE IRENG. 


Karinambo— Vampire Bats — Sketch of Brazilian Capture of Pirara in 
1840 — Snipe in Pipicho Morass — Descend Rupununi to Mora — 
Walk across Mountains to Ireng River — Peai-ing — Indian Belief — 
Indian Warfare — Stone Circle — Descend Rupununi — Obtain Breech- 
loading Cannon — Indian Murderer at Apoterie— Descend Essequebo 
to Penal Settlement. 


From Pirara landing we went down to Karinambo 
village, situated on the savanna, about 300 yards from 
the river, where we had left our surplus stores before 
our departure for Roraima. Two of the houses, with 
mud walls, were built on long posts ten feet high, with 
floors made of split palm stems. The first night we 
spent there the vampire bats (Phyllostoma bidens) 
attacked my dog, which slept beneath my hammock, 
and tapped it in three places, drawing off a quantity 
of blood. From the pools of blood on the ground 
beneath it in the morning it was evident that after the 
bats had taken their quantum, the blood had continued 
to flow copiously from the wounds for some little time. 
From each spot where the bats had sucked, the skin 
had been removed in a circular patch quite the size of 
a silver threepenny piece. During the remainder of 
my stay there I had to keep a lamp burning at night, 
as an effectual preventative against vampires. One of 
my men, who was bitten on one toe at the same time, 
told me that the wound smarted very much. 


134 TO PIRARA AND THE IRENG. Cmar. VI. 


A small armadillo, that I had brought with me from 
the Pirara savanna for the purpose of taking to the 
coast, became so weak from not partaking of any food 
that I had to let it go. Although I placed portions 
of termites’ nests, teeming with inhabitants, close to 
its nose, it would have nothing to do with them. 

From Karinambo I made a journey to Pirara, on 
Lake Amucu, and back, which occupied four days. 
On the way we stopped the first night at a village 
called Mopay. The moon was fulland the atmosphere 
misty, a thick layer of mist-cloud driven before the 
wind obscuring the whole of the sky. About mid- 
night we were all aroused by the talking and singing 
of the Indians in one of the houses, and, thinking it 
was daylight, turned out to boil our tea, preparatory 
to starting on our journey. After washing and par- 
taking of that beverage, I waited for day to break; 
but as it seemed never to get lighter I fell asleep, and 
awoke at the real daylight next morning. The men 
having fallen asleep again before their pannakin of 
water boiled, it was left on the fire all night till the 
water evaporated, and its bottom was melted out. 

Just beyond a village called Pipicho we had to cross 
the narrowest part of a large morass a quarter of a 
mile in width, over which we had to walk as rapidly 
as possible to keep from sinking in the bog. In many 
places we had to spring from one floating matted tuft 
of rush and moss to another, before they sank with us 
into the bottomless swamp. 

Our second night was spent at the village of Qua- 
tata, a large place situated on high ground, from which 
two other Macusi villages could be seen. From it we 


Cuar. VI. SITE OF EL DORADO. 135 


had a fine view of the Pacaraima mountains to the 
north, with the great stretch of open level intervening, 
savanna and Lake Amuca in the foreground. This 
lake, at the time of my visit, was low, and its bottom 
mostly covered with a rich carpeting of grass. Two 
large wooded patches in it represented the islands of 
Ipomucena. It was on the borders of this lake, 
according to Humboldt, that Indian tradition placed 
the far-famed and mythical city of El Dorado. Half 
a mile west of Quatata we came to an almost circular 
earthwork, four feet in height, and some thirty yards in 
diameter, surrounded by a shallow ditch, within which 
were the shattered remains of many beer and wine 
bottles. This is called Fort New Guinea on Sir R. 
Schomburgk’s map, and was made by the black troops 
sent up from Georgetown, in the year 1840, for the 
purpose of ejecting the Brazilian soldiers who at that 
time had been sent from the frontier fort of St. Joaquin, 
on the Rio Branco, to occupy Pirara. It would seem 
that this proceeding on the part of the Brazilians had 
been prompted by the jealousy with which they re- 
garded the establishment of a Protestant Mission at 
the spot by the Rev. T. Youd. Along with the Bra- 
zilian troops came a friar who, after Mr. Youd had 
been driven away, erected a huge cross in front of the 
mission church, and turned it into a Roman Catholic 
establishment. 

Some time after the news of this outrage reached 
Georgetown, the Government sent up some companies 
of a West Indian regiment in large boats, who, on 
arriving at Pirara landing, disembarked, and, proceed- 
ing to within about a mile of the spot where the 


136 TO PIRARA AND THE IRENG. Caap. VI. 


Brazilians had entrenched themselves, threw up the 
above-mentioned earthwork. They then gave the 
Brazilians a certain number of hours in which to 
evacuate the place, or else, if they remained, to take 
the consequences. The Brazilian commander pre- 
ferred to accede to the demand to evacuate Pirara, 
and, after all preliminaries had been arranged, returned 
with his men to the Rio Branco. 

When on the Rio Negro, in Brazil, in 1874, I met 
with a Major Mordel, who informed me that the friar, 
who was with the Brazilian troops at the Pirara, was 
the Padre Frei José,a man mentioned by Mr. Wallace 
in his book on the Amazon and Rio Negro as not 
bearing the best of characters. 

The charred hardwood upright posts of Mr. Youd’s 
church still remain, while all traces of the numerous 
Indian houses that then constituted the village have 
succumbed to the hand of time. Broken pottery, 
pieces of crockeryware, and bleached bones of animals, 
alone remain to show where the village once stood. 

In returning to Karinambo, we struck the Pipicho 
morass where it was more lake-like, and in traversing 
the strip of boggy ground intervening between the 
savanna and the edge of the water, I was astounded at 
the immense number of snipe which [ flushed. I do 
not exaggerate in the least when I state that, at every 
fifth step, a snipe rose at my feet. The place was 
covered with long grass and rushes, shaded here and 
there by gigantic Itah palms. This sloping tract ran 
along the edge of the morass for a considerable dis- 
tance, and over the whole of it were snipe lying in 
vast numbers. Unfortunately I had left my breech- 


Cuar. VI. SNIPE. 137 


loader behind at the boats, and so lost some excellent 
sport. I, however, made an attempt to enjoy myself; 
taking the long single-barrel Indian gun, charged with 
BB-shot, I walked up and down different parts of the 
morass border, and blazed away as fast as I could load 
and fire. I used up all the powder we had with us, 
and no end of BB-shot, without killing a single bird. 
This was not to be wondered at, for putting aside the 
fact of my not being used to a single-barrel gun, there 
were the great size of the shot, and the scattering pro- 
perties of the gun against me. Amongst these snipe I 
flushed a few of the double-snipe, of about the size of 
woodcocks. 

Whilst my men were caulking one of the boats 
which was very leaky, I went from Karinambo with 
a guide and Ben over the savanna to some ponds 
covered with lilies, in a morass surrounded by trees, 
which contained numbers of ducks and other aquatic 
birds. Ben had lagged behind, and the Indian guide 
and myself crawled through rushes, water, and shrubs, 
to get a shot at a flock of Wicissi ducks. When with 
infinite trouble we had got within 100 yards of them, 
the report of a gun at the farther end of the ponds 
was heard, and in an instant nearly every bird rose in 
the air, and wheeling round once or twice took flight 
to other parts. The number of Muscovy and Wicissi 
ducks was immense; and besides these were jabiru, 
negrocopes, bush ibises, gauldins, quaaks, gray and 
white cranes (Herodias leuce), and blue-winged teal. 
The latter did not leave the pools, and I shot one or 
two of them. 

On our way back I found that it was Ben who had 


138 TO PIRARA AND THE IRENG. Cuar. VI. 


fired the shot which disturbed the feathered tribes, 
and spoilt my sport. He had fired at an old white 
crane, not thinking of the dire result to me, and was 
very sorry for what he had done. 

Leaving Karinambo, we went down the Rupununi 
to the great elbow where the river, at the foot of the 
mountains, changed its course from north to east. 
Entering a small creek called Mora, I landed, and 
from that place made a long journey through the 
mountainous region across to the Ireng river, which 
occupied a period of six days. I procured a guide at 
Mora village, who turned out to be a jewel of a man for 
that sort of work, and most communicative. He knew 
every striking-looking rock amongst the mountains, 
their names, and the traditions connected with them. 
I had to make the interpreter walk close to me, to 
translate all he said ; but when the former got weary 
of so doing, he used to drop to the rear, and I lost in 
that way much of what was said. It made no differ- 
ence to the guide whether I understood him or not, for 
he would unburden his mind of the traditions con- 
nected with these mountains, when anything brought 
them to his thoughts. On the mountain side, border- 
ing the Cawulibar valley, he pointed out a large rock 
under which an evil spirit dwelt; while under a dark, 
towering hill he said that many wicked wild beasts 
had their homes, and that no Indians dare go near 
these places. There was also a hill close by on which 
he said a spirit, at the approach of the end of the 
rainy season, made a noise like the report of a gun, to 
stop the rain from falling. The noise made at this 
hill is, I suppose, similar to that which I heard at 


Cuar. VI. PEAI-ING. 139 


Issano, on the Mazaruni. He showed us a waterpool 
where a Macusi had been beaten to death by his com- 
rades, and said that the pool bears the name of the 
murdered man. He turned out to be a famed sorcerer, 
or peaiman ; and at a village called Itabay, where we 
stopped one night in returning, he left the house in 
which we had put up our hammocks, telling the inter- 
preter to inform me that his absence for the night was 
unavoidable, owing to his having to go up amongst 
the mountains to roam about for the night, whilst his 
good spirit remained in one of the houses to cure a 
sick man, who had demanded his good offices. In two 
minutes after he had left us his powerful voice was 
heard making the most discordant sounds imaginable, 
chanting, howling, coughing, and many other dia- 
bolical noises. To these were added the shishing 
sound of an instrument called a shak-shak, made of a 
small round calabash filled with seeds, and placed on 
a handle, by which it is shaken. All the fires in the 
house had been put out in anticipation of bis arrival, 
and the place was pitch dark, so that the illusion that 
he was not there in the flesh might be kept up. 
Owing to the noise I did not get a wink of sleep until 
about two o’clock in the morning, and often thought, 
during the night, what a good thing it would have 
been if his spirit had only accompanied his body to 
the mountains. 

The house being close to the one I was in, the 
interpreter could hear all he said, and at my request, 
but with evident reluctance, told me what it was. It 
seems that he entered the house silently, and then 
commenced the sort of din above mentioned, beating 


140 TO PIRARA AND THE IRENG. Cuap. VI. 


with a palm branch on the floor. He then asked in a 
deep sepulchral voice what it was that ailed the 
patient, to which the sick man’s wife responded that 
some evil spirits or kanaimas had “done him bad.” 
The sorcerer then said, “ Well, I don’t know whether 
I can cure him, but I will do my best.” He then 
called to his aid the good spirit of a bird, called the 
maroodie, the descent of which from the roof was made 
known by the shaking of the palm leaf raised up and 
gradually lowered to the floor. On the arrival of this 
good spirit, it at first complained of having had a long 
journey, and that it was much heated by the haste it 
had been obliged to make when so suddenly summoned. 
It made the usual amount of noise, interlarded with 
whistlings (in imitation of the maroodie’s call), and 
promised to do its best. When its best, which con- 
sisted of discordant sounds, had been done, its egress 
through the roof was made known by the shakings of 
the palm leaf, and a dead silence of a minute’s duration 
succeeded, when the fluttering of the leaf announced 
the arrival of the spirit of an alligator, which in its 
turn was replaced by a duraquara, and so on. 

In some instances the good spirit.for the time being 
brought with it the evil one that was supposed to have 
had some hand in producing the man’s malady; and 
then the wife asked it how it could come and injure a 
being who had never harmed it, begging it to desist and 
leave her husband alone. The only answer she obtained 
to this appeal was a most diabolical, mocking laugh. 

The Mackunaima, or great spirit, was never called 
upon by the sorcerer to assist or cure the sick man, 
but only the subordinate inhabitants of spirit-land. 


Cuar. VI. KANAIMAS. 141 


The sorcerer,or peaiman, undergoes a special training 
to fit him for his high office. Amongst other pleasant 
rites of initiation, he drinks a certain quantity of a 
decoction of green tobacco leaf, until he is driven 
temporarily out of his mind, and rushes into the 
forest, where he remains for about a week, fasting 
all the while. When he returns in his right mind, 
he is looked upon as a big medicine man, capable of 
holding converse with spirits, curing diseases by their 
assistance, and containing within him the spirit of 
some wild beast. I think in him we see a shadowing 
of the modern spiritualistic medium. 

A little before daylight the peaiman returned from 
the mountain and turned into his hammock ; the spirits 
all went to their homes; and the poor fever-stricken 
Indian was left moaning in pain, probably much worse 
from having spent the night without a wink of sleep. 
Whilst on this subject I may remark that some Indians, 
who are adepts in the art of making subtle poisons, hire 
themselves out to rid their employers of any obnoxious 
individuals; and these are called kanaimas, because 
they destroy people in the same way as the bad spirits. 
This system of poisoning, amongst people who have no 
protective laws, prevents the strong from oppressing 
the weak, but works badly in every other way. 

Upon a hill-top bordering the Uorora river valley 
the guide informed me that wild yams grew in great 
quantities, and that the people of the neighbourhood, 
when hard up for food, go there to gather them. 
Amongst the hills and valleys we obtained quan- ` 
tities of small, deliciously-flavoured guavas They 
were of two kinds, one about the size of a currant, 


142 TO PIRARA AND THE IRENG. CnaP. VI. 


the other a little larger than a gooseberry, both grow- 
ing upon low shrubs. They are found in all the 
valleys of the Pacaraima mountains. 

On reaching the Ireng we turned north, and ascended 
the hills to a village called Quonga, a place situated on 
a smooth, conical hill, 1978 feet above the sca level, 
containing some sixty inhabitants. At this place there 
was a large stilt-legged plover, about the size of a large 
curlew, called corobara (Œdicnemus vocifer), which was 
quite tame, running about the houses. It made a most. 
singular chattering cry, both in the evening and just 
before daylight in the morning. 

In returning, on our way between Quonga and Itabay, 
as we rose a narrow pass from one valley to another, 
to enter on a level plain, we had to go between two 
large rocks, which only allowed of the passage of one 
man ata time. In doing so, the guide showed me a 
number of shot marks on the two rocks, and related 
the following tale. Pointing to a hill-side to the north- 
west, he said that there was once a large Macusi vil- 
lage there, the inhabitants of which discovered that 
a band of Indians were prowling about their place. 
They therefore organized an expedition to destroy 
these individuals, who evidently meant no good to 
them. The prowlers thereupon became alarmed and 
made off, followed by the Macusis, who tracked them 
to the west of Roraima, where they found their vil- 
lages untenanted, and lost all further trace of them. 
They then gave up the pursuit, and returned home- 
wards; but, keeping on the way a sharp look-out all 
the while, they became aware of the fact, before reach- 
ing the pass above alluded to, that a large force of the 


Cuar. VI. INDIAN WARFARE. 143 


enemy was following them. On arriving at the pass 
they halted and formed an ambush, and as the enemy 
was coming up the defile in single line they fired on 
them, killing one man. His comrades returned the 
fire and then fled, and the marks of that volley still 
remain on the rocks. Instead of following up the 
blow, the Macusis retreated to their village on the 
hill, and awaited the result with anxiety. A few 
hours after, they saw a band of the enemy slowly 
advancing up the pathway to them, when they col- 
lected in front of their dwelling, prepared to give 
them a warm reception. Little did they know of 
the strategy of the enemy, or the dire fate that 
awaited them. As the attacking party drew near, 
the defenders were startled by wild cries proceeding 
from their rear, and discovered, when too late, a band 
of savages, who had gone round the hack of the moun- 
tain, rushing upon them and pouring in a deadly fire. 
Simultaneously they were attacked by those in front, 
and unable to make any resistance, were nearly all 
slaughtered, two men alone making their escape, to 
tell the tale to others of their tribe. The guide wound 
up his story by stating that close to the ruins of 
the houses lie the bleached bones of the unfortunate 
victims. 

On the pathway between the Yackali river valley 
and Itabay, in descending a steep place through some 
wood, I saw numbers of beautiful little frogs, brought 
out from their hiding places by the rain that had 
fallen over the country that morning, hopping about 
amongst the rocks. They were black, with golden- 
yellow, ` irregular patterns marked on their backs, 


144 TO PIRARA AND THE IRENG. Crap. VI. 


sides, and legs, and no two were similarly patterned. 
I captured four, and took them with me to George- 
town, where they lived for two months. They were 
placed in a wide-mouthed bottle, with water in the 
bottom, and were fed with sugar-ants every morning. 
These ants were attracted to my coffee-cup in the 
mornings, and when clustered in the saucer were 
thrown in with the frogs, and by them gobbled up. 
The poor frogs: met with a sad ending. I went to 
spend a few days with a friend out of town, and the 
servant, forgetting to supply them with water, they 
all dried up into dirty-looking chips. 

Near the Yackali we started a brown animal, which 
ran down a tree stem and bowled off on the ground. 
It was probably a Galera; the men called it a 
Hocker. 

Land shells, chiefly Cyclostomas, were seen crawling 
on the path in narrow belts of bush during this 
journey, tempted by the rain to leave their dry- 
weather resorts. 

After leaving Itabay, on our homeward route, we 
went south into a lateral valley and then turned 
northward into another, in which were two deserted 
villages; then up a steep hill, through a wooded 
country, to a small open-wooded plain. In the centre 
of this opening there was a circle of upright slabs 
of greenish felstone porphyry, through the centre of 
which the path led. The slabs were from two to three 
feet in height, and some five or six feet apart, placed in 
a true circle of about thirty feet in diameter. They 
had not been dressed, but their forms resulted from 
the manner in which they split up on weathering, and. 


Crap. VI, BREECH-LOADING CANNON. 145 


they are portions of the rocks of the neighbourhood. 
On one was a frog-like figure, cut in deeply, showing 
plainly that these stones had been placed there by 
the picture-writing people. The Macusi travellers 
had bent down and broken some of the slabs, thus 
showing that they do not view them with any amount 
of reverence. The guide said he did not know any- 
thing about them, but supposed that the Mackunaima 
had placed them there. 

During the latter part of our journey the rain fell 
heavily at intervals, and betokened the near approach 
of the great rainy season. 

From Mora creek we descended the Rupununi 
to Watama, near Annai village, and on the way I 
shot two jabiru and a water-haas. The latter landed on 
the savanna, and I got within thirty yards of it before 
it saw me; it then tried to gallop off, when a shot 
from my gun laid it low. The men took it to the 
beach, and removing its entrails, filled the cavity 
with water. They then peeled short pieces of moco- 
moco stems, and with them beat up the water until it 
became frothy, continuing the process, which they 
called frothing, for a quarter of an hour. This was 
done to remove the rank flavour of the flesh, which it 
did most effectually. 

At Annai village I saw a most curious old breech- 
loading cannon, which I purchased from its Indian 
owner and brought away with me. It is now in the 
United Service Museum at Whitehall, having been 
brought home from Georgetown by my friend 
Lieutenant Menzies, who at that time commanded the 
Marines on board Her Majesty’s ship “ Jason.” This 

L 


146 TO PIRARA AND THE IRENG. Casar. VI. 


singular piece of ordnance had uo doubt been taken 
up into the interior of Guiana by some Dutch expedi- 
tion during the last century, although at what date it 
is impossible to find out. 

In the village of Annai I was shown a pen or crawle 
full of tortoises, all of which had been procured by the 
aid of a small black and white dog belonging to an 
Indian. This dog, when taken into the forest, set to 
work to search by scent for tortoises, and as they are 
very numerous it would trace out numbers of them in 
a day, thus keeping its master well supplied with 
those animals. 

Leaving Watama creek we descended the Rupununi 
to the Essequebo in two days and a half, and landed 
at the Carib village of Apoterie. 

Not far from the mouth of the Rupununi, on its 
south bank, I visited a small settlement of Brazilian 
Indians. They wore clothing, and were a curious- 
looking lot, having their teeth filed into points like 
those of a saw. 

At Apoterie a young Macusi Indian was pointed 
out to me as a noted murderer, having killed both his 
wife and his father. He was living with the Caribs 
for protection, not daring to go back to his own tribe. 

On the morning of the 29th of March we left the 
Carib village, commencing our descent of the Esse- 
quebo, and in two days reached the Ouropocarie 
cataracts, where we portaged all our things and 
lowered the boats down. 

The wild cashew (Anacardium sp.), a large and 
handsome tree, with leaves and fruit similar to those 
of the domesticated species, was then bearing, and we 


Cuar. VI. ARRIVE AT PENAL SETTLEMENT. 147 


often obtained quantities of its ripe fruit from the 
ground beneath it. The fruit of this wild cashew 
is smaller than that of the domesticated kind, and is 
less astringent, having a more delicate flavour. 

We came down river rapidly, shooting the cataracts 
when feasible, impelled swiftly by the strong flow of 
water where it ran smoothly along. 

Part of a day was spent in visiting Comuti rock, 
and on the morning of April the 7th we ran all the 
last set of cataracts on the river, coming to the tidal 
portion in the forenoon. As we approached the Grove 
the inhabitants of all the houses along the river’s bank 
turned out to see us pass, and greet their friends 
amongst my crew. My men sang boating songs with 
choruses by the hour together, keeping time with the 
strokes of their paddles. 

At 5 p.m. on that day I landed at the Penal Settle- 
ment, where I was welcomed back to civilization by 
my kind friends the officials of that place, and thus 
ended a long, rough, but to me most enjoyable 
journey. 


148 UP THE REWA AND QUITARO RIVERS. Cuar. VII. 


CHAPTER VII. 
UP THE REWA AND QUITARO RIVERS. 


Ascend Rewa — Enter Quitaro — Wapisiana Indians — Visit Ataraipa — 

- Mysterious Sound —Quata Monkeys — Perai — Bush-hog Hunts — 
Start on Journey over Quitaro Savanna — Fly Pests — Wapisians 
Salutations — Atorai Indian Village — Carawaimintow Mountain — 
Paiworie Drinking Bout — Monkey Hunt — Visit Shea Rock — Black 
Bees — Farinha — Rattlesnake — Return to Boat — Descend Quitaro 
on Homeward Journey. 


Some four months after my return to the coast region 
of the colony I left Georgetown, in company with 
Mr. Sawkins, and ascended the Essequebo for the 
second time, on my way to explore the Rewa and 
Quitaro rivers, and the country lying between the 
latter and the Rupununi. 

In passing I obtained an interpreter for the 
Wapisiana language from the Brazilian Indians on 
the Rupununi. 

We arrived at the mouth of the Rewa on the 29th 
of September, 1869, where we parted company, Mr. 
Sawkins continuing his journey up the Rupununi; 
having arranged to meet again on the 18th of Novem- 
ber, at the village of Apoterie, in order to return to 
the coast together. 

After six and a half days’ paddling up the narrow, 
winding Rewa, we entered its branch, the Quitaro; 
and four and a half days on that river brought us to 
the vicinity of the great natural pyramid of rock, 


Cuar. VII. ATARAIPU., 149 


called Ataraipu, first mentioned by Sir R. Schom- 
burgk, who visited it many yeers ago. Determined 
to visit the rock, which lay some six miles back from 
the river, I consulted some Wapisiana Indians, whom 
we met, on the subject, and they offered their services 
as guides. They stated that the journey there and 
back would have to be accomplished in one day, as it 
would be impossible to spend a night near the rock, 
on account of a pack of Warracaba tigers that in- 
habited the district. Knowing that if I returned as 
they wished, I should not be able to complete my 
investigations of the place, I had two days’ provisions 
packed, determining to remain a night there and take 
the chance of meeting the tigers. After walking three 
and a half miles through the forest we came to a 
granite ridge, from the top of which Ataraipu could be 
seen some two and a half miles off in front of us. 
Crossing the intervening space, we passed over a small 
stream, called Ataraipuruwow, and ascending, gradu- 
ally at first, but steeply afterwards, amongst great 
blocks of granite in the forest, came to the foot of 
Ataraipu itself, which rose above us at an angle of 70° 
with the plane of the horizon. We were then at a level 
of 857 feet above the sea, and the great rock structure 
rose some 600 feet above us. In glancing up its side 
I saw little bunches and patches of a small pink- 
flowered orchid not far up, clinging here and there to 
the surface of the rock, amongst which numbers of 
little brown birds were flitting in search of insects ; 
while flocks of brown-headed swallows winged their 
way round and round the rock. Descending a short 
distance, we crossed by a spur, joining the base of 


150 UP THE REWA AND QUITARO RIVERS. Cear. VII. 


Ataraipu with the large conical granite rock on its 
west, called Coomacaba, the top and western half of 
which is covered with low trees and shrubs. The 
part facing Ataraipu is bare, and slopes at an angle 
of 43° to the horizon, so that I was just able to 
clamber up, my feet being prevented from slipping by 
the coarse quartz crystals standing out from the face 
of the granite. This near view of Ataraipu was a 
magnificent one, showing the extraordinary massive 
nature of the granite of which it is composed, and the 
curious vertical groovings down its sides, furrowed 
by the trickling of rain water from its top during the 
course of vast ages. In these furrows grew purplish 
and gray lichens, which gave the rock a singularly 
striped appearance. 

Descending to the Ataraipuruwow stream, we 
camped for the night. This was a move very dis- 
tasteful to the Indians, who came only because they 
thought that our courage would fail at the last 
moment and we should return to our boats. Seeing 
that my party had determined to camp, and being 
then ashamed to retreat, they all, with one exception, 
did likewise. Tree frogs and night insects kept up 
such a din around us, that had the tigers come we 
could never have heard their approach. We were 
undisturbed, however, during the night, and the 
Indians said next day that, luckily for us, the tigers 
had gone hunting in another direction. On the 
way in we put up a tiger that had been sleeping 
under a fallen tree-top close to us, which went 
away through the bush like a flash of lightning. It 
certainly then looked as if the Indians’ words were 


Cuar. VII. MYSTERIOUS SOUND. 151 


going to come true, and augured badly for our night’s 
repose. 

Next day we followed a granite ridge to th 
eastward, which terminated in a bare conical granite 
structure, called Calishadakeur. On arriving at its 
base part, I tried over and over again to follow three 
Indians up the only accessible part in a deep water- 
groove, at an angle of 60°. Each time I got up some 
thirty feet, when my footing gave way, and down I 
slid with rapidity, landing in some shrubs at its foot, 
which acted as a buffer and broke my fall. The 
Indians then got some bush-ropes from the neighbour- 
ing trees, in lengths of thirty feet each, and tying four 
together, hauled them up and made the end fast to a 
species of Clusia, growing on the side of the rock, 
with its roots firmly fixed in a small fissure. By the 
aid of this rope I got up the steep part hand over 
hand, and, gaining the level top, had a splendid view 
of all the surrounding country. 

Seen from the granite ridge to the south, Ataraipu 
resembled a huge conical pedestal with the unfinished 
sculptured form of a huge lion resting on its top; 
while from Calishadakeur it more nearly resembled 
a slightly leaning pyramid, placed on a tree-clothed 
conical hill. 

From Calishadakeur we returned to our boats on 
the Quitaro, meeting with a great ftock of little red- 
handed marmosetts (Midas rufimanus) on the way, 
playing like squirrels in the tree tops. 

About 5 p.m. on that day, whilst resting on the 
bank of the river after our walk, we were all startled 
by a loud report, like that produced by the discharge 


152 UP THE REWA AND QUITARO RIVERS. Cuar. VIL 


of a 32-pound cannon, half a mile distant, in the 
direction of Ataraipu. It woke up echoes midst the 
surrounding forests and hills, but was unaccompanied 
by any rumbling like thunder, or by any sensible 
vibration of air or earth. This evidently was not pro- 
duced by a fall of rock, but was in some way connected 
with the great granite masses in the neighbourhood. 
Many mountains in other parts of the world are known 
to produce similar sounds, the causes of which have 
not yet, I believe, been satisfactorily explained. 

Not far beyond Ataraipu the narrow, winding cha- 
racter of the Quitaro alters, and rocks and rocky 
islets, forming rapids and cataracts, occur, causing the 
river to widen. out and become shallower. From one 
of the cataracts, looking along a wide vista of river, a 
fine distant view of Ataraipu was obtained. The In-- 
dians call that cataract by the somewhat lengthened 
appellation of Ataraipuwanamarie, which means that it 
is the cataract from which that rock can be seen. On 
a group of granite rocks near by we saw some old 
Indian sculpture, like the image of a sort of rising sun 
with a human visage, probably representing the head 
of a deity. 

Both black flies and mosquitoes are very trouble- 
some on the Rewa and Quitaro, the latter biting by 
night and the former by day. These mosquitoes were 
long-billed, attenuated beings, but bit hard. 

The large kind of old witch bird ( Crotophaga major) 
was very numerous in the low trees growing along the 
river’s edge, where often a flock would fly in our com- 
pany for an hour at a time, keeping alongside of us as 
we paddled up stream. They fed on grasshoppers and 


Cmar. VII. TICKS. 153 


caterpillars, ferreted out from amongst the tree leaves, 
and produced bubbling sounds now and then, like that 
made by the boiling of a pot of water. 

A small dark-coloured bird (Brachypetes tenebrosa) 
is very common on the Quitaro, and in fact‘on all the 
rivers of Guiana, where it is seen sitting on the limbs 
of high dead trees, patiently awaiting for some insect 
to pass, which it catches on the wing. It is a stumpy- 
built bird, and its flight is very bat-like. It makes a 
hole in the side of a sand-hill, in which it lays its eggs 
and hatches its young. 

Two kinds of monkeys were very numerous, viz. 
the quata (Ateles paniscus), a large black, red-faced 
spider-monkey, and an ordinary-sized monkey, with 
black, negro-like face, full beard, and bushy tail, 
called beeshu. 

One Sunday, whilst remaining encamped to rest 
the men, a small species of red ant attacked us 
in thousands, and was very troublesome, its sting 
burning like a spark of fire. In the forest near the 
camp, I got a lot of small brown ticks upon me, and 
did not discover them till next day. Each disgusting 
creature bit me in four or five places, and each bite 
turned to a water-blister, and then burst, after which 
it itched dreadfully. In walking through the forest 
one often brushes against a tree or shrub laden with 
these horrid insects, when his clothes become covered 
with them from head to foot before he is aware that 
one is near him. The only thing to be done then is to 
take off the tick-laden clothes, light a fire, and hold 
them close to it, when the ticks, feeling the heat, 
drop off. 


ae 


154 UP THE REWA AND QUITARO RIVERS. Cumar. VIL 


Close to my tent at that place there was the nest of 
a common dark-coloured trogon (Trogon melanop- 
terus), called bowclorou, which merely consisted of a 
hole scooped out of a large, round termite’s nest, built 
on the stem of a tree. In it were two young ones, 
which kept up a most doleful whistling all day, while 
the mother sat on a tree bough near by, being afraid, 
from our near proximity, to approach them. 

A fish, called the perai (Serasalmo niger), which is 
met with in all the rivers of Guiana, are very numerous 
here, and took a baited hook readily. They are a 
little smaller than the cartabac, but very similar in 
general appearance, differing principally from that 
fish in the form of their teeth, which are pointed, those 
of the cartabac being chisel-shaped. They are most 
voracious creatures, and prey upon all other kinds of 
fish, going so far as to bite pieces out of the tails and 
fins of the lowlow, gilbacker, arowpaima, &c., docking 
down those appendages in some instances to mere 
stumps. 

On the Pobawow, a small branch of the Quitaro, 
I visited a grove of Brazil-nut trees (Bertholletia 
excelsa), amongst the surrounding forest. One of these, 
on measurement, I found to be 25 feet 4 inches in cir- 
cumference, at two feet above the level of the ground. 

On two successive days we had kieroonie or pec- 
carie hunts, and being successful, got a plentiful 
supply of fresh meat for our larder. In the first 
instance, on becoming aware of the proximity of a 
herd by their gruntings, as they pottered about near 
the edge of the river, [ landed, and climbing up the 
steep bank was at once detected by them. Owing to

the thickness of the low undergrowth I could not at

making a most appalling sound, and charging towards 
me, came into view, where they stood grunting 
savagely. The first I got my eye upon I covered with 
my gun, and gave it a charge of BB in the chest, 
which bowled it over. The sound of the report 
demoralized the rest, which, with much clattering of 
teeth and grunting, turned and fled like a whirlwind 
through the forest, making the ground tremble under 
their tread. 

On the second occasion of our meeting with a herd 
the bush was more open, and we approached them 
cautiously. They were scattered about, and I came 
upon two standing alone, apparently listening intently. 
I fired at the head of one, but without effect, and, as 
they dashed off, I was fortunate enough to knock over 
the other with the left-hand barrel. I then ran with 
the men in pursuit of the flying herd, and succeeded 
in killing two more pigs. There must have been at 
least one hundred individuals in the flock, but they 
were not of the savage kind, and so after champing 
their teeth only once they fled. 

There are three kinds of bush hogs, or peccaries, 
one, the smallest of the three, known as the Abouyah 
(Dicotyles torquatus), and the other two as Kieroonies. 
The abouyabs never go in large flocks, but are only 
seen in small bands of from two to five in number. Of 
the two kinds of kieroonies, one with light-coloured hair 
upon the throat and under the lower jaw, is known as 
the white-lipped peccarie (Dicotyles labiatus). These 
are the most dangerous to meet, and when found at 


156 UP THE REWA AND QUITARO RIVERS. Cuar. VII. 


certain seasons, accompanied by their young, are ex- 
ceedingly savage, and charge the hunters, causing 
them to take to trees as the herd sweeps by. After 
making one of these charges they frequently wheel 
round and recharge over the same ground. 

The other kind of kieroonie bas no distinguishing 
marks, but is of a uniform dark colour. 

They are furnished with a gland on the back, which 
gives out a most disagreeable smell, and it is by no 
means pleasant to one’s olfactory nerves to go amongst 
a flock, or even over ground lately occupied by one. 

The day before we arrived at Ataraipu landing we 
observed a large deer feeding, on a grass patch close 
to the water's edge, which I fired at from a long 
distance. It made a bound into the thicket and dis- 
appeared. We landed, and found it badly wounded, 
not twenty yards away. Springing up, it made off 
again, and after running about 200 yards was brought . 
to bay by my dog, in a shallow pool of water, where 
one of the men killed it with a cutlass. 

The party of Wapisiana Indians, who had been 
with us to Ataraipu, had gone on before, and above 
the cataracts we overtook them. They were then 
busily engaged in picking out and eating the larve of 
a wasp, from the comb of a nest of that insect, which 
they had knocked from the overhanging branches of a 
tree. The children especially seemed to enjoy the 
little white grub-like larve, as a civilized child would 
sugar-plums. 

On Saturday, October the 16th, we came to the 
landing of a path which led to the village of our 
Indian friends, and from that point our land journey 


Cuar. VII. FLY PESTS. 157 


commenced. After placing our stores in packs we 
started with the Indians for their place, and, walking 
over an undulating country through forest for eight 
and a half hours, came out on the fine open savanna, 
there clothed with fresh green grass. The air was 
clear and refreshing, and everything looked bright 
after our toilsome forest march. On the way we 
passed a large tree, on the stem of which were 
numbers of large beetles (Buprestis gigas), with elitra 
of a beautiful metallic lustre. The Indians caught a 
number of them, and stripped off their wing-covers for 
the purpose of making ornaments for personal adorn- 
ment. They string them on cotton-thread, and tie 
them round their ankles when dancing. 

We were not to enjoy the pleasures of the open 
country long without some drawback, and this came 
in the form of myriads of small flies with white legs, 
which swarmed around our heads, alighting in clusters 
round our eyelids, in our ears, and on our necks, 
to drink the moisture of the skin. They tickled me 
so that I found them more annoying and persistent 
in their attacks than regular biting flies. To be 
constantly employed brushing them off was a most 
tiresome occupation. 

We spent one day at Maturuwow village, and then 
with the head man of the place, old Yacobus, as guide, 
my own men, and seven Wapisiana Indians, I started 
on my journey for the Carawaimintow mountains. 
Traversing an undulating savanna country, dotted 
with purplish rounded hills to the west, and bounded 
by an even line of forest on the east, we reached the 
village of Curawashinang, to which place some of my 


158 UP THE REWA AND QUITARO RIVERS. Cumar. VIL 


Wapisiana carriers belonged. From this place there 
is a fine view to the north, of the high mountains 
lying in an east and west direction, between the 
Rupununi and Quitaro rivers, which, being distant, 
are of a fine blue colour, Far off to the west is a 
singular high, isolated mountain with three peaks, 
called Saererie, which is a landmark in this region, 
being visible from all parts of the district. 

Two of my men, having become disabled on the way, 
had to be left at this village, and their places filled by 
Indians. One had injured the sole of one foot, and 
the other had both thighs much swollen. 

In apportioning the loads to the Indians, one man 
laughed with scorn at his being supplied with what he 
considered to be a light one, and begged to be allowed 
to carry two. These were given him, and weighed 
conjointly fifty pounds. He was bow-legged, and as 
he walked along before me I could imagine that I saw 
his legs becoming more bowed under the weight on 
his back. This load he carried manfully for nearly a 
week before anything was used from it to lighten it. 
This shows what a vast amount of strength and 
endurance these people possess, for to carry such a 
weight day after day, in a country not more than 24° 
north of the equator, is hard work indeed. 

Our course lay south through two villages, and on 
to a third, called Watertecow, the Watuticaba of 
Schomburgk. 

Our approach to a village was always announced 
by some of our Indians playing a sort of tune on their 
bone flutes, and our entry into it by their shoutings. 
They would then enter the principal house, and sit 


Cuar. VII. INDIAN SALUTATIONS. 159 


down in a circle. The chief man of the place would 
rise from his hammock, walk up behind one, and tap 
him on the shoulder, muttering a few words of salu- 
tation, which were responded to. He then passed on 
to the next, and ‘solemnly went the round of the 
circle, holding a slight conversation with each, standing 
up behind the back of the man with whom he spoke. 
That being over he retired to his hammock, and 
another man of the place went the round in the same 
manner. 

The Macusis acted differently ; they entered the 
houses noisily, giving three stamps on the ground, 
and uttering in a deep tone of voice the sounds, 
“ Too-too-too-hah-hah-hah.”” The people who greeted 
them did not touch them, but stood up in front and 
held short conversations. These proceedings were 
evidently according to rules of etiquette amongst 
them, and the solemn manner in which they went 
through them amused me greatly. As the man who 
stood up addressed the other, pouring forth a long 
and rapidly-worded address (glancing at me through 
the corners of his eyes now and then), the Macusi 
addressed would keep up a constant fire of words, 
such as, Enah! Enonah! Enonombay! Oah! &c., 
over and over again. These replies are equivalent to 
our Yes; Yes, indeed; Yes, truly, &c. 

At one of the villages through which we passed 
there was an Indian who spoke a little English, 
having worked at some wood-cutting establishment 
near Bartika Grove. He put on a shirt, trousers, 
and hat, and tied a red handkerchief round his neck. 
Advancing to greet me, he said, in an inquiring tone 


160 UP THE REWA AND QUITARO RIVERS. Cuar. VII. 


of voice, “ You come?” I tried to enter into con- 
versation with him, but he appeared too excited to 
understand what I said. Seeing some bush-hog meat 
drying on a barbacue, I remarked, “ You have got 
some bush hog, I see,” at which he appeared much 
startled, replying in a loud and emphatic tone, “ No; 
no bushalog da dere.” . 

I felt thirsty, so I said, “ Have you got any water?” 
and received the same reply, “No; no water da 
dere.” That roused me, and I said, ‘“ Confound it, 
get me some water.” These words had an electric 
effect; he dashed to a corner of the house, nearly 
breaking his neck over a paiworie trough, and re- 
turned with a calabash of water. 

My evening and night at Watertecow will long 
dwell in my memory, from the way in which I was 
pestered by white-legged flies in the daylight, and 
sandflies and mosquitoes at night. It was impossible 
even to write down a single note of the day's pro- 
ceedings, as both hands had to be employed in de 
fending my head from these insects. Thinking to 
escape the fleas in the Indian houses, I hung my 
hammock to posts outside, and thus fell into the 
jaws of mosquitoes, which proved to be equally bad 
neighbours. 

From Watertecow we went west, to a village called 
Parearabatonwow, then south again, past the singular 
granite hill called Tomboro, which rises to a height of 
some 500 feet above the plain, and has but little 
vegetation upon it. Near its foot we passed through 
the provision grounds of the Parearabaton people, in 
which the cassava plants grew to the remarkable height 


Cuar. VII. ATORAI INDIANS. 161 


of fifteen feet, whilst Indian corn also attained an 
equal altitude. 

Not far south of Tomboro we entered the forest, 
leaving all the savanna country behind, and following 
a very rough path arrived at an Atorai Indian’s house 
called Cotoewow, where we spent a day encamped. 
This house was large, with a cylindrical-shaped lower 
part, and conical roof, slightly flattened on top. 
Around the inside, sticking in the palm thatch, were 
an immense number of jaw-bones of the red howling 
and quata monkeys, all of which had fallen victims to 
the poisoned arrows of the blow-pipe of the owner of 
the place. Indians, as a rule, have a curious habit of 
hanging up or sticking against the rafters of their 
houses the bones or skulls of various small animals 
which they have killed and caten. 

The inhabitants, ten in number, were of a light 
yellow colour, and much fairer skinned than the 
Wapisianas and Macusis. This may be explained by 
their not being so much exposed to the sun’s rays as 
those tribes that live ou the open savannas. Their 
language, though different, resembles somewhat that 
of the Wapisianas. 

Accompanied by the head man as guide I walked to 
the Carawaimintow mountain, and ascended it to a 
height of 1200 feet, along a hunting path, returning 
to the camp late in the afternoon, after having been 
ten hours on foot. On the way we passed a large 
tree, the stem of which on one side was covered with 
an army of gray caterpillars. The guide drew my 
attention to them, and then standing some three feet 
away shouted out “heigh,” upon which every one of 


162 UP THE REWA AND QUITARO RIVERS. Cmar. VIL 


the vast body threw up their heads and front portion 
of their bodies at right angles to the tree, and simul- 
taneously flopped them down again. This they 
repeated every time the word “heigh” was shouted 
at them, but took no notice of a sharp whistle or other 
less quickly uttered sound. 

Not far from the house, on our way in returning, the 
guide showed me a small hut in which he manufactured 
Wouralli poison for blow-pipe and other arrows. The 
chief ingredient in this substance is the juice of a plant 
(Strychnos toxifera), which grows in the Canucu moun- 
tains, but not in the Carawaimintow district. In the 
hut was a long fireplace made of stones, a large funnel- 
shaped gourd used as a strainer, and a small basket. 

At the village they manufactured that fine purple 
madder pigment called Caraweru. It is made from 
the leaves of a creeper (Bignonia chica), which are 
dried, soaked in water, and beaten up and boiled in 
earthenware pots, with the bark of a certain forest 
tree. The pigment, in bard little pieces, is packed in 
small boxes ingeniously fashioned from the young 
leaves of the Curua palm (Attalea speciosa). Some of 
the leaves of the Chica which I pulled turned purple in 
half an hour after they were packed. 

Some of my men made a famous swing from a long, 
stout bush-rope hanging from the branches of a large 
tree to the ground, which was some fifty feet in length. 
They tied a bar across its end about three feet up, 
upon which one man at a time took his seat, while 
another drew bim back and then launched him forward 
with a shove, so that he swung majestically backwards 
and forwards through an arc of some sixty feet. 


Cuar. VII. OLD NEGRESS. 163 


Leaving Cotoewow we went in a south-west di- 
rection through the forest, crossing two large streams, 
one called Totohwow, the other Sabernawow, and 
passing through one village came to a second called 
Heherap, at which we put up for the night. At this 
place there were two somewhat similar looking 
curiosities, one being an old negro woman, the other a 
young quata monkey. The aged negress had snow- 
white, woolly hair, and no clothing except a bead apron 
like the Indian women, with whom her black colour 
and different figure formed a most curious contrast. 
She differed from them in her waist, which was 
smaller ; her shoulders, which were narrower; and her 
hips, which were wider. This poor old creature —a 
runaway slave from Brazil—had lived for many 
years amongst these people, aud had adopted their 
language and customs. The quata monkey was not 
unlike a young negro in the face, and its body was 
covered with very sparse black hairs. It sprawled 
about on the ground like a helpless baby, and by its 
actions begged most piteously of any Indian woman 
who passed it to be taken up and nursed. 

Three-quarters of a day’s walk from Heherap 
brought us to a village called Waru, situated in a 
small savanna, from which Wintower and Wassarie 
mountains could be seen to the southward, situated in 
a vast forest-covered country. Near this place we 
crossed a path which leads to the Taruma Indian 
country, on the Upper Essequebo, which was made by 
those Indians who used to come from their part to 
barter with the Wapisianas, giving cassava graters 
and hunting dogs in exchange for calico, axes, knives, 

M 2 


164 UP THE REWA AND QUITARO RIVERS. Csar. VII. 


&c. For some years they had not used the path, and 
now the Wapisianas go to their country via the 
Cuyuwini river instead. From this place we went in 
a southerly direction to a small branch of the Takutu 
river called Takutuwow, and then returning and fol- 
lowing the westerly continuation of the Taruma path 
came to the head waters of the Rupununi river, 
bordered by a narrow belt of trees, where that stream 
was only five feet wide and one foot deep. Just before 
reaching it we came out of the forest on the great 
savanna which stretches north to the Canucu moun- 
tains. From this we took a path leading north-east, 
and in two days reached the village of Parearabaton- 
wow, near Tomboro, stopping one night at an Atorai 
village called Waripuow, where amongst the Indians 
dwelt two men and a woman, who were half negro 
and half Atorai. One of them spoke English very 
well, from having worked on woodcutters’ grants 
down near the coast. He told me that his father 
was a negro cook at one time to Schomburgk, and 
had afterwards settled at this place. His mother, 
whom he pointed out to me, was a small-sized, ordi- 
nary-looking Indian woman. This man was very 
dark complexioned, and had straight hair, resembling 
a Madras coolie in features and tint. His brother was 
much lighter, but had a more negro-like head, nose, 
and lips, and frizzly hair, while his sister could only 
be distinguished from the other Indians by her frizzly, 
curling hair. His children were dark, but with Indian- 
shaped countenances, whilst his brother’s children still 
showed the negro cast. 

On approaching the next village we met a band of 


Cuar. VII. PAIWORIE DRINKING. 165 


fifteen Atorais, headed by a man beating a native 
drum, who it seems came to meet us. They turned 
and marched with us to their village, the drummer 
beating away at his instrument in a most lively man- 
ner. It turned out that they had a little drink on 
hand, and thus invited us to join in the festival. My 
men and Indians did join in, and drank as fast as the 
horrid decoction could be handed round. Weak as 
paiworie is in the amount of alcohol it contains, a 
bucketful or so will make a man drunk. But as no 
man’s stomach, not even an Indian’s, can hold that 
amount at one time, the drinkers resort to a method 
for obtaining a good ‘‘ drunk,” which by long practice 
becomes feasible. They absorb it in such vast quan- 
tities that the stomach becomes overladen, and returns 
it by the way it was poured down, leaving, however, 
all the contained alcohol in tbe drinker’s blood or 
brain. As a quart or so is returned the women 
hand a fresh supply to be swallowed, and down it is 
gulped, till a state of intoxication is reached. Hard 
old drinkers do not seem to feel sick during the opera- 
tion, but laugh and seem to enjoy the fun of vomiting. 

The band of Wapisiana Indians with me were very 
jolly fellows, full of fun and laughter, joking and chaff- 
ing each other incessantly, so that at their urgent 
solicitation I remained a few hours at the place, to 
enable them to indulge their natural propensity for 
the beer of the country. My men did not, of course, 
drink to excess; but my Indians did most effectually. 

When I was ready to depart they formed a deputa- 
tion, with the guide Yacobus at their head, to ask me 
to allow them to stay all day and nigbt, which I 


166 UP THE REWA AND QUITARO RIVERS. Cmar. VII. 


refused, ordering an immediate start. I had my 
doubts about this order being obeyed by Yacobus, 
without whom neither I nor my men could have 
found our way on, but was most agreeably surprised 
to see the promptitude with which he responded to 
my call. Hoisting his load in a drunken way, he 
started, and, as he went along the path, would now 
and then turn aside to vomit, look at me and grin, and 
then resume his erratic walk. 

From Parearabatonwow village we struck off north- 
east, without a path, and in two days reached the 
village of Curawashinang, where I found that my 
two men left there had quite recovered. The walk- 
ing without a path was very rough, from the large 
stalks of a curious tufted grass growing thickly over 
the savanna, causing one’s foot to come down unevenly, 
and frequently twisting the ankle. 

During this journey the Indians had a monkey hunt 
of a most exciting kind. Two unfortunate gray monkeys 
had got into a small patch of semi-detached wood, part 
of a narrow belt along a small river, where the Indians 
espied them. Being much in want of meat-food at the 
time, and not relishing our salt fish, they determined 
to obtain these monkeys for their dinner, and went to 
work at them with sticks, stones, and bows and arrows. 
The monkeys thus pursued few wildly from tree-top 
to tree-top for a time, and then cunningly tried to 
hide, but without avail. Smart as they were, they 
were no match for the gang of savages, and after 
half an hour’s shouting, laughing, stone-throwing, and 
arrow-flying, the poor monkeys’ corpses were in the 
hands of their assailants. 


Cuar. VII. SAVANNA ON FIRE. 167 


As we came along in one place the Indians set the 
dry savanna grass on fire, and as the low wall of flame 
advanced before the wind, throwing up puffs of dark 
smoke, numbers of large savanna kites (Polyborus 
cheriway) sprang up, as if by magic, from low neigh- 
bouring trees, and hovered in advance of the line of 
fire, on the watch for snakes, mice, rats, lizards, &., 
that were flying before the destructive flame. One 
of these birds dropped suddenly in the grass close to 
me, and as quickly rose again, with a rat in its talons, 
which it carried to a low tree, and then giving it a 
peck or two swallowed it whole. 

In a water-hole, at the foot of a group of Itah palms, 
the Indians obtained numbers of the nuts of those trees 
by diving. They were small, being covered with a 
set of beautifully-arranged plates of smooth, brown 
outer shell, which was easily rubbed off by the hand, 
disclosing a thin coating of soft, yellow pulp, having a 
sour, cheesy, but rather pleasant taste. 

On a low ridge of solid granite, which we crossed 
in the way, I observed some pink-flowered orchids, 
two kinds of cacti, and a curious little red plant, with 
jointed cell-like stem, to which was attached a coating 
of a hairy cotton-like substance. 

My cook, a white Creole, named Griffiths, was seized 
with fever on the last day’s march, and by the time we 
reached Curawashinang was completely exhausted. 
Soon after he became delirious, and I felt quite 
alarmed about him. 

At Curawashinang I shot a small bird, which is 
very common all over the savannas. It flies very 
steadily, and offers a capital shot when on the wing. 


168 UP THE REWA AND QUITARO RIVERS. Cmar. VII. 


It is a large species of starling (Sturnella ludovi- 
cianus), with a yellow breast, black throat, and snipe- 
like feathers on the back. It is provided with a long 
hind toe on each foot, terminating in a long spur-like 
claw, and its beak is sharp and curved. 

The night we spent at Curawashinang village the 
savanna grass was on fire some five miles to the north- 
ward, and as the blaze flashed and flickered in the 
dark night it formed a fine sight, dying away in one 
part and shooting out in another, the whole forming a 
long line. When low, one could fancy that it was the 
lights of a large city on the plain. 

On gaining Maturuwow, the place from which we 
originally set out, I left my servant in the care of 
William, and went on to the northward to Shea 
mountain; then on to Cotoewow river, a branch of 
the Rupununi; and returned to Maturuwow, after an 
absence of three and a half days. 

From the top of Shea rock, an enormous mass 
of solid granite, a fine view of the surrounding 
country is obtained. Near its top, on the south side, 
there is a small shelving cave, the bottom of which 
is covered with dusty loam, and in and upon this 
loam are numerous bones of deer, fish, tortoise, 
&c., the remains of repasts of Indian parties who, 
from time to time, for unexplained causes, make 
visits there. Under the shelving roof of one part 
of the cave was a bees’ nest, built of a brown, 
leathery-looking substance, part of which our guide 
knocked down with a stick, and seizing as much 
honeycomb as possible, rushed off ‘to us with it, 
accompanied by a buzzing crowd of small, black 


Cuar. VII. BLACK BEES. i 169 


bees. Though they did not sting, yet they nipped 
with their mandibles, and followed us about as we 
partook of their honey. The honey cells were large 
and irregularly formed, and the honey had a slightly 
acid flavour, while the wax was of a dirty-browp 
-colour. These black bees (Melipona sp.) are very 
common, and frequently when we had shot game, and 
left it hanging to a tree for a short time till it was 
convenient to carry it away, we would find it covered 
with them, all busily engaged feeding on its flesh. 

Scattered about in the vicinity of Shea are a 
number of Indian houses, at one of which lives a half- 
breed, between a negro and Indian, who, like the Carib 
half-breeds, has short curly hair. He had the skins of 
two large jaguars which he had shot, hanging up 
in his house. In hunting one of these jaguars he 
badly wounded it with the first shot, and before he 
had time to load again the animal chased him. He 
had to dodge about amongst the low curatella trees 
for a considerable time, with the jaguar close upon 
him, before he got a second charge in his gun, with 
which he finished off the animal. 

At his place I saw for the first time an article of 
diet called Farinha. It is made from the meal of 
the cassava, which has been soaked in water for a 
considerable period, baked on a large, open oven, and 
stirred about all the while. This process hardens it 
and causes it to adhere in little pellets, as hard as 
pebbles. On the Amazon and its tributaries this 
substance is the chief staff of life. 

At Shea village old Yacobus and three other Indian 
men commenced drinking paiworie. By 10 a.m. they 


170 UP THE REWA AND QUITARO RIVERS. Cmar. VII. 


were all tipsy and commenced dancing with measured 
tread, round and round the house, and up and 
down, dressed out in feathers and paint. When I 
returned to the place in the afternoon I found them 
still dancing in a very tipsy condition, while old 
Yacobus was actually foaming at the mouth. The 
four simpletons cut a most sorry and unmauly figure, 
but evidently enjoyed themselves. When we got 
back to Maturuwow on the following day, Yacobus did 
not seem the worse for his debauch, for he imme- 
diately started off to shoot a deer for me, taking his 
wife with him, with a cartowerie on her back to bring 
back the game, with which sure enough he returned 
in the afternoon. 

In getting some of the provision packs overhauled 
in preparing for a start on the following day on our 
journey to our boat on the Quitaro, we discovered 
a rattlesnake, three fect in length, coiled up amongst 
them. These packs were under one end of my 
awning, under which Griffiths, who was left there ill 
with fever, had slept. 

The snake had made its home within a few feet 
of Griffiths’ back as he lay in his hammock, and it 
was a mercy that in moving about he had not come 
in contact with the reptile. An Indian shot an arrow 
into it, which passed through its neck and into one 
of its coils. In this harmless position I teased it with 
a stick, at which it became enraged and bit its own 
body, the large poison-fangs only entering a short 
distance through its scales, owing to its position 
preventing its getting a fair bite. Its poison, 
if it entered its body, had not the slightest effect, 


Cuap. VII. RETURN TO BOAT. 171 


and after a short time I knocked it on the head with 
a stick. 

Soon after starting from Maturuwow, on our way 
through the forest to the Quitaro, I felt the chills that 
precede an attack of fever come on, followed by ague- 
pains in both arms and legs. Though I walked as 
fast as I could, under the circumstances, I could not 
“turn a hair,” but my skin was dry and parched. 
I know of nothing more unpleasant or of harder 
work than walking under such conditions. Yacobus, 
who was walking in front of me, once or twice gave 
a deep groan; then stopping, he took my hand and put 
it on his forehead, to show me that-he was suffering 
from headache. His skin was also dry and hot, 
though he was carrying a heavy load, and I then knew 
that he was a victim to fever like myself. Showing 
him that I was in the same condition, we gave each 
other a mutual look of sympathy, which lightened 
considerably each one’s load of woe, and continued our 
weary walk. After five hours’ walking in this 
feverish state, a profuse perspiration broke out, and 
the enemy left me. 

On arriving at the river I found most of the Cura- 
washinang Indians encamped there, and what with the 
barking of dogs, screeching of children, and talking 
and laughing of people, the place was almost un- 
bearable. One woman there applied one of the most 
extraordinary remedies for ophthalmia, to her child of 
five years of age, that I ever heard of. She actually 
rubbed the juice of a large red pepper into both its 
eyes, which made the poor little creature scream 
and cry most bitterly. I abused the woman for her 


172 UP THE REWA AND QUITARO RIVERS Casar. VII- 


cruelty, but it was too late to stop the operation and 
had no effect, for she did not understand a word I 
said. 

On the 9th of November, the day after our return 
to our boat, we commenced our journey down the 
Quitaro, and on the 18th of the same month arrived 
at the Carib village of Apoterie, at the mouth of the 
Rupununi river, where I found Mr. Sawkins waiting 
for me, having just come down from the foot of the 
first set of cataracts on the Upper Essequebo. 

We descended the Essequebo in company, and 
arrived at the Penal Settlement on the 29th of Novem- 
ber; and thus terminated my fourth expedition.

Ants — Owenteik — Indian Game —F rog Pits — Kibihies — Enapowou 
— Descend Potaro— Discover Kaieteur Great Fall — Partamona Indian 
Village — Reach Tumatamari — Arrive at Settlement. 


On February the 10th, 1870, I again left the Penal 
Settlement with two boats, the “Cuyuni” and “ Wa- 
ramambo,” and, ascending the Essequebo, reached 
the mouth of the Potaro river on the morning of 
the 17th. 

During my journey up I met with a severe loss in 
the death of my faithful little dog Chickerine. It had 
accompanied me on all my journeys, from the time it 
became attached to me on my return from the Maza- 
runi till its untimely death, hardly ever being out 
of my sight fora moment. For deep attachment and 
affection it was one dog in a thousand. 

My first object being to explore the Potaro river, 
we accordingly entered it, accompanied by some 
Indians whom we met on the Essequebo on their 
way down to the Demerara river. They told me that 
it had falls of great magnitude which would not admit 
of the ascent of my boats; and on our arrival at the 
foot of the first cataract (four miles up), called Tuma- 


174 UP THE BURRO-BURRO, ETC. Cuar. VIII. 


tamari, I found their information to be perfectly 
correct. This cataract has a total height of twenty-four 
feet, and is divided into two portions by a rocky island. 
The northern portion is very precipitous, the river 
thundering down it over a long slope; the southern 
portion is at first perpendicular for a few feet, and 
then follows a similarly steep descent. My boats 
being old, heavy, and not strongly built, we concluded 
that they would not stand the strain of dragging over 
the rocks without serious injury; and a hill prevented 
our taking them round overland. 

On entering the Potaro one is struck with the 
intensely dark colour of its water, which resembles 
a strong decoction of tea, owing to the tannin derived 
from vegetable matters which it contains. 

I made up my mind to abandon the idea of as- 
cending it, but to continue up the Essequebo and 
ascend the Siparuni instead, crossing from thence by 
land to the Potaro head, and then descending it in 
woodskins. Accordingly, we re-entered the Essequebo, 
and after four days’ toilsome ascent reached the mouth 
of the Siparuni. This river is about 100 yards wide 
at its entrance, and also contains black water. It is 
comparatively free from cataracts, and those upon it, 
with one exception, are diminutive. About six miles 
up we passed the mouth of a branch on the south, 
called the Burro-burro river, and on the third day 
came to a place where the river branched. Taking 
the northerly one to be the main river, we ascended it 
for a day’s journey, amongst small cataracts and 
rapids, and found it decrease so rapidly in size that 
we concluded we had gone astray. We therefore

Cuar. VIII. COMUDIES. 175 


returned to the other branch, which proved eventually 
to be the main river. | 

We were altogether eleven days on our journey 
up the Siparuni, and traced it out until it became so 
narrow that the old trees, which had grown on its 
banks and fallen, lay quite across it. Numbers of 
them were under water, and as the river was rapidly 
falling, we feared that our boats would not be able 
to pass over them in returning. When at last we 
reached a high cataract, produced by a bar of green- 
stone, we gave up all hopes of going farther, and 
turned back. ‘Though there were numerous signs of 
Indian travellers having been on the river, yet we 
met with none, and were disappointed accordingly, 
being much in need of Indian assistance and infor- 
mation. Before us was the escarpment of the sand- 
stone mountains, somewhat similar to that on the 
Mazaruni, but less precipitous. 

During our ascent of this river we saw four large 
Water-boas or Comudies (Eunectes murinus), three 
of which I killed by shooting them in the head. 
The first I fired at was shot in the body, and made 
off into the water seemingly but little damaged. 
We had rather an amusing scene whilst killing one 
of these snakes. It was coiled up on the bank at 
the end of a fallen tree, which, sloping into the water, 
formed a bridge for us to approach by. Seeing by 
the swollen condition of a portion of its body that 
it was digesting some animal, and was consequently 
in a sluggish state, I walked up the log, gun in 
hand, to kill it. I fired at its head, which was 
pretty nearly hidden in its coils, and thinking I had 


176 ` UP THE BURRO-BURRO, ETC. Cuar. VIII. 


killed it, approached nearer; when of a sudden it 
darted out its head to within a foot or two of me, 
straightening out one or two of its inner coils, 
Luckily it could not straighten out more, or it would 
undoubtedly have seized me. It is almost superfluous 
to state that I then fled and, in so doing, dashed against 
one of my men who stood at the head of the sloping 
log, causing him to collide against a man next him; 
this second man fell against a third who, having un- 
fortunately nothing to support him, tipped over into 
the water off the log, midst shouts of laughter from 
those left in the boat. When I found my retreat thus 
cut off by the men, I turned to face the snake, and 
found that it was not giving chase, but that its ele- 
vated head offered me a splendid shot. Putting a 
charge of BB into it I had the satisfaction of ter- 
minating its existence. 

One of these snakes measured sixteen feet in length, 
and twenty-two inches in girth round the largest 
portion of the body, although it appeared much larger 
than those measurements indicated. Its back was black, 
softening on the sides to gray, and then to yellow on 
the underneath portion. Along its sides were large 
yellow spots, placed in a line at irregular distances 
apart, while along the sides of its head, over each eye, 
was a broad band of a brown colour. Its tail ended 
abruptly in a round blunt end, and about six inches 
back, on the under part of its body, were two little 
toe-like processes, one on each side of the cloaca, each 
terminating in a bird-like claw. 

In returning down river past one of these dead 
snakes, which we had slain on our way up, I sawa 


Cuap. VIII. WATER-SNAKE. 177 


pair of king vultures (Sarcoramphus papa) in charge 
of the corpse. It is a well-known fact that where the 
carcase of a Comadi snake is, there will two king 
vultures be gathered together. 

We met with a large water-snake of another kind, 
called the Haimaralli, which is said to be poisonous, 
and to attack people venturing into the water in its 
vicinity. Its poison does not act rapidly, but causes 
the bitten limb to decay gradually away till death 
releases the sufferer. This snake is of a blackish 
colour, marked with brown. 

The bird-stuffer, young Paulie, whom I had taken 
with me to procure specimens of birds, shot a small 
bird on this river called a Maam-licken, so called by 
the Arowaaks from its similarity in appearance to the 
small Tinamous. It does not, however, belong to 
that family from the fact of its having scansorial feet. 

During the greater part of our journey on this river 
we experienced very wet weather, not a day passing 
without rainfalls, either in heavy downpours or light 
showers. The sun seldom shone for any length of 
time, and everything was damp and sodden. From 
the time we entered the river until Sunday, March 
the 6th, there was not a single chance of getting 
clothes dried, so that when the sun shone, upon that 
day our camp resembled a laundry, all hands being 
busy washing and hanging up clothes to dry. 

When, on the morning of March 9th, we reached the 
mouth of the Burro-burro we turned up it, and in 
one day’s journey reached the landing of a Macusi 
village. The village itself was some distance back in 
the forest, and contained two large houses, one having 

N 


178 UP THE BURRO-BURRO, ETC. Cmar. VII. 


an upper story. On the ground floor of this occupied 
house was the grave of its late owner. We had to 
remain a few days at the village for the arrival of a 
man who was to go on with us as guide, and in the 
meantime I despatched one of the boats, the “ Wara- 
mambo,” to the Penal Settlement to bring up the 
remainder of our supplies which had been left behind. 

One night, at our camp at the.landing, we set fire 
to the bottom of a large hollow Mora tree, and soon 
the whole of its decayed inner portion was in flames, 
which rushed out at its top with a loud, roaring noise, 
throwing up vast quantities of sparks, which fell in 
curved showers like fireworks, forming a most beautiful 
sight. The branches burnt off at their bases an hour 
or two after the fire had got well under way, and 
came to the ground with a heavy crash, while the 
stem burnt all night, and fell towards morning with a 
most terrific noise. The roaring of the fire was heard 
by the village Indians, but as it sounded high in the 
air they could not imagine its cause, and became 
greatly alarmed. Three of them came out to us in the 
middle of the night to ask what we thought of the 
extraordinary noise, and as they approached our camp 
at once learned the cause. They stated that they 
thought that the “world was on fire.” 

With a Macusi in his woodskin as guide we con- 
tinued our journey up the gloomy winding Burro- 
burro, and in two days reached a branch of that river 
coming in on the north, called Sipariparu. One day 
on this river brought us to a village, which we visited 
for the purpose of obtaining woodskins for our further 
journey. 


Camar. VIIT. EDIBLE LIZARDS. 179 


In passing a large inlet on the south bank the guide 
said that some Kanaimas of the Partamona tribe, a 
branch of the Ackawoise, lived far up it, who only 
came out to kill or kanaima members of the other 
tribes. 

We lost much time during the two days on the 
Burro-burro in cutting with axes some very large and 
hard dead trees, which lay across the river from bank 
to bank ; and on the Sipariparu had to cut through the 
stems and branches of a soft-wood tree, which grew 
out from either bank and met in the middle of the 
stream. A large corial, containing fourteen Caribs 
from Itaballia, overtook us on the way, some of whom 
we pressed into our service, making them share half 
our cutting work. They caught lizards of a peculiar 
kind with slip-knots on the ends of sticks, and placed 
them in baskets previous to feeding on them when a 
sufficient quantity had been obtained. These lizards 
inhabit the.stems of trees on the water's edge, and can 
half swim and half run on the surface of the water. 
They are called Yamourous, and are not unlike small 
Tguanas. 

The head man of the village on the Sipariparu, a 
feeble old fellow, who bore a bad character as a 
destroyer of his kind, felt ill at ease at our visit. He 
provided the necessary woodskins, and seemed not to 
care for payment, only wishing us to depart from his 
place. We did so, and returned to the Burro-burro. 
Leaving my boat there, with Captain Peterson and 
another man in charge, I went up that river one and 
a half day’s journey, and came to the landing of the 
Village of Surama. I killed on the way a fine 

N 2 


180 UP THE BURRO-BURRO, ETC. Cuar. VIII. 


Muscovy duck and a bush hog. Game was plentiful, 
and beaten paths of Maipourie or Tapir (Tapirus 
Americanus) came to the river from all directions. 
Tapirs are very fond of the fruit of the hog-plam 
(Spondias sp.), and visit places where these trees 
grow for the purpose of eating their fallen fruit. Birds 
of edible qualities were also numerous and tame, 
which was to me a matter for wonder, seeing the 
number of Indians living near by who constantly 
hunted this part. White-headed Marondies (Penelope 
pipile) were very common, and at daybreak in the 
morning, when flying from their roosting places, pro- 
duced a curious rattling noise with their wings. Small 
and bright-plumaged Toucans (Pteroglossus aracara) 
were also frequently seen. 

On this part of the river the heavy forests of Mora 
and Greenheart (Nectandra Rodan) gave way to 
growths of a small and graceful bamboo, which was 
exceedingly pretty. Amongst the bamboos were vines, 
long climbing grasses with sharp cutting edges, called 
razor grass, wild canes (Gynerium saccharinum), and 
wild plantains. | 

The village of Surama which I visited was 24 miles 
from the river, in the midst of a large savanna, from 
which fine views of the surrounding mountains were 
obtained. At one of the houses I obtained the pods 
of the Simiri, or Locust tree (Hymenea courbaril), 
and some seeds of the Cucurite palm (Mazimiliana 
regia). In the former, around the seeds, is a dry 
fluffy substance of a light, yellowish colour, which is 
good to eat, tasting not unlike a dry cake, being 
sweet, and melting in the mouth. On the nuts of the 


Cuar. VIII. BELL-BIRDS. 181 


latter is a yellow, juicy pulp, which is sweet and 
pleasant to the taste. 

I formed a camp at the Surama landing, and, as I 
intended starting on my walking journey from it, I 
sent back all the available men and woodskins to the 
boat, to bring up stores, whilst I went on up river to 
explore, taking only the guide, his boy, William the 
interpreter, and another man with me. The river 
was exceedingly narrow, and in one place obstructed 
by granite rocks, over which the canoes had to be 
carried. Whilst this operation was being performed, 
early in the forenoon, I stretched myself out on a 
rock on my back to enjoy the cool shade of an over- 
hanging tree, and, gazing up to the sky, to my 
astonishment, saw a planet which, owing to the re- 
markable clearness of the atmosphere, shone of a white 
colour, like the moon when seen in the daytime. 

On the way we heard numbers of bell-birds (Cas 
morhynchus carunculatus) calling in the surrounding 
forest ; wishing to obtain one I landed, and, guided 
by their calls to their vicinity, stood for some time 
under a tree in which one was perched before I 
discovered its whereabouts. The one I shot proved 
to be a male in full plumage, of a snow-white colour, 
with black beak and eyes, and with a black, leathery 
excrescence of nearly an inch in length, rising up from 
the top of the base of its bill. When close to these 
birds the sounds they produce are of two kinds, one 
resembling the word Dér-dng, and the other Kong- 
kay, the latter syllable of each being prolonged. 
There is a sort of ventriloquism about the utterance 
of the Dor-ong, which is made when the bird holds its 


182 UP THE BURRO-BURRO, ETC. Cear. VIII. 


head up, that renders it almost impossible to find the 
exact whereabouts of the bird when using this form of 
call; when, however, it puts its bill and head down- 
wards, and calls Kong-kay, it can be seen at once. The 
females are of a gray colour, with a yellowish cast. 

I saw a dead porcupine (Cercolabes prehensilis) on 
the edge of the river, which was of a gray colour, its 
quills being small and yellow tipped with black. Its 
head was extremely porcupinish in form, but its tail 
was prehensile.
