A very large and lofty species of Bamboo ( Bambusa 
sp.) grows along the river above Surama, which, un- 
like the generality of bamboos, has each stem growing 
singly from an isolated root. 

On the second day after leaving Surama we came 
to a landing place, beyond which the river was too 
shallow and rapid for woodskins. We therefore landed, 
and following an Indian path, ascended a steep hill to 
a height of 500 feet above the river, amongst the 
mountains. One hour and a halfs walking brought 
us to a Macusi village, called Archiecullock, from 
which there was a fine view looking west up the 
wooded valley of the Burro-burro, nestling on the sides 
of which were two distant villages. A little Macusi 
boy, some seven years of age, took a fancy to me, and 
followed me about everywhere, talking the whole 
time, but seemingly much astonished at my not being 
able to understand his conversation. As a rule, all 
Indian cbildren and some youths fled at my approach, 
being unused. to a creature with a white skin; and 
this made it all the more extraordinary that this little 
boy should be so friendly. When preparing to leave 


Cuar. VIII. MACUSI BOY. 183 


the village, I got the interpreter to ask him if he 
would like to come with me, He seemed delighted, 
and ran to ask his mother’s leave ; but as she was not 
in the house, he tried to get his little hammock untied, 
so as to be able to accompany me. As it was tied 
tightly and too high up for him to reach, he came 
back to me in tears to say that as he could not get 
his hammock he could not come. 

At this village there was a man whose head was 
bent down on one side almost to his shoulder, from an 
injury to the sinews of his neck, produced by the bite 
of a tiger. He and another Indian were returning 
from hunting when a jaguar (Felis onça) sprung upon 
him and bore him to the ground. His companion ran 
at the beast, which jumped off the prostrate man to 
attack him, when he fired and killed it. He then 
looked at the fallen man, who was insensible, and 
thinking him dead, left him and returned to the 
village. At nightfall the people of the place were 
astonished to see the wounded man crawling slowly 
to his home. After a long illness he recovered, but 
the sinews of the neck being injured, he never could 
straighten his head again. 

Five fine handsome young Macusi men came out to 
our camp, who, on learning that we were going by 
land from Surama to the Ireng, informed us that the 
people of Quonga in that district were dangerous, and 
had recently killed a negro who came amongst them. 

On returning to Surama landing, I spent two days 
there, and then went in and took up my quarters at 
the village. My men were employed making journeys 
from the landing to the boat, to bring up stores for 


184 UP THE BURRO-BURRO, ETC. Caar. VIII. 


our land journey; and on their last trip down I had 
three small woodskins placed inside of three large 
Ones, so as to economise labour. These were to 
remain till the “ Waramambo” arrived to bring up 
her men and stores. 

At this time the Indians of the district were having 
a grand fish-poisoning excursion somewhere in the 
neighbourhood, and it was with great difficulty that I 
could get any assistance from them. I, however, pre- 
vailed upon the son of the aged, wrinkled head man 
of Surama to go with me, as guide, by a roundabout 
journey to Annai, on the Rupununi, and back. We 
were a day and a half going across, and passed through 
two large villages, one at which we stopped a night 
being called Palearouta. It was composed of seven 
large houses, two of which were raised on posts, and 
one had a sort of verandah in front. From our leaving 
Surama savanna, till we came out on the Rupununi 
savanna, most of our way lay through bush, swarming 
with horrid ticks of all sizes, so that I had to be 
constantly picking them off my clothes. 

The evening I spent at Annai was delightfully cool, 
and the dove-coloured glow that spread over the sky 
as the sun sank behind the western hills was more 
marked and beautiful than usual. Waking about an 
hour before sunrise next morning, I watched the thin 
strip of waning moon rise above the horizon, following 
on the steps of the bright morning star; and saw both 
grow paler and paler, fading away as the purple 
morning clouds gathered above the horizon before the 
rising sun. 

Leaving Annai, guided by an Indian, named Jermain, 


Cmar. VIII. FIRE AT TIGER. 185 


we struck northward through the forest, and after nine 
and a half hours’ walking, got back to Surama, where 
I learned that the “ Waramambo” had arrived at the 
place where I left my boat. I was detained four days 
at Surama getting things ready for our overland 
journey, and experienced great delay in having to send 
so far down river to our boats, for owing to the fallen 
timber they could not be brought higher up. 

In crossing the Surama savanna one afternoon, I 
saw a small tiger crossing the path with a stealthy 
gait. Ben was not far behind me with a single-barrel 
gun, and, on my beckoning to him, he ran up with it. 
I then advanced in a crouching position towards the 
tiger, but before I got well within range it saw me 
and ran. Standing up quickly, I took a short aim and 
fired, the charge being as usual BB-shot. It was then 
within ten yards of a large grove of trees, but on 
receiving a peppering with shot, sprang round, heading 
away from the bush, and taking a short circuit came 
back to the same spot, where with one or two bounds 
it plunged into the thicket. The way it sprang round 
to the side I aimed at, and bounced upwards, clearly 
proved that it was hard hit. At the village there 
were some twenty. lean dogs, which I got the Indians 
to bring into the grove next morning to try and find 
the animal, in case it had remained there in a wounded 
state. They were not a bit of use and would not try 
to hunt, but trotted slowly about amongst the trees. 
We therefore returned to the village, leaving most of 
them behind. Some were weak, imbecile, old dogs, 
which soon lost themselves in the bush, where they 
remained howling for succour all the afternoon. 


186 UP THE BURRO-BURRO, ETC. Cuar. VIIL 


After the slight adventure with the tiger, I went on 
to the village, and whilst sitting down enjoying the 
cool evening air, I saw a large rattlesnake crossing 
the clean intervening space between the grass and the 
thatched wall of one of the houses. Simultaneously 
an Indian saw it, and seizing a stick each, we ran to 
despatch it before it could enter the house, which was 
crowded with women and children. One half of its 
body had passed under the wall when our sticks came 
down together upon the other half, completely smash- 
ing it in two places, and depriving it of all power of 
advancing. The Indian then drew it out by the tail 
and battered its head. 

One afternoon some Macusis arrived who belonged 
to Peropoo village, having with them an Ackawoise 
Indian, who said that he came from the Potaro river, 
and was fleeing from the Kanaima. It was evident 
that he had committed an evil deed in his own terri- 
tory, and was fleeing from punishment ; and no wonder, 
when that punishment would be death. He wore his 
hair long and parted down the middle in the usual 
Ackawoise way, a custom which contrasted strongly 
with the style in which the Macusi, Wapisiana, and 
Atorais wear theirs. The men of those tribes keep 
their hair cut straight behind, in a line with the lobes 
of the ears, and straight across the middle of the fore- 
head to each temple in front, letting it hang in a fringe. 
The women also have the fringe in front, but allow the 
hair to grow long behind. 

On the morning of April 4th, I left Surama, my 
party consisting of thirteen of my own men and three 
Macusi Indians. We were accompanied by the old 


Cuar. VIII. COMMENCE WALKING JOURNEY. 187 


head man, who came of his own accord, bringing a 
little boy to carry hishammock. My object was to cross 
over to the Ireng river, then take a northerly course 
past the head of the Siparuni, and make my way on 
to the Potaro. Our course, at first, lay through forest 
in a westerly direction over a rough country. We 
passed through Peropoo village, and there obtained an 
Indian as guide, after which we passed three villages, 
and stopped at a fourth for the night. The people of 
the place were away, and in their stead were mos- 
quitoes and an old cock. The old bird seemed quite 
delighted with our company, having no doubt been 
very lonely by himself. From this we proceeded up 
the valley of the Taramu river, following a path near 
it until it became a mere mountain stream flowing 
down a steep incline. Late in the afternoon we had 
reached a spot at a height of 1200 feet above the sea, 
at the source of a small stream containing a pool of 
greenish semi-stagnant water, at which we were 
obliged to camp. On the way we passed through a 
pretty little wood-locked savanna, on the edge of which 
we rested for a time, and there under some low bush 
saw the nest of a powie (Craz alector), which con- 
tained two white eggs. After departing from this 
camp, we soon left the forest behind and entered upon 
savanna mountains, passing through Itabay village, 
and arriving at Quonga. 

On approaching the latter village, we saw some 
Indians fleeing away, while those that remained when 
we arrived appeared alarmed and ill at ease. Nota 
man was visible from the time of our arrival in the 
forenoon till dusk in the evening, when, as I was in 


188 UP THE BURRO-BURRO, ETC. Cmr. VIII. 


one of the houses where we had taken up our quarters, 
some twenty men entered and squatted round its 
sides. One of the lot, who appeared excited, came 
and spoke to me in English, saying that since my 
last visit he had come up from the Demerara river, 
where he had been working for some years, bringing 
with him a black man, named Harper, who wished to 
purchase hammocks; that one day Harper went 
shooting and accidentally shot himself in two places, one 
being in the stomach and the other in the back of the 
neck, from the effects of which he died. This Indian 
was so full of this story with all its particulars, and so 
anxious that I should believe him, that it seemed to 
me as if he and some others had a hand in the death 
of the poor negro. 

The steep mountain climb, from the valley of Uorora 
to Quonga, told on my men, who were all heavily 
laden, and nearly finished off the old head man of 
Surama, who fell once or twice, and bruised his legs 
on the rocks. He and two of our Indian carriers 
turned back from that place, and were replaced by 
two Quonga Indians. From Quonga we went on toa 
village called Cara-cara, where my men rested whilst 
I spent one day in going to the Ireng and back. 
Although most of the party reached Cara-cara at 
4 P.M., yet some did not come in till dusk, and two, 
having broken down on the way, did not arrive until 
the following morning. 

The Cara-cara people told us that a few days before 
our arrival they discovered three strange Indians 
lurking about their village at night, and knowing they 
had come to kill some of their people they had hunted 


Cuar. VII. INDIAN LEGEND. 189 


about and staried them from their hiding place in 
some bush. Giving chase, they came up with one 
fording the river, and as he landed on the opposite 
side sent a shower of arrows after him, one of which 
pierced his side, and he fell. Springing quickly up, 
he broke off the shaft of the arrow and made off. 

Before reaching the village we met a band of 
Indians, twenty in number, all fully armed and 
painted, going south, who said they were hunting; but 
I imagine the object of their journey had something 
to do with the above-mentioned Indian Kanaimas, 
whose place of residence they had probably dis- 
covered. . 

On my way from Cara-cara to the Ireng, amidst 
splendid scenery, we passed one of a number of lofty 
- mountains of about 3000 feet in height, named 
Waetipu, near the foot of which the guide pointed 
out a row of small white quartz rocks placed close 
together, and occupying a length of some fifty yards. 
These, he said, had been deposited there ages ago by 
a party of Carib Indians, who came from the westward 
to kill a gigantic “ Kikushie” (tiger), which lived in 
acave near by. This tiger was an exceedingly fierce 
black one, but the brave band surrounded its lair, 
and transfixed it with poisoned arrows as it tried to 
escape. To commemorate the event they painted 
some figures in red on the rocks of the cave which 
were pointed out to me, and each man then picked up 
a stone, and, standing in a line with his comrades, 
placed it on the ground. If this be correct, there 
must have been about 300 warriors engaged in this 
undertaking. They then went to a mountain near 


190 UP THE BURRO-BURRO, ETC. Caap. VIII. 


by where a dangerous eagle lived, which they also 
attacked and slew. After accomplishing these doughty 
deeds the band went back towards the setting sun, and 
were never seen again. 

We came upon the Ireng river at the Caroni falls, 
where its black water was precipitated over a number 
of steps of quartz porphyry into a large inky pool 
below. This pool was teeming with fish, according to 
the statement of the guide, and judging from the im- 
mense number of fish bones at an Indian camping place 
near by, I should say that the statement was correct. 
Amongst these bones I saw the skulls of five large 
Lowlow. On the edges of the river there grew some 
low trees bearing a small hard fruit, which evidently 
was a species of Pimenta (Eugenia). In taste it was 
hot and bad flavoured, yet the Indians ate it readily. 

After a day’s rest at Cara.cara I sent back Peterson 
and five men to the boats on the Burro-burro, with 
orders for them to descend to the mouth of the 
Siparuni river, and wait tbere for nine days in case 
we could not get paths leading northward to the 
Potaro, and should be forced to descend the Siparuni ; 
then at the expiration of that time to go down the 
Essequebo to the Potaro river and await our arrival 
at Tumatamari fall. With seven of my men and 
three Macusi I pushed on northward, and, entering 
the forest-covered country, ascended a steep hill until 
we gained an altitude of 2000 feet, on the top of 
which the Cara-cara Indians had their plantations. 
In a hut we took shelter for a short time from the 
inclemency of the weather, but as the rain did not 
cease we were compelled to go on and stand a 


Cuar. VIL. MONOORIE ANTS. 191 


soaking. Near the hut were some curious rude figures 
of men cut on some large blocks of quartz porphyry. 
Descending the back of the mountain, we arrived at a 
village in the valley beyond, and as we entered the 
door of one house a strapping Indian woman and a 
little girl bolted through the palm-thatched back wall, 
making in their fright temporary doors for themselves. 
One woman remained, the sole inhabitant of the place. 
I sat down on a log to rest outside the house, and on 
looking down at my socks, found, to my dismay, that 
they were covered with jiggers seeking an entrance 
through them. As they were soaking wet the insects 
seemed to have their ardour somewhat damped, and 
I was able to pick and rub off most of them. 
On we went again, and had it been a dry day 
. I should have shaken off the dust as well as the 
jiggers from my feet on leaving the place. Late in 
the evening we came to a new village, the owners of 
which were not at home, where we put up for the 
night. A number of that large and poisonous ant, 
the Monoorie, were about, so that when night came 
on we took to our hammocks, and dreading the stings 
of those insects dared not get out of them till daylight 
next morning. 

In the forenoon, on the following day, we came out 
of the forest upon a magnificent and extensive savanna 
valley, the views from which in all directfons were 
truly grand and charming. To the north-west was a 
high, bare, table-topped mountain, called Tawailing, 
rising gradually up to a height of about 4000 feet, the 
last 500 feet or so of which was quite perpendicular. 
On the west was the valley of the Ireng river, formed 


192 UP THE BURRO-BURRO, ETC. Cmar. VOT. 


of grass-land, with savanna mountains to the west- 
ward. To the south and east the country was wooded 
and rough, and to the north a great wooded escarp- 
ment, called Mowarieteur, stretched away to the east- 
ward. In a deep gorge between Tawailing and this 
escarpment ran a large river, called Echilebar, which 
is a tributary of the Ireng. 

In passing a low tree on the side of the path a 
brilliant red and blue macaw (Macrocercus macao) 
uttered a shriek over my head, and on looking np it 
closed its eyes, and nodded its head in a most idiotic 
manner. I at once saw that it was a tame bird, 
and knew that a village must be close at hand. Rising 
a slight eminence some Indian houses lay before us, 
and soon after we reached them. 

We reached the edge of the Echilebar river late in 
the afternoon, at an elevation of 1004 feet above the 
sea. Pressing on up the steep, wooded, sandstone 
escarpment of Mowarieteur, on the east side of the 
gorge, we climbed 1085 feet in the last hour of that 
day’s journey, and reached a village on top which, 
being exposed to the full force of the north-east wind, 
was cold and dreary. Being heated by my climb, I 
soon became thoroughly chilled by the searching wind, 
and had to walk about to warm myself. 

Next day we did not go far before we came to the 
large village of Owenteik, on reaching which the 
Macusis with me said that they wisbed to return to 
their homes. One of these was the son of the old 
chief of Surama, who had avowed his determination to 
go through to the Potaro with us. He now complained 
that the mountain climbing was too much for him and 


Cuar. VIII. AT OWENTEIK. 193 


he could go no farther. The Indians of Owenteik 
appeared to be a morose lot, and took very little notice 
of us, all remaining in their houses. Wishing to get 
three Indians to replace those that left me, I sent the 
interpreter from house to house, to ask for men, but 
the answer from all sides was, “ Wait till the head 
man comes home in a day or two, and then you will 
get men.” As we were powerless to move without 
guides, and not being able to waste time on account of 
our small stock of provisions, I took the interpreter 
with me, and entering the nearest house, approached the 
strongest-looking man in it, and then told the inter- 
preter to inform him that I wanted him to carry a 
load and guide me onwards towards the Potaro. The 
effect of this personal appeal was magical. The man 
looked pleased, and said he would come as far as the 
next village, where I could obtain men to go on with 
me. I tried two other men in a similar way in other 
houses, who also agreed to go at once. 

After this I saw a party of Indians approaching the 
village, but they turned off along the path which led 
past it. Getting abreast of the place they stopped, 
one man, in a loud and angry voice, making a long 
harangue, beating his breast wildly all the time. He 
seemed to be in a terrible rage with one or more of 
the Owenteik people, and was evidently denouncing 
them. After performing for a quarter of an hour, he 
and his departed on their way and were soon lost to 
view. | 

From the heights of Owenteik we looked north 
over an enormous plain bounded by grass-covered 


mountains, 
0 


194 UP THE BURRO-BURRO, ETC. Cuap. VIII. 


After two hours’ delay we started on our way, taking 
a northerly direction down to the Echilebar, which we 
forded in shallows at the head of a large fall, and con- 
tinued over the vast plain, passing along its western 
side not far from some hills of soft friable. sandstone, 
where landslips disclosed great faces of white, red, and 
pink argillaceous beds. 

Early in the afternoon we passed the main source 
of the Siparuni river running north-east through the 
plain, and at 4 p.m. turned off to the westward 
amongst the hills. As we tramped along we saw an 
Indian coming along the path about half a mile before 
us. Suddenly he paused for an instant, and then made 
off like a deer towards the hills on the left. It was 
evident that he saw our party and had received a scare 
from our numbers. The Indians with us said he must 
be a Kanaima, and on reaching the spot from which 
he made off, went in search of him, but without success. 
Resuming our march we soon saw him coming towards 
us, and falling in on our rear he joined our party. 
He guided us to his village, called Encaco—a dull 
forlorn-looking place, with the wind whistling around, 
and light mist-clouds passing across it. 

Going next morning in a north-westerly direction 
from Encaco, across savanna hills and bush alternately, 
we ascended a wooded sandstone mountain to a terrace, 
some 300 feet up, on which was situated a large village 
belonging to Indians of the Partamona tribe. Our 
Owenteik Macusis knew nothing of the onward path 
to the Potaro, and, having accomplished their mission, 
left us to return to their homes. After a few hours’ 
delay the head man of the place arrived, accompanied 


Cuar. VIII. ATHLETIC SPORT. 195 


by fourteen men and boys, all armed with bows and 
arrows, the last man of the band carrying a large 
deer on his shoulders, which they had killed. He 
sent to another village near by for men, a party of 
whom soon arrived, headed by their chief, an old 
man with one eye. They said they would take us on 
to the Potaro, but would not start till the following 
day. 

In the afternoon the youths and boys of the place 
played a game over which they made a good deal of 
noise and seemingly enjoyed. They formed a line 
across one of the wide approaches to the village, while 
one of the strongest of the number made an effort to 
break through, by rushing against it at his greatest 
speed. Any man who approached the village had 
likewise to break through before he could enter the 
place. 

Message after message had to be sent on the follow- 
ing morning to our new guides before they eventually 
came, and that was not until they had finished all the 
paiworie in their place. Eight came instead of four, 
and the one-eyed head man would not carry a load ,as 
it was necessary to devote all his attention to a gun he 
carried, the hammer of which would not catch at either 
half or full cock. He fired it by hauling back the 
hammer with his thumb and keeping it in that position 
till he got aim, when he would lift his thumb, setting 
free the hammer, and off it went, seldom missing the 
object aimed at. 

-= From the village we continued in a northerly direc- 

tion along the sandstone terrace, which, beyond the 

end of the wooded mountain to the west, widened out 
o 2 


196 UP THE BURRO-BURRO, ETC. Cmar. VIII. 


westward into an extensive open table-land. Upon 
this was but little soil, the coarse white sandstone 
forming an almost even pavement, on which grew 
shrubs, mosses, and some curious flowering plants; of 
which a ground orchid with Cattleya-like blossoms was 
the most beautiful. Traversing this along the edge of 
the terrace we entered the forest again, and crossed a 
small dry circular pond having its bottom all grass- 
covered. It was situated in a small clearing some 
fifty yards in diameter, and had evidently been artifi- 
cially made. In the centre of this the guide paused, 
and directed my attention to numbers of small circular 
pits, that had been dug all over the bottom of the 
pond. These averaged from three to six feet in 
diameter, and from six to eight feet in depth. Lean- 
ing against a tree on one side was a bundle of sticks 
with slightly curved ends. Selecting one, the guide 
stooped down, and stirring up a large mass of gela- 
tinous froth in the bottom of a pit, dislodged a small 
flesh-coloured frog, which he tapped on the head. In 
the grass around the pits’ mouths were also patches of 
this froth containing little white frogs’ eggs, about half 
the size of peas. These frogs are considered great 
delicacies by the Indians, and the pits are dug to 
entrap them when they come to the spot to deposit 
their eggs. 

We passed through one village and came to a second, 
where we had to stop for the night. The people of 
both villages had collected here to have a dance and 
drink, and one Indian youth led off the affair, strutting 
about in a large tiger skin, with bundles of bird skins 
attached to it for tassels. The ends of his toes were 
turned up off the ground to keep them from touching 


Cuar. VIII. KIBIHIES. 197 


anything, for being crammed full of jiggers, contact 
with a foreign substance would be painful. I was 
offered a calabash of a pink drink made from the sweet 
potato (Batatas edulis), called casserie ; being thirsty, 
and at the same time not thinking that any chewed-up 
cassava had been put in it, I drank it, finding it cool 
and of a pleasantly sour taste. I afterwards wit- 
nessed the manufacture of the same drink, and to my 
horror found that a certain amount of chewed-up 
cassava was one of its ingredients. 

After leaving the village the path led up a steep 
mountain side to a level of 2800 feet, and then tra- 
versed an almost level country in a northerly direction. 

As we went along the guide shot two trumpet-birds 
and a maam, which were very acceptable, my food 
from leaving Surama having consisted of salt codfish 
and rice for every meal. 

The dogs ran down and killed a Kibihie or Coati 
(Nasua solitaria), which had a gray muzzle and ringed 
tail, covered with short hair. Soon after that we met 
a whole drove of a larger kind of kibihies (Nasua 
socialis), with bushy tails and no gray hairs about 
their muzzles, which were rooting amongst the dead 
leaves for worms and seeds. One fellow was passing 
close by when it saw me, and being seized with a panic, 
ran swiftly up a tree stem to a height of some fifteen 
feet, and then as suddenly jumped down, coming 
heavily on the ground. It did not seem much hurt, 
but made off at a good pace. 

In the afternoon we came to the edge of the moun- 
tain facing north, its crest being just 3000 feet above 
the sea level. Descending on a long and narrow spur 
we came to a river in the valley below, called the 


198 UP THE BURRO-BURRO, ETC. Cuar. VII, 


Emoy, and there camped, after a most einen walk 
of ten hours’ duration. 

In many places on the path we had to step over 
arrangements of little sharpened sticks, placed loosely 
together in a variety of ways. These, the guide said, 
were put by the Indians using this path for the pur- 
pose of keeping the pumas and jaguars from traversing 
it. These sticks were not meant to injure the animals, 
in fact they were too loosely stuck up for that, but 
were merely intended by their artificial appearance to 
scare off the tigers. They seemed to be of little 
service, for in many places small trees on the edge of 
the pathway had been bitten and scratched, and the 
soil scraped up by jaguars. 

From where we struck the Emoy river one day’s 
walk brought us to a rolling savanna, in the centre of 
which, near a grove of Parapee palms (Guilielma 
speciosa) stood a village, called Taiepong. 

In coming along I saw two of the Indians walking 
a little in advance suddenly take to their heels and 
flee precipitately, waving their arms wildly about. 
Whilst wondering what on earth possessed them to act 
in that insane manner, I passed on to the place from 
which they broke into a run, and then heard a buzzing 
sound round my head, followed by sting after sting on 
face and neck from a swarm of wasps. Maddened by 
pain, I likewise fled, and did not stop until I had out- 
run the last of my tormentors. My ruffled feelings 
did not become soothed until the last twinge of pain 
had died out of my swollen wounds, about an hour 
afterwards. 

Taiepong savanna is covered with a remarkably 


Caar. VIII. TAIEPONG. 199 


coarse, Sparse grass; its soil is poor in the extreme, 
the underlying rocks of coarse sandstone and conglo- 
merate showing at the surface every here and there. 
Patches of coarse fern grew in many places, together 
with pretty ground orchids of three kinds; two with 
purple flowers, and the third with a white flower having 
a yellow centre. It is surrounded by tree-clothed 
mountains, and away to the west some forty miles off 
the faint blue outlines of high table-topped mountains 
can be seen, one of which, called Salieng, has a sharp 
peak at its northern end. 

When we arrived at the village the only inhabitants 
to be seen were two or three women; but after a time 
more individuals came dropping in, with their ham- 
mocks over their shoulders, looking as unconcerned as 
possible, though a short time before they had been so 
alarmed as to flee at our approach. 

The village was a good-sized one, but most of the 
people had gone away to the Potaro to attend a drink- 
ing bout. Our first inquiries were whether they had 
woodskins on the Potaro, which was only half a day’s 
walk off to the northward, and the reply we received, 
that they had none, was very disappointing. 

The Indians advised me to remain a few days at this 
place, whilst they sent on to the village to the north- 
west, called Enapowou, on the borders of the Potaro, 
to see whether they could get woodskins there, telling 
us that it would be of no use for us to go ourselves whilst 
the people were drinking. As there appeared to be a 
doubt whether we could get canoes, I consulted with 
my men on the advisability of making rafts of light 
wood, and so floating down on them with the stream. 


200 UP THE BURRO-BURRO, ETC. Cuar. VIII. 


We all agreed to adopt this plan if we could not get 
woodskins, feeling that it was better to go down stream 
on pumpwood rafts, than to retrace our steps to the 
Rupunupi. 

Game in this part was scarce, and as all our salt 
fish had been consumed, we had to subsist on rice and 
cassava, and any little dried meat that could be pur- 
chased from the Indians. Some dry, tasteless, mildewed 
tapir meat, which we procured, was looked upon as a 
treat, and eaten with gusto. 

Taiepong village was composed of seven large and 
four small houses, together with a long bark-sided 
building, twenty-eight yards in length. This building 
had one door, outside of which were two high posts with 
large pieces of flattened wood fastened to them, after 
the manner of sign-posts. On these boards were burnt 
in, some curious characters, like letters, resembling in 
form the tattoo marks which ornament the faces of 
Ackawoise Indians. One Indian told my interpreter 
that the signs on one board meant “ Come,” and those 
on the other “ Go”; while another said that they were 
put up the previous year “ to bring the sun,” the rainy 
season having lasted too long. They said this building 
is an imitation of a church, and that an Indian acts as 
preacher in it, his gospel being that all Indians who 
come and listen to him will in time turn white and 
have clothes, and the village will become a large and 
flourishing place like Georgetown, the capital of British 
Guiana. In this would-be church there were no seats 
or even logs to sit upon, but a huge paiworie coriara 
(trough) clearly indicated that it is chiefly used as a 
dancing house in times of drinking bouts. 


Car. VIL. ECCENTRIC INDIVIDUAL. 201 


On the first day of our stay at the village a most 
eccentric individual arrived from a neighbouring place, 
accompanied by his wife, who was at least ten years 
older than her husband. They seemed very fond of 
each other, and she watched his every movement with 
pride and delight. Presenting me with three cakes of 
cassava, he commenced a long harangue, which he 
flattered himself was English, but which was really no 
language under the sun. Not a word of it could any- 
one except his wife understand. He remained a whole 
day there, never uttering a word except in the strange 
tongue he had himself invented, even when addressing 
his own tribe. ; 

During our stay at ‘Taiepong the nights and fore- 
noons were rainy, but the afternoons were fine, when I 
made excursions over the savanna in the neighbour- 
hood. 

The natives grew quantities of fine pineapples 
(Ananassa sativa), from which they manufactured an 
intoxicating drink, by grating the fruit on a wooden 
sieve, and allowing the juice to remain until fermenta- 
tion set in. They also prepared a black substance, by 
burning an aquatic plant, called houyah, then mixing 
the ash with burnt cassava bread and drying it in 
shapes in the sun. This they put in their paiworie, 
making it the colour of ink, and giving it a most 
revolting appearance. | 

The man sent on about woodskins returned in due 
course with the glad tidings that we could obtain as 
many canoes as necessary, and brought another man 
with him, who spoke English, and offered his services 
as guide. 


202 UP THE BURRBO-BURRO, ETC. Caap. VIII. 


The old one-eyed guide, who had brought us to the 
place, would go no farther, being afraid of the vengeance 
of a man of the village to which we were going, whose 
brother, wife, and two children he had murdered in 
cold blood a few years before. He one day very 
coolly took the lock off a gun he found hanging in one 
of the housé¢s, got my servant to take the necessary 
parts from it, and patch up the defective lock of his 
gun. When this was done, the catches for half and 
full cock worked splendidly, but not fitting exactly, 
the hammer, when at full cock, used to lie almost over 
on its back. 

After a forced stay of three days at the village we 
went on through the miserably wet forest, in pouring 
rain, to a small place on the edge of the Potaro river, 
in a direction west-north-west from Taiepong. From 
thence we went up river to a creek, called Enapowon, 
where a vast number of Indians were still engaged in 
drinking black paiworie. There we obtained four 
small canoes and one corial, after a day’s delay, and 
with four Indians to bring back the canoes, started on 
our journey down the black and swollen waters of the 
Potaro. | 

When on the point of leaving, a woman stepped 
forward to an old Indian in one of our canoes and held 
up her head. He tapped her forehead with his fingers, 
muttered a few words, and then blew on her temple. 
This was done to charm away a pain in the head, the 
old fellow being a peaiman, and capable of effecting 
such cures. On our arrival at villages I have some- 
times seen a woman carry her infant round to one 
after another of the Indians of my party, each man 


Cuap. VIII. DISCOVER KAIETEUR FALL. 203 


as she passed stooping down and blowing gently on 
the face of the child. 

Descending the river rapidly all day we came 
within sound of the roar of a large fall, and there 
camped under some old banaboos. Our guide said 
that this fall was very high—“ as high as the trees,” 
and we should have to walk past it and down the 
mountain beyond. We went but a few hundred 
yards in our canoes next day (April 24th), when we 
came to the end of a path leading across a small 
savanna past the fall, the roar of which we could 
plainly hear. The surface of the savanna was com- 
posed of bare patches of sandstone and conglomerate, 
the pebbles from the disintegration of which formed 
little patches of gravel here and there. Shrubs, 
mosses, and pretty flowering plants grew over it in 
abundance. | 

When we came to the northern end of the savanna 
I observed that heavy masses of vapour were drifting 
before the north-east wind, making the trees, grass, 
and shrubs on our right dripping wet. This came 
from the great fall to which we were in close prox- 
imity, but which was hidden from view by a grove of 
trees. Making a detour to the right through this 
grove we came out on the flat rocks at the head of the 
great fall, and walking to the edge of the precipice, 
down which the water was precipitated, I gazed with 
wonder and delight at the singular and magnificent 
sight that lay before me. Not being prepared for 
anything so grand and startling I could not at first 
believe my eyes, but felt that it was all a dream. 

There, however, was the dark, silent flow of water 


204 UP THE BURRO-BURRO, ETC. Cuar. VIIL 


down which we had travelled, passing: slowly but 
surely to the brink of a great precipice, and breaking 
into ripples as it approached its doom. Then curving 
over the edge in a smooth mass of a brownish tinge, 
changing into snow-white fleecy foam, it was pre- 
cipitated downwards into a black seething cauldron 
hundreds of feet below. Of how many hundreds I 
could not with certainty make out; but, judging from 
the appearance of the river below the basin, I thought 
it must be near upon 900 feet. I was prepared to 
meet with great falls on our way down, knowing from 
our altitude by aneroid that we were some 1200 feet 
above the sea on the Upper Potaro, and that the 
mouth of the river was at a level of only sixty-five feet, 
but nothing of so grand and extraordinary a nature as 
this ever entered my mind for a moment. Feelings 
of delight for a time overcame me, and I astonished 
one of my men by grasping his arm and pointing out 
to him the glories of the scene. In fact, both myself 
and my usually stolid men were all in a state of great 
excitement. 

Standing on the edge of the water near the brink I 
looked down a long and comparatively straight valley, 
bounded by wooded bluffs and cliffs, with precipices 
of gray sandstone rock in places. In the bottom of 
this valley curves of the river, in its onward course 
from the foot of the fall, could be seen, the distant 
ones resembling little streaks of silver. Beyond 
the mouth of the valley lay a great wooded plain, 
stretching away to the horizon northward. 

The Potaro was swollen at the time by the late rains, 
and where it poured over the curved edge of the fall it 


Cuap. VIII. KAIETEOR. 205 


was over a hundred yards in width, containing a vast 
volume of water, which, as it reached the basin below, 
sent up great creamy spurts of foam with a steady 
thundering roar. A dense mass of mist rose from the 
western side of the foot of the fall, which, whirling 
upwards above the top, was caught by the wind, and 
blown like a cloud-scud over the savanna, while a 
smaller one rose from the eastern edge. The preci- 
pices on either side curving round from the fall to the 
sides of the valley were of gray and reddish colours, 
with patches of shrubs and mosses clinging here and 
there to their faces. On the western side a mass of 
talus, composed of enormous rocks, sloped to the 
basin’s edge, almost meeting with a smaller similar 
mass in front of the basin, coming from the eastern 
side. The outlet, therefore, from the basin appeared 
to be constricted, but vast quantities of water find 
their way in channels beneath these confused heaps of 
rock. 

It was with difficulty that I could stand near the 
precipice edge, a little to one side of the fall, and 
gaze down past the rush of water to the foot, the 
downward movement of the white mass producing a 
sensation of giddiness. One could see the dim out- 
lines of the entrance to a great cave behind the water, . 
in and out of which through mist and spray a number 
of large black swallows wheeled and circled. 

After spending an hour and a half at the head of 
the Kaieteur—the Indian name of this fall—I was 
obliged to tear myself away, the stern necessities of 
life forcing me to depart. After the old dried tapir 
flesh we procured at Taiepong, the only article of 


206 UP THE BURRO-BURRO, ETC. Cuar. VIII. 


meat diet of which I partook was a small duraquara, 
a bird not as large as a partridge. We had sufficient 
hard cassava bread to eat, and kept nibbling little bits 
when hungry, without having any regular meals; but 
nature revolted at such poor treatment, and craved for 
fish or flesh food of any sort. It was owing to this 
scarcity of food for my men and myself that I was 
compelled to push on. 

Resuming our line of march on the little savanna, 
we entered the forest to the north, and descending the 
steep mountain side arrived at the edge of the lower 
Potaro, at the foot of the last cataract which inter- 
venes between that spot and the basin of the great 
fall. There the river was flowing on steadily and 
peacefully towards the sea, as if its level course had 
never been broken. By aneroid the difference of level 
between this and the top of the fall was a thousand 
feet. Allowing a hundred feet for the difference of 
level between this and the basin, I roughly estimated 
the height of the fall at nine hundred feet. 

The path ended there, but a track along the same 
bank led on to a village not far off, to which our 
Indian guides and four of my men proceeded, in order 
to procure woodskins to carry us down river. The 
reason they gave why we could not all accompany 
them was, that, the river being swollen, our things 
could not be transported over some branch creeks. 

We spent the night at the landing, and next day 
went down to the Partamona village, at which the 
woodskins had been procured. On the way down, 
from two reaches of the river, I obtained glimpses of 
the upper half of the great fall, but the early morning 


Cuar. VIII. DESCEND POTARO. 207 


fog clinging to the surrounding mountains partially 
obscured it. : 

At the village I found some forty Indians assem- 
bled, belonging to this and other villages, all on the 
point of starting for the Demerara. It was, therefore, 
with great difficulty that I was able to procure just 
enough provisions to last my party for our journey 
down to Tumatamari. Thus I was prevented from 
visiting the foot of the fall. I knew, however, that I 
could easily return on a future occasion, provided with 
instruments for measuring the full height of the fall, 
and therefore made light of my disappointment. We 
fortanately got some fowls, which were soon cooked 
and shared amongst all hands. My servant untied his 
cooking pack on the occasion for the first time for 
many days, and found my tinned-iron plates, knives 
and forks, all stuck together in a mass of rust. 

Amongst the Partamonas at the village were some 
men who wore curious long thin beards, arranged in a 
line on their chins like the teeth of a comb. This 
appeared strange, for Indians, as a rule, pluck out any 
hairs that grow on their faces. 

With great difficulty, I persuaded some men to sell 
me two woodskins, in which we started down river, 
accompanied by two guides in a third canoe. After 
proceeding a short distance down stream, we came to 
a cataract, called Waratu, where we had to transport 
our canoes over a short portage, and at noon came to 
another set of large cataracts, called Amatu, situated 
across the river at the entrance to the large valley. 
The canoes had again to be portaged for a considerable 
distance, and were then on the river where it wound 


208 UP THE BURRO-BURRO, ETC. Cuar. VIL 


about along the great undulating forest-covered plain, 
on its way to join the Essequebo. Passing down the 
great valley from the village to Armatu, some magni- 
ficent views of the great sandstone precipices on the 
valleys’ sides were obtained, and many beautiful 
cascades, and streams swollen by the recent rains, fell 
over their sides. On the following day, we had to 
carry the canoes overland for a quarter of a mile, so 
as to evade a set of cataracts, called Pacutout, and 
again at another set we had to carry everything over 
a portage of over a mile in length. 

On our third day from the Partamona village we 
passed the mouth of a large river on the north, called 
the Curiebrong, and by 10 a.m. came in sight of 
Tumatamari cataract, which we recognized with most 
ecstatic feelings, knowing that our toilsome journey 
was nearly completed, and that an ample supply of food 
was near at hand. Landing at its top, we traversed the 
portage path on the south bank to its foot, and there 
had the pleasure of seeing our boats made fast at a 
fine white sand beach, on the north shore, upon which 
was a snug camp containing their crews. Shouting 
lustily and firing a gun, we attracted the attention of 
the latter, who, coming over, soon ferried us across to 
camp, where we congratulated each other on the 
successful completion of the journeys assigned to each 
party. Being travel-stained and weather-beaten, we 
spent a day on the sand-beach to rest, and leaving 
Tumatamari on April 30th, arrived at the Penal 
Settlement on the morning of May the 3rd. 7 


Cuar. IX. KAIETEUR FALL. 209 


CHAPTER IX. 
KAIETEUR FALL. 


Difficulties of Ascent of Potaro River — Walk from Landing to Foot of 
Kaieteur — Take Measurements of Height of Fall — Legend of Origin 
of Name of the Fall — Swallows — Views — Visit Basin at Foot of 
Kaieteur — Difficulties of Approach — Descend Potaro — Arrive at 
Georgetown — Table of Measurements. 


Waern I arrived in Georgetown from my journey 
down the Potaro river, and described the magnitude 
and beauty of the great fall I had seen, the inhabi- 
tants of that city were filled with surprise, and many 
with doubt. They rightly considered it strange that 
such a gigantic natural wonder—one of the grandest 
falls in the world—existed in their colony and was un- 
known to them. They argued, that Indians from the 
Potaro had frequently worked on wood-cutting grants 
on the Demerara, and had never as much as hinted 
that such a thing existed. The Dutch, who owned the 
colony for so many years, had a military post at the 
mouth of the: Potaro, and they concluded naturally 
enough that the postholders had made excursions up 
that river; and if such a fall existed, would have 
fallen in with and recorded its existence. 

It was, therefore, the universal wish that something 
definite should be ascertained of its true measurements. 

His Excellency the Governor, Sir John Scott, 
commanded me to make preparations for a journey 

P 


210 KAIETEUR FALL. Cuar. IX. 


back to the Potaro, in order to make a thorough 
examination of this fall and ascertain its true altitude 
and magnitude. 

On the 28th of June, 1870, I left Georgetown for 
the Penal Settlement, in the contract steamer, accom- 
panied by Sir George Young, Mr. Mitchell, and Mr. 
King; the two former gentlemen being at the time on 
an official visit to the colony. It was well on in the 
rainy season at the time, and when we commenced our 
boat portion of the journey we found the Essequebo 
river in flood, its waters in some places above the first 
set of cataracts, having risen above its banks and 
spread far inland through the forest. In these places 
we had no ground to camp or cook meals upon, and 
had to sleep in our boats. The current ran strong, so 
that our progress was slow, and it took us eight and a 
half days to reach the cataract of Tumatamari. There 
we formed a camp at the end of the portage, on the 
south side, the sand-beach being all under water; and 
leaving our large boat, went on with a small bateau 
and two small corials. 

These we got up the cataract on the south side, over 
the rocks, the operation being facilitated by the height 
of the water of the Essequebo, which dammed back 
the water of the Potaro and raised it at the foot of 
the cataract, Bere bye lessening the height of that 
impediment. 

When we arrived at the foot of the Auritout set of 
cataracts, we portaged the stores in two places, for a 
distance of half a mile in the first instance, and 594 
yards in the second; but hauled the boats up in the 
water. To pass Pacutout cataracts we had to take 


Cuar. IX. "~~ ASCEND POTARO. 211 


our boats up a: bill to the level of fifty feet above the 
river, and along the portage on rollers, which we cut 
‘and placed there for a distance of 1078 yards. This 
fatiguing operation, including the carriage of the stores, 
called into requisition the whole strength of the party, 
consisting of thirteen men and ourselves, and took us 
nine hours in its performance. 

It took us the whole of one afternoon to pass the 
Amutu cataracts by the portage, 440 yards in length. 
Leaving its head early, on the morning of our fourth 
day after leaving Tumatamari, we entered the end of 
the great Kaieteur valley, and for the remainder of our 
water journey had high mountains on either hand. 

With the exception of the short portage for stores 
at Waratu cataract, we had smooth water, and not a 
very strong current to contend with, and passed the 
Partamona village early in the forenoon of the day on 
which we entered the valley, arriving at the landing 
at the foot of Tukuie cataract, three miles below 
Kaieteur fall, at 2.30 p.m. on the same day. 

I have given the above outline of the voyage up the 
Potaro to show the difficulties we encountered in 
passing its cataracts. These cataracts, no doubt, had 
hitherto proved effectual bars to the ascent of this 
river by colonists, and had jealously guarded the Great 
Fall from the sight of all but the natives themselves. 

The first fine view obtained is on approaching 
Armatu, where along a vista of river one can see that 
high foaming cataract, backed by dark gloomy-looking 
mountains, the uniformity of which is broken by deep 
gorges and gaps. Upon entering the great valley the 
rain began to fall heavily, producing some beautiful 

P 2 


212 KAIETEUR FALL Cuar. IX. 


cascades down the sides of some of the great precipices 
of sandstone, and continued until we reached Waratu, 
when, as if by magic, the rain clouds broke away, and 
the sun came out brightly, melting the mists and fogs 
clinging to the mountain sides. Then we obtained our 
first view of the upper portion of the Kaieteur, far off, 
at the head of the valley, pouring its foaming water 
over the precipice edge into the depths below. 

At 8 a.m. on the morning following our arrival at 
the landing, we started off to visit the foot of the great 
fall, accompanied by a party of Indians, two of whom 
we had engaged at the village as guides. We followed 
the well-beaten path which leads to the head of the fall 
for a few hundred yards, and then struck off through 
the forest to the river, coming out on its western side 
at Orinedouk cataract. There we followed the river's 
edge for a short distance, sometimes jumping from rock 
to rock, or wading in shallows along its bank, until 
large blocks of sandstone on its sides prevented our 
further advance. Then we took to the forest, clamber- 
ing sometimes up and down over confused heaps of 
enormous rock masses, at others going through natural 
subways; having, in every instance, to cut through 
the tangled mass of vines, bush-ropes, shrubs, and 
undergrowth that pervaded the whole place. The 
footing was dangerous and difficult, and at times we 
came to a wall of rock which prevented any farther 
advance, when a new line of route had to be sought. 
In two hours and three-quarters after leaving our 
camp, we came out again on the river’s edge, on a 
spread of large flat rocks directly facing the fall, at 
about a quarter of a mile from its foot. Here the 


Cuar. IX. WALK TO FOOT. 213 


regular forest ended, and all beyond on either side of 
the river was a confused mass of rocks, coming to the 
water's edge, and covered with a matting of low plants, 
shrubs, and mosses. There we halted, as it was abso- 
lately necessary to choose this as the lower barometer 
station, there being no chance of getting one farther 
on. Before us thundered the fall, its contact with the 
water of the basin being a confused scene of fleecy 
masses of tossing waters, spurting high in the air in 
front of the downpour, and giving birth to mist clouds 
which rose continuously upwards, and over the preci- 
pice on the right. 

Owing to some misunderstanding at starting, our 
men with the provisions of the party took the path up 
the mountain, and came out at the head of the fall a 
short time after we reached its foot. They returned 
and rejoined us at 5 P.M. 

After comparing the Standard and Gay-Lussac 
barometers together, I attempted to sketch the fall, 
whilst Sir G&G. Young and Mr. Mitchell performed the 
difficult feat of swimming across the river, and visiting 
the edge of the basin on the eastern side. 

On their return, after arranging about the upper 
barometer station, and a code of signals for simultaneous 
observations to be taken on the following day, they set 
off on their return to the landing, taking the standard 
barometer with them. All but three of our Indians 
went back with them, not liking to spend the night in 
such a mysterious place. 

Mr. King and I had poles meres up and lashed 
together, under a large rock, which formed a sort of 
cave, where we slung our hammocks for the night. A 


214 KAIETEUR FALL. Cuar. IX. 


little antechamber, with a natural stone seat along one 
side, formed what my servant called the “kitchen,” 
wherein we spent the evening chatting over the fire. I 
got my interpreter to question our Indian friends about 
the meaning of the word Kaieteur, and learned that, 
rendered into English, it stands for “ Old Man Fall.” 
The way in which it acquired this hame was from a 
tragedy enacted on the spot, in connection with the 
fall, at a time of Indian history, about which there is 
no certainty of date, and which may be called the 
traditionary period. 

This is the tale as related to me: “Once upon a 
time,” there was a large village above the fall, situated 
on the little savanna, amongst the inhabitants of 
which was an old Indian, who had arrived at that 
period of human existence, when his life had become a 
burden to himself and a trouble to his relatives. 
Amongst other duties, there devolved upon his near 
relations the tedious one of extracting the jiggers 
from his toes, which there accumulated day by day. 
These duties becoming irksome at last, it was arranged 
that the old man should be assisted on his way to his 
long home, that spirit land lying two days’ journey 
beyond the setting sun. He was accordingly trans- 
ferred, with his pegall of worldly goods, from his house 
to a woodskin on the river above the head of the great 
fall, and launched forth upon the stream. ‘The silent 
flood bore him to its brink, where the rushing waters 
received him in their deadly grasp, bearing his en- 
feebled body down to its watery grave in the basin 
below. Not long after, strange to relate, his woodskin 
appeared in the form of a pointed rock, which to. this 


| 


Cuar: IX. NIGHT AT FALL 215 


day is seen not far from our lower barometer station ; 
while on the sloping mass of talus to the west of the 
basin, a huge square rock is said to be his petrified ` 
pegall or canister. Thus has the fall been named 
Kaieteur, in memory of the victim of this tragic event. 

Becoming sleepy after our day’s fatigues, Mr. King 
and I withdrew from the kitchen and sought our ham- 
mocks. Lying in a position facing the fall, I had only 
to open my eyes, and there was its grim, white, 
ghostly outlines before me, rendered somewhat indis- 
tinct in the deep gloom of night. Unprotected from 
the keen moisture-laden night wind of this misty 
place, which swept with sighing sounds through our 
rocky house, I spent a most uncomfortable night, 
dozing off and waking up at intervals. Each time I 
woke my eyes sought the fall, and my ears caught the 
sound of its ceaseless roar. 

On one of these occasions of wakefulness I could 
see that the darkness was not so intense, but that a 
subdued light penetrated even into our valley of 
shadows, and I knew that the moon must have risen 
above the eastern horizon. By this accession of light 
I could make out the brink of the fall against the sky; 
and as I gazed upon it two bright stars rose slowly 
beyond, looking as if they had emerged from the water 
itself. Then the first rays of the moon, as it rose above 
the mountain in the east, shed a silvery light across 
the fall’s crest, which had a most beautiful and charm- 
ing effect, growing more and more so as the moon rose 
higher, and lit up a portion of the descending fleecy 
column. 

Just at daybreak I left my hammock and joined the 


216 KAIETEUR FALL. Cmar. IX 


unclothed Indians, who were crouching over a small 
fire out on the flat rocks, where they had spent the 
night, and were shivering with cold. They, at any 
rate, had had enough of Kaieteur in that one night, and 
saw nothing romantic or poetical in the situation. 

About 8.45 a.m. we observed the arrival of Mr. 
Mitchell, and some of our men at the top of the 
western edge of the fall, and exchanged signals with 
regard to five simultaneous barometrical observations, 
taken at long intervals. 

During the day the sun’s rays, shining on the mist, 
produced a most beautiful rainbow, reaching from the 
top to the foot of the fall, which, moving slowly out- 
wards with the mist, faded gradually away, while with 
each accession of mist a new one was formed. 

Late in the afternoon we returned to the landing 
camp, and rejoined Sir G. Young and Mr. Mitchell, who 
had arrived from the top of the fall. There we spent 
our last evening together, and had our usual game of 
whist, with a tin canister in licu of a card table. On 
the following morning Sir G. Young and Mr. Mitchell, 
whose duties compelled them to return to Georgetown, 
bade Mr. King and myself adieu, and started in the 
two small corials, manned by six men, accompanied by 
two Indians, in a woodskin, on their homeward-bound 
journey. Mr. King and I then ascended the mountain 
to the head of the fall, and, without hurrying ourselves, 
arrived at that spot in two hours and three-quarters 
after leaving the landing. 

We formed a camp in the low bush, on the edge of 
the river, about fifty yards above the edge of the fall, 
and conducted a series of measurements of the width, 


Cuar. IX. SWALLOWS. 217 


depth, velocity of the river, &c. Finding an old 
canoe in a creek near by, we made paddles for it, and 
on my first attempt to measure the width of the river, 
250 yards above the fall, I nearly came to grief. I 
was accompanied by Ben, who paddled in the bow, 
while I paddled and steered. In taking out the line, 
we had got half-way across the river, when it caught 
on rocks at the bottom and checked our way, so that 
the current swept us rapidly down. Casting off the 
end of the line, we gave way with all our might, and 
reached the opposite side just as we were in the grasp 
of the first broken water, where it commences to gain 
considerable velocity. Hauling our canoe up along 
the bank by the branches of trees out of the strong 
current, we went back and made a second and suc- 
cessful attempt. 

On both evenings of our stay at the fall’s head we 
watched with great interest the ‘“‘swallows’ homeward ” 
flight to their roosting places in the great cave behind 
the fall. Late on each afternoon, these birds were 
observed coming in large flocks from all quarters of 
the compass towards our vicinity, and there wheeling 
round in great circles at different altitudes. Gradually 
one flock amalgamated with another, till at last near 
sundown they had gathered into two or three immense 
bodies, which kept wheeling round in a compact mass, 
about one hundred yards above us. On asking Mr. 
King how he would describe their numbers, he replied 
that be thought the words “ myriads of millions” 
would about do it. 

Whilst we were wondering how the birds proposed 
to get into the cave from their awkwardly chosen 


218 KAIETEUR FALL. Cuar. IX. 


position above it—feeling almost sure that they would 
have to get gradually into the valley in front, and 
then fly straight towards their roosts—the question 
was solved by a portion of the mass swooping down, 
with extraordinary velocity, to the edge of the fall, 
seemingly close to the face of the column of water, and 
then being lost to our view. The rushing sound of 
their wings in their downward flight was very strange, 
and produced the feeling that birds of ill-omen were 
about. Approaching the edge of the precipice, we 
waited to see the next lot go down, so as to observe 
how they managed to get behind the water. We had 
not to wait long before down dropped a cloud of them 
over the edge, past the face of the fall, for about one 
hundred feet; then, with the rapidity of lightning, 
they changed their downward course with a quick turn 
to one at right angles, and thus shot through the mist 
on either side into the gloomy cave. Their motions 
were so rapid that we could hardly make out how they 
were executed. It appeared to me that, as they 
swooped down, their wings were but half spread, and 
their heads downwards, but after passing the edge they 
turned their bodies in a horizontal position, descending 
by gravity alone until they arrived at the required 
level, when they again made use of their wings and 
flew off at right angles into the cave. Just before dusk 
the greater portion descended in a continuous stream 
for a considerable time, but small flocks and single 
birds kept arriving until it was quite dark. When a 
single bird shot down, its velocity was so great that it 
appeared to form a short continuous black line against 
the sky. 


Cuap. IX. LABARIA. 219 


These birds (Acanthylis collaris), called Noroquah 
by the Macusis, belong to the Hirundinide. They are 
of a blackish colour, with a white ring round the neck, 
widening into a patch under the throat. 

The view down the valley was always beautiful ; 
but in the late afternoon, when the sun was sinking 
behind the mountains on the west, great shadows were 
cast across it, which hada grand effect. The colouring 
was then very beautiful, the opening of the valley being 
lit up by the golden reflection of the sky over the great 
plain beyond. 

I was able, with somé degree of pride, to point out 
to Mr. King the mountains far away to the south and 
south-east, in the vicinity of Taiepong, over which I 
had come on my journey from Surama to the upper 
waters of the Potaro. 

On the afternoon of our third day’s stay at the head 
of the fall, we descended the mountain in a terrific 
downpour of rain to our landing camp; and on the 
following day I again visited the foot of the fall, 
accompanied by five men and an Indian, in order to 
obtain the difference in level between the lower baro- 
meter station and the edge of the basin. 

On the way thither, the Indian guide, in the act of 
stepping over some rocks, nearly put his foot on a 
large labaria, one of the most poisonous snakes of 
Guiana. It was coiled up in the angle between a flat 
rock and another in a vertical position. The Indian 
immediately cut a sapling, and using it like a spear in 
both hands, aimed at the animal’s head ; but, missing 
it, pinned the creature against the angle of the rock. 
Its head and some six inches of body being at liberty, 


220 KAIETEUR FALL. Cuar. IX. 


with its huge mouth wide open, it drew back, and 
striking with great force, seized the stick in its mouth, 
repeating the process four or five times in succession. 
The Indian coolly kept it pinned down, and, drawing 
his knife from his waist cord, hacked a piece off the 
top of the sapling. This he took in his right hand, 
and, stooping down, without easing the pressure on the 
snake, quietly knocked it on the head. 

When we reached the lower barometer station the 
chief difficulties of our walk began. Bad as was the 
part between the landing and the station, it was 
nothing compared with that on to the basin. The whole 
distance in a straight line is not more than 350 yards, 
yet, with climbing over and creeping under enormous 
boulders, we must have gone almost double that dis- 
tance. For the first 250 yards we had to cut our way 
through the shrubs and agaves, interlaced with tangled 
vines; and frequently came to walls of rock, which 
stopped our farther progress, forcing us at times to 
return and seek another route. Passing the agave 
growth, as we approached the basin, the rocks were 
covered with a coarse moss, which grew upon the 
soft, saturated earth, covering their flat portions. 
On this we all slipped, slid, and tumbled on our 
onward course, and had a precarious footing when 
jumping across deep fissures. Long before we got to 
the basin’s edge I was almost covered with mud 
and moss from head to foot, and just before reaching 
the basin was thoroughly soaked with moisture 
produced by the mist and rain coming out from the 
fall. 

On the sides and crannies of the moss-grown rocks 


Cuar. IX. THE BASIN. 221 


were pretty little plants with scarlet bell-shaped 
flowers, and orchids with greenish sack-shaped flowers. 

Some fifty yards in front of the edge of the basin we 
entered a region pervaded by fine mist, which, acted 
upon by the sun’s rays, produced small dazzling rain- 
bows in front of our faces, and almost prevented our 
Seeing the way in passing over the deep fissures. Here 
we felt the influence of the strong breeze produced by 
the downward rush of water, and our guide, whose 
naked skin was thereby reduced in temperature, dread- 
ing that something supernatural would appear, refused 
to goany farther, but beat a hasty retreat. 

On arriving at the basin’s edge, in an hour and a 
half after leaving the barometer station, the mist and 
spray completely surrounded us, shutting out the sun- 
light, while a storm of rain assailed us, driven out by 
the hurricane produced by the falling water. It was 
only by getting shelter behind some rocks, and peering 
through gaps between them, that we could at all look 
upon the strange scene before us. The turmoil in the 
basin was wonderful to behold, the surface of the 
water being lashed into great irregular waves, whose 
crests were blown into spray as they drove outwards 
in an irregular semicircle, and dashed against the rocks 
in front. Where the descending column of water met 
that in the basin, the contact was rendered indistinct 
by the mist, but great fleecy masses of foam spurted 
upwards, many yards in height, in clouds mixed with 
spray and mist. I fancied that I stood on a rock- 
bound lee coast, exposed to a raging storm of wind 
and-rain, whilst the sullen roar that assailed my ears 
added to the awful wildness of the strife of the 


222 KAIETEUR FALL. Crip: 


elements. I was so carried away by this idea, that, on 
returning, and getting beyond the mist-covered portion, 
I was astonished to find that the outside world was 
calm, and the sun was shining brightly overhead. 

In returning, I observed that wherever we passed 
over deep fissures between the rocks we could see the 
water which escapes from the western portion of the 
basin rushing and gurgling beneath. By this means 
a large volume of the water of the basin finds its way 
to the river below, and thus the quantity flowing over 
the lip of the basin in front is reduced considerably in 
volume. | 

Between the smooth water below the fall and 
Tukuie, there is one long and steep cataract and three 
smaller ones, the latter bearing the names of Serika- 
baru, Orimedouk, and Osiedouk. At Serikabaru, on 
the eastern precipice, there is a large cascade, part of 
which is perpendicular for about two hundred feet ; 
and near it, on the precipice edge, is a large natural 
stone pillar, with two flat slabs on top, having a very 
artificial appearance. 

Late in the afternoon I got back to our camp at the 
landing, and on the following day Mr. King and I set 
out on our return journey. Jn running down War- 
ratu cataract the bowman, who was a new hand at the 
work, did not perform his part well, so that when the 
boat shot over and cleared the dip of the fall, it ran 
with great violence on some sunken rocks. The bow- 
man was standing up at the time, and on the boat 
coming to a sudden stop he shot fairly over the bow 
into the foaming water, but grasping the gunwale 
saved himself, and was soon on board again. We 


Cuar. IX. MEASUREMENTS. 223 


were all more or less knocked over by the blow, and 
remained a short time in our perilous position, the 
boat grinding heavily on the rocks. The men jumped 
overboard and kept her from broaching, otherwise she 
would have rolled over like a barrel. Becoming much 
lighter when the men got out, she floated off, and was 
guided at once to a sand-beach near by, gaining it 
just as she was half full of water, which came in 
through a huge rent in the thick greenheart plank 
forming the keel. The repairing of this rent caused 
a few hours’ delay, and was effected with oakum, 
tallow, canvas, and a board from a salt-fish box. 
This fortunately was our only mishap during the 
voyage. 

On arriving at the Curiebrong we made a short 
journey of two days up it, but finding our stores 
failing we turned about, and continued our homeward 
journey, reaching the Penal Settlement on the last 
day of July, and Georgetown on the first of August.
