CHAPTER X. 


TO AMAILAH FALL AND THE UPPEB ESSEQUEBO. 


Leave Georgetown — Ascent of the Curiebrong — Visit Amailah — 
Cocks of the Rock — Return to Essequebo — Funeral Dance at 
Apoterie — Adventures with Cayman — King William IV.’s Fall — 
Manarowah Fall — Make Portage — Shoot a Tapir — Habit of Jaguar 
— Sand-wasps — Arrive at Village of Taruma Indians — Maopityans 
— Reach farthest Point attainable — Haimara — Descend Essequebo 
— Dangerous Cataracts — Meet our Provision Boat — Reach Apoterie 
— Joined by Mr. Pollard. 


FURNISHED with two new boats, built under my own 
superintendence, I left Georgetown in the revenue 
cutter “ Petrel” on the 3rd of September, 1870, 
thirty-two days after my return from the Kaieteur, 
and arrived at the Penal Settlement on the following 
day. Leaving that place on the 7th, with a crew of 
eight men to each boat, I proceeded up the Essequebo 
and reached Tumatamari on the 14th. 

On the way up we spent a Sunday at Yucurit 
island, camping on the sand-beach at the island's 
point, near the house of a Brazilian Indian who re 
sided there. I was much amused with the good 
relations existing between three young tame birds at 
the house; they were fully feathered, but as their 
wings were clipped they could not fly—not that they 
seemed to care to do so, as they appeared to enjoy 
themselves thoroughly, trotting about in company, 
watching “every pot boil” in the house, and making 


Crap. X. THE THREE FRIENDS, 225 


little excursions to the edge of the forest, or the 
water’s edge. The leader of the band was a small 
fall-bird (Passerina gularis) with a black and white 
body and crimson head; the others were a black 
corn-bird (Scaphidurus ater) and a young “ Q’est-que- 
ce-dit” (Saurophagus sulphuratus). Wherever the 
fall-bird went its two companions trotted close behind, 
side by side, the big corn-bird now and then opening 
its mouth, in a babyish fashion, to try and induce the 
little leader to feed it. Whilst I was watching them 
they made one of their journeys to the river’s edge, 
and began hopping about in some low shrubs over- 
hanging the water, when, in an unlucky moment, the 
“Q'est-que-ce-dit ” lost.its hold and fell in, where it 
straggled round and round in its futile endeavours to 
get out again. Great was the sorrow and concern of 
the other two, which chirped and chattered in a most 
excited state, hopping about above its head on the lowest 
twigs, as if wishing to try and render it every assistance 
in their power, and in their excitement nearly falling 
into the water themselves. Going to the spot I fished 
the little fellow out with a stick, and no sooner was 
it on dry land than its companions were round it, 
evidently greatly relieved in mind at its escape from 
a watery grave. They all trotted off to the house 
together, the wet and draggled little bird marching 
between its two sympathising friends. 

The Potaro being low, we were able to haul one of 
our strong boats’ up the north side of Tumatamari 
without much difficulty, and then proceeded up the 
Curiebrong river, on which, after the usual fighting 
and buffeting with large cataracts for five and a half 

Q 


226 AMAILAH FALL AND UPPER ESSEQUEBO. Czar. X. 


days, we reached the farthest point on the river to 
which we could go. We were guided by two relays 
of Indians, the first set of two men, whom we obtained 
at the first village on the river just below the cata- 
racts, taking us half-way up; and after the loss of their 
woodskin, in a pool below a village called Partamong, 
handing us over to some men belonging to that place. 

The manner in which they lost their woodskin is 
worth recounting, and was a rather amusing sight, 
knowing as I did, that both men were excellent 
swimmers. They were crossing the river right in 
front of our boat at the foot.of a rapid, where a spit of 
sand formed a shallow above a deep pool, and in doing 
so, a tree branch sticking up from the sand beneath 
over which they passed, tore a hole in the canoe’s 
bottom. Seeing this, both paddled madly for the 
shore across the strong current, while the craft settled 
down rapidly under them. When about midway, 
down it went, and as it was going, each man seized 
his hammock and paddle in one hand, and the canoe’s 
gunwale in the other, making an attempt to keep it 
up and swim ashore with the whole lot, by the use of 
their legs only. As might be supposed, this was too 
much for human efforts, and they were dragged under 
water and disappeared. Letting go the woodskin, 
when they could hold on no longer, both popped up to 
the surface like corks, and laughing heartily at their 
mishap, swam ashore to the village. They saved their 
hammocks and paddles, however, but did not recover 
the woodskin, though my men assisted them in diving 
to look for it. 

When we reached our farthest point, where we were 


Cuar. X. AMAILAH FALL. ZZN 


stopped by enormous blocks of sandstone filling up 
the river’s bed, we were in the mouth of a valley with 
sandstone table-lands on either side—the western 
extension, in fact, of the Potaro table-land. Here we 
left the boat and followed a path up the mountain for 
a considerable distance; then went along its side to 
a small stream, and then finally straight up the moun- 
tain at a steep angle to the top. From this we had 
a grand bird’s-eye view of the great undulating forest- 
covered plain, which stretches away northward as far 
as the eye can reach. Resuming our walk along the 
level table-land we came out at the head of the 
Amailah fall, an examination of which was partly the 
object of this journey up the Curiebrong. I had been 
told by the Indians that it was a perpendicular fall as 
high as the Kaieteur, and was therefore much dis- 
appointed at finding that it was nothing of the sort. 
The water was very low at the time, so that its size 
was inconsiderable, and even when the river is at its 
highest point, it can only be 110 feet wide. It is true 
that it commences to fall from a level of equal altitude 
to, or slightly greater, than that of the Kaieteur, but 
then it only descends perpendicularly for the first 
144 feet, and then rushes down a long inclined plain, 
amongst immense blocks of sandstone and conglo- 
merate, for a couple of miles to the level of the river 
below. Forming a camp near the head of the fall, I 
descended the valley’s side to its foot. Underneath is 
a small cave into which I went, being first warned by 
the Indians of the risk I ran of being destroyed by a 
remarkably large eagle, that had its perpetual dwelling 
there. 
Q 2 


228 .AMAILAH FALL AND UPPER ESSEQUEBO. Crap. X. 


A lot of the Kaieteur swallows visited the place, 
and I succeeded in shooting one on the wing, which 
the bird-stuffer skinned and preserved for me. On 
the second day of our stay, I again visited the foot 
early in the morning, and saw a flock of the same 
swallows in their roosting place, clinging in a cluster 
to the rough pebbly surface of the coarse sandstone 
beds. Their position appeared to be a most trying 
and uncomfortable one, and the wonder is, how the 
birds can manage to sleep and cling there at the same 
time. Their feet are furnished with strong claws, and 
their legs are short and stout. Their tail feathers are 
also strong, having hard pointed tips to each shaft, 
which they spread with the points resting on inequa- 
lities of the rocks’ surface, thereby helping to support 
the weight of the body. The members of this flock 
were not early risers, for at nine o'clock in the 
morning they were still roosting, until a shot from the 
bird-stuffer's gun brought three to the ground and put 
the rest to flight. 

“Cocks of the Rock” (Rupicola Cayanna) were 
numerous in the surrounding thickets, where their 
sharp disagreeable cry was frequently heard. They 
are so restless, jumping and flying from bough to 
bough and tree to tree, that Paulie, who was trying to 
shoot them, started five, but only succeeded in ob- 
taining one specimen. He found a dancing place of 
these birds in a thicket, the ground being beaten down 
quite smooth by their feet; and on visiting it early 
in the morning with Ben, the two together succeeded 
in shooting two cocks and a hen bird. They told me 
that there were numbers around the dancing place, 


Cuar. X. DOWN THE CURIEBRONG. 229 


and that the two cocks they shot were strutting about 
with their feathers distended, showing themselves off 
before the rest. 

Some Indian boys, who accompanied our guides, 
tapped a few indiarubber trees, which grew near the 
fall, and with the milky juice that exuded therefrom, 
made little balls of rubber. 

After a sojourn of two days at the fall, we returned 
down the mountain to our boats, passing on the way a 
large white sandstone rock ornamented with figures in 
red paint. We then commenced our descent of the river, 
stopping the first night at a village called Tamari. | 
There was a fine little Indian boy there, about four 
years of age, whose feet and toes were in a shocking 
state with jiggers, owing to his parents having 
neglected them. I got the interpreter to ask them. 
why they did not do something for the poor little 
fellow, and as I did so, stood looking at him. Catch- 
ing the direction of my eye, he glanced down at his 
toes, and putting on a pitiful expression burst into 
tears, sobbing as if his heart would break. The 
parents’ excuse was, that so many jiggers had accu- 
mulated, the pain of digging them out was greater 
than the child could bear. 

Going on to the next village, to which our guides 
belonged, we spent part of a Sunday there. One of 
these guides, a young man, had a wife who, though 
not more than fourteen years of age, had an infant of 
about one year old. He told William that he and his 
wife could not agree together, and were going to 
separate, he having selected another girl in her place. 
This latter was of his own choosing, while his wife 


230 AMAILAH FALL AND UPPER ESSEQUEBO. Cuar. X. 


was given him by his parents, who now agreed to 
sanction the separation on the ground of non-agree- 
ment of the parties. 

On the 29th of September we reached Tumatamari 
camp, where [ remained four days awaiting the arrival 
of Mr. G. R. Pollard, who was to have come up and 
joined the expedition at this stage, for the purpose of 
making collections for the Museum of the Agricultural 
Society in Georgetown. As he did not arrive, I con- 
tinued my journey up the Essequebo, reaching the 
Carib village of Apoterie on the 13th of October. I 
there spent a few days, preparing for our ascent of the 
Essequebo, intending to follow it as far as my boat 
would be able to ascend. 

The first night of my stay was a wretched one, 
owing to the spirit of disaffection abroad in the 
village, engendered by too frequent and heavy pota- 
tions of casserie on the part of the natives. The row 
commenced at dusk by one Indian’s wife holding a 
funeral dance in honour of the death of a former 
husband, with whom she had not lived for years, who 
had departed this life a month previously. She per- 
formed the part of leader, doing all the frantic work, 
such as throwing her arms about, and howling with 
crocodile tears in her eyes, while most of the other 
women danced solemnly in unison. They had a large 
piece of Haiawah tree (Jcica heptaphylla) gum burning 
on the ground to give light to their proceedings. 
The row they made continuing till 9 P.M., with all 
appearance of lasting until morning, prevented any of 
my party from getting sleep, so some of my men went 
with all the musical instruments they could muster to 


Cuar. X. FUNERAL DANCE. 231 


get up a counter irritation, thinking thereby to stop 
it. These consisted of a penny whistle, an Indian 
drum, and a banjo manufactured out of string and a 
piece of salt-fish box by the ingenious Paulie. The 
music discoursed was anything but sweet, still it had 
not the desired effect of breaking up the whirling 
dervish party, though it did make the members thereof 
laugh. Rescue, however, came from an unexpected 
quarter. The Carib captain’s third wife in a family 
quarrel bit his arm, and, he came down on the funeral 
party to get his second wife to come and assist at the 
chastisement of the third; but she refused, thereupon 
he began to beat her. Another man also had a row 
with his wife, who was of the party, and in the turmoil 
which ensued the dead man’s funeral rites were for- 
gotten, or adjourned to some future day. 

On the 17th of October I sent back the “ Ataraipu,” 
one of my boats, to the Penal Settlement for the crew 
to be paid off, agreeing to re-hire them on the 9th of 
November to bring up a fresh supply of stores, to 
meet me at, or below, King William IV.’s fall in the 
second week of December. Upon the same day I left 
Apoterie in my boat, the “ Roraima,” with a crew of 
eight men, accompanied by the Carib captain to act as 
interpreter between us and any Taruma Indians we 
might meet at the head of the Essequebo. He was 
the only interpreter we could get, and proposed to 
communicate with the Tarumas by means of the 
Wapisiana language, which he supposed the former 
could understand as they traded with the Wapisianas. 

Our way for the first four days lay up the wide 
and dark-watered Essequebo, over a winding sluggish 


232 AMAILAH FALL AND UPPER ESSEQUEBO. Cuar. X. 


portion, with banks lined by a heavy forest of Mora 
trees, which gave it a sombre monotonous aspect. 

Fish of various sorts were either shot with the 
arrow or caught easily with the hook and line on 
this part, foremost amongst which were the Perai and 
Aruana. 

In the clay banks in this part, and in fact on most 
of the rivers, one sees immense numbers of circular 
holes of various sizes, which are bored either by crabs 
or fish when the river is high. My men said that 
they are made by a fish called the Watta-watta, a 
singular, plated, sculpin-like creature. | 

Caymans (Champsa nigra) of large size were 
numerous and fearfully tame, and at night collected 
in the river in the vicinity of our camping places, 
seeking what they might devour. Whilst camping on 
some rocks on our third night out from the Carib 
place, the mosquitoes attacked us in such numbers 
that sleep was out of the question, so we took to 
catching and slaying caymans, and succeeded in des- 
patching two. The proceeding was not very conducive 
to comfort, for the rank musky smell, emitted by the 
victims and their enraged companions, hung over our 
camp for the remainder of the night with sickening 
effect. . 

One day we fell in with a herd of bush hogs, and 
landing amongst them I succeeded in shooting one. 
That night, whilst camped on a low piece of ground 
at the mouth of a small creek, one of the men told off 
to cook went into the boat after dark to procure a 
piece of the hog for dinner. As he was stepping 
back from the gunwale of the boat, near its bow, to 


Cuar. X. ADVENTURE WITH CAYMAN. 233 


the shore, a huge cayman, which was lying in wait, 
struck at him with its tail, for the purpose of sweeping 
him into the water within its reach. Fortunately he 
did not receive the full force of the blow, only the 
end of the tail catching him on one shin, but nearly 
breaking his leg. The sound of two more blows, 
given by the infuriated reptile against the boat’s side, 
was so very loud that we were afraid it had smashed 
a plank; but such was not the case. Taking the 
boat lamp we went to the spot, and seeing the huge 
brute between the boat’s bow and the shore determined 
to punish it without delay. A large hook baited with 
hog’s meat was swung over it, when it made a spring 
and seized the bait savagely. After much champing 
and chewing it threw its head back, opened its mouth 
wide, and down went hook and bait. Five men then 
hauled in on the line, but the reptile, on feeling the 
hook in its stomach, made such fearful struggles that 
we feared the line would break, and so proceeded to 
play it by slacking out a bit. It then landed some 
thirty yards up the creek near our camp, and we 
heard it crashing amongst the small undergrowth in 
the forest. The line was therefore held fast again to 
stop it, while, with gun in hand, I went with a man, 
carrying the lamp, to dislodge it, as its presence on 
shore so close to us was anything but pleasant. 
Getting close to it I took the best aim I could, in the 
indistinct lamp-light, and fired a ball at its head, 
which evidently missed, for it wheeled towards me, 
and the sound of crashing of trees was greater than 
before. The man with the lamp fled, while I made 
the best of my way after him—no easy job in the 


234 AMAILAH FALL AND UPPER ESSEQUEBO. Cmar. X 


dark under ordinary circumstances, but one that I 
accomplished somehow, feeling convinced that a huge 
cayman was after me. It turned out that our flight 
was unnecessary, for while we were going one way it 
was making for the water in the opposite direction. 
Crossing the narrow creek it landed on the opposite 
side, where all efforts to dislodge it by hauling on the 
rope proved unavailing, till at last the hook pulled off 
the line, and the cayman was free to go anywhere it 
chose to digest the hook. Meanwhile many other 
caymans, attracted by the smell of the blood of the 
bush hog and the splashing of the hooked one, collected 
around in such numbers that at one time we feared 
they would come ashore and attack us. They re 
mained about all night; and many times I was awoke 
by hearing single combats going on amongst their 
ranks, the splashing noise of which was accompanied 
by heavy tail blows and loud belching sounds. As 
to the stink of musk, it was simply abominable, 
floating like a deadly miasma round our camp, and 
finding its way even to our palates. 

On our fifth day’s journey we came to the first 
serious obstruction on this part of the river, called 
Murray’s cataract, and after getting above it left our 
cayman friends for good. Upon the banks of the 
river in this part I saw the beautiful Urua palm 
(Attalea speciosa) growing, for the first time; the sin- 
gular arrangement of the upper portions of its leaf 
stems, whereby its leaves stand edgeways in planes 
radiating from the tree’s centre outwards, strikes one 
at once as peculiar and graceful. 

Ascending ditficult and dangerous cataracts was the 


Cmar. X. KING WILLIAM IV.’S FALL. i 235 


order of the day, from that out for sixteen working 
days, until we reached a point some distance below the 
mouth of the Cassikityu river, where from that on 
to our journey’s end we had comparatively smooth 
water, and only a few cataracts of inconsiderable size 
to contend with. 

The first fall we encountered, that really offered a 
dificult barrier to our progress, was one called by Sir 
R. Schomburgk, King William IV.’s fall. It certainly 
bad a formidable aspect, and in 1836 prevented. 
Schomburgk from proceeding farther up the river on 
his way to its sources, causing him to change his route, 
return to the Rupununi, and cross by land to the Cuyu- 
wini from which he entered the Essequebo. Thus, about 
seventy miles of latitude on the Essequebo had never 
been explored ; although an Indian chieftain, named 
Manarowah, nearly a hundred years ago, had succeeded — 
in accomplishing the feat of passing up it, probably 
with small canoes, on a journey he made to the 
Taruma country to capture Indians, as slaves for the 
Dutch, who then were masters of the coast of Deme- 
rara. This man, however, encountered so many diffi- 
culties that he returned by way of the Cuyuwini river, 
through the Wapisiana country to the Rupununi. 

Difficult as the ascent of the fall looked, there was 
nothing in it to appal men who had taken a boat past 
the cataracts of the Potaro. 

There were, in fact, two falls, one on each side of a 
steep hilly island, bounded on the east and west by 
hills rising steeply from the water’s edge, so that it was 
impossible to pass them by hauling the boat overland. 
The fall on the east side of the island, twenty-four feet 


236 AMAILAH FALL AND UPPER ESSEQUEBO. Cuar. X. 


‘in total height, contained the greater body of water, 
which rushed over it with terrific force, excluding all 
possibility of taking the boat up it, as the rocks ou 
either side did not afford enough level surface upon 
which to haul the boat. The fall on the west side, 
twenty feet in height, however, had ledges of rock 
extending along its east side, rough and steep enough, 
it is true, but still affording room for the boat and 
footing for ourselves. On these we placed skids and 
cross poles, all firmly lashed together in ladder form, 
and with excessive labour hauled the empty boat 
over, transporting the stores afterwards. 

Not far above the fall the river became very narrow, 
being constricted to a width of not more than fifty 
yards by masses of gneiss rock, through which it ran 
with great velocity. 

Our second great difficulty lay in passing a great 
fall, half a day’s journey above King William IV.’s, 
which I have named Manarowah, after the above- 
mentioned Indian chief. - This fall is a most singular 
one, being produced by the river, spread out to a 
great width amongst tree-clothed islands, passing down 
a mass of gneiss rock, fifty feet in height. Above is 
a succession of smaller cataracts, which makes the 
total height ninety feet. From the foot of the fall 
the river runs at right angles to its last course, 
flowing away in a narrow cleft, thirty yards wide, 
amongst the rocks along its face, with extraordinary 
velocity. Dense masses of foliage cover the inter- 
vening spaces of the fall’s front, unoccupied by water; 
and the effect produced by the white foaming cascades, 
bursting out here and there amongst the trees, is very 
singular and charming. 


Cuar. X. PARMU IN DESPAIR. 237 


After examining the steep face of the fall and find- 
ing not a ray of hope in its grim looks, nor a shadow 
of a chance of getting up it, I directed my attention 
to the exploration of the land on the west side, and 
with my men crossed over through the forest to the 
head of the fall. Finding the land pretty even and 
the slope gradual, we all agreed to make an effort to 
take the boat overland and launch her above. 

Our Carib interpreter, whom we called “ Parmu,” 
that being the Caribise for “my friend,’ had by 
this time got weary of his journey or frightened at the 
falls, and concluded in his own mind that we should be 
unable to pass this fall. His spirits, therefore, rose 
considerably, and he looked forward to a speedy return 
to his home. Indian like, he would ask no questions 
as to what was to be our next move, when we returned 
from exploring the overland route and were break- 
fasting. At the conclusion of that meal he was sitting 
hear me examining his pegall, containing his most 
valued worldly possessions, such as arrow-heads, files, 
flint and steel, red paint, feathers, &c., when I gave the 
order to the men to commence cutting our portage 
path. The effect this had upon him was most ludicrous 
to witness, for though he did not understand the words 
] used separately, he divined the meaning of the whole, 
and his face took upon it the stony gaze of despair. 
Bundling his things into his pegall, he placed it on the 
ground in front of him, giving it a kick that sent it 
flying and scattered all its contents. The whole action 
said so plainly—‘‘ You may go to the devil; I'll never 
have use for you again; I am going on to my death,” 
—that I could not control myself, but with the men 
burst intoalaugh. There the old pegall lay till evening 


238 AMAILAH FALL AND UPPER ESSEQUEBO. Cuar. X 


when we returned from our first day’s road cutting, by 
which time Parmu took a more cheerful view of things, 
and gathered his treasures to his heart again. 

Our second day’s labours completed the entire work 
of cutting down the small trees level with the ground 
for a width of about ten feet, along a distance of 
exactly half a mile by measurement; of laying down 
rollers every two yards apart; and of bridging two 
gullies with large trees, having cross skids lashed to 
them. On the third day of our stay, we commenced the 
laborious task of hauling the boat over our portage, no 
easy matter, when it is considered that she was twenty- 
eight feet in length, and built of strong heavy wood. 
Six of us hauled on the tow-rope in front, while two men 
on either side of the bow, and two men at the stern, 
also assisted in dragging and keeping her at the same 
time on an even keel. In this manner we succeeded 
in getting her across and launched in the river beyond 
the fall, at a level of ninety feet above the point from 
which we started three hours before. By three o’clock 
that afternoon all the stores had been carried across 
and deposited in ber, and we were on our journey once 
more. That evening, from our camp, on a small rocky 
island in mid-stream, I watched a splendid golden sun- 
set, repeated by reflection, in the still dark water; and 
influenced by the death-like stillness of the place, a 
pleasing but utter sense of loneliness came over me, 
when I reflected upon our isolated position, cut off as 
we were from the civilized world by the two great 
rocky barriers we had so recently passed. 

When engaged in the forest, cutting our portage, we 
were frequently visited by bands of large Quarta 


Cuar. X. SHOOT A TAPIR. 239 


monkeys, which were very numerous on the banks of 
the upper part of this river. They were the black- 
bodied, red-faced kind of large spider monkey, and 
uttered a sort of barking grunt. Their cry, when 
calling to each other, has a wailing sound, and is 
very loud. On seeing us, they used frequently to 
hurl down large dead branches, some of which came 
rather too close to our heads at times to be com- 
fortable. The manner in which they performed this 
was singular; they held on by tail and hind feet to a 
live bough in a tree top, alongside of a dead one, and, 
pushing with their hands with all their force against 
the latter, generally succeeded in breaking it off, when 
down it came. Their actions, when swinging from 
bough to bough, are very graceful, and though per- 
formed with care and deliberation, carry them rapidly 
through the forest tree tops. 

Whilst Ben was in pursuit of a flock of trumpet- 
birds, an eagle-hawk (Harpyhaliaetus coronatus) swooped 
down at him, and passing close to his head, lit on a 
bough before him; when, to punish it for its audacity, 
he shot it. 

Paddling along close to the edge of the river, the 
men espied a tapir (Tapirus Americanus), or Mai- 
pourie, as my men called it, trotting through the 
forest. I immediately landed, gun in hand, and 
going about twenty yards came up to it, standing on 
a hill-side, about fifteen yards above me, seemingly 
pot at all alarmed at my presence. Standing there 
amongst the tree stems, surrounded by beautiful shrubs 
and plants, in its native haunts, it looked a noble 
animal, with, perhaps, a vicious expression of counten- 


240 AMAILAH FALL AND UPPER ESSEQUEBO. Caar. X. 


ance, and a general antiquated appearance. One thing 
it sadly lacked to carry out the symmetry of its form, 
and that was, a tail, of much larger dimensions than 
the little stump that nature had provided it with. 
Levelling my gun, I took aim behind its shoulder, and 
firing, brought the poor creature to its knees. Strug- - 
gling wildly to its feet, it made a plunge, and rolled a 
short way down the hill, where it soon expired, the ball 
having broken the humerus of the fore leg, and passed 
through the point of the heart. 

Its snout was long, overhanging the aih ; its 
crest high, covered with a short mane. The men soon 
cut it up, carrying off the best parts, of which the crest 
was considered a bonne bouche, and that night we had 
a good feed off its beef-like flesh. It had three hoofed 
toes on its hind, and four on its front feet, all of which 
the men took off and saved, for the purpose of using 
when occasion required as charms for bites of snakes, 
stings of ray fish, and fits of all kinds. They said that 
the hoofs are first singed and then placed in water, 
which is drunk as a remedy. Can it be that the am- 
monia of the hoof is the substance that produces any 
good effect? No doubt, if plunged quickly into 
water when burning, some ammonia would be con- 
densed and dissolved. 

On a subsequent occasion, above the Taruma vil- 
lages, when our larder was well supplied with dried 
meat, we came upon a tapir, and not requiring its flesh 
to roast paddled softly to within thirty yards of it 
unobserved, to watch it take its midday bath. It did 
one good to see the way in which it enjoyed its “ tub,” 
diving in the deep water, and plunging and splashing in 


Cuar. X. TAMENESS OF ANIMALS. 241 


the shallow, throwing up its head every now and then, 
and twisting its proboscis about disclosing its teeth. 
When at last it observed us, it made for the shore, and 
galloping up the steep bank, with surprising agility 
for so cumbrous a looking animal, disappeared in the 
forest. 

Owing to the entire absence of representatives of 
the human race in this portion of the Essequebo, all 
members of bird and mammal life met with were 
exceedingly tame. Capybaras, which were numerous, 
appeared not to know in what light to view us; and 
when we were encamped at a large shallow cataract 
where there was a great spread of semi-submerged 
rocks, covered with aquatic plants (lachis), one of 
these animals came up so close to Ben, who was wash- 
ing clothes, that he had to throw stones at it to drive 
it away. At one place the men gave chase over the 
rocks and shallows to three young water-haas, which, 
with their parents, were gazing stupidly at us. They 
succeeded in capturing two of the little ones after an 
exciting hunt, but gave them their freedom again. 

The bush ibises, white cranes, and muscovy ducks 
would let, us approach close to them before they at- 
tempted to fly. I shot twenty-seven of the latter on 
this journey, having some fine right and left shots. In 
the crops of some were found small river crabs and the 
flower buds of aquatic plants. 

Our larder was kept plentifully supplied with Pacu 
fish, which were shot by the men as we hauled up cata- 
racts. On one occasion, when one of these fish, pierced 
by an arrow, had gone down to the foot of a cataract, 
it was attacked by Perai, and when one of the men who 

R 


242 AMAILAH FALL AND UPPER ESSEQUEBO. Cuar. X. 


went after it, seized the shaft of the arrow, he found 
only about a quarter of the fish’s body left upon it. - 

At various places we saw the large Comudi (water- 
boa) lying coiled up in a plethoric condition upon 
grassy spots on the river’s bank, surrounded by num- 
bers of cow-flies, which live upon its blood. 

Numerous flocks of blue and yellow macaws (Mocro- 
cercus ararauna) flew with discordant cries up or 
down the river, always approaching to have a look 
at us; while the blue and red macaws were seldom 
seen. 

I learned a curious fact regarding the habits of the 
jaguar which is worth recording. On one occasion 
when we had landed, and were hunting a herd of bush 
hogs, two men were left in charge of the boat. We 
had not been away in the forest for more than two or 
three minutes, when the men in the boat heard a 
heavy foot tread on the bank above them, and looking 
up saw a large jaguar gazing down on them from the 
very spot up which we bad clambered. They imme- 
diately pushed the boat off into the stream for safety, 
fearing an attack from the tiger, as they had no gun 
to defend themselves with. They told me that this 
jaguar was what the Indians call the master of the 
flock of hogs we had been hunting; that it follows 
them wherever they go; and that when it is hungry, 
and finds a pig a little distance from the rest, it pounces 
upon it, killing it with one blow of its huge paw. The 
squeak of the stricken hog brings down the whole 
herd to the spot, whereupon the jaguar climbs a tree 
for safety till the storm it brewed is over and the pigs 
have left the spot, when it descends from its perch 


Cuar. X. JAGUARS AND HOGS. 243 


to feed on the flesh of its victim. I suppose when it 
has “ cleaned out” an entire flock it seeks another one 
and proceeds as before. 

A story is toldof an Indian who was enekin up 
a flock of hogs to get a shot, when he saw a jaguar 
knock over a member of it as just described. When 
the flock bore down on the jaguar, it jumped upon a 
low fallen tree where it was surrounded immediately 
by the enraged hogs. Unfortunately for the jaguar 
it was not beyond their reach, and in a minute was 
dragged down amongst them, trampled upon, and 
ripped to pieces. 

One can easily conceive how the numbers of bush 
bogs are kept down in this way by large tigers of 
various sorts preying upon them, but. it is more 
difficult to understand what keeps the numbers of 
large tigers from increasing. They appear to have no 
enemies, nothing preys upon them, and the few killed 
by Indians would not affect their number in any 
sensible degree. Both animals increase at the same 
rate, the jaguar having from two to three cubs at 
a birth, and the peccarie about the same number of 
young. 

At the last great cataract we passed we were in an 
ace of losing “the numbers of our mess,” while 
crossing above one of the watery steps of which it was 
composed. Owing to the boat’s bow not being kept 
sufficiently up stream, she was swept down this step 
and hurried backwards towards the foaming cataract 
bristling with rocks, and it appeared to me as if 
nothing could save us from perdition ; but the strenuous 
efforts of the crew, combined with skilful steering, 

R 2 


244 AMAILAH FALL AND UPPER ESSEQUEBO. Cuap. X. 


brought her out of the rush of the current just at the 
nick of time, and we were rescued from our perilous 
position. 

On the 9th of November we passed the month of 
the Cuyuwini river, a fine broad stream, and soon 
after came to an old Indian camp of five banaboos, 
the first signs of recent Indian life met with since 
leaving the Carib village of Apoterie. Near this 
were some large granite rocks, in passing which our 
Carib “ Parmu ” turned away his face in an opposite 
direction. Upon questioning him as to his reason for 
so doing, I learned that if he looked at them he would 
get fever. 

On many of the large clusters of rocks in mid-river 
were the nests of a small species of Caprimulgids, 
called “ Wockariyou.” These can hardly be called 
nests, for the bird only lays its two small speckled 
eggs on a hollow in the bare rock, or in a little 
depression scratched in the gravel upon them. The 
parent birds do all in their power to entice one away 
from the nest, by fluttering along the ground as if 
wounded. 

At Bubumana cataract there is a lot of Indian 
picture-writing cut on a large granite rock, being 
chiefly representations of monkeys and frog-like 
individuals. 

After three days and a half’s journey above the 
Cuyuwini river, we passed a large Indian clearing on 
the west bank containing cassava plants, plantain, 
banana, cashew, pineapples, cotton trees, &c., and a 
short distance on, came to the landing place of a 
village of four houses which had evidently been tem- 


Chie X. SAND-WASPS. 245 


porarily abandoned, as all the earthen pots, &c., for 
culinary purposes were still there. 

We spent a Sunday encamped on a sandy island 
near the mouth of the Cassikityu or Dead river. As 
usual in such places there was an immense number of 
large sand-wasps hovering over it with a ceaseless 
buzz, and digging holes in which they deposit their 
eggs. I watched their movements with interest, and 
though they wearied one with their noise and flittings, 
yet I had no ill-feeling against them, for though 
armed with tremendous stings they never used them 
against me, even when tramping over their nests. 
The manner in which they book up the sand with 
their incurved fore legs and send it flying out behind 
them in a shower, is wonderful to behold. And what 
is still more so, is the manner in which one can ferret 
out its own nest, after it has covered up the orifice, 
on returning with captured insects as food for its 
larvee when hatched. The value of these insects 
became apparent that day, when I observed that the 
victims they captured were chiefly different kinds of 
cow-flies, of which there is a superabundance in these 
parts, and which bite the human subject as well as any 
other animal. It seems that the poison of the sting, 
acting upon the system of their victims, reduces them 
to a comatose condition, in which tiey remain until the 
eggs of the wasp are hatched, when they form fresh 
and healthy food for the larve. As night approaches 
these wasps gradually disappear to return again to 
their toil at daybreak ; but whether they go away to 
sleep or remain in their burrows, is not clear. 

Off the sides of the river in this part were numerous 


246 AMAILAH FALL AND UPPER ESSEQUEBO. Cuar. X. 


inlets or “ pirihaas,” the banks of the river near them 
being very low and covered with the prickly Soweri 
palm (Astrocaryum jauari), midst vegetation of a 
swampy character. 

As we crept slowly up river, on our thirtieth day’s 
journey since we had seen any human beings except 
those of our own party, we observed a small corial 
containing three redskins coming down river. The 
instant they saw us they turned their craft round in a 
frantic manner, and disappeared behind some large 
rocks. My men gave way with a will after them, and 
rounding a bend we came to their village on the east 
side of the river; landing immediately, we entered the 
village, where we found four men, a woman, and a 
girl, all in the greatest state of perturbation. None 
of them had ever seen a white man before, or people 
wearing clothes, consequently they looked upon us as 
supernatural visitors at first. They all trembled with 
fright, and quivered like leaves. Parmu at once 
addressed them in the Wapisiana language, stating, as 
instructed, that we were merely travelling in their 
country, were comparatively harmless, and that all we 
wanted from them were fresh provisions. They got 
us a few cakes of cassava and a few yams, for which 
we gave them some negotie. This prompt payment 
seemed to assure them of our pacific intentions, and 
the men became very friendly, but the women kept as 
far off as possible. Soon others who had fled at our 
approach dropped in one by one, until their number 
amounted to fourteen in all. Of these thirteen were 
Taruma Indians, and one a Maopityan or Frog Indian, 
who differed from the rest, and other Guiana tribes, in 


Cuar. X. FROG INDIANS. 247 


the shape of his head, which was exceedingly long, 
narrow, and high, and had a most extraordinary 
appearance. This form of head is a manufactured 
one, being produced by the application of two flat 
pieces of wood to the sides of the head of the infant 
Maopityan immediately after its birth. There the 
wood is firmly bound until the head becomes flattened 
at the sides, and of course heightened at the top. 
This is somewhat similar to the custom of the flat- 
head Indians of North America, differing only in the 
application of the boards to the sides, instead of to the 
front and back of the head. The Caribs and Macusis 
place woven bands of cotton round their infant’s legs 
and arms—below the calf on the former, and below 
the biceps on the latter—for the purpose of enlarging 
the muscles of those limbs. As the child grows they 
change them for larger bands, and wear them as orna- 
ments in the grown-up state. 

Another tribe of Indians, called Toonahyannas, or 
Waterpeople, are said to live more to the south, near 
the head waters of the Trombetas, who have ponds of 
water encircled by stockades, to which they retire for 
the night, sleeping with their bodies submerged. It 
strikes me that this is an Indian yarn, though it is 
true that there is a tribe called Toonahyannas. 

The language of the Tarumas is a harsh and un- 
pleasing one, in sound somewhat resembling Chinese. 
Their houses are conical and thatched with plaited 
palm leaves. Although they grow cotton they do not 
manufacture hammocks out of that substance, using 
those of twine, made from the young leaf of the Itah 
palm, instead. 


248 AMAILAH FALL AND UPPER ESSEQUEBO. Cmar. X. 


They had no guns, but knew them well by their 
Indian name of “ Aracaboosa.” One old man begged 
leave to be allowed to fire off one of ours. When the 
gun was placed in his hands he appeared a good deal 
excited, and putting it to his shoulder, braced himself 
up firmly, spreading his legs wide apart so as to get a 
good base for the operation. Bang went the piece, 
followed by shouts of delight from his companions, 
while the old fellow turned round towards them with 
a face beaming with smiles, looking as if it was the 
proudest and happiest moment of his life. 

These people told us that the members of their 
tribe, whose houses we had seen lower down river, 
had gone with three Wapisianas up to visit the 
Woyawaise tribe, who live near the sources of the 
Essequebo. The Wapisianas had come through by 
the Cuyuwini river for the purpose of bartering with 
those Indians for cassava graters and hunting dogs. 

After a few hours’ stay at the village we continued 
our journey, finding the river onwards very narrow, 
and in one place, where it had formed a new course 
across a point, we had great difficulty in getting our 
boat over the shallows. That day we had our first 
taste of rain, though ever since we had been on the 
Upper Essequebo we had in the afternoons heard the 
rumblings of distant thunderstorms. 

Next day, whilst wending our way onwards, we met 
the Tarumas—twenty-two in number—belonging to 
the lower village, coming down river in corials from 
their visit to the Woyawaise. They did not appear 
at all alarmed, but came alongside. They had a large 
number of black-and-white hunting dogs, which barked 


Cuar. X. MEET IMPEDIMENTS. 249 


fariously the whole time, more in fear than in anger 
it appeared, for some jumped overboard and swam 
ashore to get away from us. One good-looking young 
man of the party who spoke the Wapisiana language, 
informed the interpreter that they had left the 
Wapisianas and some of their own people up in the 
Woyawaise country, and that most of their journey 
had been overland for a great distance. 

Amongst these Tarumas were two more flat-headed 
Maopityans, a man and a woman; the latter had a 
large tumour on the side of the abdomen. 

At midday we reached a fork of the river where it 
divided almost equally, so we took the one to the 
east, which looked the deeper of the two; following 
its narrow winding course we came in the afternoon to 
a landing place where three corials were tied, and 
from which a path led inland to the Woyawaise 
country. Soon after passing this we found our ad- 
vance completely cut off by large fallen trees lying 
across the river, and therefore returned to the other 
branch. Up it we proceeded a few miles when we 
again came to fallen trees, through five or six of 
which we cut our way, and then came to a great mass 
of prostrate trunks, through which I did not consider it 
advisable to cut, feeling sure that all the remainder of 
the river beyond must be choked in a similar manner. 

Having reached the farthest point attainable, and 
having examined the lithological character of the 
country, nothing remained but to return to the Lower 
Essequebo as rapidly as possible, before the short 
rainy season, then near at hand, should raise the 
water and cut off our retreat at King William IV.’s 


250 AMAILAH FALL AND UPPER ESSEQUEBO. Cuar. X 


fall, where a rise of a few feet would be sufficient to 
cover the ledges of rock by which we ascended. 

At midday on the 18th of November we com- 
menced our return journey, from a point 528 miles 
up from the moath of the Essequebo, and 828 feet 
above the level of the sea, and running rapidly down 
stream, arrived at the Taruma village on the fol- 
lowing day. On our way down we passed all the 
Taruma Indians we had met before encamped, who 
took to their boats and followed us to the village. 

Upon our landing the Indians brought us the food 
they had prepared for us, and were delighted with the 
negotiæ they received in payment. When the men ap- 
proached us their wives walked close behind them, and 
though wishing to see our boat, they were too timid 
to come near. When, therefore, a man came to ex- 
amine it, his wife abandoned him and took refuge 
behind a relative or neighbour; but if he stood up at 
a distance, she stood close behind him, with her arms 
clasped around his waist, peering at us round his 
shoulder. If he sat down, she sat down behind him, 
with her arims clasped round his neck; and it was 
quite touching to see a row of front-rank husbands 
and rear-rank wives, squatting in this apparently 
loving condition. 

These people asked our interpreter many questions 
regarding the Essequebo river from the Rupununi 
upwards, as they never had been farther north than 
a little way past the mouth of the Cuyuwini river, 
which they called the Canuwan. They wanted to 
know whether we had met Indians, kanaimas, bad 
spirits, and wild beasts on that portion of the river. 


CaP. X. TARUMA INDIANS. 251 


We continued our descent of the Essequebo, and, 
arriving at the mouth of the Cassikityu, ascended it 
for a day and a quarter. We then returned to the 
Essequebo, going down to the first Taruma village, 
where the owners, our travelling friends, had already 
arrived and prepared some cassava, yams, and pine- 
apples for us, being very anxious to procure articles 
of negotig in exchange. They had Avocado or Alli- 
gator pears (Persea gratissima), which they said grew 
on trees in their plantations, and had originally been 
procured farther up river. 

All these Tarumas were healthy, strong-looking 
people, with but one exception. Some were fishing in 
the river for young cartabac, with hooks baited with 
the berries of the common calaloo, and were very 
successful. The hooks they used were old-fashioned, 
and apparently not of English manufacture. 

After three hours’ stay at the village, we bid adieu 
to the Taruma tribe for ever, and continued our 
journey. 

On the way down we had some hog hunts, at one of 
which an amusing incident occurred. Parmu shot a 
pig with a poison rappu-bamboo arrow in the back, 
and leaving it standing in a semi-paralysed condition, 
ran on after the rest of the drove. I shot one pig, 
and then waited for the men to return from the chase, 
when Ben and Griffiths both passed me, armed with 
guns, the former considerably in advance of the latter. 
Ben came up to the hog suffering from Parmu’s arrow, 
and, thinking it was standing there as a free agent, 
fired at it. The sound of the report. startled it, when, 
making an effort to run, it fell; Ben supposing he had 


252 AMAILAH FALL AND UPPER ESSEQUEBO. Camar. X. 


killed it, ran on. It must then have straggled to its 
feet again, for when Griffiths came up and spied it, he 
also fired with like result. On the return of the men 
this hog was knocked on the head, the poison bamboo 
arrow pulled out, and the animal brought to the boat, 
where Ben claimed it as the result of his prowess. 
Griffiths was astounded at this, declaring that he had 
slain it himself. When, however, it had been scalded 
and scraped, it was found that not a single shot had 
touched it, and the arrow-head formed its only wound. 
The subject was a sore one to both men after their 
warm discussion, and was frequently brought up by 
the rest of the party, when the topic of conversation 
turned on shooting. | 

Another drove of hogs which we met were exceed- 
ingly fierce, and on hearing our footsteps charged 
down at us, passing close to the place where I was 
standing. Parmu, bow and arrow in hand, was 
‘directly in front of them, and not seeing a big tree 
to get behind, was nearly run over. He had just time 
to throw down his bow and arrows and spring into a 
small tree as the whole drove, closely packed, passed 
over the spot he evacuated; the back of one hog 
brushing his foot as he struggled upwards on his tree 
stem, causing him to call out lustily from fright. The 
cause of their fierceness was attributed by Parmu to 
the fact of their having been lately harassed by tigers. 
Parmu’s plan of shooting hogs was to get in their 
front, and when they charged or ran, he stepped be- 
hind a large tree, round which the drove passed on 
either hand. As they held on their way, he discharged 
his arrows into the mass with good effect. Although 


Cuar. X. HAIMARA. 253 


it seemed to be a dangerous proceeding, hemmed in 
for a few moments on both sides by hogs with a tree 
at his back, it was not so, for they never attempt to 
turn when rushing forwards in this way. 

One night when camped on some rocks bordering 
the river, I tried my hand at Haimara (Erythrimus 
macrodon) fshing, and in half an hour landed five of 
these fine large fish, each measuring about two feet 
six inches in length. To catch them you throw out 
about two yards of line, and let the baited hook hang 
over the side of the rock. The fish bites heavily when 
you give a quick pull and hook it. One of them, 
when opened by the cook, was found to have two 
shin bones of bush hog in its gullet, which the men 
had thrown into the water just before I commenced 
to fish. 

We spent a Sunday encamped near the mouth of 
the Cuyuwini, where I observed a large gray monkey 
busily foraging for spiders, &c., in the top of a high 
tree. He knocked down a large wasp or Marabunta 
nest, and then dislodged a large green snake which 
jumped out of the tree top, falling into the water from 
a height of fifiy feet, evidently very anxious to get out 
of the monkey’s way. 

Our work in getting down some of the large cata- 
racts was hardly less arduous than in ascending them, 
and certainly much more hazardous. At many of 
them we had to unload, and transport the stores over 
the rocks, and then run the empty boat down. One of 
those we ran in the main channel, where the water in 
a mass of great depth poured through a gap some 
fifty yards wide, like a green sea, curving down at an 


` 
254 AMAILAH FALL AND UPPER ESSEQUEBO. Crap. X. 


angle of 40°, was six feet in height. At the foot it 
was a mass of foam, tailing off to a point a couple of 
hundred yards beyond, the intermediate space being 
occupied by a succession of short waves, curling their 
crests back towards the fall, as they rose and fell. 
The word “ headway” was given as we neared the 
top, and with full speed went at it, reached the edge, 
and plunged down the slope with almost lightning 
speed, the boat burying herself forward in the foam 
below, and rising quickly as soon as the first wave 
caught her, sending the spray flying back over us and 
deluging the bowman and the next two paddlers. 
Then we tossed up and down on the waves as we ran 
on, until we came to smooth water beyond. 

Another cataract, where a great rock lay in mid- 
channel at its foot, upon which the whole body of 
water drove with great force, was skilfully run; but 
in spite of every effort, the boat brushed against the 
rock in passing, causing us no little anxiety at the 
moment. 

We hauled our boat over the portage of Manarowah 
fall on the 5th of December, and on the following day 
passed successfully over the rocks of King William IV.’s 
fall. On reaching Murray’s cataract on the 7th we 
expected to meet our other boat on its way up river; 
but though there were footprints on the sands, my 
men concluded that they were not the tracks of our 
men, but those of Indians. I greatly feared that we 
might have passed it amongst the islands above this 
cataract. This however was not the case, for on the 
following day whilst breakfasting the “ Ataraipu” was 
seen coming up river, and soon afterwards joined us. 


Cuar. X. RETURN TO APOTERIE. 255 


It was with great pleasure that I perused some 
intelligence of the outer world, from newspapers and 
letters sent me by Georgetown friends. When I left 
that city in September, news had but lately arrived 
of the breaking out of the French and German war, 
and now F learned that the Emperor Napoleon was a 
prisoner in Germany. Not having had any news 
leading up to this result, I was not prepared for such 
astounding intelligence as the fall of the Second 
Empire. 

I learned from my men that Mr. Pollard had come 
up with them, and that they had left him ill with 
fever at the Carib village in the care of Paulie. We 
therefore continued our downward journey with all 
possible speed and reached Ud on the afternoon 
of the 9th of December. 

On the way down we came up with a corial con- 
taining three Indians from Apoterie — subjects of 
Parmu—who had been sent up in search of him. 
They had got as far as Murray’s cataract, and seeing 
no signs of their chief, had concluded that he and all 
of us had been lost. 

I found Mr. Pollard much better, and only in need 
of cheering up, a little quinine, and removal from the 
Carib place, of which he had got thoroughly weary. 
It was a great pleasure to me to meet him and have 
a companion of kindred tastes for the continuation of 
my journey. 

Old Parmu’s entry into his capital, rigged out in 
war paint, and crowned with a feather hat he had 
brought from the Taruma country, was worth seeing ; 
while the yarns he told his people must have been 


256 AMAILAH FALL AND UPPER ESSEQUEBO. CHap. X 


worth hearing, for they all crowded round him and 
dwelt upon every word he uttered. Native drinks 
were brought him in such quantities, that before the 
evening was over he was helplessly intoxicated. 

My servant Griffiths, who had been unwell and very 
irritable for some time, was seized that evening with 
an epileptic fit, in which he remained for a consider- 
able time. It took three men to hold him: his suffer- 
ings were painful to witness. The men put salt in his 
mouth and rubbed it also into the palms of his hands, 
while an old Indian woman was very attentive, burning 
some dried leaves of a peculiar kind under his head, 
that he might inhale the smoke and obtain relief. 

We turned-in that night under good roofs for the 
first time since the day we left Apoterie on our 
journey up river; and thus terminated the first 
portion of my journey. 


Cuar. XI. UP THE COTINGA. 257 


CHAPTER XI. 


UP THE COTINGA. 


Ascend Rupununi — Christmas Day — Walk to Takutu — River Porpoises 
— Spoonbills — Ascend Ireng to Pirara River — Cross over to Aring- 
wong — Descend Takutu to Cotinga — Ascend Cotinga — Black Flies 
— Savanna Fox — Meet Wapisianas — Journey Northward — Amusing 
Scene crossing the Karnang — Ascent of Ireng — Orindouie Fall — 
Return — Indian Huntsmen — Descend Cotinga — Wild Cattle — 
Fort St. Joaquim — Return to Karinambo. 


Leavine the Carib village the day after our arrival, 
we ascended the Rupununi river to Karinambo village, 
from which place I sent back the boat “ Ataraipu”’ to 
the Penal Settlement, with orders to the crew to 
return again with the last load of provisions on the 
first day of February. 

Near Watama creek we met a party of Indians 
fishing, near whom were five caymans, with their heads 
above water, lazily watching their proceedings. One 
Indian was perched in a tree top overhanging the 
water, bow and arrow in hand, watching for turtles. 
He hailed us in the usual Indian mode with the deep 
sounding “ ha-ie,” and we recognized in him our old 
friend Germain of Annai village. 

I shot a Wallaba bird (Cancroma cochlearia), of 
a grayish colour, with large boat-shaped bill. These 
birds are nocturnal in their habits, roosting in trees 
over swamps during the day, where they utter a 
curious frog-like call. 

S 


258 UP THE COTINGA. Cnap. XI. 


In passing the bend at Mora, we came up with a 
party of Mora Macusis, with whom was the guide 
who had taken me to the Ireng river in 1869. 
Amongst this party was an unfortunate woman, whose 
attenuated body formed a most shocking sight. She 
was a living skeleton, being nothing but skin and 
bones, with the exception of the face which was not 
reduced in proportion to that of the rest of the body. 
They told me that this woman had come to them from 
the Ireng river district, where she had been poisoned 
by Kanaimas, and that this accounted for her wasted 
condition. 

From Karinambo we went on to Pirara landing, 
which we reached on the 20th of December. On the 
three succeeding days we were engaged in conveying 
all our stores to a village, three miles inland on the 
savanna, near the Marooca river, called Aringwong. 
This was a most tedious job, as we had to get a corial 
up the narrow Awaricurru channel into the Murooca 
inlet, transport the stores to it, and then convey them 
up the inlet to the mouth of the Marooca river. From 
that spot they had to be carried overland for one mile 
to Aringwong, and there stored in one of the houses. 
In these operations twenty-four men were engaged, 
twelve of whom had been sent to assist us by Captain 
Passico of Carenacru. 

Christmas Day was spent at Aringwong; what I — 
suffered on the night preceding that festive day will 
never be effaced from my memory. In a small house 
given up to us for our stores and selves we slung our 
hammocks. Retiring to rest at 10 r.m., we—that is 
Mr. Pollard, myself, Ben, and William—fell asleep. 


Cmar. XI. A MISERABLE NIGHT. ‘ 259 


After a doze of two hours’ duration, filled with troubled 
dreams, I awoke to find that I was literally covered 
with fleas, which directed their attacks principally 
against my back, head, and hands. Lighting the 
lamp, I examined my hammock and found it swarming 
with them. Asa rule, one flea is enough to deprive 
me of a night’s rest, so I abandoned all idea of fur- 
ther sleep, and went out in the starlight for a walk. 
Tiring of this, I returned and read an old newspaper 
through from beginning to end, smoking savagely. 
What made my case all the more unbearable; was to 
see the way in which my companions slept, the fleas 
apparently not biting them. At 2.30 a.m. I turned 
into my hammock again amongst the fleas, had another 
nightmare doze of an hour and a half, and was again 
fairly driven out. I then walked about outside the 
house watching the splendid constellations as they 
passed slowly overhead; saw the morning star rise, 
the red clouds gather in the east, and lastly, the 
glorious sun rise majestically from the plain ushering 
in the Christmas morn. 

During the day, Ben shot some pigeons and manu- 
factured a most creditable pie, sticking their little feet 
up through the crust in the most orthodox style. The 
men had likewise constructed a pudding made of flour, 
water, and raisins. It was an unfortunate affair from 
beginning to end, for when dinner time arrived, and it 
was taken out of the bag in which it had been too 
loosely tied up, and emptied upon a piece of coarse 
rice bag, it was found to be only half cooked. Conse- 
quently it burst, the interior running out and gathering 
some gravel in its lava-like flow. It was gathered up 

s2 


260 ` UP THE COTINGA. Cuar. XI. 


and re-boiled, and when turned out for the: second 
time, consisted of two large pieces, one of a cubical 
form, the other triangular, while the molten centre 
had turned into a sort of soup. When subjected to 
mastication, it was found that the cook had forgotten 
to put in any sugar; but this was easily remedied. 

On Christmas night, 1 had my hammock, which had 
spent the day in the sun, erected some three feet from 
the ground, and rushing into the house, bounded into 
it before a single flea had time to cling to me, thus 
obtaining a pleasant night’s rest. Fleas can jump a 
good distance in a vertical direction, but they cannot 
span a space of three feet. 

We left Aringwong next day, with our eight men 
and ten Indians carrying loads, our intention being to 
make an overland journey to an Indian village, called 
Yarewah, on the Takutu, at the western end of the 
Canuku mountains, in order to procure corials for a 
journey up the Cotinga river, the Indians having in- 
formed us that we could not obtain any sort of craft 
on the Ireng river, or, in fact, anywhere nearer than 
that place. The day was remarkably hot without 
any breeze whatever, which made our walk an ex- 
ceedingly unpleasant one. Still more unpleasant was 
the wading of the wide black-mud bog near Carenacru. 

On the way, in passing a grove of trees, a large 
jabiru was seen perched upon a tree. I took a long 
shot at it and brought it to the ground, but being only 
slightly wounded, it escaped by hiding in the thicket. 
Curious to relate, just nine days after, as we were 
approaching Aringwong on our return, we saw two 
jabirus in the distance; on stalking them they flew 


Cmar. XI. FALSE ALARM. 261 


away, but one did not go far, however, before it came 
to the ground and took to its legs. Seeing that some- 
thing was wrong, I gave chase, accompanied by one 
of the Indians carrying a gun. Coming up with it the 
Indian’s gun snapped, and not having another cap the 
weapon was useless. The Indian then got a stick 
and slew the poor bird, which turned out to be the 
very one I had wounded on our way to Carenacru. 
It showed fight when the man approached, making a 
rattling noise with its great upcurved beak, and- 
puffing out the scarlet skin round the base of its neck. 

On the evening we spent at Carenacru, an Indian 
from the Canucu mountains came in with the intelli- 
gence that an armed party of Brazilians from Fort 
St. Joaquim had come up the Takutu in five bateaux, 
and seized some Macusis, who subsequently escaped. 
He said they had horses, and were then on the English 
side of the Takutu marching on Passico’s village. 
The villagers became alarmed, and old Passico accom- 
panied us next day to inquire into the truth of the 
matter. At the second village through which we 
passed, we learned that the Brazilians had turned 
back; whereupon Passico and his retainers left us. 
We subsequently learned at Yarewah that no Bra- 
zilians had been lately as far as the Takutu, and that 
there was not a word of truth in the tale brought 
by the Indian to Carenacru. 

We crossed the Nappi, a few miles from the foot of 
the Canucu mountains, but owing to the dryness of 
the season that river was not running at the time, 
only containing pools of stagnant water here and there 
in its bed. One of the pools by which we passed 


262 UP THE COTINGA. Cuar. XI. 


contained a mass of dead and live fish, and stank 
horribly. These fish were chiefly hoorie, and had 
been thus entrapped as the river water gradually 
evaporated. Some other pools, which the water had 
entirely left, had moist, muddy bottoms, in which the 
Indians dug with their hands and hauled out numbers 
of Hassa fish, all in good condition. These fish can 
live for a great length of time in moist earth or mud. 
They are small, being covered with a complete armour 
of elongated scales. 

We had various bogs to cross, and a large branch 
of the Nappi, called Haiawahouta, to wade, in which, 
strange to say, there was plenty of water. 

On our third day’s walk we stopped to cook 
breakfast at a large dilapidated house, which leaned 
away from the direction of the trade wind, being 
propped up with poles to keep it from falling. In it 
were seven people, amongst whom was an exceedingly 
old man, wasted by age and ill health. He could not 
live long, and the old house was propped up so as to 
last his time. When next we passed that way, on a 
subsequent journey by land to the head of the Takutu, 
we found the house still standing, but deserted for 
ever, and the old man’s remains interred in its earthen 
floor. 

The guide took us to a village at the foot of the 
Canucu mountains, and then across a level plain ina 
westerly direction. In passing over this plain in the 
afternoon the wind died away, rendering the heat 
almost unbearable, the direct rays of the declining 
sun striking upon our faces. Qn coming to the 
Macumuca river we rested awhile on its banks in the

s ss

COWARIE AND SARQUAH. 


Ctar. XI. CABOW ROOS. 263 


shade of the belt of trees growing along it. Heated 
as all the Indians were, they immediately plunged. in 
and bathed. 

Late in the afternoon we reached a village of 
Wapisiana Indians, where we stopped for the night. 
Two hours’ farther walking brought us to the village 
of Yarewah, on the east bank of the Takutu river, 
where the head man of the place, a tall Macusi, with 
an aquiline cast of countenance, hired us three old 
corials for an unlimited period. 

The river near the village was wider than the 
Rupununi at Annai, but owing to the lowness of the 
water at the time it was shallow, with large sand- 
beaches at the inner sides of its bends. Bathing in it 
was rendered a painful proceeding by those horrid 
pests, the small black flies, or Cabowroos, which 
literally blackened our skins the moment we emerged 
from the water, when thousands were rubbed to death 
under the towel. 

We sent back all our Indians, save Cowarie (or 
John, as we called him) and Sarquah, two fine young 
Macusi men, who remained with me during the re- 
mainder of the journey. 

Next morning we started on our journey down the 
Takutu, in order to take our boats to the Ireng, which 
joins this river, and then up it to the mouth of the 
Pirara river, so as to get as near as possible to Aring- 
wong, our provision depdt. 

We were now on branches of the Rio Branco, and 
therefore on the river system of the Amazon valley, 
while those rivers we had left and lately traversed 
belonged to the Essequebo valley. Our stores were 


264 UP THE COTINGA. Cuar. XL 


near the Rupununi, whilst the nearest point to them 
that we could hope to bring our boats, now that all 
the rivers were low, would be thirty-seven miles away. 
Had the rainy season prevailed at the time we could 
have got our boats up the Pirara to Lake Amucu, and 
by bringing our things in boats up the Marooca to 
Quatata, would then have only a portage of about half 
a mile to span from one river system to the other. 

The Takutu runs with a general northerly course 
until joined by the Ireng coming from the north-east, 
when it turns and flows with a south-west course to its 
junction with the Cotinga river, from which point 
onwards it runs towards the south. It at first wasa 
mere chain of great deep pools with little or no 
current, except where one emptied itself into the next 
below, over sand shallows of forty yards in width and 
eighteen inches in depth. These shallows, occurring 
every hundred yards or so, delayed our progress 
considerably, the largest corial having to be partially 
dragged over them. Its banks were from twenty to 
forty feet high, with but few trees or shrubs on them, 
the surrounding country being open savanna. As its 
bed was wide, and its bends long, we obtained some 
fine views of the Canucu mountains from its extensive 
sand-beaches. 

The water of the Takutu belongs to the milky class, 
but where it lies in deep pools its colour, by reflected 
light, is from grayish-blue to greenish. 

At noon, on our second day’s journey down the 
Takutu, we came to the mouth of the Ireng, and 
turned up it. This river contained at the time a 
greater body of water than the Takutu, and its 


Cuar, XI. RIVER PORPOISES. 265 


brownish-black colour formed a marked contrast, at 
its junction, with the greenish water of that river. 

Upon the sand-beaches of the Takutu we saw 
numbers of Orinooco geese ( Chenalopez jubata), and a 
large species of ibis ; the former being very tame, from 
not being molested by the Indians, who do not consider 
their flesh a fit article for human food. In the inlets, 
off the sides of the river, were flocks of beautiful 
roseate spoonbills ( Platalea Ajaja) and wicissi ducks; 
while savanna kites and vultures soared above us in 
the air. At the mouth of one inlet two large river 
porpoises (Delphinus sp.) swam out, showing their 
heads and backs above water for an instant, and 
blowing up small jets of water through their nostrils 
with a puffing sound. 

In crossing shallows we frequently saw large fish 
darting up river, at which the men made blows with 
their paddles, and in one place, after a most exciting 
chase, succeeded in killing one, which proved to be a 
coulite or tiger-fish (Platystoma tigrinum). It had no 
scales, but its body was marked with broad black 
stripes. 

We spent Sunday, January lst, 1871, encamped on 
a small sand-beach on the Ireng river. Mr. Pollard 
and I crossed over to the western side, and, climbing 
the high bank, set fire to the savanna grass, which, 
being dry, caught easily, and spread with great 
rapidity, the broad band of flames rushing off before 
the strong breeze with great velocity, leaving behind 
it a long lane of blackened waste. Our fire got into 
a small grove close to the river, and there blazed away 
all the evening. Over it wheeled large owls above 


266 UP THE COTINGA. Cap. XI. 


the smoke, their bodies gleaming with a golden hue 
when seen by the light of the flames below. In the 
clay bank were holes, probably made by kingfishers, 
but then tenanted by small owls, and on the slopes 
beneath them were skulls and bones of mice, and other 
small rodents, which they had killed. 

A tapir came to the river after dark and swam down 
past our camp. 

Next day we reached the Pirara river, and entering 
it for a short distance were obliged to land, the shallow 
condition of the river preventing our farther ascent. 
Here we formed a camp, where we left our surplus 
stores, hauling our boats up on some rocks in readiness 
to receive repairs on our return from Aringwong. 
Leaving camp on the following day we walked across 
the savanna to Quatata village, where we spent the 
night, and next morning went on to Aringwong. 

At Quatata I saw a most curious Æolian musical 
instrument, of Indian manufacture, composed of three 
large leaf stalks of the Itah palm, stuck upright in the 
ground, a portion of the hard outer part of which was 
cut longitudinally in three parts, and raised on a 
bridge like the strings of a fiddle. The wind blowing 
through these produced very sweet but mournful 
musical sounds. When starting from the same place I 
wished to buy a fowl ; the Indian women brought me 
an old bird, with spurs a couple of inches in length, 
betokening great age and toughness. Upon my saying 
that it was too old and would not do, they went and 
caught a little chicken, with scarcely six feathers on 
it, and inquired of the interpreter, in a very chaffy 
manner, whether that was young enough for me. 


Cuap. XI. CROSSING THE SAVANNA. 267 


At this point of our journey Mr. Potlard came to 
the conclusion that it would further the object he had 
in view, of making collections of plants, birds, &., 
were he to remain at Karinambo instead of going with 
me up the Cotinga. From our late journey, he found 
that he would be unable to do much in that way when 
moving from place to place, especially when our corials 
offered so small a space for the storage of his collecting 
materials. We were both sorry to lose each other’s 
companionship, but a sense of duty prevailed over 
private feelings, and we bid each other adieu. I sent 
him down in the boat to Karinambo, with Paulie as 
his attendant, and as soon as my men returned to 
Aringwong, I made my way back to the place where 
we had left our corials on the Pirara river, accompanied 
by my seven men and twenty-one Indians, all carrying 
heavy loads of provisions. 

The first portion of our journey, after leaving 
Quatata, was over undulating savanna, while the latter 
part was over a level open plain. On one of the 
undulations there was a large grove of trees in which 
the Indians said a horse took shelter, coming out at 
times on the open to feed. They pointed out his 
tracks to me on the sandy places, saying that he was 
very savage when approached. He had evidently 
strayed from Fort St. Joaquim, and crossed the Takutu 
river to this savanna. 

Over a large portion of the level tract the grass had 
been recently burnt, and the ground was covered with 
its blackened ashes, which blew up into our faces as we 
walked along, and made us look like a set of sweeps. 
Numerous small circular whirlwinds swept across the 


268 UP THE COTINGA. Cuar. XL 


plain, carrying up clouds of ash and dust. One of 
these, of unusually large dimensions, rose rapidly, not 
far from us in a cylindrical form, somewhat resembling 
the smoke of a large fire. So like was it that it com- 
pletely deceived a kite, which, flying towards it, rose 
up to hover over the supposed smoke, in order to 
pounce on vermin, started by the fire which it inferred 
was béneath. This bird never made a greater mistake 
in its life, for the upward revolving current of air 
caught it, and with wings outspread, apparently un- 
able to escape, upwards it went with great rapidity. 
Feeling deeply interested in its fate, I watched it until 
jt became a mere speck, and then faded completely out 
of sight in the grasp of the whirlwind. 

Small coveys of quail (Ortyz cristatus) frequent 
these savannas, which are most delicious eating when 
roasted on a stick over the fire. 

We spent two days at our camp, repairing our 
corials with materials brought over from our stores at 
Aringwong. Each corial had a single plank along 
its sides to make them higher, which, being in a shaky 
condition, had to be nailed on afresh; and their nu- 
merous splits and cracks had to be caulked with 
oakum and coated with pitch. The place we occupied 
in this work resembled a boat-builder’s yard, and re- 
sounded with the noise of the caulking hammer in a 
most enlivening manner. The sun was awfully hot, 
and its rays beat down upon us, whilst at work, ina 
most unmerciful way. Late in the evening, after the 
day’s work was over, I went fishing with Ben and 
William in one of the corials, and enjoyed the sport 
immensely, the fish taking the hooks with avidity. We 


Cuar. XI. SAILING DOWN THE TAKUTU. 269 


hauled in five Baiara (Hydrolycus scomberoides) and 
one perai, and hooked a large porowarima, which 
dragged our craft rapidly through the water. It soon 
became exhausted and was hauled up to the edge of 
the boat, when the hook, which had only gone through 
its upper lip, was quickly freed by one cut with a 
knife, the great fish sinking quickly out of sight. 
Whilst thus engaged, a fish resembling a shad jumped 
out of the water, and falling into our boat, was captured. 

On the 10th of January we started on our journey 
down the Ireng to the Takutu; and then on down 
the Takutu as far as the mouth of the Cotinga river, 
up which we turned on the afternoon of the following 
day. As the strong north-east trade wind blew down 
the Takutu in our favour, we hoisted impromptu sails, 
and went along at great speed. One boat had half of 
the canvas awning rigged as a square sail, another 
two blankets, and the third a large tree branch. I 
enjoyed that sail immensely, so silently and swiftly 
did we glide along past great wide stretches of light- 
coloured sands, now on one side, now on the other; and 
on past gray clay banks, sometimes capped with belts 
of trees, at others with waving grass. The breeze was 
very squally and used to blow in strong gusts, sending 
clouds of sand, like drifting snow, along the great 
stretches of beach. On one occasion when the boat 
with a blanket sail ran ap more woollen material to 
try and overtake us, we shook out a reef in our canvas 
just as one of these squalls came on, when down went 
the whole arrangement of mast, sail, sheets, and shrouds, 
blown fairly over the bow, while the other boat passed 
us on the wings of the wind. Trifling episodes like 


270 UP THE COTINGA. Caar. XI. 


this gave us something to laugh at and talk about, and 
relieved the dull monotony of our voyage. 

The Takutu, from bank to bank, averaged between 
three and four hundred yards, but scarcely one-half of 
this was occupied by water at the time. 

We camped for a night on one of the great sand- 
beaches, where the men dug up numbers of the large 
river turtle’s eggs, obtaining a hundred and fifty-two 
eggs from one nest. 

Porpoises were continually coming up close to us, 
and frequently followed us for long distances. 

Just as we entered the Cotinga we heard a curious 
sound, somewhat like heavy breathing, proceeding 
from some trees at the river’s edge, which we found 
was produced by a bird rejoicing in the euphonious 
name of “stinking pheasant.” It is the crested cecelia 
( Opisthocomus cristatus), which lives in trees border- 
ing river edges. Its flight is short and heavy; it 
appears to be very clumsy, falling forwards and appa- 
rently losing its balance whenever it alights, remain- 
ing for a short interval with its wings outspread. 
On bringing the head of a specimen I shot, within a 
few inches of my nose, I inhaled a whiff of its perfume, 
which, to me, resembled a mixture of bad musk and 
ammonia. 

Our work in ascending the Cotinga in no way dif- 
fered from that on our journeys up other rivers. 
There was the usual crawling up stream under the 
dull monotonous thud of paddle strokes; there were 
the usual stretches of smooth water, interlarded with 
the usual amount of cataracts; and there was the 
daily struggle to obtain a supply of meat food with 


Cmar. XI. THE MIKANG. 271 


the war cry of “death to every edible animal.” One 
pleasant thing about this river was, that it ran through 
savanna country, and though trees lined the tops of 
its banks in places, they were only in narrow bands; 
so that on landing I could enjoy the sight of grass- 
covered plains, with distant mountains, and freely 
breathe the dry fresh air. 

One Sunday, when encamped at Warara-sararu 
cataract, I took some long walks inland from both 
sides of the river. On the west side we passed over un- 
dulating country, and found a large pond surrounded 
by long grass and rushes, teeming with muscovy 
ducks, wicissi, blue-winged teal, spoonbills, &c. What 
surprised me most was to see the tracks of a herd of 
cattle, and the bleaching bones of one of its late 
members. We at first thought that the herd might 
belong to the Brazilian farm, near Fort St. Joaquim, 
some fifty miles to the south, but afterwards found 
that they were wild cattle, which must have strayed 
from that farm many years ago. After potting some 
of the ducks, Ben and I continued our walk, and 
started a savanna fox (Vulpes cancrivora), which 
went away in fine style. It is called Mikang by the 
Indians, and is a diminutive grayish animal, with a 
bushy tail. 

From a low hill-top, the views I obtained of the 
Pacaraima and Parima mountains to the northward 
were very fine. The grass on the savanna was 
singularly green and fresh, and the low scattered 
trees grew at such regular distances apart, that it 
almost appeared to me that I was gazing upon a huge 
orchard of young fruit trees. 


272 UP THE COTINGA. Cuar. XI. 


Flocks of muscovy ducks were numerous on all 
parts of the river, and we partly lived upon them 
during our journey in the corials. At night, before 
dark, they flew up and down river like bats, affording 
me some flying shots. 

The chief drawbacks to our comfort were cabowroos, 
or biting black flies, and those other torments, the little
