white-bodied savanna flies, which were in myriads, 
and gave us no peace throughout the day. With the 
setting sun they retired to rest, to reappear at the 
break of day. We all had our heads tied up in hand- 
kerchiefs to keep them off, and looking like a parcel of 
old Highland Scotch-women. When a puff of wind 
came these tormentors disappeared, dodging down on 
the sand or on the bottom of the boat until it 
passed, when up they came again and renewed their 
attacks. | 

I was greatly amused with the appearance of two 
young but fully-fledged jabirus, which stood on their 
large flat nest, composed of sticks entwined together, 
on the branch of a large isolated tree, growing on the 
river's bank. They looked like two shipwrecked 
mariners on a rock in mid-ocean, waiting to be de- 
livered from their lonely watch by a passing ship. 
They stood there as if scanning the horizon, appa- 
rently deep in thought, shifting their position now and 
then from one leg to the other, or taking a solemn 
and stately stroll round the confines of their nest. 
Thus we left them, to await the time when their 
powers of flight would be sufficiently developed, to 
enable them to go forth into the world and forage for 
themselves. They were fully feathered with a gray 


Cuar. XI. ANCIENT GRAVES. 273 


plumage, which on ponlting would change to pure 
white. 

Beyond Warara-sararu cataract we came to huge 
solid granite rocks, forming two hills on either hand. 
That on the east side of the river is called Mikang- 
yepatori, which means “father of the savanna fox.” 
Not far on we passed the mouth of a large branch on 
the west, called the Zuruma river, and spent a night 
near Mount Piriwai. At the southern foot of this hill, 
a few hundred yards from the river, a large area of 
level land is covered with broken pottery, indicating 
the existence at one time of an extensive village on 
the spot. There are also slabs of rock placed flat 
on the ground, in shapes resembling graves, probably 
marking the resting places of the ancient people of the 
village. 

After passing the cataract of Aratiari, about two 
days’ journey up river, we saw an Indian corial made 
fast to the bank, and smoke arising from savanna 
grass recently set on fire. These were the only signs 
of natives we met with, until we reached the end of 
Mikang-pati mountain, where an Indian path led from 
the river to a cassava field. Not far beyond that place, 
and near the mouth of the Tuanu river, we caught 
sight of six redskins, amongst some low shrubs on the 
right bank of the river, watching our movements. 
We hailed them, when they immediately approached 
us, making most anxious inquiries of the interpreter as 
to whether we were Brazilians or English. Learning 
that the latter was the case, they threw off their 
timidity and welcomed us as friends. Judging from 
our coming up the Cotinga that we were Brazilians 

T 


274 UP THE COTINGA. Cuar. XI 


from the Fort, they were in a fright, knowing that the 
“ Cariwas ”—as they call both Portuguese and Brazil- 
ians—would not visit them for any good purpose. 
They told us that the party of Indians, whose canoe 
we had seen at Aratiari, had spied us, and fearing 
that we were Cariwas had come home overland to 
warn the rest. Consequently our movements had been 
watched for some days, and the news of our ascent of 
the river was known to all the inhabitants of the 
vicinity. These Indians, though Wapisianas, spoke 
the Macusi language, and my interpreter was able to 
converse freely with them. We went on a short 
distance and camped at Tuanu-sararu cataract, whilst 
the Indians went off to their village to tell their 
people that no danger was to be apprehended from us. 
It was not particularly pleasant to reflect upon the 
intelligence communicated to us by the Indians, that 
we had been taken for enemies, and watched as such 
for days, and that we might at any time during those 
five days have been set upon and slaughtered. 

About dusk that evening we were visited by a 
party of forty-one Wapisianas, who brought us some 
fresh provisions, amongst which was a small quantity 
of unshelled rice, which they bad grown themselves. 

At Panatsikameru cataract we were met by some 
Macusis, who had come to meet us, who followed the 
bank of the river till we came to the landing of their 
village at Wirina creek, where, in answer to inquiries, 
they informed us that it would be better to leave our 
boats whilst on our land journey over the mountains, 
as the Cotinga at the time was not navigable much 
farther on. 


Cuap. XI. TAMARCHELLE CHIEF. 275 


Our course up the Cotinga had been in a general 
northerly direction through the great savanna, and we 
had now arrived close to the place where it debouched 
from the mountains, from whence its channel was- 
impeded by cataracts, rapids, and rocks. 

Leaving our boats in Wirina creek, we carried our 
stores to the village of Tamarchelle, which consisted 
of six large houses, containing some forty Macusi 
inhabitants. It is situated on the grassy plain, about 
one mile from the river and two from the southern 
foot of the Pacaraima mountains. The inhabitants, 
who were exceedingly dark-skinned, had amongst 
them seven remarkably old people, with gray hair, 
and deeply wrinkled bodies. The chief, or head man, 
was of small stature, old, and dried-up. looking, but as 
lively as a cricket. He wore a hat, with a wide rim, 
made of young palm leaves, and always carried a 
hard-wood war-club, as a sort of badge of office, 
whereby he received the nickname of “ policeman ” 
from my men. 

I had great trouble in getting men to carry loads for 
me on my journey northward through the mountains, 
and was almost at my wits’ end, when two Indians 
came from a village near by, called Terchillewan, and 
offered their services. The morning after our arrival 
everything was ready, my seven men, two Carenacru 
Macusis, and three Terchillewan Indians, having their 
loads, but still leaving two packs unappropriated. 
The old chief then ordered two of his people to take 
them, which they reluctantly did; at the same time 
he informed me that he would accompany us himself, 
merely as a companion. With his hat, oe ap- 

T 


276 UP THE COTINGA. Cuar. Xi. 


peared to be a trouble and a curse to him, and his 
war-club over his shoulder, he joined the line of march, 
and we started on our tramp. 

Arriving at the foot of the mountains we traversed 
a low spur, and following a path not far from the 
eastern bank of the Cotinga, crossed the Waiquah river 
to a Macusi place, from which we went on to our 
guide’s village. On a low hill above the Waiquab, 
where we stopped for a short time, I observed a huge 
artificial mound of earth and small stones, which the 
guide said was the grave of the Mackunaima’s brother. 
It would seem that the Great Spirit is a dweller in 
this region, for an isolated rocky mountain, seen 
from the Cotinga lower down, at the head of the 
Mauitzie river, is called Mackunaima-outa, which 
means the “ Great Spirits house.” I have frequently 
observed small artificial heaps of stones, some three 
or four feet in height, on the sides of the paths over 
the savanna mountains, to which the Indians with me 
in passing have added, by picking up a stone near by 
and dropping it on the heap. I could never learn 
their object ip so doing, for when questioned about it 
they only laughed. 

Not far from Mackunaima’s brother’s grave there is 
a large isolated granite rock, which has been rent from 
top to bottom, presenting a very singular appearance, 
being similar to the large split rocks so frequently 
met with in the cataracts of the large rivers. 

Our Terchillewan guide informed me that he re 
membered the time when Schomburgk spent two 
months at a place called Torong, close by, and that 4 
brother of his had been taken by Schomburgk away 
across the seas. 


Cear. XI. EASY CAMPING. 277 


At Terchillewan our party was joined by the 
guide’s brother, an elderly man, and three women, 
who were going in the direction of Roraima, and 
wishing to have the protection of our party, induced 
our guide to lead us if possible in their direction. 
We had not, therefore, gone far from this place next 
day before he brought us to the Cotinga, evidently the 
direction in which his brother was bound, and said 
that we should have to ford it. I had him told that 
his little game would not do, and that he must take 
me the way I wanted to go, or not at all. The brother 
and party then forded the river in some swift running 
shallows, where the water took them up to their 
waists. Carrying their loads on their heads, and 
holding each other by the hand, they got across with 
some difficulty. 

We then proceeded up the valley of the Cotinga, 
and following it to its junction with that of the Cum- 
paruyamo river, turned up the latter for some little 
distance. On our way up the guide pointed out three 
or four cassava fields, in small groves high up the 
mountain side on the left, some of which, he said, 
belonged to his village, the others to a village a few 
miles off. These fields were at least seven miles from 
his house, and must have been made on account of the 
soil in that part being well adapted for the growth of 
the cassava. 

Having no awning to put up, the act of camp- 
ing was rendered a simple matter, consisting merely 
in tying our hammocks between trees, in any small 
clump of bush selected as a residence for the night. 
The leaves of the trees, having been affected by the 
drought, had curled up so, that they did not afford 


278 UP THE COTINGA. Cuar. XI. 


much protection against the moon’s rays; but I used 
to get some shelter from one side or other of my ham- 
mock. As it is a popular belief in the tropics that 
sleeping in the moonlight twists one’s mouth, I used to 
go to sleep haunted by the pleasant reflection that 
perhaps on waking next morning I should find my 
mouth inconveniently turned towards one ear. 

From the Cumparuyamo valley we uscended the 
porphyry mountains, climbing the precipitous side of 
a deep gorge, and coming to a valley over 1000 feet 
above the sea. Traversing it on the following day in a 
north-easterly direction we passed some small villages 
„and came to the Sacaouta river, crossing it a few miles 
from the spot where I passed it on my way to Roraima 
in 1869. We then ascended a pass to the east of 
Cumararing mountain, 1672 feet above the sea, and 
down into the valley of the Ireng river, putting up at 
a half-finished house for the night. Thus in twelve 
hours’ walking, including some stiff climbing, we ac- 
complished the entire distance between the Cotinga 
and Ireng rivers, while, by the position of the Ireng 
on the old map, the distance is such that it could not 
be done in thirty-six hours. 

Our Indians had three dogs with them which 
started a mikang, and went away in full cry, but the 
little fox covered the rough ground more rapidly than 
they could, and left them in difficulties after a short 
and exciting chase. 

From Cumararing we went north over four great 
ridges until we came to a village, called Tacara-eri- 
mone, where the inhabitants, taking us for Cariwas, fled 
at our approach, but returned after a short absence. 


| 


Cuap. XI. RAINY WEATHER. 279 


Beyond this we passed over a wooded mountain, 
and went down the long sloping side of the valley of 
the Wailongteur river to a village, where we had to 
remain a day, whilst provisions were being prepared 
for us. 

Up to this time the weather had been fine, but as 
we went northward, it began to break up, and rain- 
squalls passed frequently across the country, while 
heavy black clouds hung over the mountains to the 
northward. 

A rainy day in an Indian village is not by any 
means pleasant, for from early morning till night 
gangs of natives squat about watching every move- 
ment, and making one feel what an extraordinary 
individual he must be to form such an object of 
wonder. 

I crossed the Wailongteur in a long corial which had 
Jost one side, and had to be paddled in a very 
gingerly manner on its beam-ends. We traversed 
the valley and ascended the steep sandstone moun- 
tains to the north, from the top of which a magnificent 
view of the valley and plain beyond was obtained, 
where the course of a large river called the Karnang 
was marked out by lines of Itah palms. Beyond lay 
some gloomy-looking, tree-clothed mountains bearing 
various names, foremost amongst which was one 
called Toobabouromé. 

On arriving at the Karnang it was found to be in 
flood, and our Indians said that it was impossible to 
cross. 

The usual fording was in some shallows at the head 
of a fall, thirty feet in height, but owing to the high 


280 UP THE COTINGA. Cagar. XL 


state of the river this was not practicable. I sent 
my men to look for a fording higher up, and after 
some delay they found a place where the river spread 
out to a width of 100 yards. 

Stripping off all our clothing and carrying it in 
bundles on our heads, we waded over with great 
difficulty. One of my men assisted me in keeping my 
footing on the smooth slippery rocks, which formed 
the bottom in the deepest portion, where the water 
took me to my armpits. Where the water ran with 
its greatest force it was not more than waist deep. 
Then the men carried over their loads on their heads, 
in doing which William lost his footing and precipitated 
his pack into the stream. 

We were all greatly amused with my captain, old 
Peterson, and the Tamarchelle chief, who were crossing 
at the same time. Peterson was not very steady on 
his pins in places of that sort, and fearing that he was 
going to be swept away, endeavoured, like a 
drowning man grasping at a straw, to seize the old 
Indian by the shoulder for support. To this the 
Indian objected, and exerted his greatest powers to 
elude the fatal grasp. With his palm-hat and war- 
club, he scuttled along sideways like a withered crab, 
while Peterson’s long arms were waving about within 
a few inches of him. He, however, escaped and 
landed first midst shouts of laughter from the whole 
party. When Peterson gained the bank he appeared 
to be greatly annoyed, seeing nothing laughable in the 
affair. 

That afternoon we turned aside from our line of 
march and visited the Ireng falls of Orindouie. These 


CHar. XI. DISTANT VILLAGES. 281 


consist of three steps of rocks, at intervals of 
100 feet apart, constituting a height of fifty feet, over 
which the river thundered in sheets of white foam. 
We spent a night in some old banaboos built by 
Partamona Indians, who visit the falls for the purpose 
of collecting the aquatic plants, called Houyah, which’ 
they burn for the sake of its ash, which forms an 
ingredient in their mouth pellets. The water above 
the fall, at a height of only 1590 feet above the sea, 
was so cold that on bathing none of us cared to 
remain in it for any length of time. 

The surrounding scenery was wild and beautiful. 
On distant ridges to the eastward I saw some Indian 
villages, the inhabitants of which, our Cumararing 
guide informed us, were turning white and in time 
would be white people. 

From Orindouie we went north until we struck the 
Ireng again, at its junction with a small creek, in 
which the guide searched for, and found, a corial. In 
this we were all ferried in relays to the other side of 
the Ireng, and following a path leading along it, 
arrived in the afternoon at a small village near the 
edge of the forest. From the time of leaving the 
Rupununi until we reached this spot we had been 
travelling continuously through open grass-covered 
country, where trees in thin belts skirted the river's 
banks in places, or nestled in groves here and there on 
mountain sides, as well as formed what are called 
“ Islands ” on the level plains. But here we were at the 
southern edge of the great forest which spreads away 
northward continuously over mountain, vale, and plain. 

We learned that from this on, for some distance, 


282 UP THE COTINGA. Cuar. XL 


there was no path, and that we should have to pro- 
ceed up river in corials. We had to wait a day for 
the arrival of these, and when they came were much 
disappointed at their appearance, which more nearly 
resembled coffins than canoes. They were simply 
long logs of wood hollowed out and rounded at both 
ends, but not spread amidships. The measurements 
of one were as follows: length 33 feet, width 1 foot 
9 inches, and depth 1 foot 2 inches. 

At the village I saw an Indian from the Potaro, 
who had been at Enapowou village on the occasion of 
my visit to that place. The wife of this man had the 
most remarkable head of hair, for quantity and luxuri- 
ance of growth, that I have ever seen. It fell down 
her back the whole length of her body, and was cut 
off square at the end, giving a section at least a foot 
in width, and three inches in thickness. i 

When we were loading our canoes for a start, a 
number of Indians passed quickly up river, propelling 
their canoes up the shallows with long poles, not 
one turning his head to look at us. An old woman 
steering one, kept up a constant howling and wailing 
noise as they passed. We could not find out exactly 
the reason of this, but William said he believed that 
her husband had died while working at a wood- 
cutting grant on the Demerara river, and that the 
sight of our clothed party, resembling the people of 
that locality, had recalled to her memory the loss of 
her dear departed. 

When we came to start, our canoes were so narrow 
and crank that three of them only just held my party 
of twelve individuals, with their loads; consequently 


Cuar. XI. UNCOMFORTABLE CANOES. 283 


the old Tamarchelle chief was obliged to remain 
behind. As the men paddled them they rolled down 
to the water’s edge, first on one side and then slowly 
back on the other, after the fashion of a long iron 
screw steamer in a cross sea. My position was not a 
comfortable one, for being obliged to sit as low as 
possible on three round sticks, I was doubled up, my 
knees and chin being in close proximity. Showers of 
rain fell repeatedly and did not add to the pleasure of 
my situation. 

The river, swollen by recent rain, ran like a mill 
race, so that we could hardly make headway against 
it, and in one rapid ran great risk of an upset, which 
was just averted by the judicious action of one man. 
We passed many single Indian houses or clusters of 
houses, here and there, and got to a landing at a late 
hour, where some fifty people had collected to see us, 
having heard of our presence on the river. They 
were very light colonred, strong and well built, and 
of the Partamona tribe. Their village, called Waipah, 
was about half a mile back from the river, in the 
forest, where we spent the following day (Sunday, 29th 
January). They told the interpreter, William, it was 
currently reported amongst the surrounding inhabi- 
tants that now that a white man had come amongst 
them, their country would sink under water and 
other misfortunes would befall them. Geologists 
have shown that large areas of country are being 
depressed, but this is the first time that a geologist 
has been accused of being the cause of such a sinking 
process. They seemed rather downhearted about the 
expected loss of their country, so to cheer them up I 


284 UP THE COTINGA. Cuar. XL 


got the interpreter to tell them that nothing of the 
sort would follow our harmless visit; adding also 
that when they went to the Demerara to cut or haul 
timber, their presence there did not cause the land to 
subside, then why should a white man’s travelling 
amongst them produce such bad effects? They 
admitted the force of my argument, but nevertheless 
remained of their own opinion. 

They said they had heard of my being at Kaieteur 
fall; of my going under it and coming out “at the 
other side,” which was really news to me. 

During the day spent at Waipah, two parties of 
Indians arrived, one on their way from the Potaro to 
their homes near Roraima, the other going in the 
opposite direction. The latter party having heard of 
our existence, had come considerably out of their way 
to have a look at us. They stated that the Indian 
sorcerer who had drawn such numbers of people to 
his district at Wakopyeng, near Roraima, was then on 
his way to meet me. 

Soon after leaving Waipah we came to large and 
swift-running rapids above two villages which it was 
impossible to pass with corials ; we therefore followed 
a portage path, and leaving our crafts at their foot 
obtained others at a village at their head, in which 
we proceeded only a mile and a half before another 
set of rapids headed us off once more, obliging us to 
land and take shelter for the night at a small village. 

Lying in my bammock that evening under the 
smoke-blackened roof of an Indian house.I listened to 
the steady patter of the rain on the thatch, and its 
splashing drip from the low eaves to the ground, with 


Cuar. XI. EVENING IN INDIAN HOUSE. 285 


gloomy forebodings as to the next day’s work. Cock- 
roaches, as usual, rustled in and out amongst the dry 
leaves encasing the Indian’s stores of corn and dried 
cassava on the sort of loft overhead, whilst now and 
then one of these insects would wing its flight from a 
dark corner and settle with a bang on my hammock. 
The dim little fires beneath the Indian’s hammocks 
gave just enough light to reveal the gloomy nature of 
the abode. 
The Indian men lay in their hammocks talking and 
smoking their bark cigarettes, while the women spun 
by hand, or manufactured their bead dresses, as the 
case might be. An old woman sat over a fire near 
the hammock of my guide, and in a dull, monotonous, 
whingy tone related a long story to him. As night 
comes on this whingy, complaining tone is always 
adopted in conversation by Indian women and 
children, and to a slight extent by the men, and is 
very curious. [I got so tired of hearing the old 
woman’s voice, that to try and break the spell I asked 
William, whose hammock hung close to mine, what in 
the name of goodness was the old woman talking 
about; he replied that she was relating how, not long 
before our visit, the people of her village, including 
herself, made a pilgrimage to see the sorcerer of 
Wakopyeng. That they were nearly starved to death 
owing to the great number of people there consuming 
all the food, and that after a lengthened stay they 
returned home to find their neglected fields contained 
but little cassava; so that now they were on short 
commons until what they had planted had time to 
grow. She also said that this sorcerer, amongst other 


286 UP THE COTINGA. Cuar. XI. 


wonderful performances, could make himself invisible 
at times, and had a little bottle full of fire. 

Many a night of this sort comes back to my 
memory as I pen these lines, and the recollection of 
them is, on the whole, despite the cockroaches, not an 
unpleasant one. 

From this place we went on by a path close to the 
river, which led across branch creeks swollen with 
water, some of which we waded, while others we 
crossed on bridges made of single felled trees. Rain 
fell incessantly, and the trees through which we strug- 
gled were laden with raindrops. At times we had to 
cross mountain spurs with steep ascents and descents, 
so that on the whole the day’s journey was not a 
pleasant one. In the afternoon we met an Indian 
with light hazel eyes, who, with two children, was 
fishing in a creek. He gave up his task and con- 
ducted us to his place in a large sandy fern opening, 
close to the river's edge, where we found two large 
houses containing fifty people, and remained for the 
night. Hearing that we were on the way up, these 
people had erected a small shed, and partially roofed 
it with palm leaves ready for my reception. As the 
weather was showery, and the shed roof small, I slung 
my hammock in one of the houses, but owing to the 
number of fires the heat and smoke soon became so 
unbearable that I had to bolt out and take refuge under 
the shed. When a rain-squall came on I had to take 
down my hammock and retire well under the little 
roof for shelter until it passed off, and on those occa- 
sions the night air, laden with mist, felt cold as I 
turned out of my cosy hammock. 


Cuar. XI. ORINDOUIE FALL. 287 


Just after dark that evening I observed two little 
Indian boys emerge from the house with a firebrand, 
which they coolly, and I suppose thoughtlessly, stuck 
under the thick thatch of the eave of the house. The 
rain not having penetrated the thatch to any distance, 
it was as dry as tinder beneath, and soon caught fire. 
I called out to my men at once, who with some 
Indians ran to the river for water, with which they 
soon extinguished the flames. The Indians treated 
the matter very coolly, saying the children knew no 
better, but were only playing. If I had not seen the 
affair the whole house must have been consumed, and 
then perhaps they would have looked at it in a more 
serious light. 

We went up stream next day in canoes, past two or 
three villages against a strong current, and landed 
near the junction of the Ireng with its large branch, 
the Suquadie river. From thence we ascended a hill 
and walked along a sandy level tract, 1962 feet above 
the sea, coming out on a little savanna at the head of 
a beautiful fall called Orindouie. 

The measurements of this by barometer and line 
gave 213 feet as its total height, and 56 yards as its 
width. 

Descending for a short distance along a slope, 
amongst tall agaves, high grass and mosses, and over 
blocks of rock on its eastern side, I had a good view 
of its front. All the surrounding mountains were 
tree-covered, and looked gloomy and wild. 

Leaving this fall we crossed the little savanna, and 
passing through a village, descended in a corial to a 
twin fall of Wantuana and Wotowanda on the Su- 


288 UP THE COTINGA. Canar. XL 


quadie river, one on either side of a large island. 
Upon some flat jasperous sandstone rocks exposed on 
the savanna were numerous curved figures of the sun, 
snakes, spirals, and circles, formed by Indians of a 
bygone time. 

We breakfasted at the fall on cassava bread and 
some extract of meat, all our tinned meat and salt 
fish having been finished. Owing to the Ireng being 
in flood, its fish would not take the hook, and the 
Indians living on its banks at the time had little 
in their pepper-pots but calaloo leaves, water and 
peppers, wherewith to make their cassava bread pala- 
table. Game being very scarce, owing I think to 
the number of inhabitants in the district, we could 
not obtain any meat food, but at the same time had 
ample supplies of rice and cassava. 

Having arrived at the farthest attainable point 
on the Ireng we turned about on the afternoon of 
February lst, and commenced our return journey in 
corials, being able, owing to the river having fallen 
so much, to pass the rapids without danger, and 
reached Waipah village on the following day. From 
that place I made a land journey to the Chemapeur 
river, and then descended the Ireng to the spot where 
we were ferried across the day after we left the 
first Orindouie fall. After passing the village of 
Uwahparadouie, where we obtained our corials, we 
left the wooded country with its deep shades behind, 
and ran merrily down river, between grass-covered 
mountains and large level tracks of open field-like 
country. The afternoon was fine and the sun shone 
brightly where we were, but on looking back to the 


Cuar. XI. INDIAN HUNTSMEN. 289 


mountains we had left great masses of dark rain- 
clouds still hung over them: 

We put up for the night in some large banaboos 
on the east bank of the river where we had just 
got settled, when a great halloing was heard, and a - 
large party of Indians with a pack of dogs came 
down along the river’s edge in pursuit of a tapir they 
had been after in the wooded country all day. They 
-all appeared greatly excited, and said that it was a 
stray tapir which had recently come to the district. 
They were confident of success, but gave up the chase 
for a time to go back to the village for some food, 
stating that they would return and resume operations 
when the moon rose. About midnight I awoke, hear- 
ing great talking going on, and found that the tapir 
hunters had returned and were taking most of the 
corials we had come down in, which belonged to 
them. 

Crossing the Ireng next day we plodded on to the 
Karnang, which we forded above the fall, owing to 
the river being much lower than when we visited it 
before. In traversing the Wailongteur valley I found 
two stone hatchets, amongst some rocks, and at the 
village, after crossing the river, obtained a very per- 
fect one from the Indians. 

At the village we procured some labba meat and 
a fowl, which was a great treat to us after our meat- 
less diet of the last few days. 

The night we spent at Cumararing was an ex- 
tremely uncomfortable one, for at midnight rain began 
to fall heavily, and the small fringe of sun-shrivelled, 
temporary thatch, forming the roof of the house, 

U 


290 UP THE COTINGA. Cuar. XL 


leaked like a sieve. Wakened by the dripping water, 
we at once took down our hammocks, and placing 
them close to the walls, where a little shelter could 
be obtained, squatted there for hours until the rain 
ceased. A more dejected, miserable-looking set of ob- 
jects than we were, as we saw each other by the light 
of the Indians’ fires, could not be imagined ; some 
with heads tied up in handkerchiefs, nodding drowsily ; 
some seeking consolation in a pipe; and others trying 
to pass the time in conversation. The clay floor of 
the house was soon reduced to a state of mud, which 
the men called by the negro term of “ putta-putta.” 
The rain continued next morning until we had cleared 
Cumararing pàss, when nature putting on a drier 
aspect the sun burst out and soon dried our clothes. 

When resting at breakfast time all hands were 
engaged fishing in the water-holes of a little stream 
amongst great boulders, and caught numbers of small 
fish (Acara sp.), which were at once roasted and eaten 
with great relish. 

We left the Ireng that morning, and after a long 
day’s tramp camped within a couple of miles of the 
Cotinga river, at a water-hole in the Cumparuyamo. 
In such a hot climate as that of Guiana a good walker 
will find that from twenty-five to thirty miles a day, 
over a comparatively level tract, is as much as he 
can accomplish without being completely knocked up. 
Owing to the roughness of the paths, together with 
the heat, the feet become tender and easily chafed, 
and when once chafed are with difficulty healed, un- 
less a few days’ rest can be obtained. 

During my journeyings at this period I seldom 


Cuap. XI. THE SAW-FILER. 291 


slept after three o’clock each morning, usually lying 
awake until daybreak, listening for the call of a little 
savanna hawk which, with extraordinary exactitude, 
heralded the coming morn. Its peculiar call some- 
what resembling the sound produced when sharpening 
a saw, I named it the “saw-filer.” When I heard the 
first call of one, I used to turn my eyes to the east, 
and in a minute or so would see the first red glow of 
morning spread upwards from the horizon. Then 
numbers of other birds would begin their morning 
songs, amongst which the sweet notes of the fly- 
catchers were very predominant. 

A large hawk, called the Cowah, used to utter its 
cries of “cow-cow-cowah ” just at sunset, and until 
a little after dark. The small toucans or bill-birds 
(Rhamphastos vitellinus) are also fond of uttering 
their yelping calls of an evening, or during the day 
on the approach of a shower. When doing so they 
bob their heads up and down in a most extraordinary 
manner, with their large bills pointed upwards. 

At the village near the termination of our journey 
on the Ireng, we were joined by three Indians, who 
stuck to us like leeches, and made extra mouths to 
feed. We could not shake them off, and, to make 
matters worse, at Cumuraring, were joined by four 
more, also bound to Tamarchelle. One man of the 
former party was the tallest Indian I ever met with 
in Guiana, measuring five feet nine inches in height. 

From Cumparuyamo we entered the Cotinga valley, 
and then went by a different and shorter route across 
the mountains to Terchillewan, passing on the way 
through the ruins of Torong village. 

U 2 


292 UP THE COTINGA. Cuar. XI. 


That evening I strolled a little way from the 
village to a knoll to enjoy a quiet pipe, and observe 
the beautiful scene before me. On the little plateau, 
in front of the doors of the houses composing the 
village, sat the red-skinned inhabitants, chatting in 
groups, and forming an interesting sight. The last 
rays of the setting sun lit up the mountain tops with 
reddish-golden lights, while the shadows in the valley 
looked deep and gloomy. Soon the crimson flush in 
the west faded away, and the tropical night settled 
over all. To watch these quiet sunsets always 
afforded me the greatest delight, and were ever fresh 
and new, never losing in the least by repetition. The 
beauties of scenes, viewed in early morning and late 
evening time, will ever remain amongst my pleasant 
recollections of that sunny land. 

Arriving next morning at Tamarchelle, we found 
the old chief with his war-club safe back in his 
dominions again. When I paid our guide and his 
companions for their faithful services, and “old war- 
club's” wife for having taken care of our extra stores 
left in her charge, a number of the people of the place 
demanded pay. As they had done nothing for me, I 
refused to comply with their modest request, upon 
which they became troublesome, and I had to place a 
guard over our stock of negotiz. 

On the morning of February 10th we left Wirina, 
landing in our corials, and descended the Cotinga 
river, running down small channels in many shallow 
cataracts in most extraordinary manner, the coria) I 
was in, a remarkably strong one, bumping frequently 
on the rocks. 


Cmar, XI. ` SHOOT A BLACK TIGER. 293 


At one place we saw a black tiger walking along 
the top of the high bank of the river. Remaining 
quiet for a short time, so that it would not observe us, 
we saw it saunter away inland. Landing at the foot 
of the cliff from which it had disappeared, I climbed 
up, gun in hand, with some difficulty, and not without 
some apprehension that it might show fight on my 
arrival at the top, where it would have me at a dis- 
advantage. Raising my head slowly above the edge, 
I peered round amongst the grass, but could see 
nothing of it. I then gained the top, and, rising into 
a standing position, perceived it crouching in the 
grass, with its back towards me, not more than 
forty yards off. Creeping cautiously to within twenty 
yards I took aim, and sent a charge of BB-shot into 
its body behind the shoulder, whereupon it sprang 
convulsively upwards, and then falling forwards, ex- 
pired after a few struggles. 

It stood about two feet in height, and had a long 
body : none of my men had ever seen one of the kind 
before. Its head was of a brownish-gray colour, and 
its body clothed with black fur, scattered sparingly 
with white hairs. Its under parts were light black, 
with slight indications of barred markings; its tail 
gray and legs black. 

In passing Warara-sararu ponds, we stopped for a 
short time to replenish our larder with ducks, and 
whilst there observed thirty wild cattle feeding at a 
distance. When we got some three hundred yards 
to leeward of them they observed us, and moved 
about uneasily. We then shouted to attract their 
attention, which it did with a vengeance, for they 


294 UP THE COTINGA. Cuar. XL 


all started at a run towards us, keeping close 
together, and wheeling into line came to a stand 
within about one hundred yards of us. Knowing 
that tame cattle are sometimes fierce when at 
large, we argued that wild cattle ought to be very 
much fiercer. We therefore deemed it prudent to 
tree at once, and, selecting the largest tree about, 
swung ourselves gracefully, but swiftly, into its 
branches. The herd, not seeming inclined to ad- 
vance nearer, worked more to leeward to scent us. 
They were full-grown, well-conditioned animals, of 
uniform blackish-brown or light red colours. More 
than half of the lot were bulls, which tore up the 
ground with their fore hoofs in anything but a playful 
manner. I descended from my perch to get a nearer 
look at them, when a gust of wind bore down our 
scent to them, and in one instant they wheeled about 
in the greatest terror, making off at a pace that a 
savanna deer might envy. We could see more than 
a mile in the direction in which they went, to where a 
swell of land hid the country beyond, and this distance 
they cleared in an amazingly short space of time. 
Arriving at the mouth of the Cotinga on the 14th 
of February, we again took advantage of the wind, 
and hoisting jurymasts and sails, scudded down river 
on our way to the Brazilian frontier, Fort of St. 
Joaquim, at the junction of the Takutu with the Rio 
Branco. When night overtook us we camped on a 
sand - beach within some twelve miles of the fort. 
After dark we heard the strokes of the paddles of a 
boat coming up river, the pullers of which were 
singing a Portuguese song. They landed below us 


Cap. XI. FORT ST. JOAQUIM. 295 


where the barking of dogs inland proclaimed the 
existence of an Indian village. From the savanna on 
the west came the pleasant sounds of the lowing of 
cattle borne on the slight evening breeze. 

Early next forenoon we landed at the fort and were 
met by two Brazilian soldiers, one of whom escorted 
me through the entrance into the courtyard of the fort, 
and up a flight of steps to the verandah of a large tile- 
roofed house, where I was met by the Commandant, 
who shook me heartily by the hand. Wishing to 
explain the object of my visit, and not knowing a 
word of Portuguese, while the Commandant did not 
know any English, I called up one of my men 
(Johannes Cornelius) as interpreter. Johannes had 
learned a language called Nicariecaru from the Bra- 
zilians who live on the Essequebo in the vicinity of 
Yucurit island, and one of the soldiers also knew it. 
Telling him what I wished to say to the Commandant 
in English, he told it to the soldier in Nicariecaru, 
and the soldier passed it on in Portuguese; while the 
answer of course worked back in the reverse order. 
When we had exhausted the patience of our inter- 
preters we dismissed them, and sat and smoked 
together for a time. 

Soon after a happy thought seemed to strike the 
Commandant, Senhor Abreo, for he jumped up, and 
hastening into an inner apartment, returned with a 
pint bottle of Allsopp’s India pale ale. Pouring its 
foaming contents into a tumbler, he handed it to me 
with a bow, and an expression of countenance that 
plainly said, this is the most graceful offering I can 
make you, Senhor Englishman—and it was, for had 


296 UP THE COTINGA. Cuar. XL 


not five months and ten days passed away since I had 
partaken of “ the bitter.” 

At 3: p.m. we dined, the Senhéra (Commandant 
Abreo’s wife) presiding, with three of their four little 
children sitting at table. The repast was like a feast 
to me, served as it was on a table with glasses, table- 
cloth, plates, &c.; and consisted of corned beef, fowls, 
and various preparations of farinha. 

After dinner he had three horses saddled, one of 
which I mounted, and, accompanied by a soldier on 
another, took a long ride over the great grassy plain 
to the south. The third horse would let no one 
mount it; the two soldiers who tried to do so hardly 
reached the saddle before it threw them over its head. 

In the plain through which we passed were large 
shallow ponds with rushes and ‘lilies, tenanted by 
flocks of large white cranes and jabirus, as tame as 
common geese to us on horseback, allowing us to ride 
close to them without seeming the least disturbed. 
The water which lies over this plain for a part of the 
year produces fine grass for the eighty or ninety head 
of cattle kept in the vicinity for the use of the fort. 
Beyond the plain was ordinary elevated gravelly and 
sandy savanna, from the edge of which, looking across 
the marshy level, the old fort looked grim and lonely. 

I enjoyed a good gallop homewards over ground as 
smooth as a carpet, although the high peaked Spanish 
saddle had its drawbacks, being hard and stony; while 
the lightest touch on the reins acted on the extra- 
ordinarily severe bit in my horse’s mouth, bringing 
him instantly to a dead stand. When I got back to 
the fort my men mounted the horses one after the 


| 


| 


Cuar. XI. FORT ST. JOAQUIM. 297 


other, to try a short ride, most of them never having 
ridden in their lives. One man had just mounted 
when he dropped the reins and begged the others to 
help him down, which they did, thinking that he was 
only frightened. He was immediately seized with a 
sort of sea-sickness, which turned into fever and 
headache, from which he did not recover for two 
days. 

The fort, which is built of red sandstone on a low 
ridge, not much above the level of floods, has a large 
‘gateway in front, set back so as to be commanded 
from the battlements above. The walls are of con- 
siderable thickness, and the embrasures have been 
built in with masonry. There were five unmounted 
cannon lying about, one of which was of English 
manufacture, having a crown and the letters G.R. 
upon it. On either side of the gateway are guard 
houses occupied by the five soldiers who man the 
place, while the Commandant’s house occupies the full 
width of the back of the fort. The whole place was 
in a most untidy dilapidated condition, pigs, dogs, and 
fowls roaming about everywhere. 

There are three other old buildings along the river’s 
edge, west of the fort, one being the remains of an old 
chapel, and the other two, houses of the burriqueiro or 
cattle minders. On the west bank of the Parima or 
Rio Branco is a government cattle farm or fasenda, 
called St. Bento, with 3000 head of cattle, while on 
the east bank of the Parima higher up lies the fasenda 
called St. Miguel. A large batelon or undecked craft, 
covered aft with a toldo of palm thatch, which was 
lying at the landing of St. Bento, is used for conveying 


298 UP THE COTINGA. Czar. XI. 


cattle down to Manios, the capital of the province of 
Amazonas, at the mouth of the Rio Negro. 

Small government steamers come up the Rio Branco 
to some rapids not far below the fort, and at the time 
of my visit one was expected daily with stores, and 
twenty soldiers to put the fort in thorough repair. 

Two soldiers were on duty that night, pacing up and 
down the battlements, and calling out the hours. At 
8 p.m. the guard turned out and sang the tattoo in a most 
extraordinary and unmilitary style. Next morning 
the Commandant presented us with two quarters of an 
ox just slaughtered, expressing his deep regret that he 
had nothing of greater value to offer as a parting gift, 
and begged me to accept his hospitality for another 
day. Thanking him most sincerely for his generous 
gift, and stating how impossible it was for me to 
accept his kind invitation, I bid adieu to him and his 
good Senhéra and departed on my journey. 

The first night out on our return journey to Pirara 
landing, we dried our ox meat on a barbacue, and 
though thereby rendering it almost tasteless preserved 
it from speedy decay. 

On the succeeding day, as we were cooking break- 
fast on the river's side, a soldier and a Macusi, both on 
horseback, rode up and inquired whether we had seen 
anything of three of the fort soldiers, who had deserted 
during the night; but ‘on our answering in the nega- 
tive they immediately rode away. 

As we worked slowly up stream against the strong 
current and breeze, we frequently saw great numbers 
of large river turtle sunning themselves upon the low 
sand-banks at the water's edge, which scuttled rapidly 


Cuar. XI. RETURN TO KARINAMBO. 299 


off and disappeared in the river at our approach. It 
was then their laying season, and all the sand-banks 
were full of their nests and eggs. 

In a low band of treés on one part of the river's 
side, there was a band of red howling monkeys. How 
on earth they got from the great forests to this isolated 
position it is impossible to say, unless they had 
repeatedly crossed over large stretches of open 
savanna. 

It was very pleasant of an evening, when camped 
on the vast sand-beaches of the Takutu, to watch the 
flocks of jabiru, negrocopes, white cranes, and ducks 
coming out to the river from the savanna ponds, 
where they had been feeding all day, to their roosting 
places in groves in the vicinity of the river. 

When we arrived at the Ireng river mouth we hid 
one corial amongst the trees and continued our 
journey up that river in the other two. We spied 
some Indians on the opposite side who followed up 
the bank of the stream for some distance, and cutting 
across the hends, came out on the river’s edge as we 
were passing. They said they were Arecunas from 
Roraima, on their way to the Rupununi, with cotton 
balls and blow-pipes for barter; and asked us to ferry 
them across as they had no other means of gaining the 
other side. This we did, and they went on their way 
without a word of thanks. 

We arrived at the mouth of the Pirara river on the 
22nd of February, and leaving our boats walked 
across to Pirara landing; from thence we descended 
the Rupununi in my boat to Karinambo village, where, 
on the 24th of the same month, I had the pleasure of 


800 UP THE COTINGA. Cuar. XL 


rejoining Mr. Pollard once more. I found him in 
most comfortable quarters, occupying the upper story 
of the stilted house, and on taking up my abode with 
him felt as if I had come home again. Those who 
have never roughed it in the wilds, cannot form any 
idea of the pleasures of mind attending the satisfactory 
completion of a portion of that roughing it, or of even 
then gaining a haven of temporary rest. 


Czar. XII. OVER THE TAKUTU SAVANNA. 301 


CHAPTER XII. 
OVER THE TAKUTU SAVANNA. 


At Karinambo — Walk to the Takutu — From the Takutu to Cursato 
— Ascent of Cursato Mountain — Chase Ant-bear — Pinniyettinow — 
From the Takutu to the Rupununi— Double Snipe — Cartunariba 
—Coolie— Down the Rupununi— Visit Chewow River — Leave 
Karinambo and descend Rupununi— Ascend Essequebo to Primos 
Inlet — Cross through Forest to Berbice River — Descend Essequebo 
to Penal Settlement. 


WE were nine days at Karinambo, awaiting the arrival 
of the provision boat, “ Ataraipu,” which we expected 
on the lst day of March at the latest, but which did 
not arrive until the morning of the 6th, owing to 
illness amongst the crew and an accident to my mes- 
senger, Cameron. This consisted of a severe wound 
in the sole of one of his feet, caused by treading upon 
a sting-ray, whilst wading in the water in hauling 
the boat up Twasinki Itaboo. These rays are large 
fish of the Trygonidæ family, and are armed with two 
bony processes or spines at the base of the tail, covered 
with a sort of slimy skin, and having serrated edges. 
One process is usually longer than the other, being 
from two to three inches in length, and lies flat upon 
the tail. When, however, anyone touches or treads 
upon the fish, it raises the spines and also strikes with 
the tail, producing a large jagged wound, which is 
attended with acute agony, not only in- the part but 
over other portions of the body, lasting for hours. 


302 OVER THE TAKUTU SAVANNA. Cear. XI. 


The wound takes weeks to heal, and I believe the 
effects of the poison remain in the system for a con- 
siderable length of time. 

The days spent at Karinambo passed slowly, but not 
unpleasantly, as many amusing incidents occurred to 
pass the time away. When Mr. Pollard and I took 
our afternoon bathe from a sand-beach near the 
landing, a huge cayman used invariably to swim slowly 
down stream until opposite us, when it closed in to the 
opposite shore and took up its station there to watch 
us. We used to swim and dive within forty yards of 
it, but it never made any attempt to come across and 
attack us. The little black flies that covered us as we 
emerged from the water, were far more to be dreaded 
than it was. 

Near its station were some ferruginous rocks fall of 
cavities under water, in which skin fish of irregular 
form took up their abode, and were easily shot with 
arrows. My men called them “old boots,” from their 
resemblance in form to those articles, but not from 
any toughness of texture, for when boiled they were 
soft and gluey. 

An Indian youth, called Maipourie, used to keep 
the one family of the village in fish, and every after- 
noon he was to be seen sauntering forth, bow and 
arrow in hand, to the river or neighbouring inlets, 
from which he returned at night laden with all sorts 
of fish. Another and smaller youth, who went by the 
name of Kanaima, had a trick of passing a string up 
his nose and bringing it out of his mouth. In most 
Indian houses pieces of thick roughly-plated fibre or 
cord, as thick as codline, and a yard in length, are 


Caap, XII. ANT-LIONS. 303 


seen hanging up in the roof. These have all been 
used once in the way I have just described, that is, 
passed up through the nose of the owner of the house, 
and drawn out by the mouth, for the purpose of giving 
him good luck in hunting. 

A white man, named Christy, passed up river one 
day with a Brazilian Indian, on his way to the abode 
of the latter, on the Rupununi, in the Canucu moun- 
tains. He stopped and had a chat with us, stating 
that he had come up to barter for hammocks. We 
asked for news of the French and Prussian war, when 
he said that “the French had driven the Prussians 
back from Paris to Metz.” Doubtless he invented 
this piece of news at the moment, rather than give us 
none at all. 

I used sometimes to watch the manœuvres of the 
larvee of the ant-lion (Myrmeleontide), which form 
little conical pits in the sand and dust, on the floors of 
sheds or unused houses, by jerking out little showers 
of sand, as they work their bodies round and round in 
the bottoms of their pits. If an ant fell into the pits 
and tried to climb out, it was knocked back repeatedly 
by well-directed showers of sand, until it became ex- 
hausted, when the owner seized it in its long nippers, 
and there held it until it died. The rapidity with 
which they can work backwards in sand, and the way 
they feign death when caught, are very curious. 

Early on the morning following the arrival of the 
provision boat, I sent it back to the Penal Settlement 
to be paid off, and then with Mr. Pollard went up to 
Pirara landing, to make a walking journey round the 
western end of the Canucu mountains and over the 


304 OVER THE TAKUTU SAVANNA. Cuap. XIL 


Takutu savanna, intending, if possible, to descend 
the Rupununi through the Canucus. With eleven 
Indians from Carenacru, two of whom were women 
(wives of some of the men), and five of my own men, 
we journeyed across the savanna to Yarewah on the 
Takutu, where we met two of my men and three 
Indians, whom I had despatched to Pirara river to 
bring the corials we had used on the Cotinga, up to 
Yarewah to deliver them to their owner. 

The women of our party, like all other Macasi 
females, had the usual pin ornament between the 
lower lip and chin. This consists of from one to three 
pins passed through a small hole in the lower lip with 
their points outwards and heads inside. 

From Yarewah I sent Peterson and one man on to 
Fort St. Joaquim, with a present of brandy to the 
Commandant, and with the rest of the party, Mr. 
Pollard and I continued our journey. We made an 
attempt to ascend the Takutu in corials, but on going 
a mile or two, found it was so shallow and barred 
across with rocks, that we gave up the idea. Leaving 
Yarewah we made straight for the Canucu mountains, 
and on arriving near the foot of Mount Damikipang, 
we turned south-west, skirting their base past their 
south-western termination to some ponds, at which we 
camped. Some of our Indians went hunting that 
evening, and shot a fine Beyou deer, which was a very 
acceptable addition to our larder. Whilst hunting 
they started another deer, which cantered close past 
our camp, apparently without seeing us; but when it 
got well to leeward, and crossed our scent, it wheeled 
round, coming to a stand for an instant, like a pointer 


Cmar. XII. - A BEYOU. 305 


when it strikes the scent of a snipe. It then evidently 
came to the conclusion that we were enemies, for 
wheeling round again it made off at fall speed, and 
was soon lost to sight. It is well known that the 
beyou trust very little to their eyesight, depending 
almost entirely on the powers of their nasal organs 
for ascertaining the whereabouts and quality of their 
enemies, 

We crossed the Suwaraowra river and came to a 
Wapisiana village called Berkutone, from which we 
went west to the Takutu, and spent a day in a corial, 
washing unsuccessfully, in likely-looking spots, for 
gold. At Berkutone we learned that the deserters 
from the fort had crossed the Takutu, and passed 
through that village on their way to the Rupununi. 
They had been followed by two men on horseback as 
far only as the banks of the Takutu. 

From Berkutone we went on to Rumutokoke, where 
we spent a night. There the people had a tame jabiru, 
which stalked about in a most deliberate and stately 
way, as if every step it took was a matter of deep 
deliberation. In spite of its sage looks there was 
some grim fun in it, as it seemed to enjoy playing a 
practical joke now and then on the village dogs; for 
whenever it had a chance it stalked stealthily behind 
one and delivered a well-directed prod with its pointed 
beak, full on the dog’s back, which caused the dog to 
fly and the old jabiru to “smile inwardly.” 

The water used by the villagers, as usual on many 
parts of the savanna, was obtained from holes dug in 
a boggy place, surrounded.by trees; it was dirty- 
looking, but cool and good for drinking purposes. 

x 


306 OVER THE TAKUTU SAVANNA. Cuar. XII. 


In bathing at them we had to bail out water with a 
calabash, walk a little way off so that it might not run 
back, dash it over our bodies, and then return for 
more. Whilst bathing there a lot of very pretty 
birds collected in the surrounding trees in order to 
obtain their evening drink, one of which, a dark- 
coloured thrush, was exceedingly tame, drinking from 
a pool within a few yards of me. 

Our course from Rumutokoke lay for a considerable 
distance in a southerly direction, over a dry sandy 
savanna, within a mile or two of the Takutu, and 
parallel to its course. After passing through three 
villages, our day’s walk terminated at a fourth, 
called Dahdaad, close to Tatat hill. There was very 
little breeze during the day, and the afternoon’s walk 
was hot and stifling. The house we put up in at 
Dahdaad was a large square mud-walled one, unlike 
the generality of Indian houses, The Takutu passed 
within a quarter of a mile of it, and was very low, its 
water covered with a slight scum, hardly showing any 
signs of running. 

Next day we went easterly, coming to a large 
village, called Cocobeanaruwow, close to Cursato 
mountain. From it, in the afternoon, Mr. Pollard 
and I went with a guide and three of my men to 
Cursato. The path up the mountain was rough, 
steep, and rocky, and lying on its leeward portion 
was cut off from even the slightest indication of a 
breeze. The shrivelled leaves of the small trees with 
which it was clothed formed very little protection 
against the fierce rays of the afternoon sun, and 
consequently the heat was something fearful. Being 


Cuap. XII. VIEW FROM CURSATO. 307 


in good training, I determined to see what I could 
accomplish in the way of climbing under the above- 
mentioned trying circumstances, and with the naked 
guide in front started from the foot at a quick pace. 
When about three-quarters of the way up the guide, 
down whose back the perspiration was trickling in 
little rills, stopped and looked at me, as if to propose 
a rest. I anticipated this, and was ready with an 
answer in similar dumb show, for neither of us could 
understand the other’s language. Waving my arms 
upwards I pointed towards the mountain’s top, and 
started on. He responded manfully to my call, and 
on we went, but every now and then between his 
heavy breathing I heard a suppressed sigh. 

The path did not lead straight up, but wound 
about considerably, and in one part led along a fern 
opening where there was no shelter. There I stopped 
for an instant to look down on the surrounding 
country, as well as to relieve two kinks in sinews 
above the top of each knee, induced by rapid and 
continuous climbing. On gaining the top we threw 
ourselves down on a small open grassy space, where 
a slight movement of the air felt delightfully re- 
freshing, and gazed down on the splendid bird’s-eye 
view to the southward, ranging from south-east to due 
west. 

This presented to the eye a vast undulating open 
plain, stretching away southward in the far distance 
until it seemed to melt gradually into the hazy 
atmosphere. Lying out upon it were a few very 
singular isolated hills, not far from Cursato, which 
relieved the dead level of the foreground. In the 

xX 2 


308 OVER THE TAKUTU SAVANNA. Cuar. XII. 


south-west, beyond the Takutu, were groups of high 
mountains just discernible in the distance. The light 
yellowish savanna, relieved here and there by the 
bright green of fresh springing grass; the dark green 
bands of trees lining the watercourses or grouped in 
groves on the hills in the foreground; the purplish 
cast of the rock exposures on the isolated hills; and 
the faint blue of the distant mountains, formed the 
various colourings of this splendid scene. 

Dead stillness reigned around, and all bird and 
mammal life seemed to have hidden away from the 
burning sun. All nature was at rest and peace, and 
in this spot one felt as if it would be pleasant to 
dream away existence, free from the trials and cares 
of busy life. 

On looking at my watch I found that we had come 
up in forty minutes, having ascended in that time a 
vertical space of 1060 feet. This would be nothing 
of a climb in a temperate climate, but is by no means 
an easy task in the torrid zone, within 22° of the 
equator. 

After resting we went to an Indian house, on the 
edge of the forest which capped the extensive undu- 
lating top of the mountain, where we got some ripe 
bananas from a number of bunches hanging up to 
the roof. The guide went to a spring near by and 
procured some delicious water, which was very accept- 
able to our parched throats. 

My three men arrived about a quarter of an hour 
after I did, and said they had left Mr. Pollard resting 
on the mountain’s side. I sent one of them down to 
him with some water and bananas to freshen him up, 


Cuap. XII. ANT-BEAR HUNT. 309 


and after an hour’s stay, during which he did not 
appear, I descended, overtaking him just as he arrived 
at the foot. 

The following day being a Sunday, we remained at 
Cocobeanaruwow to rest, having a house assigned to 
us. Four Indians came in the afternoon with cassava 
and dried fish for us, who, being partially intoxicated 
with paiworie, made themselves disagreeable, so I had 
them told to leave the house. On finding out im- 
mediately after that one of them was the owner of 
the house, I sent for him, and got the interpreter to 
explain that it was quite through a mistake that he 
was turned out of his own house, and that, of course, 
he could come in and out as he liked. 

In the houses of this village, as in most all the 
Indian houses on the savannas, were numbers of 
deers’ antlers, with skulls attached, hung up to the 
centre pole or rafters, which are kept, I suppose, as 
trophies of the chase. 

On leaving the village next morning we observed a 
large ant-bear crossing the plain about half a mile off. 
It could not go with any speed, but lumbered heavily 
along, even the little Indian boys being able to keep 
up with it. My men headed it off, and drove it up to 
the village, whilst Mr. Pollard and I stood looking on. 
It made straight for the nearest house, and bolted in 
at the only door of that edifice. No sooner had it dis- 
appeared within than out poured a lot of screaming 
women and children, mixed up with dogs and fowls, 
presenting a most laughable sight, and close on their 
heels came the affrighted creature. Away it went 
through the village, followed by a crowd of boys and 


310 OVER THE TAKUTU SAVANNA. Cuar. XII. 


men, but before it got far it was knocked on the head 
with a stick and stretched senseless. 

We took an easterly direction from the village over 
the savanna, midst clusters of hills, coming at the end 
of a day’s tramp to a village called Pinniyettenow, 
about midway between the Takutu and Rupununi 
rivers. | 

During the day’s walk we had fine views of the 
distant Canucu mountains to the north, and that 
singular three-peaked mountain, called Saeraerie, 
which from some points of view resembles a steamer 
under sail. 

The worst feature in walking over the savannas is 
the manner in which the dry spiky seeds on the top of 
‘each grass stem work through one’s trousers, and 
stick like needles into one’s legs, where they produce 
such irritation, that no peace is got until they have 
been pulled out. 

At Pinniyettenow we engaged a sturdy little Indian 
man as guide onwards, and a capital one he made. 
When we were on the point of starting, one Carenacru 
carrier, who had sprained his ankle, and limped along 
for several days, said he could go no farther, and at 
the same time five others announced their intention 
of returning to their homes. Having no means of 
making them go farther, we had their loads put up in 
one of the houses, and continued our journey without 
them. This’ was the first and only time, in all my 
walking journeys, that any Indians abandoned me in 
this manner. 

We went southward past Pinniyette mountain and 
across the Macudood river, a branch of the Rupununi, 


Cmar. XI. MUSICAL FROGS. 811 


coming soon after to a branch of the Takutu, called 
Rhawow. 

There we were shown a Enae a quarter of a 
mile in length, across which some Indians, during the 
last rainy season, had taken a large corial from the 
Takutu to the Rupununi. 

That night we camped on the Mepitewow river, 
where, owing to the rain coming down in torrents 
about midnight, I was obliged to take down my 
hammock and sit till morning under the awning in 
which Mr. Pollard’s hammock was slung. With the 
splash of the rain from the ground upon me, I spent a 
miserable and sleepless night. Most of the men and 
Indians got shelter under some low kennel-like huts 
near by. 

Some frogs, probably of a tree variety, aroused from 
their torpid condition by the rain, and hailing its 
advent with joy, uttered their cries during the night 
and until early morn. They were the most musical 
frogs I have ever heard, and it was really pleasant to 
listen to their bell-like voices, uttering a call like the 
words “ clong-clarng ” lengthened out. 

We reached the blackened remains of a burnt 
village, called Coarawow, with a charred cassava field, 
not far from the Takutu, some ten miles N.N.E. of 
Tuarutu mountains. Our guide, who seemed aston- 
ished at the desolate condition of the place, was at a 
loss to account for its destruction. 

From this we went zig-zagging across the savanna 
to Cau-urua on the Rupununi, passing through two 
villages, and walking the greater part of the way 
without a path. 


312 OVER THE TAKUTO SAVANNA. Cuap. XII. 


At pools in a river's bed, the men caught by hand 
and shot with arrows numbers of a small fish called 
Yarrow, which, in their endeavours to escape, wriggled 
over the pebbles lying between one pool and another, 
with great rapidity. A small crab, caught in one of 
these pools, had an immense number of minute, fully 
formed young adhering to its under part, between the 
base of the cephalo-thorax and abdomen, in the place 
in which they had been hatched from the ova. 

In this portion of the journey I recognized with 
pleasure many distayt landmarks on the Quitaro 
savanna, sach as Tomboro, Carawaimintow, and other 
mountains that I had visited a year and a half 
previously. 

Before reaching the site of the old village of Cau- 
urua, we camped close to a village where, during the 
night, a number of birds on the wing uttered a most 
peculiar call, resembling the words, “ What a clock— 
what a clock.” Before uttering their cries, they 
evidently swooped down from a considerable height 
in the air, as indicated by the rushing sound of their 
wings. My men said these birds were the large 
double snipe. : 

Amongst the groves of large Itah palms, which 
grow in swampy hollows all over the savannas, were 
flocks of the small green macaws, which wheel about 
making fearful screeching noises. The beautiful little 
orange, red, and green parroquets, called kissi-kissi 
(Conurus solstitialis), are not seen in this part, but 
frequent the valleys of the Pacaraima mountains. 

Whilst journeying from Cau-urua to mount Patti- 
ghetiku, I had a disagreeable touch of fever, which 


Czar. XI. ATORAI CAPTAIN. 313 


made my walk very unpleasant. Late that afternoon, 
the guide pointed out a line of trees, about six miles 
off, marking the place where we would camp for the 
night; but feeling unequal to more walking in my 
feverish condition, I got him to take us to the 
Rupununi, which was about a mile and a half away, ` 
and there camped. Just as we were about to enter 
the belt of bush lining the river, we came suddenly 
upon two large deer which were feeding in some long 
grass, making eight seen that day. 

From that camp we went in a westerly direction 
to Cartunariba village, the residence of an Atorai, 
named Robert, who spoke very fair English, and 
styled himself the ‘‘ Captain of the Atorai nation.” 
The village was close to Pinniyettenow, where we had 
left some of our stores, and where we now sought 
them, but found that one pack had been taken away 
by the party of Indians who had left us at that place. 
The Suwaraowra river lies close to Cartunariba, and 
at the time contained only one pool of milky water 
(near the village) of about fifty yards in length. 
From one end of this pool the Indians dipped water 
for drinking, using the other for bathing purposes. 
Captain Robert, strange to relate, possessed a fine 
Brazilian pony, and a high peak saddle and bridle, 
of which he seemed rather proud. 

The day we spent at Cartunariba, two Indians came 
in who had escaped from a farm near Fort St. Joaquim, 
on which they were serving as cattle minders. They 
were both dressed in light-coloured clothes, and looked 
fat and well, though they said they had been badly 
treated. Fever of a malignant type was making 


314 OVER THE TAKUTU SAVANNA. Cgar. XIL 


havoc in the village of Pinniyettenow, and that day a 
little boy died of it. 

Mr. Pollard, who was not feeling well, owing to the 
bad quality of the water we had been drinking on the 
journey, thought it better to borrow the horse and 
ride the next day’s journey to Darawow. He stood 
the jolting pace of the horse, and the hard, uncomfort- 
able saddle for about a mile, and then jumped of, 
preferring any amount of walking to that mode of 
progression. 

On reaching Daruwow village we put up in a large 
house belonging to a young Atorai Indian, named 
Christian, who we found was another swell like Robert, 
wearing clothes, and also claiming to be the chief of 
the Atorai Indians by lineal descent. He was an 
Indian in ten thousand, for he had been sent by his 
father, the old original Atorai chief, to the Grove to 
school and afterwards to a school in Georgetown, 
where he had learnt to read, write, and cipher. He 
spoke English, I might almost say fluently, and asked 
me what was the news. The only other male occu- 
pant of the place was the East Indian Coolie I had 
seen before living with the Caribs at Itaballia. He 
was now acting the part of companion to Christian, 
and taking life easily, for, as he said, he had ‘‘ plenty 
eat, plenty drink, no work.” 

A Creole trader from the coast, named John Bracey, 
had a residence in this village, but at the time was 
away from home, having gone down to the Grove 
with hammocks which he had purchased from the 
Indians, and taken most of the inhabitants of the 
place with him. 


Cuap. XII. DESCEND RUPUNUNI. 315 


Next day we crossed the Rupununi, opposite the 
end of Tarucupani mountain, and entered the valley 
of that river where it passes through the Canucu 
mountains. Coming to a Wapisiana village called 
Cabarooda we inquired whether corials could be got 
to descend the Rupununi, and were told that they 
could, but that the river was then too shallow for the 
purpose. Visiting it we found it very shallow indeed, 
with bars of rock across it, and returned to the 
village for the night. 

On the following day we got two corials from dif- 
ferent villages and dragged one a short way over the 
rocks, losing half a day in so doing, when we found 
the river's channel beyond so insignificant, that we 
gave up the idea of trying to descend in that way. 
The Indians then informed us that if we walked down 
the river’s bed to a creek, called Perapoko, we would 
find the river navigable from that on, and that there 
we could get corials. This seemed a feasible under- 
taking, and better than walking back round the 
western end of the Canucu mountains, so we started 
at noon to try the proposed route. At the convex 
sides of the river’s bed, as. it wound about, were 
extensive sand-beaches occupying its greater portion 
and in many instances its full width, the small body 
of running water finding its way by percolating be- 
neath. We walked along these, and on coming to 
the end of one waded across to the commencement 
of another—sometimes with the water only tó our 
ankles, at others with it to our knees. When a 
deep hole intervened we had to land and make our 
way, for some distance, through the dense foliage 


316 OVER THE TAKUTU SAVANNA. Cuar. XIL 


of moras on the narrow level strip of alluvium form- 
ing the banks, at the foot of the high wooded moun- 
tains which bounded it on either side. The walking 
on the deep dry sand was most tiresome, whilst the 
heat of the sun’s direct rays, added to the radiated 
heat from the sand, was almost insupportable. Four 
and a half hours of this weary plodding brought us 
to a sand-beach, at the end of a creek, beyond which 
there appeared to be deep water and no sand-beaches 
as far as we could see. We concluded that this was 
Perapoko creek, and therefore camped, after securing 
two small canoes which we found hauled up on a sand- 
beach. 

On searching next morning we found a path which 
we followed through a heavy forest, coming in half an 
hour to a Macusi village, the owner of which led us 
by another path to a landing farther down river, and 
provided us with some small wretched old corials. 
These, together with the two we got at the creek 
mouth, held all our party, and onwards we went re- 
joicing. During the whole of that day’s journey and 
part of the next we found the river very shallow, 
and often had to drag the corials over places where 
there was only a depth of two or three inches of water. 

In the afternoon of the day we left Perapoko, on 
approaching a village on the river’s bank, we saw a 
white man standing at the waters edge, who we 
found, on landing, was the man Christy, that had 
come up to try his hand at trading with Indians. 
He told me he was living on farinha, left there by 
the Brazilian Indian who had brought him up, and 
had nothing else to eat. Thinking under the cir- 


Cuap. XIT. ACCIDENT IN RAPIDS. 317 


cumstances he would be anxious to get away, I offered 
him a passage down in my boat, which, however, he 
refused. I told him that I would leave him some salt 
fish and biscuit at Karinambo, and on his sending 
down he would find it stowed away at that-place. 

After passing the mouth of the Mapari river, 
where the Rupununi receives a large accession of 
water, we had no more difficulties in the way of 
shallows. We, however, suffered considerable incon- 
venience from the heavy rain which constantly fell, 
and were obliged to make leaky substitutes for um- 
brellas with a few urua palm leaves. 

A little beyond its junction with the Mapari, the 
Rupununi leaves the Canucu mountains, flowing first 
through a wooded and afterwards an open plain. 

In running down the small rapids of Urua, one of 
our corials containing five Macusis and their loads, 
was upset through the mismanagement of the two 
Indians, who acted as bowman and steersman. In 
this rapid there are two channels with two steps each; 
whilst running down the first step, those having con- 
trol of the boat had their choice of continuing straight 
on in one channel over the second step, or of turning 
off at right angles and passing over the second step in 
the other channel. The Indians did not agree upon 
a joint course of action upon arriving at the head of 
the rapid, as they should have done, but on reaching 
the second step the steersman endeavoured to turn the 
corial off at right angles, while the bowman kept its 
bow up for the straight run. Thus the corial was 
swept broadside on to the second step, upset, and 
rolled over and over down it like a barrel. Mr. 


318 OVER THE TAKUTU SAVANNA. Cear. XIL 


Pollard and I had run down in safety and awaited the 
coming of the other canoes in the pool below, never 
expecting to see one come down in the manner I have 
related, and then drift slowly away in the pool, 
bottom upwards, with three Indians clinging to it, 
and two others swimming for the shore. 

We all paddled to the spot to rescue those on 
the boat’s bottom, whilst my men dived in the deep 
pool for the sunken materials. The Indians seemed 
to have lost all presence of mind, and gazed stupidly 
on, never attempting to assist in the search. After 
some time occupied in diving, all the missing things, 
with the exception of a saucepan and a load of cassava, 
were secured. Amongst the things recovered was a 
gun, which being swept by the strong current down 
the smooth rocks of the pool’s bottom, was found 
some distance below the spot. Getting the corial to 
rights, we continued our journey and reached Pirara 
landing that evening. 

After a short journey from that place to the 
Chewow river, near the Ireng—made for the purpose 
of examining the salt incrustations on the savanna, 
from which the Indians manufacture an impure salt— 
we continued our journey to Karinambo. There I 
found Peterson, who had returned from the fort, 
whither I had sent him from Yarewah, bringing me a 
letter of thanks, in Portuguese, from the Commandant, 
and a present of a parrot from the Senhéra. 

I paid off all our Indians, with the exception of 
faithful Cowarie and Sarquah, who remained with me, 
and delivered our Perapoko corials up to the Indians 
who came for them. 

On Saturday, April 8th, with regret I bade adieu 


Cuap. XII. LEAVE KARINAMBO. 319 


for ever to old Karinambo and the pleasant savanna 
country. The Indian family, who remained at Kari- 
nambo during the time our stores were left there, were 
to leave soon after us to take up their residence at 
Simoni, so the old village would be deserted. A year 
or two will suffice to hasten the decay that has already 
commenced in the remaining houses, when they will 
fall, or perhaps be burnt by a savanna fire, leaving 
few traces of the village behind. I had frequently 
watched the moon rise over the great plain to the 
east, casting its pale light over this little village, with 
its happy Indian inhabitants, and my band of men, 
filling it with life and good cheer; but now, it was sad 
to reflect that the next and subsequent full moons 
would lighten up only the deserted houses, and then 
after a time nothing but their ruins. | 

Running down the Rupununi, we arrived at Apo- 
terie on the Essequebo, where we were welcomed by 
our Carib friends; then proceeding up that river to 
Primos inlet, we cut our way through the forest to the 
Berbice river. In olden times there was an Indian 
path across, of which not a vestige remained; we 
therefore steered by compass, marking our track 
with the cutlass, and performed the distance from 
river to river in eight hours, At this period the 
long rainy season had thoroughly set in, so that 
from the time of our leaving Karinambo until the 
conclusion of our journey, we had almost constant 
showers of rain, day and night, with but few lucid 
intervals of sunshine. Our walk, therefore, through 
swamps and across streams, amongst wet undergrowth 
and dripping trees, was anything but pleasant. 

In crossing one small stream the men obtained a 


320 OVER THE TAKUTU SAVANNA. Cuar. XII. 


number of small fish called daree, by catching them 
in their hands after they had maimed them with the 
cutlass. These fish were to be seen darting in shoals 
up the stream, or in little parties of eight or ten 
nestling together, with their backs above water, in 
shallow places on beds of decayed leaves, where they 
were spawning, having taken advantage of the flooded 
state of the stream to force themselves far up it. 

The Berbice, where we struck it, was between thirty 
and forty yards wide, running swiftly, being swollen 
by the rains. The moment we came upon its edge a 
cayman uttered its bellowing snort, and came swim- 
ming rapidly down river to have a look at us. My 
men answered it.in cayman fashion, which seemed to 
enrage it considerably, and on arriving opposite us it 
headed up stream, coming to a stop within twenty 
yards of us. Then it closed in to the bank under a 
tree, where it remained with its cruel-looking eyes 
fixed upon us. Having no means of dislodging it, we 
were obliged to bathe upon some rocks, which were a 
foot or two under water, within ten yards of it, one 
of us keeping an eye upon it while the other dipped, 
in case it should dive and make a rush at our legs. I 
must confess that taking a bath under the circum- 
stances was rather risky, for if we had slipped off the 
rock we should have been in the cayman’s clutches 
in a moment. Of course we were quite prepared to 
pop out of the water if it only winked an eye, but it 
never stirred. l 

Our camp was enlivened during the evening and 
night by the bellowing of all kinds of frogs in the 
surrounding swamps. Amongst these I distinguished 


Cmar. XII. ASCENT OF MACCARI. 321 


frog voices that I had never heard before, one re- 
sembling the shouting of a human being, and the 
other the rapid revolution of the wheels of a clock. 

Cutting down some trees on the rivers edge, we 
marked the spot in order that it might be easily 
recognized when I ascended this river from its mouth, 
and then returned to the Essequebo, accomplishing the 
distance from river to river in the space of five hours 
and a half. We continued our homeward: journey 
down the Essequebo until we reached the vicinity of 
Maccari mountain, where we landed, and cutting our
