way through the forest to its foot, ascended to its 
summit. Owing to the precipitous nature of its sides 
and the thickness of the undergrowth all the way 
from the river’s edge, this was a difficult task. 

A sharp-leaved species of Bromelia, growing 
thickly on the level, was most difficult to pass, and a 
path had to be cut through it with cutlasses. In 
performing this task the leading man narrowly escaped 
being bitten by a large poisonous labaria snake, 
which was coiled up in a bed of leaves. Another 
man was nearly struck by a huge dry limb of a tree, 
thrown down by a member of a gang of quata 
monkeys. These monkeys also acted in such an 
offensive manner towards us, that we were obliged to 
rid ourselves of their company by sending a shot or 
two amongst them. 

We gained the top by clambering up gullies in 
some places and along narrow ledges in the sandstone 
rock in others, having frequently during the ascent to 
cling to tree-roots for support. From the top we had 
a splendid view of the surrounding forest-covered 

Y 


322 OVER THE TAKUTU SAVANNA. Cear. XIL 


country, and of the range of mountains from Makara- 
pan to the Essequebo. Being all dreadfully thirsty 
from our climb we made a search for water, and 
finding a patch of saturated moss on the face of a cliff, 
where water oozed out of some beds of sandstone, we 
were obliged to quench our thirst by squeezing the 
moisture from handfuls of moss into our mouths, at 
the risk of swallowing all sorts of minute insects. 

When we arrived at Comuti mountain, we ascended 
it in the afternoon for the purpose of taking some 
observations. From its summit near the great Comuti 
rock, looking out over the river’s valley, at the 
clusters of mountains beyond, we witnessed the ap- 
proach of a heavy rain-storm. Low clouds, as black 
as night, advanced rapidly from the eastward, from 
which fell torrents of rain upon the already soddened 
earth, whilst the sun shone brightly in front of the 
dark scud. Gusts of wind soon ruffled the calm sur- 
face of the river down in the valley beneath our feet, 
and hummed ominously through the tree tops around, 
bringing with it the sabdued roar of the storm of rain 
on the forest leaves. Gradually the rain-cloud swept 
down upon us, enveloping the whole scene in an inky 
pall, having almost the darkness of night. Under 
large palm leaves, that we had cut and lashed in 
groups to tree stems, we got some shelter till the 
storm passed on. 

Then the scene before us changed, and in place 
of the angry-looking black scud, a low layer of white 
fleecy-looking mist moved rapidly before the wind, 
broken up into patches, between which the forest 
looked clean and refreshed by its bath. This soon 


Cuar. XII. AFTER A STORM. 323 


passed off, leaving only those curious patches of white 
mist, which rest over various parts of a forest-covered 
country for a considerable time after a rain-storm has 
passed over it, especially in the hollows on mountain 
sides. After hanging for a time over one spot, one of 
these clouds will rise swiftly up with a swirling 
motion, like the smoke rising from a fire, and then 
completely vanish in the air. 7 

The rain-storm having delayed our movements, we 
were obliged to return to our boats for the night, and 
re-ascend the mountain on the following morning. 
What a different scene met our gaze from its top, at 
that early hour, to what we had witnessed on the 
previous day. Now, the whole face of the country 
below us, away out to the horizon eastward, was 
covered with a sheet of mist, which, lit up by the 
sun’s rays, resembled a sea of molten silver. We 
traversed this table-topped mountain westward for a 
considerable distance, and then returning to our boats 
resumed our homeward journey. 

On the mountain’s top we saw a number of the 
large, black, poisonous Monoorie ants, the sting of 
which is very dangerous, producing most serious 
effects on some constitutions. One of my men told me 
that he nearly died from the stings given by three of 
these insects. 

Not finding water on the mountain, we had to resort 
to that method of quenching thirst which is oftener 
spoken of than accomplished. I refer to the custom 
of procuring water from a vine called the Water- 
with (Vitis sp.). My men sought for and found a 
number of these vines, which had wound themselves 

Y2 


324 OVER THE TAKUTU SAVANNA. Cuar. XIL 


round the stems of large trees. Cutting them off as 
high as they could reach, they severed them quickly 
lower down, obtaining portions of stems some five feet 
in length, and from three to six inches in diameter. 
Holding these vertically, the sap, which appeared to 
be nothing but pure, clear, cool water, ran quickly out, 
and was caught in a cup and drank. From one length 
of the largest size we obtained at least a pint of water. 

When we reached that portion of the Essequebo 
where it approaches nearest to the Demerara river, at 
a place called Cumparu, we crossed over to the latter, 
following a well-beaten Indian path. About three- 
fourths of the way across we emerged from the forest 
upon an open, sandy tract, covered here and there 
with shrubs, ferns, and coarse grasses, and having 
numbers of blackened dead tree-stems, either standing 
or prostrate, which showed plainly that it had origin- 
ally been covered with forest. A great fire, many 
years ago, in some particularly dry season, swept 
through it, devouring the forest, and leaving only 
those blackened stems remaining. The forests in this 
country are not, as a rule, at all liable to ravages by 
fire, owing to their dense foliage keeping their stems 
moist, and to the trees forming them being of an 
incombustible nature. 

We came out on the Demerara at an old deserted 
wood-cutting grant, where we spent the night, and 
returned to the Essequebo next day, accomplishing the 
distance from river to river in four hours. The weather 
at the time was execrable, constant rain being the 
order of both day and night; in fact we had but little 
fine weather after our homeward journey commenced. 


Cuar. XII. RETURN TO GEORGETOWN. 325 


Just before reaching our boats on the Essequebo, I 
started three abouyah on the path; firing at one I was 
lucky enough to bowl over two with the one charge. 

As our voyage drew toa close we all looked forward — 
with pleasure to our speedy return to civilization. 

We reached the Penal Settlement two days after 
leaving Cumparu path end, and finally arrived at 
Georgetown on the 4th day of May, where we were 
welcomed back by many kind friends.

CHAPTER XIII. 
ON THE CORENTYNE. 


Journey to Skeldon — Oreala — Description of Journey on the Corentyne 
— Discovery of New River — Jaguars and Pumas — Mode of hunting 
them ‘with Dogs — Scarcity of Indians — Game — Perai — Haimars 
Sting-Rays — Trees in Flower— Monkeys breaking Brazil Nuts — 
Mosquito Worms — Fraternity of Wasps, Ants, and Mocking Birds — 
Attack on Tamrian Ants by Ecitons — Return to Skeldon. 


AFTER the long rainy season of 1871 had passed 
away, I made arrangements for a journey up the 
Corentyne river to its source, and having sent my 
men, boats, and stores by a schooner to Skeldon, at 
the mouth of that river, I left Georgetown on the 
30th of August. Travelling down the coast eastward, 
first by railway and then by mail cart, I arrived at 
New Amsterdam, the capital of the county of Berbice, 
and from thence was driven to Skeldon by my friend 
and companion at Kaieteur, Mr. King. 

At Skeldon I was joined by the Rev. W. Veness, 
who, wishing to visit his Mission station at Oreala, 
some sixty miles up river, took the offer of a seat in 
my boat, and accompanied me to that place, where we 
occupied quarters in the schoolroom for a day. 

The Mission is situated on a low strip of alluvium 
at the foot of sloping wooded cliffs, which, extending 
along the river’s edge lower down, become almost per- 
pendicular, and lose their forest covering. These cliffs, 


Cuar. XIIL OREALA. 327 


composed of red and white clays and sands, are about 
sixty-five feet iu height. From their top, stretching 
back for a mile or two, is a small open tract of grass- 
covered country or savanna, with the characteristic 
foliage of such regions. The residences of the Indians 
are poorly built and dirty looking; but the members 
of the Arowaak, Warrow, and Carib tribes inhabiting 
them, were clean and dressed in civilized costumes. 

Leaving Oreala, accompanied by Mr. Veness, I pro- 
ceeded up river, and passing Siparuta cliffs, arrived at 
an Arowaak village, called Apura, on the Dutch side 
of the river, where we spent the night. This was our 
last night under a roof for a period of four months’ 
duration, and, strange to say, at the end of that time 
this same roof was the first to give us shelter. 

Just before dusk immense numbers of parrots, of 
the kind called “ Screechers” (Psittacus aestivus), flew 
over us, making for Parua island, where they settled 
on low trees. They appeared to be in a most excited 
condition, constantly screeching and rising up in hun- 
dreds over the trees, where they hovered and resettled 
at intervals, until night closed in and put a stop to 
their flittings. Next morning, as we were passing 
the island, they were making as much noise as on the 
previous night, and, leaving in gangs every now and 
then, flew back in the direction of the coast to their 
feeding grounds for the day. 

A short distance above Tomatai Mr. Veness left 
me, to return in his corial to Oreala, and I was once 
more without a companion. 

The travelling on this river was in every way so 
similar to that experienced on former journeys, that it 


328 ON THE CORENTYNE. Cuap. XII. 


would only be a tiresome repetition of what has been 
before recorded were I to give an account of our 
labours amongst countless cataracts, our anxieties and 
fatigues. I will therefore confine myself to an outline 
of the journey, only recording any incidents that are 
worth relating. 

On the seventh day after leaving Oreala we passed 
up the first set of cataracts, and on the eighth arrived 
at the foot of a great cataract, called Wonotobo. 
There we were engaged for five days in cutting a 
portage path, three-quarters of a mile in length, and 
conveying our boats and stores along it to the head 
of the fall, at a level of 90 feet above its foot. From 
that on, for a period of eight and a half days, until we 
arrived at a fall called King Frederick William IV., 
we were combating with cataracts, many of which 
were of considerable size. We were detained six days 
at this latter fall, in making a portage three-quarters 
of a mile in length, and getting stores and one boat 
over ; then we formed a rendezvous camp on an island 
immediately above it. Sending back the other boat to 
Oreala to bring up a supply of stores that were to be 
sent from Berbice, I went on with one boat, intending 
to explore the main Corentyne; but, taking channels 
along the western side of the river, amongst a con- 
fusing array of islands of all sizes, discovered a river 
hitherto unknown. This we followed up for three 
weeks, until, on the 25th of October, we came to huge 
granite rocks, which, lying close together, formed a 
barrier to our farther progress. We could of course 
have gone on with some little difficulty; but as we 
had traced the river up to the boundary of Brazil, to a

Czar. XIII. `~ LAND JOURNEY. 329 


part where it was only twenty-four yards wide and very 
shallow, and where trees had in places fallen across it, 
I considered it advisable not to follow it farther. We 
were at a distance of 470 miles from the mouth of the 
Corentyne, and 670 feet above the level of the sea. 

Leaving our boat in charge of two men, I made 
a journey across to the Essequebo river and back 
through the densest forest I ever traversed; and 
which, owing to our having misjudged the difficulties 
of the undertaking, occupied us twelve and a half 
days. Not having taken sufficient provisions at first, 
we were obliged, when halfway across, to return to 
our boat for more, and thus experienced considerable 
delay. 

We then descended the New River, as I have called 
it, to our rendezvous island, where we found our boat’s 
crew with the stores they had brought up. With 
both boats we ascended the Corentyne, and, keeping 
along its eastern side, followed what Schomburgk 
considered to be the main river, as far up as it was 
possible to go, where numerous large fallen trees 
lying across the stream had to be cut through to 
allow our boats to pass, to a place some eight or ten 
miles above the site of the old Drio Indian village, 
from which Schomburgk embarked on the occasion of 
his memorable and hazardous descent of this river in 
the year 1843. We had thus traced up the Corentyne 
to where it was only twenty yards wide, at a distance 
of 475 miles, by its windings, from its mouth, and at 
an altitude of 700 feet above the level of the sea. 

We then returned to a branch called the Aramatau, 
and ascended it for a couple of days; and finally 


330 ON THE CORENTYNE. Cuap. XII. 


descended the Corentyne to a place above Wonotobo 
falls, where a path once led across to the Berbice river. 
Not a sign of this path remained, so we cut our way 
through the forest to that river, an operation which 
occupied us three and a half days; while in returning 
we accomplished the distance from river to river in 
eight and a half hours. 

We then descended the Corentyne, arriving at 
Oreala on the 18th of January, 1872. 

When we commenced our descent of the New River 
on the 9th of November, the short rainy season had 
set in, and from that time until our return to Skeldon, 
we experienced very wet weather, with lucid intervals 
of sunshine now and then. I had left my boat’s tent 
at the foot of the Wonotobo fall, and during this 
rainy period had only the slight protection afforded 
by an umbrella, 

During our journey up and down the Cutari and 
Aramatau rivers, the rain fell incessantly, causing 
their waters to rise to overflowing. Our labours upon 
them were exceedingly arduous, the fallen trees lying 
across them being very numerous, and giving the 
men constant and difficult axe work. Those trees 
that were half immersed were the most difficult to 
sever, the force of the blow of the axe being lessened 
considerably by its passage through the water. They 
were invariably of the hardest and toughest wood, 
and some I measured were two feet nine inches in 
diameter. 

We had many close shaves in running down steep 
and dangerous) xtaracts, owing to my bowman not 
working with', .ch skill—in fact he seemed to get 


Cuar. XIII. JAGUARS. 331 


nervous as we approached a cataract, and lose all 
command over himself. My former bowman, Johannes 
Cornelius, was, during this and subsequent journeys, 
captain of my boat, in place of Peterson, who had 
become rather weak in the arms for such difficult 
work, though none knew how to steer in a cataract 
better than he did. 

In ascending the portion of the Corentyne below 
Tehmeri rocks we saw a large jaguar standing on a 
granite rock, close to the river’s bank, which imme- 
diately bolted into the forest as we paddled to the 
spot. Glancing up at the place at which it had 
disappeared I saw it sitting down gazing intently at 
us, without showing the least signs of fear. I took 
aim behind its shoulder and gave it a charge of large 
shot, which caused it to bound forwards, fall, and roll 
over. But, immediately regaining its feet, it made off 
into the forest. We landed and tracked it through 
the forest by the blood drops on the leaves for a 
quarter of a mile, and then lost all further trace; but 
there was little doubt that it was badly wounded and 
would probably die. With a dog to track it out, it 
would most assuredly have been found. 

During different periods of our journey on four 
other occasions I obtained shots at jaguars, and was 
equally unfortunate in every instance. Of these 
two were wounded, and two were missed completely, 
for which I cannot account, unless it was my over 
anxiety to slay that made my aim uncertain. There 
is no doubt that the tenacity of life in these animals is 
very great, and that, even when $ rely wounded, 
they can run a long distance before fi ty succumbing. 


332 ON THE CORENTYNE. Cuar. XIIL 


In all but one instance my shots were fired from the 
boat, the unsteady movement of which would no doubt 
spoil the aim in some degree. 

The ‘second jaguar we met with was lying upon a 
fiat rock sunning itself, and allowed us to paddle up 
to within thirty yards of it—close enough to see it 
scan us with a puzzled but by no means timid look, 
as it wondered what manner of animals we were. We 
could see it shake its ears to drive off the flies which 
settled upon them. It was an exciting moment to me 
as I covered it with my breechloader, then charged 
with a bullet cartridge, and drew the trigger, expect- 
ing fully to see it stiffen out in death; but simul- 
taneously with the sound of the report, it bounded 
into the forest and was gone. We traced it for about 
one hundred yards by the blood which must have 
flowed from a serious wound, and then lost all further 
signs of the animal. 

The third was lying full length on a sloping log, 
one end of which rested on the bank, the other in the 
water. We were coming down stream at the time, so 
we swung the boat round when opposite it, not more 
than ten yards off. As we approached it worked its 
claws on the log, after the manner of cats, but never 
moved its body. I thought to myself that, if wounded, 
it must fal] into the water and be there despatched ; 
but such was not the case, for at the report it jamped 
round with surprising agility and ran up the log into 
the forest. 

The fourth jaguar which I fired at was badly 
wounded, and on the rock where it had stood we 
picked up a bunch of fur, with the outer coat of one of 


Cuap. XIII. JAGUARS. 333 


its claws, which, in the scramble it made when struck, 
had been broken off on the rough surface of the rock. 
In tracing it a short distance by the blood, we came 
upon an enormous toad, fully a foot in length. 

My last shot at a jaguar was obtained in the forest, 
and was equally unsuccessful. One of the men 
happened to go a few yards behind one of our camping 
places, when he heard a movement behind him; 
turning round he saw a jaguar leisurely surveying 
him. He fled to camp with his story, and I went in 
search of the animal, accompanied by one man armed 
with a cutlass. We did not go far before we saw its 
tracks in the sandy bed of a dry watercourse, and 
concluded that it had gone off. We gave up all hopes 
of seeing it, and, turning round, were on the point of 
making our way back to camp, when my companion 
suddenly exclaimed, “ Look! look! the tiger!” 
Glancing at the spot indicated I saw it crouching in a 
thicket with its head bent down, its body swaying 
from side to side, glaring at us with eyes of a greenish 
metallic hue. The brute had evidently been following 
us whilst we were searching for it, and was working 
itself into a rage. I took as good aim at its head as I 
could, and fired; but instead of seeing it lying dead, 
I heard it bounding and crashing through the forest. 
at a fearful pace. 

One of my men got a shot at a jaguar on a sand 
beach, where it passed within twenty feet of him, as 
he crouched on some rocks. The only effect the shot 
had on the animal was to make it gallop away a few 
yards, then turn for an instant and look at him. The 
men whom I left in charge of the boat on the New 


334 ON THE CORENTYNE. Cuar. XIN. 


River, cut open a hollow log containing young 
accouries, and took them out. Their squeals on being 
seized attracted a puma, which ran close up to the 
men, apparently wishing to get the accouries, when 
one of them fired at it and it made off. 

= One evening, whilst returning to camp along the 
portage path that we were cutting at Wonobobo falls, 
I walked faster than the men, and got some two 
hundred yards in advance. As I rose the slope of an 
uneven piece of ground, I saw a large puma (Felis 
concolor) advancing along the other side of the rise 
towards me, with its nose down on the ground. The 
moment I saw it I stopped; and at the same instant 
it tossed up its head and seeing me also came to a 
stand. With its body half crouched, its head erect, 
and its eyes round and black, from its pupils having 
expanded in the dusky light, it looked at once a noble 
and an appalling sight. I glanced back along our 
wide path to see if any of my men were coming, as at 
the moment I felt that it was not well to be alone 
without some weapon of defence, and I knew that one 
of them had a gun; but nothing could I see. As 
long as I did not move the puma remained motionless 
also, and thus we stood, some fifteen yards apart, 
eyeing one another curiously. I had beard that the 
human voice is potent in scaring most wild beasts, and 
feeling that the time had arrived to do something 
desperate, I waved my arms in the air and shouted 
loudly. The effect on the tiger was electrical ; it 
turned quickly on one side, and in two bounds was 
lost in the forest. I waited until my men came up, 
however, before passing the place at which it dis- 


Crap. XIII. ACCOURIE AND TIGER. 335 


appeared, in case it might only be lying in ambush 
there; but we saw nothing more of it. 

When returning down the portage and dragging | 
our boats over, we saw a jaguar sitting on a log near 
the same spot, watching our movements with evident 
curiosity, and although the men were singing as they 
hauled the boats along, it did not seem to mind the 
noise. As soon as it saw that it was observed, it 
jumped off the log, and with a low growl made off. 
From this I infer that the flight of my puma must 
have been owing more to the windmill-like motion of 
my arms than to my voice. 

During our journey across from the New River to 
the Essequebo, we were cooking breakfast one morning, 
when we beard a tremendous rushing and crashing 
noise coming towards us through the forest, and then 
caught a glimpse of an accourie flying for dear life 
before a black tiger. Just after they passed the ac- 
courie gave a heartrending scream as the tiger seized 
it, but on my men rushing up to the spot, the tiger 
left its prey and fled. When picked up the accourie 
was quite dead, but on examination showed no marks 
whatever of the tiger’s teeth. The tiger had evidently 
killed it by springing upon it with its legs close 
together, the weight of its body giving such a blow, 
that the accourie’s life was fairly knocked out. The 
men found its dead body just beyond a large log, 
slightly raised from the ground, under which it had 
bolted and lost some headway, while the cunning tiger 
took the log in its stride and so came, as it intended, 
on the poor accourie’s back, with the result we have 
seen. 


336 ON THE CORENTYNE. Cuar. XIIL 


On returning to the head of the New River for 
provisions, we were followed for many miles by a 
tiger, for on going back we saw its huge tracks in the 
swampy places on our path. 

With good hunting dogs fine jaguar and puma 
hunting might be obtained on the banks of this river, 
where without doubt they are exceedingly numerous. 
Many of the Indian hunting dogs trained for deer or 
tapir will hunt tigers. When on the track of either 
of those animals, should they come across the scent of 
a tiger, their eager and confident manner of pressing 
on after the game is immediately changed, and with 
the hair on their backs erect they become cautious 
and nervous to a degree, jumping at even the snap- 
ping of a twig. Abandoning the hunt they take up 
the tiger's track and follow it. But should the hunts- 
man call them from it, or not cheer them on with his 
voice from time to time, they exhibit great fear, and 
keeping close to his heels cannot be induced to hunt 
any more in that district for the day. On the con- 
trary, if allowed to follow the tiger, they track it up 
with caution, being fully aware of the cunning dodge 
practised by that animal; which is, when the dog is 
close at hand, to spring to one side and lie in ambush 
until it passes, when with one spring the dog is seized. 
Ordinary dogs would fall a prey to this trap, but not 
the self-taught tiger dogs. Their fine powers of scent 
warn them of their near approach to the quarry, when 
they advance with great caution, never failing to 
detect the tiger in time, and when once their eye is 
upon their enemy it has no chance of escape. In its 
pride of strength, the jaguar scorns the dogs, and with 


Cuap. XIII. TIGER HUNTING. 337 


a rush like a ball from a cannon springs madly at one 
of them, feeling sure that it cannot escape. It has 
reckoned, however, without its host, for the dog eludes 
the spring with ease, and with great quickness flies 
on the tiger’s flank, giving it a severe nip. As the 
tiger turns with a growl of pain and disappointment, 
the dog is off to a little distance, yelping lustily and 
never remaining still an instant, but darting first on 
one side and then on the other. After one or two 
ineffectual charges the tiger gives it up, and on the 
approach of the hunter springs into the nearest suit- 
able tree, which it seldom leaves alive. 

According to the Indians there are various kinds 
of tigers and tiger cats, each of which hunts one kind 
of animal in particular, the call of which it can 
‘imitate. Thus there is the deer, tapir, abouyah, and 
kieroonie tiger, &c. The deer tiger is the puma, and 
I have heard it affirmed that Indians have been 
deceived by the manner in which it imitates the 
bleating of the forest deer. The wailah or tapir tiger 
is pure black, and is said to be of great size. 

The tameness of jaguars and pumas on this river is 
owing to the almost entire absence of human beings 
in this part of the country, for along its winding course 
from the fifth to the third parallel of latitude, on 
the main Corentyne, there are no Indians now living; 
nor do any Indians travel over the portion included 
between King Frederick William IV. cataract, and 
Wonotobo. The New River also is entirely destitute 
of human inhabitants. 

From the last outlying Indian settlement in the 


tidal portion of the Corentyne until we came to that 
Z 


338 ON THE CORENTYNE. Cuar, XIIL 


portion of river lying midway between the mouths of 
its branches, the Pani and Sipariwini, we never met 
with any signs of Indians. Near the latter branch, 
however, we saw two landing places, and paths leading 
inland, one on each side of the river. From that on 
the west bank, ten minutes’ walk brought us to a 
village of three houses in a clearing in the forest. 
Judging from the appearance of the weeds which 
had sprung up around the houses, we concluded that 
their owners had gone away some two months pre- 
viously ; and, from the fact that they had left all their 
large earthen cooking pots, two bows, and three cut- 
lasses, we concluded that they were only temporarily 
absent, on a visit perhaps to some neighbouring place. 
Two of the cutlasses were of Dutch, the third of 
English manufacture. In the clearing grew plantains, 
cashew, cassava, and sugar-cane. 

The path on the east bank, which we followed for 
a quarter of a mile, was evidently a track leading 
across to some of the large rivers of Surinam. 

At a creek mouth not far south of this we saw an 
old encampment and a large felled locust tree stripped 
of its bark, from which a woodskin had been made. 
Above the village we also met with an encampment 
which had been used not more than two weeks pre- 
viously, and at the spot a woodskin had also been 
made from a large purple-heart tree. 

On hauling up Sir W. Raleigh’s cataract we found 
a woodskin tied to a tree on the edge of an island, in 
which were three paddles, the string by which a dog 
had been tied, and on the land close by some ripe 
plantains and an earthen pot. It was evident that 


Cuap. XIII. GAME. 339 


the owners of this canoe had been taking it over the 
cataract when we arrived at its foot, and becoming 
alarmed at the sight of our large party, had fled. 
In returning we looked in the same place, and found 
that the canoe, plantains, and pot had been taken 
away. 

We saw the posts of the houses of the old Drio 
village, on the Cutari river, at the point from which 
Schomburgk embarked when he descended the Coren- 
tyne. 

Some very curious and gigantic specimens of In- 
dian picture-writing were seen on rocks in portions 
of the river, from Tebmeri to the mouth of the New 
river. In two places also, in the forest, were the sites 
of ancient villages, marked by a deep black soil mixed 
with broken pottery. 

Bush-hogs, deer, capybara, tapirs, and all manner of 
animals before met with were abundant in the forests 
bordering this river. Of the first mentioned we killed 
a large number for our larder, living chiefly on them 
for a length of time. 

The Corentyne and its branches were literally 
teeming with fish of various kinds, the greater number 
being Haimara and Perai. The latter were so abun- 
dant and ferocious that at times it was dangerous, 
when bathing, to go into the water to a greater depth 
than up to one’s knees. Even then small bodies of 
these hungry creatures would swim in and make a 
dash close up to our legs, and then retreat to a short 
distance. They actually bit the steering paddles as 
they were drawn through the water astern of the 
boats. A tapir which I shot swimming across the 

Z 2 


340 . ON THE CORENTYNE. Cmar. XIII. 


river had its nose eaten off by them whilst we were 
towing it to the shore. 

Of an evening tbe men used to catch some of them 
for sport, and in taking the hook from their mouths 
produce a wound from which the blood ran freely. 
On throwing them back into the water in this injured 
condition they were immediately set upon and de- 
voured by their companions. Even as one was being 
hauled in on the line, its comrades, seeing that it was 
in difficulties, attacked it at once. One day, when 
the boat was hauled in to some rocks, a few of the 
men were engaged shooting fish near by, and in so 
doing wounded a large haimara. Having escaped 
from its human tormentors, it made for the open river, 
but was instantly attacked by perai attracted by the 
blood escaping from its wound, and was driven back 
to the shelter of the rocks close to the boat, from 
which I had a good view of the chase. The large fish 
followed by its savage enemies reminded me of a 
parallel case on land—a stricken deer pursued by 
wolves. 

The perai, fortunately, lie only off sand-beaches and 
in quiet pools, not frequenting the cataracts, where 
their presence would be anything but acceptable to 
the men when working in the water. I was fortunate 
enough to find the spawning place of some perai 
on the matted clusters of fibrous roots of some lianes, 
which hung from the branches of a tree into the 
water, amongst which much earthy sediment had 
collected, and many small aquatic plants had grown. 
The sediment gave weight to the roots which kept 
the clusters under water, and the force of the current 


~ 


Crap, XIII. PERAI NESTS. 341 


made them buoyant, giving the lianes a slope when the 
river was high, which kept them not far from its 
surface. My attention was attracted to them by two 
perai lying close to them, with their heads up stream, 
as the men said, engaged in watching their eggs. 
Procuring one of the roots I examined it, and found 
amongst it numbers of single eggs and clusters of 
small jelly-like young, which had been already 
hatched. The eggs were white and of about one- 
eighth of an inch in diameter, with a hard exterior. 
The young were very little larger, and had a glutinous 
surface, which caused them to adhere together on 
being taken from the water. They had not acquired 
any powers of locomotion, but could just wriggle their 
tails like tadpoles. Under a lens they resembled the 
egg devoid of its covering, with a gelatinous ridge 
around three-quarters of its circumference, one end of 
which expanded into a knob (probably the head), while 
the other termination was flattened and tail-like. I 
could not detect any eyes or mouth in them, but their 
bodies were speckled with gray markings of colouring 
matter. 

The huge lowlow frequents the river, but, strange 
to say, the common pacu was not met with. In its 
place, but by no means common, was a small kind—a 
true pacu—of probably a new species, a few of which 
were shot by the men in two localities. They are 
about the size of the common cartabac, but not so deep 
bodied. Their general colour is a bluish-black, with 
two large orange-coloured oval areas on both sides of 
the body, which occupy about one-half of the surface 
of each side, extending from the back of the gills to 


342 ON THE CORENTYNE. Cuap. XIII. 


the base of the tail. The scales on the orange- 
coloured portion are very minute, especially towards 
their centres. On each side of their throats the scales 
have a purplish and golden play of colours, while 
the iris of their eyes is red. They possess the same 
nutty flavour as the common paca. One of my men 
affirmed that he had shot a few of these fish on the - 
Essequebo, whilst all the others said they had never 
seen the kind before. I regretted exceedingly not 
having a jar of alcohol with me in which to preserve 
a specimen. 

' The haimaras haunt the mouths of branch streams 
amongst the rocks and sunken logs, and lie in the 
cavities under the masses. of rock in mid river. 
From places of this sort the men would obtain, in 
the course of a few minutes, as many of these fish 
with hook and line as we required for daily use. 

In hauling the boats up the shallow rapids near 
the mouth of the Cutari the men, whilst wading, were 
frequently struck by conger eels. Every now and 
then a man would call out “Congler, congler,” and 
jumping into the boat, rub his shins which had been 
benumbed by a touch from one of these fish. After 
half a minute or so the numbness wore off and he 
took to the water again. The boat being in a critical 
position at the time it was impossible for the men 
to leave the water. They had therefore to brave out 
the shocks from these batteries, which must have 
been very slight, given probably by small eels, or 
they could not have stood them. 

Small long-bodied fish were very common, and 
one kind, called Couramai, took the baited hook as 


Cuap. XIII. STING-RAY WOUND. 343 


long as the fisherman who threw the line was out of 
sight. | 

The lukenanie or sunfish was captured by my men 
in a singular manner. They manufactured an exceed- 
ingly rude fly out of a bunch of silk grass (Bromelia 
Karatas) fibre, and attached it to a large hook with 
a short line and rod. Drawing it rapidly over pools 
amongst the rocks it was immediately taken by the 
lukenanie, as the artificial fly is struck by the 
trout. 

Sting-rays were frequently seen on the sandy 
bottom or grovelling for worms in the muddy banks ~ 
under water. My interpreter, William, was unfor- 
tunate enough to step upon one, which, being of the 
colour of the bottom, was not observed. It drove 
its spine or sting into the side of his instep, pro- 
ducing a jagged wound which bled profusely. I 
immediately put laudanum on the wound and gave 
him a strong dose of ammonia. In a quarter of 
an hour after he was writhing on the ground in 
great agony, actually screaming at times with the 
pain which he felt in the wounded part, in his groin, 
and under one armpit. His foot and leg were so 
cold that he got one man to light a fire and support 
his foot over it, persisting in trying to put it in the 
flames. I gave him two doses of laudanum, one 
shortly after the other, without relieving his sufferings 
in the slightest degree. After three hours of intense 
pain he became easier, but had returns of it at in- 
tervals during the night. For a week he was unable 
to put his foot to the ground, and the wound did not 
heal thoroughly for six weeks. 


344 ON THE CORENTYNE. Cuar. XM. 


One kind of ray very frequently seen was of a dark 
colour spotted with circular yellow patches. 

Another dangerous creature which we met was 
the poisonous water-snake, the haimaralli, one of 
which I shot as it lay in shallow water with its head 
raised above the surface. It was eight feet six inches 
in length, and of a pale reddish-brown colour, with 
twin rings of black at intervals across its back, which 
converged together on its sides; while its under parts 
were whitish-yellow speckled with black. It had two 
short poison fangs, one on each side of the upper jaw, 
sheathed in large fleshy sockets like the claws of 
a cat. 

One night, whilst taking meridian altitudes of stars 
for latitude on the New River, I became aware of a 
strong smell of musk, and at first thought it pro- 
ceeded from an alligator; but on looking round I 
observed, by the aid of the lamplight, the head of a 
snake poked out from a hole between the rocks. One 
of the men made a blow at it with a stick, upon which 
it disappeared. Most snakes emit a musky smell 
when disturbed. 

We did not see a single cayman during our stay 
on the Corentyne. It may safely be inferred that 
there are none on that river, a singular fact that 
cannot be accounted for. 

Small alligators, of about four feet in length, are nu- 
merous, however, and one of them one night carried off 
a young cat which the men had brought from George- 
town. Poor puss had gone to the water’s edge to 
drink, when the alligator, with one blow of its tail, 
swept it into the water and carried it away. On 


Cuar. XIII. ALLIGATOR AND CAT. 345 


the following morning we saw the alligator with its 
snout resting on a rock near by, so I shot it; the 
men dragging it out of the water and leaving it on 
the rocks. On returning, some months afterwards, 
we camped at the same place, and there amongst the 
bones of the alligator saw those of the cat bleaching 
in the sun. 

Iguanas were numerous, and on one occasion when 
one in a tree overhead was shot at with an arrow, 
it jumped down to gain the water, but not calculating 
its distance accurately, landed on the back of. one 
of the men, who, seeing it coming, ducked his head 
and dropped his paddle overboard. The paddle being 
made of paruru or paddle-wood was heavy, and sank, 
and the man was afraid to dive for it amongst the 
numerous perai. 

The only kind of powie, or curassow bird, ma with, 
was the common yellow-billed one (Crax alector), 
which was in abundance along the river's banks, where 
we shot aS many as we required in passing along. 
Maroodies of two kinds (the common and white- 
headed), the duraquaras, trumpet-birds, and muscovy 
ducks were also numerous. 

On Christmas day, within fifty yards of our camp, 
the men shot eight maroodies, four of each sort, which 
were feeding on the berries of trees. 

The white-headed maroodie (Penelope pipile) makes 
an extraordinary rattling noise with its wings in early 
morning and late in the evening, evidently amusing 
itself or following a custom of its kind, for when it 
likes it can fly noiselessly enough. I examined their 
wings and found that the males have four curiously 


346 ON THE CORENTYNE. Cuar. XIII. 


shaped feathers at the tip of each wing with which 
they make this noise. The end portion of these 
feathers is stiff, with very short penules. The white- 
headed females have only three of these feathers in 
each wing which are not so intensely modifed as in 
the male; while the male of the common kind (Pene- 
lope cristata) has only two of these feathers in each 
wing, which are modified in a less degree than those 
in the females of the white-headed species. 

One day we saw a large eagle-hawk on the sloping 
bank of the river, standing upon a large Salempanter 
lizard (Teius Teguexin), into the head and neck of 
which its large talons were deeply imbedded. The 
lizard seemed powerless in its grasp and could just 
writhe its body a little. When we came within about 
fifteen feet of the bird it did not attempt to stir, until 
a piece of stick was thrown at it, when it tried to fly 
and carry off the lizard. Failing in this, it gave two 
or three tight and sudden grasps with its talons on the 
already almost lifeless reptile, and letting go its hold, 
flew up into a tree overhead. This hawk was a very 
handsome bird, its back and wing feathers being 
crossed with gray and white bars; its head gray, pass- 
ing into rufus at the nape of the neck; its throat and 
breast white ; and its tail gray, barred with black. The 
upper portion of each leg was clothed with feathers of 
a white and dark gray barred pattern, resembling, as 
one of the men remarked, ‘‘a pair of plaid trousers.” 

On the upper Corentyne we saw a few of the 
scissor-bill and small gray gulls. 

Upon the Cutari were nests of humming birds on 
branches of water guavas, sticking up above the surface 


Cuap. XIII. _TREES IN BLOSSOM. 347 


of the river then in flood. One of these was partially 
under water, its late occupant, a fully fledged young 
one, being seated on a twig just above it, with its 
feathers wet and in a most sorry plight. 

We frequently heard the song of the “fiddler,” a 
small brown bird which whistles a regular little tune. 
Though its notes are sweet and liquid, they have a 
marked similarity to those produced by a human 
being. 

On many parts of the banks of the Corentyne as we 
passed along, I could not but admire the beautiful 
appearance of some tree blossoms, especially of two 
sorts of gigantic forest trees, the heads of which 
towered above their fellows. These had no leaves, 
but wore a crown of blossoms of a mauve colour in 
one case, and of a saffron yellow in the other. 

A low tree with a large stem, growing near falls, 
was in full blossom towards the end of September, 
having large pea-shaped pink flowers which covered 
it, and attracted numbers of butterflies and humming 
birds. 

On high ground or hills near the river, the grand 
silk cotton tree (Bombaz ceiba) grew of immense size, 
with its even dome-shaped top of light-green leaves. 
The light gray stem of this tree, which enables one to 
distinguish it at once, is the favourite haunt of a 
kind of bee which builds a large brown oval nest on 
its side. o 

Another tree, the name of which I could not 
ascertain, attains a greater height than the silk cotton, 
and has an evenly dome-shaped head of dense dark 
green foliage. 


348 ON THE CORENTYNE. Cuap. XII. 


A species of tree, allied to the silk cotton, was met 
with, the inner portion of which is much softer than a 
turnip, and not unlike that vegetable in appearance. 
It has an exceedingly hard, gray, woody bark, about 
three-quarters of an inch in thickness, enclosing a 
pith-like material, which, however, has concentric rings 
and radiating fibres like ordinary exogenous wood. 

On the banks of the Cutari I observed numbers of 
the Orinooko tree, which grows on the coast of Deme- 
rara, to which place it was originally brought. from the 
Orinooko river. It does not occur lower down stream 
or in any other parts of the interior of the colony. 

Upon the Cutari also I saw a small species of 
bamboo in blossom for the first time. Its flowers 
were in clusters resembling heads of green wheat in 
form. Each blossom had from four to six white semi- 
transparent stamens with light yellow anthers, and 
two long, delicate, feathery pink stigmas. 

A species of Genipa met with, bears a very beauti- 
ful large white flower with pink-edged petals, and has 
a flat circular disc of a greenish-yellow colour, with a 
central cone forming the inside of the flower. 

The great belt of mora trees, growing on the 
alluvium on both sides of the river from the coast 
region up, cease at the foot of King Frederick 
William IV. fall, and not a single specimen of that 
tree occurs beyond. 

Upon the borders of the.New River and main 
Corentyne, above the last-mentioned fall, we met with 
large groves of Brazil-nut trees, and on the ground 
beneath them obtained numbers of their nuts. I was 
fortunate enough to find some of the nut-cases con- 


Cuar. XIII. BRAZIL NUTS. 349 


taining nuts that had commenced to germinate, each 
nut sending out long roots from one end and young 
plants from the other. The roots were all twisted 
and matted together, quite filling up the cavities in 
the case around the nuts; yet the nut-case was hard, 
and showed no signs of decay, so that it is difficult to 
say how the young plants free themselves. There is 
a small aperture where the fruit-stalk was once at- 
tached, but in only one instance did I find a case in 
which one of the young plants had found its way out 
through- this and sent forth leaves. It seems to me 
that when this happens, one plant alone survives of 
the twelve or fifteen that commenced to grow, and 
that its matted roots gradually filling the nut-case 
eventually burst it, when the plant is free to take root 
in the earth. The strong cover of the growing nut is 
a necessary protection to the young plant, for without 
such it would be devoured by one of the host of 
animals that are ready to eat it. : 

I planted some of the sprouting nuts, cut out of 
their hard outer covering, on my way up river, but 
on returning found that they ahd all been dug up and 
eaten by rats or other small vermin. I therefore had 
a lot planted in a box at our camp above King 
Frederick William IV. fall on my first return to that 
spot, and placed on the stem of a small tree cut off 
some five feet from the ground. In this position they 
were free from the attacks of small animals, and 
being covered with a shelter of small palm leaves, 
thrived wonderfully. These plants were subsequently 
sent to Kew, where they arrived in a fine healthy 
condition. 


350 ON THE CORENTYNE. Cuap. XIII. 


We found many nut-cases with holes cut in them 
by accouries, the marks of the gnawing teeth of those 
animals being plainly shown. My men used to open 
them by chopping off their ends with a cutlass, which, 
owing to their hardness, was no easy operation. The 
quatas, or large black spider monkeys, spent a good 
deal of their time in trying to open them by beating 
them against the branches of trees, or on hard logs 
upon the ground; and as we passed a grove of Brazil- 
nut trees it was amusing to hear the hammering sounds 
produced by these fellows at their self-imposed tasks. 
Where a single monkey was thus employed the blows 
were most laughably “few and far between,” the 
creature showing its true indolent character by the 
slow way in which it performed its work, resting for a 
few minutes between every blow. It also showed an 
amount of perseverance, however, that one would not 
look for in a monkey, and a knowledge that it would 
eventually reap a reward for its hard labour. 

Goodness knows how long it takes one of these 
monkeys to break a nut-case; but the time must be 
great, for on one occasion, during our journey from the 
New River to the Upper Essequebo, we got quietly 
amongst a lot of the nut breakers, and secured a nut- 
case which one in its hurry had left upon a log, and 
which was worn smooth by the friction of the mon- 
key’s hands. ‘This had evidently been pounded for a 
length of time, but showed no signs of cracking. 
Its natural aperture was large enough to allow the 
monkey’s finger to touch the ends of the nuts inside, 
which were picked and worn by its nails. Near the 
same place we saw a nut-case split in two, on the flat 


Crap. XIII. BAD-SMELLING TREE. 351 


surface of a large granite rock, that had evidently 
been broken by a monkey, for there were no Brazil- 
nut trees, from which it could have fallen, overhanging 
the spot. 

The blossoms of the Brazil-nut tree are large and 
yellow, having a delicate aromatic perfume. They 
are similar, but larger than the Caccarali or monkey- 
pot tree (Lecythis Ollaria), whose flowers are so 
powerfully scented. In passing over a part of the 
forest between the Berbice and Corentyne rivers in 
December, the ground beneath the last-mentioned 
trees was strewn with their decaying blossoms, which 
emitted a most disagreeable smell. 

My men had a habit, on seeing a tree whose name 
was unknown to them, of cutting a notch in it with a 
cutlass to try its quality. True to this instinct, on 
one occasion when encamped in the forest one man 
tried the wood of a large whitish-barked tree. As 
the chips flew-from the blows of the cutlass the wound 
in the tree sent forth one of the most disagreeable and 
disgusting smells that ever smote on mortal nose. 
The sickening smell of boiling nitric acid was nothing 
to it; everybody exclaimed at once how dreadful it 
was, while some actually fled from the spot. We got 
some mud and dabbed it quickly over the notch, but 
apparently without any good effect; and as it was too 
late to move our camp, we were forced to spend the 
night in the polluted atmosphere. 

Prickly Soweri palms (Astrocaryum jauari), so 
common at the heads of all rivers in swamps near 
their banks, are absent on the Curuni and Cutari, 

above Sir Walter Raleigh's cataract. 


352 ON THE CORENTYNE. Cuar. XIII. 


The graceful urua palm is seen on the banks of the 
Corentyne, while the Moromorita (Astrocaryum muru- 
muru) is common in the forest on the higher lands. 
The nut of the latter palm when young contains a 
white edible pulp not unpleasant to the taste. In one 
encampment deep in the forest, under the arching 
branches of these low palms, the camp fires lit up 
the arch-like festoons of leaves with most beautiful 
effect, giving them the appearance of Gothic archi- 
tecture. The long prickly dead leaf-stalks scattered 
on the ground under them made walking a torture to 
my men, for, in spite of every care, the thorns got 
into the soles of their feet, and had to be extracted at 
once before they could proceed. 

In the nut of one palm—the urua, I believe—the 
men obtained the large grub-like larve of some 
beetle, which they looked upon as a great delicacy, 
roasting them on the fire, and sometimes even eating 
them uncooked. 

Of insect pests that attacked us for our blood, the 
Corentyne contained an abundance. Amongst these 
were mosquitoes, black-flies, and cow-flies, on the main 
river ; and ticks, bête rouge, and cow-flies in the forest. 
The mosquitoes, however, seemed very local in their 
habitats, some islands being infested with them, while 
others, a mile or two off, had none. 

One day I felt a curious nipping sensation under 
the skin of my back, which, on examination, was 
found to be produced by the larve of a species of bot- 
fly, which the men called a “ mosquito worm.” These 
grub-like larve are similar to those called warbles, 
which form tumours in the backs of cattle, and are 


Cuar. XIIL WASP NESTS. 353 


deposited by a fly when one is bathing. I got one of 
the men, skilled in the extraction of these grubs, to 
operate upon me, who, having put tobacco juice into 
the orifice of its abode, made it what he called drunk, 
so that it let go its hold with its hooks, and was 
squeezed out. One man had five or six in his back, 
which appeared there a few days after mine. A red 
howling monkey which I shot had a huge one in its 
side, as well as a film over one eye covering the sight. 

The long and barrel-like nests of a wasp, called the 
boraserie, were frequently seen suspended from the 
‘branches of trees. As a rule, their form was that of 
a long truncated cone of about eighteen inches in dia- 
meter and three feet in length. Portions of the sides 
of these nests were covered with wasps, probably young 
ones sunning themselves. It was impossible to go 
near enough to one to get a good look at it, for its 
inhabitants did not belie their name, but were very 
waspish. The wasps were of a dark colour with 
brown abdomens, and stung like fiends, as I found to 
my cost when examining some rocks one day, under 
a tree in which a nest was suspended at least fifty 
feet up. 

There was always a large spindle-shaped ants’ nest 
attached to a branch close to these wasps’ nests. The 
ants inhabiting them are probably the small amber- 
coloured kind so frequently met with, which when 
touched emit such a disagreeable resinous smell. 

What feelings the two kinds of insects can have in 
common, that they should live together in harmony, 
it is hard to understand, unless it be that they mu- 


tually protect each other. 
2 A 


354 ON THE CORENTYNE. Camar. XIIL 


This social group requires the mention of a third 
party to make it complete, and this is no other than 
the yellow-backed mocking bird (Cassicus persicus). 
Around and almost touching the habitation of the 
wasps are the long hanging nests of these birds, in 
and out of which, during the breeding season, their 
owners go with impunity. The wasps, by their pre- 
sence, no doubt, protect the young and eggs of the 
bird from the attacks of monkeys, but of what service 
the bird is to the insect is not so easily understood. 
It seems strange that the wasps should tolerate the 
presence of such busy, noisy, chattering neighbours. 

There is a large ant called the tamrian, with a 
brown abdomen, black thorax, and dark-brown head, 
which does not build a nest, but occupies old wasps’ 
nests or hollow trees. Some of these took up their 
abode in one of the lockers of my boat, and had to be 
destroyed with scalding water. They had their young 
and eggs in one mass, the former being grub-like, and 
larger than the parent ant, while some were enclosed 
in web cases in a sort of chrysalis condition. 

. Whilst my boats and stores were being portaged at 
King Frederick William IV. fall, I had an oppor- 
tunity of witnessing an interesting attack made by 
a band of yackaman ants (Eciton sp.) on a colony 
of tamrians, in a hollow tree near the water’s edge, 
surrounded by a patch of white sand. A band from 
a column of yackamans crossed the sand, and climbing 
the tree entered the home of the tamrians, where they 
were met by the inhabitants and the fight commenced; 
but as the former poured in by thousands, the fight 
was carried on in the hollow tree, lasting for about 


Cuar. XIII. YACKAMANS AND TAMRIANS. 355 


a quarter of an hour. During that time, many 
yackamans were seen coming out of the hole carefully 
bearing their wounded comrades, some of whom were 
legless, and making off across the sand in the direction 
taken by the main column. Then soon all began to 
pour out again, but these carried the dead bodies of 
the unfortunate tamrians whom they had slain in 
fight, probably not a single one escaping. The band 
then marched off in a long line, and on their way had 
to ascend a steep rise in the sand, up which many of 
them, who had lost a leg or two, could not climb, but 
kept perpetually rolling down after having partially 
ascended. Then it was that the most singular part 
of the occurrence took place, whereby they exhibited 
such a powerful instance of sagacity. 

As a wounded one was struggling up, many un- 
loaded and unwounded comrades massed themselves 
together below it, and as it gained inch by inch they 
advanced slowly in a compact mass behind, so that 
when it lost its footing and rolled down it did not go 
far before it was brought up against their bodies. 
Sometimes they almost shoved it up, and getting it 
safely to the top, made off and left it to plod along 
the level by itself. This was not an isolated instance, 
but was done repeatedly by separate gangs of ants. 

At one place on the Aramatau when watching 
great columns of these yackamans hunting, accom- 
panied as usual by numbers of little birds, I observed 
that the latter did not touch a single ant, but had 
splendid feeding on the insects that flew up from the 
ground as their foe advanced, which they caught on 
the wing, or as they alighted on tree limbs. These 

2a 2 


356 ON THE CORENTYNE. Cear. XIIL 


birds were small, and of four or five different kinds. 
They kept hopping from twig to twig over the ad- 
vancing ant columns, chirping and singing merrily. 

As the ants marched along it was amusing to see 
the grasshoppers, crickets, small cockroaches, &c., 
scuttling along in a most agitated state, evidently 
fally aware of the approach of the ants, a yard or 
so behind them. They bolted out from under the 
dead leaves in numbers, and in many instances seemed 
too frightened to look whither they were going. Thus 
they frequently hopped the wrong way and fell into 
the middle of the column of their enemies. What the 
ants did not get the birds secured, so between them 
they cleared out their victims over a large area. 

The main column of this lot, composed of closely- 
packed ants, was two feet in width and of unknown 
length. 

The bodies of these ants were of a dark reddish- 
brown colour, while their abdomens were of a similar 
but lighter tint. Their last pair of legs was much 
longer than the two other pairs, and it appeared as if 
they did not use them in running, but dragged them 
along. They are called howroorays or yackamans by 
the Indians. Another species of the same ant very 
frequently met with is of a chestnut colour. 

I frequently observed a large species of dragon-fly 
tossing out large drops of water from small pools on 
the hollows of rocks, with the extremity of its ab- 
domen as it hovered over them. The men said that 
when these insects bailed out water in that manner 
it was a sign that the dry season was at its height. 

Whilst examining a red cliff section on the New 


Cuar. XIII. OTTERS. 357 


River, numbers of crabs came out of their holes in 
the soft clay under the water’s edge, to have a look 
at me, stalking slowly along sideways with their claws 
elevated in a defiant sort of way; and some actually 
attempted to climb up my legs when I stood still. 

Between the New River and the Essequebo a 
species of frog, called the divi-divi, which has beau- 
tiful ultramarine markings on a black ground, was 
very numerous. 

In the forest between the Corentyne and Berbice 
I found a curious little reptile, of about five inches in 
length, which had rudimentary fore limbs and two 
flaps representing hind limbs. Its body resembled 
that of a snake, while its head was lizard-like, and its 
scales were arranged in rings. It belonged to the 
Amphisbena family, most probably of the genus 
Chirotes, 

Water-dogs or otters (Pteronura Sanbachii) were 
exceedingly numerous on all portions of the river, and 
often appeared as if they wished to attack us but 
lacked the necessary courage. We found a young one 
at the foot of a steep clay bank, vainly endeavouring 
to climb back to the mouth of the burrow, from which 
it had fallen. Having become exhausted in its en- 
deavours to regain its nest, it made no effort to escape, 
and was captured by the men. We then placed it 
higher up near the mouth of the burrow, just as its 
mother came swimming down river. Seeing us and 
hearing the cries of her young, she hurried up to 
within twenty feet of us, diving and coming up re- 
peatedly in a most anxious and excited state. When 
we pushed off from the spot she landed, seized her 


358 ON THE CORENTYNE. Cuar. XIII. 


infant in her mouth, and disappeared with it into her 
burrow. Presently, to our surprise, she appeared on 
the top of the bunk a few yards off, having evidently 
left her house by a back door. 

I shot a large female water-dog, which, being busily 
engaged eating a cartabac on some rocks, and snarling 
at her husband, who wished to deprive her of it, did 
not observe my approach. Its length from the nose 
to the end of its tail was five feet four inches. 

After all our toils, troubles, and fatigues, we arrived 
safely at Skeldon, at the mouth of the Corentyne, on 
the 10th of January, 1872, where [ was hospitably 
entertained by Dr. Leary until my departure for 
New Amsterdam a few days later. Mr. N. Winter, 
the attorney of Skeldon estate, was on a visit to that 
place, and he and I examined the site of an ancient 
Indian village of the stone period, where, amongst an 
abundance of pottery and bones of animals, we found 
a curious little polished stone amulet, representing a 
sort of human figure. 


Cuar. XIV. BERBICE AND DEMERARA RIVERS. 359 


CHAPTER XIV. 
BERBICE AND DEMERARA RIVERS, 


Leave New Amsterdam — Description of Journey up Berbice, across to 
Demerara River, and down to Couchman’s — Toads eat Fire — River 
Shells — Roaring of Jaguars — Tapir rashes through Camp — Shoot 
Quata Monkey — Archway of Ants— Scenery on Berbice — Reach 
Georgetown. 


I LANDED at the town of New Amsterdam, at the 
mouth of the Berbice river, on the 14th of January, 
1872, having come with my men and boats in the 
schooner “ Daylight,” from Skeldon, and spent some 
hours in plodding about the streets in search of a 
resting place. Unfortunately, I found the only hotel 
of the place closed, while all the lodging houses were 
full. I therefore made my way to the quarters of some 
gentlemen, amongst whom were my friends Mr. Payne 
and Mr. Bagot, whom I had known in Georgetown, 
where I was kindly received and entertained during 
my two days’ stay. 

Procuring stores for my journey, I left town on the 
morning of January 17th, and proceeded up the 
Berbice river. On arriving at the Etuni creek, I 
made a journey across to the Demerara, coming out 
on that river at a place called Quitaro, near the head 
of the first rapids. Returning to my boats on the 
Etuni, I continued my journey up the Berbice, 
arriving at the first fall, called [tabru, on the Ist of 


860 BERBICE AND DEMERARA RIVERS. Czar. XIV. 


February. Placing skids on the rocks along the side 
of this steep but narrow fall, we got our boats over, 
and, working up numerous cataracts and rapids for a 
space of three days, came to Christmas cataracts, 
where we again had to lay skids on the rocks and 
make a path over which to portage the stores. At the 
foot of this cataract I left one boat and three men, 
and taking the other, with twelve men, and stores for 
one month’s consumption, pushed on up river. We 
ascended a few more cataracts above which the river 
narrowed considerably where it ran amidst low swampy 
tracts. 

In these swampy portions, where the banks were 
low, the water was shallow and ran swiftly, the whole 
river being not more than fifteen yards in width. 
Beyond them it snddenly expanded to a width of forty 
yards, having high clay banks lined with mora trees, 
and ran with a very sluggish current. 

The prickly Soweri palm grew in abundance on all 
the low muddy spots adjoining the river, some of which 
had most curious tops. Instead of a single head many 
of them branched into three distinct portions, having 
a curious, un-palm-like appearance. In the narrow 
parts, also, long trailing vines and creepers hung 
down from trees whose branches stretched across 
overhead, forming beautifal wall-like patches of 
foliage. These, as well as the large dead trees that 
lay in numbers across from bank to bank, had to be 
cut through before the boat could pass. The river 
being only some thirty inches in depth, enabled eight 
men to wade in advance and cut the fallen trees, or 
tacoobas, with axes, and tree branches and vines with 


Cuar. XIV. DESCEND BERBICE RIVER. 361 


cutlasses ; while four men were employed bringing up 
the boat. From these narrow holes we at times 
emerged upon fine open reaches where we paddled 
quickly along, feeling greatly relieved for a time, 
until we plunged again into bush and narrows. 

Working in this way we only made a southing of 
14 miles in five days. 

Finding by altitudes of stars that we were in 
4° 4’ 41” N., and therefore not many miles from 
the spot where I struck this river in 1871, I deter- 
mined to perform the remainder of the journey on foot. 
Leaving two men in charge of the boat, I went on 
with the rest, cutting our way through the bush, not 
far from the river’s bank, from bend to bend for three 
and a half days; when we had the satisfaction of 
arriving at our old camp of April 14th, 1871. 

The first time we arrived at that place a cayman 
welcomed us; this time we were greeted by a jaguar, 
which serenaded us from the forest close to the 
opposite side of the river. 

Having accomplished our task we descended the 
river in safety to the end of a path, below the first 
rapids, which led to the Demerara, where we formed 
a camp and made preparations for our journey to that 
river, for the purpose of descending it to George- 
town. 

In coming up we had passed a small Ackawoise 
village, where a few Indians of that tribe resided, the 
head man of which promised on my return to guide 
me to the Demerara. I therefore sent down for 
him, but my messengers returned with the unwelcome 
news that the place was entirely abandoned, and a 


362 BERBICE AND DEMERARA RIVERS, Caap. XIV. 


newly-made grave occupied the centre of the floor of 
one of the houses. 

We therefore started, without a guide, along the 
faint trail, to try and find our way across. Fortu- 
nately some Indians had performed the journey a 
few weeks previously, and had marked the obscure 
portions of the track by breaking twigs and small 
trees here and there, besides leaving footprints on the 
sands of the open country traversed by the path. In 
a little over a day and a half we reached the banks of 
a small stream, called the Itaboro, where the path 
ended, from which point onwards it was evident 
that Indian travellers proceeded in woodskins. We 
therefore searched for these craft, in hopes that some 
might have been left by Indians, and were successful, 
finding one good one, a second imperfect one with a 
split bottom, and a number of mere remnants. 

We set to work and repaired the two canoes, 
having to lash half a moco-moco stem along the split, 
and sew it on with thin bush-rope, through holes 
bored here and there in the bark, and then caulk the 
crack with scrapings of the inner bark of the trumpet- 
tree. 

The day following our arrival at Itaboro creek, I had 
our small supply of stores, &c., placed in the wood- 
skins, with a man each to propel them, and keeping 
two other men besides, sent the remainder back to 
boats on the Berbice to take them down that river, 
and on to Georgetown. Then at 9 a.m. on the llth 
of March we commenced our perilous journey, two of 
the men walking with me in the forest along the edge 
of the creek in a muddy swamp, while the other two 


Crap. XIV. PERILOUS JOURNEY. 363 


brought down the leaky overladen woodskins. Rain 
fell heavily and added to our discomforts, causing 
everything to wear a gloomy aspect. All retreat to 
our boats in case of accidents to our canoes was now 
cut off, and we did not know how far we would have 
to go before reaching the Demerara; or whether we 
would meet with Indians on the part where we would 
come upon it. Every now and then we shouted to 
the men in the woodskins, who were hidden by the 
tall reeds and shrubs on the river’s edge, to learn 
their whereabouts, and prevent our getting separated. 

After plodding on for some hours over a low tract 
that got more swampy at every step, owing to the 
water of the creek spreading in pools over it, we heard 
a shout from our woodskin men, and hastened to the 
spot, fearing that some accident had happened; when 
to our joy we found that they had discovered an old 
woodskin hauled up on the creek’s side. We set to 
work and repaired it by sewing a large bark patch on 
a hole in its side, and to it transferred the load of the 
split and leaky canoe. I then went in it as steersman, 
with one man in the bow as paddler, while the other 
man, who had been walking with me, went with my 
interpreter in the split craft. Thus the whole party 
was accommodated with seats, and I went on down 
stream in a more cheerful frame of mind. It was 
well that we found the third canoe, for the banks 
of the creek had by that time become submerged, and 
we should have had fearful work in wading from tree 
clump to tree clump through the dismal swamp. 

In some places we had to lie on our backs as we 
shot under fallen logs where our bodies touched the 


364 BERBICE AND DEMERARA RIVERS. Camar. XIV. 


log above, and in others grazed over sunken ones or 
with difficulty evaded them. The split woodskin, 
getting worse and worse, was kept afloat with great 
difficulty. Late in the afternoon we found an old 
wooden corial bottom up, partially under water, which 
we joyfully righted, bailed out, and caulked. In so 
doing the men neglected to bail out the split canoe, 
and it sank under them just as they scrambled into 
the corial. 

That night we camped on a little spongy island in 
the swamp, formed of rootlets, dead leaves, and loam, 
around the matted roots of a cluster of corkwood trees; 
and being without any shelter were of course visited 
by a heavy fall of rain. 

Next day our troubles ended on entering the Deme- 
rara river, where, with a width of twenty yards, it ran 
swiftly in a north-westerly direction. Borne down 
by the current, we came to a village landing, and 
walked up to the houses of some Ackawoise Indians. 
This place is called Paintecobra, and I determined its 
latitude to be 5° 6’ 53” N. Hence we descended the 
Demerara, and in passing an Indian village, called 
Booboo, purchased good strong woodskins, leaving our 
rattletraps behind. 

We portaged our craft at Oruru-mallali without 
any difficulty and arrived at a village called Enowdah, 
where we stopped for a day or two, making from 
thence a short journey to Mabooroo mountain. 

Our next stoppage was at Mr. Couchman’s wood- 
cutting grant, where I left my woodskin, and through 
the kindness of that gentleman obtained a small 
bateau to continue my journey in, as the rough water 


Cuar. XIV. FIRE-EATING TOADS. 365 


in the lower reaches of the Demerara would swamp 
our canoes. 

Many of the incidents of this journey were so 
similar to those which had occurred on previous 
occasions already recorded, that I will not trouble the 
reader with a repetition of them, but will merely note 
down one or two that I think are perhaps worth 
mentioning. 

One of my men on this journey was Alexander 
Paulie, who, having seen a good deal of bush life, 
was acquainted with the habits of many birds and 
quadrupeds. The former he could skin and preserve 
as well as the best taxidermist; and about the latter, 
especially tigers, he could tell many wonderful tales. 
His stories of the fabulous “water-mules” and “ water- 
monkeys” prepared me to accept just as much of his 
other sturies as I liked, so that one night in camp, 
when he asked me if I had ever seen a toad eat fire, I 
conscientiously replied in the negative, and added that 
I did not believe that anyone else had seen them do 
so either. He said that when he next saw a toad he 
would show me that it could swallow a live coal 
without any apparent inconvenience. When, therefore, 
one evening in an opensided house at Anaimapeur, on 
the Upper Demerara, a number of large common toads 
came hopping about on its clay floor, Paulie said that 
he would prove his words. He got a few small live 
coals about the size of dice, from the wood fire, and 
threw them on the ground in front of a toad, while I 
closely watched the result. The toad hopped up to 
one of the red-hot pieces of wood ember, leant forward, 
darted out its tongue to which the coal adhered, and 


366 BERBICE AND DEMERARA RIVERS. Casar. XIV. 


drawing it in hastily swallowed the tempting morsel 
seemingly to its entire satisfaction. It remained 
stationary for a short space of time and then hopped 
merrily away. 

Paulie then informed me that though red-hot wood 
ember did not hurt these animals a piece of red-hot 
nail was too much for them. He tried the experiment 
once with a short piece of red-hot nail, which the 
unsuspecting toad swallowed, but the iron burnt its 
way out, and the toad went into convulsions which 
terminated in death. I imagine that the toad is taken 
in by the wood ember after all, and does not devour 
it from any morbid taste, but because it imagines that 
it has got hold of a firefly. 

Another statement of Paulie’s was a rather singular 
one, but requires confirmation. He affirmed that 
when a dog licks up the blood of a powie (Craz 
alector), or chews up the quill-pen feathers of that 
bird containing blood, that it will go raving mad and 
die. My interpreter went further, saying that the 
uncooked flesh of the powie has a similar effect upon 
a dog. They were both particular on the point that 
true madness was produced, and not only a condition 
of temporary fits. If true, this is very curious, 
and suggests the question as to whether hydrophobia, 
in other parts of the world, may nat be produced in 
dogs by their having partaken of some particular sort 
of food. 

River crabs, as a rule, feed upon dead animal 
substances or fruits, but in one instance I observed 
a crab which had seized upon a live burabararoo frog 
by one hind leg and drawn it into a cavity between
