Cmar. XIV. CRAB AND FROG. 367 


some rocks, where the frog’s body was tightly jammed. 
The poor victim was clinging with all its might by 
its fore feet to the rock on either side, but could not 
get its leg out of the claw of the crab. How long 
this unequal struggle had been going on I cannot 
say, but it must have been for a considerable length 
of time, for on stirring these animals with a stick, the 
foot of the frog came off, and the crab retired grace- 
fully with it into its house, while the poor frog hopped 
slowly away in a most exhausted condition. 

In the sands at the bottom of the shallows in the 
narrow parts of the Berbice river I found numbers 
of long narrow shells, probably of a species of Myce- 
topus. Fine river shells are very abundant on many 
of the rivers, especially of the Melania family, which 
inhabit the running water of the cataracts. Castalvas 
are found on the Rupununi, and Hyria syrmatophora 
and H. corrugata on the Essequebo, near Ouropocari. 

The Berbice river, from the foot of Itabru cataract 
upwards, is the home par eacellence of the cayman. 
These unsightly monsters are seen there in numbers, 
and are exceedingly bold. One day whilst stopping to 
breakfast, one of the men hauled in some perai with 
hook and line, and left them on the shore within 
ten feet of the boat. A cayman, which had been 
watching the proceeding with interest, swam hastily 
up and seized one of the fish. To punish it for its 
boldness I gave it a charge of shot behind the eye 
which apparently killed it, so I had it hauled ashore 
and made a sketch of it. After finishing this, one 
of the men took a cutlass and chopped off the end 
of its tail. ‘The moment the blow fell the brute made 


368 BERBICE AND DEMERARA RIVERS. Cnaap. XIV. 


a fearful struggle, opened its eyes, and apparently 
returned to life again, but was soon despatched with 
an axe. 

One night some haimara had been caught and 
hauled into the boat, where their blood got mixed 
with some water in its bottom. A cayman, attracted 
to the spot by the smell of the blood, made several 
efforts to upset the boat by striking it heavy blows 
with its tail. We captured two young caymans, each 
of about three feet in length, and tying a cord round 
their bodies kept them in the boat for some time, in- 
tending to take them to Georgetown, but they died on 
the voyage down. 

On the rocks at Christmas cataract we saw a 
common alligator sleeping soundly. One of the men 
approached it softly and killed it with my geological 
hammer, by giving it three or four decidedly heavy 
blows on the top of the head. 

Upon going to my hammock one night I saw by 
the lamplight a sticklike thing, which I touched acci- 
dentally with my foot. It rolled away like a hoop, 
and then straightened out again, when I saw it was a 
snake and despatched it. On examination it turned 
out to be a young poisonous labaria, two feet in 
length. I had hardly “turned in” that night before 
the sweet sound of two jaguars, calling to each other, 
smote upon my ear. One was near our camp, but the 
other far off; and they kept up their conversation for 
a long time. The low deep notes of the call of the 
nearer one seemed to make the air quiver and vibrate. 
It was no doubt a grand sound, with a true noble ring 
in it, but one that I would gladly have dispensed with 


Cnap. XIV. A NIGHT VISITOR. 369 


in our situation, with intense darkness around, and no 
fire near. 

So used had we become to forest life that we slept 
the sleep of the innocent amidst many an unsuspected 
danger. Some nights, however, we had stirs in camp, 
the quick ears of the men detecting the footsteps of 
an animal prowling about us. Then efforts were 
made to blow life into some smouldering remains of 
a cooking fire, often without success. When we 
were traversing forests away from our boats the men 
took good care to keep a fire smouldering which could 
be blown into flame in a few moments. 

One night an unusual occurrence took place, which 
caused some merriment after it was over, but which 
for the moment startled us all a good deal. We were 
in our hammocks and most of the men were asleep, 
while I had just finished my last pipe and was on the 
point of composing myself to rest, when I heard a 
heavy body splashing in the river, and then coming 
thundering up the bank, crashing through the trees 
towards us. The moonlight fell upon the ground here 
and there through gaps in the tree-tops and lit up the 
place, disclosing the network of white hammocks, 
where the men slept. Towards this spot the crashing 
noise was making its way, so I sat up to see the result 
just as the men, roused out of sleep by the noise, and 
not comprehending its cause, began to yell out in a 
most appalling manner. This evidently turned the 
frightened animal, for in an instant I saw the re- 
maining brands of the fire scattered in every direction 
as it plunged amongst them, and next moment its 
huge dark form brushed past my elbow like a flash of 

2 B 


370 BERBICE AND DEMERARA RIVERS. Camar. XIV. 


lightning. This was rather too close a shave to be 
pleasant, for had the animal—a large tapir—been 
going at right angles instead of parallel to me I should 
have been completely destroyed. It was very evident 
that the tapir was going somewhere, most probably in 
advance of a tiger, and we did not feel very safe until 
some minutes had elapsed, in case that animal might 
likewise come headlong through our camp. 

From a flock of hogs the men obtained a young one, 
which we kept for a few days, feeding it on the inside of 
the nuts of the Soweri palm, but it eventually escaped. 
The full-grown hogs feed upon these nats, breaking 
their extremely hard shells with their strong teeth. 

At daylight one morning we awoke to find a large 
quata monkey directly overhead, which, at the 
moment we emerged from our hammocks, threw down 
some dry limbs amongst us, causing us to scatter for 
safety, to our great annoyance. Ben fired at it to 
drive it away, never imagining that it could be 
wounded at such a height, when, to our astonishment, 
down it came to the ground amongst the hammocks 
from a height of over 120 feet. This fall, it might be 
supposed, would have been enough to kill it; but on 
the contrary, it seemed rather to arouse it to life, for 
with a hurried look around, it sprang up, climbed a 
hammock pole, and from the top waved its long arms 
about as if to reach the branches overhead. With its 
red face, in which were a few shot holes, and its black 
shaggy coat, it was a curious-looking creature. De- 
scending rapidly from the pole it scuttled quickly to 
a small tree, up which it ran, and climbing from its 
top into the branches of a large one, sat looking down 


Cuar. XIV. WOUNDED SLOTH. 371 


upon us for a short time in a most comical way. Then 
it slowly made off as if nothing had happened. 

I shot a sort of polecat, called a hocker (Galera 
sp.), an animal about the size of a cat, of a dark- 
brown colour, which was running nimbly amongst the 
tree branches. 

On Wahmarra mountain, on the Upper Demerara, 
we saw a sloth (Bradypus torquatus) lying on its 
back at the foot of a tree, which it grasped with its 
hind limbs, covering its head with its fore legs. It 
was in a very weak condition, having a large wound 
in its back which was swarming with maggots. It 
had most probably been attacked by a harpy eagle, 
which had attempted, unsuccessfully, to drag it from 
its hold on a tree branch. Weakened by loss of blood, 
it must have subsequently fallen to the ground. I 
got one of the men to put it out of its misery. 

We found the nest of a hanaquah ( Ortalida 
motmot), and also of a duraquara (Odontophorus 
Guianensis), both containing eggs. That of the former 
bird was built of sticks on a low tree, and contained 
four speckled eggs, little inferior in size to those of a 
fowl. The nest of the latter was built of leaves and 
twigs, placed under a low shrub on the ground, and 
was roofed over, having a small entrance in front. It 
contained eight white eggs, in size between those of a 
fowl and pigeon. 

The hanaquah is easily tamed, and is said to breed 
with common fowls, the hybrid being a very pug- 
nacious bird. 

The stinking or Canjé pheasants were seen in great 
numbers on the low trees bordering the estuary of the 

2B 2 


372 BERBICE AND DEMERARA RIVERS. Crap. XIV. 


Berbice, between New Amsterdam and Mara sugar 
estate, and in the month of January were sitting on 
their twig nests. 

During our journey across, from the Etunie to the 
Demerara, we killed three labaria snakes; and on 
our way to the Mabooroo rock, destroyed a large 
bushmaster that was coiled up asleep under the edge 
of a log over which we had all stepped. The last man 
that passed roused it, when it made a blow at his leg, 
but fortunately missed him. 

In a still wide portion of the Upper Berbice 
we saw the Victoria regia growing luxuriantly, with 
leaves four feet five inches in diameter, at most 
probably the same spot where Schomburgk first dis- 
covered this beautiful plant. The inside of its flowers 
was full of large brown beetles. 

I observed a most singular ant procession, on a log 
forming a natural bridge over the river. My atten- 
tion was first attracted to it by seeing a broad band 
of red ants clinging together in a continuous cluster 
the whole length of the log, and forming a covered 
archway with their bodies, beneath which ran the 
stream of ants. They were of three sizes, but all of 
one species, called the arua or tiger-ant. The spring 
of the arch was formed of ants facing outwards, 
supported on their hinder legs and the back of their 
abdomens. Others clung to their heads, and thus 
formed the rest of the arch. 

Of a sunny morning, whilst going along in a boat 
on any of the rivers of Guiana, one is often annoyed 
by a large kind of bee, which buzzes round and round 
one’s head, and is with difficulty driven away. We 


Cuap. XIV. AN ACCIDENT. 373 


used frequently to rid ourselves of them by batting 
them with a paddle. I was told that an Indian once 
had the misfortune to kill his wife whilst thus en- 
deavouring to drive away one of these bees. He was 
sitting in the’ bow of his canoe, with his back to his 
wife, and in swinging round his paddle its edge caught 
her on the side of the neck, probably on the jugular 
vein, and killed her instantly. 

There are many fine examples of Indian picture- 
writing on the Berbice, the most curious being those 
below the first fall. It is difficult to say why they 
are placed so low down on the rocks, for at the time 
of my visit, though the river was very low, still parts 
of some were under water. 

From seeing me make sketches my men were all 
imbued with a mania for doing likewise, and many of 
them brought little blank cash-books and pencils, in 
which they made some of the most curious sketches of 
scenery imaginable. They made the parts of a picture 
in different planes, without any connection between 
them. Thus a mountain rose from:a ruled line, and a 
tree in the foreground rose from another, leaving a 
blank between which they could never fill up to make 
it look like one picture. When I stopped to sketch 
picture-writing it was amusing to see how some got 
out their pencils for the same purpose, while others 
scratched their sketches on the bottoms of their tin 

pans. 

-= The scenery on the Berbice is very monotonous, 
the usual short reach of river, bordered by forest on 
either side, palling after a time upon the sight. In 
some parts below the cataracts some cliffs of white 


374 BERBICE AND DEMERARA RIVERS. Cuar. XIV. 


clay, with open savanna on their tops, form a grateful 
change to the traveller. Above Itabru cataract 
glimpses of mountains on the west are obtained. I 
visited two of these on my way down river, and from 
the top of a precipice 720 feet above the sea obtained 
a fine view, ranging from south-east to north-east, 
the principal feature of which was a slightly undu- 
lating expanse of forest-covered country, broken only 
by one or two small and distant ridges, without a 
single patch of open country to break the monotony 
of the dark-looking forest foliage. This tract melts so 
gradually into the sky at the horizon that no abrupt 
line of demarcation is perceptible. It was not sur- 
prising to me to hear one of the men remark, that 
from this spot “ you can see quite out of sight.” 
After having been imprisoned, as it were, in the 
forest, where no greater distance of view than that 
afforded by a reach of river a quarter or half a 
mile in extent could be obtained, it was a great treat 
to get a view like the above, where the eye could 
roam freely over a boundless region. 

From Mabooroo mountain, west of the Demerara 
river, at an elevation of 844 feet above the sea level, 
an extensive view of forest-covered country is obtained, 
which well repays the short walk to it from Enowdah 
village. 

Indians are scarce on the Berbice, and are seldom met 
with on the river itself, but members of the Arowaak 
tribe live between it and Demerara in their lower 
courses: On the Demerara there are a number of 
Indian villages above the Oruru-mallali, or great fall, 
but only a few below. ‘Their inhabitants are chiefly 


Cuar. XIV. RETURN TO GEORGETOWN. 375 


Ackawoise, amongst whom are many Indians from 
the Potaro and Siparuni rivers, and from the Paca- 
raima mountains, who come there to trade, or work 
on the wood-cutting establishments lower down river. 
At one of the villages, called Pongabi, there was 
an old Indian woman with her grown-up son and 
daughter, between whom there was the greatest dis- 
similarity. The son was the offspring of a Mulatto 
man, and did not look as if he had any Indian blood 
in his veins, having frizzly hair. The daughter was 
exceedingly dark, and very pretty, with a splendid 
figure, being the offspring of a Madras coolie. 

The night I was at Oruru-cobra village I sat up 
taking meridian altitudes of stars for latitude, while 
Paulie, wrapped in his blanket, sat by the artificial 
horizon, to wipe the heavy dew that settled on its 
glass from time to time. Next morning the inter- 
preter overheard the Indians discussing the proceeding, 
and all agreed that we had been practising sorcerers’ 
arts. 

On the way down river I landed at Seba to have 
a look at the spot rendered classic by being men- 
tioned in the ‘ Wanderings ° of the great naturalist, 
Waterton. 

Upon the 27th of March, under the cover of night, 
I landed at Georgetown, in a very travel-stained 
condition, and made my way to my usual quarters at 
Beckwith’s Hotel, glad to be once more within the 
confines of civilization. 


376 THE UPPER MAZARUNI. Cuar. XV. 


CHAPTER XV. 
THE UPPER MAZARUNI. 


Leave Georgetown — Reach Peaimah Fall — Difficult portage at Aruwai 
Falls — Sericoeng Falls — Difficulty in obtaining Woodskins — Send 
back Boats to the Merumé River — Pass “ Starvation Landing ” of 
Apiopai — Mysterious Footprints — Deep Pools the Haunts of Tigers 
— Guacharos Cave — Waterfall on Roraima — Chichi Portage — 
Ayangcanna — Journey from Haiacker to Ourindouie Fall on the 
Ireng — Return — Ow-weang Portage — Cross mountains to Merumé 
— Descent of Merumé Mountain — Rejoin our Boats on Merumé River 
— Descend Mazaruni — Arrive in Georgetown. 


I STARTED from Georgetown with Mr. J. Bagot upon 
my last journey to the interior of British Guiana on 
the 3rd of September, 1872, for the purpose of 
exploring and mapping the Upper Mazaruni river, 
and on the Ist of October reached Peaimah fall, 
where that stream debouches from the sandstone 
mountains on to the great plain of its lower course. 

Not being able to ascend this fall, we made a 
portage on its eastern side of three-quarters of a mile 
in length, over which we transported one boat, leaving 
the other and most of the stores at its foot. 

Although only a long sloping cataract, with a total 
height of fifty feet, Peaimah has a most formidable 
appearance, owing to the great body of water it 
contains. About midway down one side there is a 
huge overhanging flat rock, on which I sat for a short 
time looking upon the seething foam spurting upwards 
and onwards in fleecy masses, here sweeping forwards, 


Cuar. XV. ARUWAI FALLS. 377 


there curling backwards, scattering spray and mist 
high into the air. 

Leaving its head we paddled up the black water 
stream between high bare sandstone precipices on 
either hand of its narrow valley. These precipices 
were flanked by low hills rising some 300 feet above 
the river, and hemming it in on both sides. Late in 
the afternoon we reached a landing place on the right 
hand, not far below a cataract, where the river was 
narrowed to about one hundred yards in width, and 
ran with a swift current. 

There we camped, and next day followed the path 
through the forest, until we came out on the river 
again above the falls. We then traced the edge of 
the river down for a considerable distance, at times 
upon flat sandstone rocks along its edge, at others 
in the thick bush amongst a confusion of great rocks ; 
and passed on the way numbers of falls, some in 
steps of a few feet each with rapids between, while 
others were from ten to twenty feet in height. The 
foliage around these falls was most varied and beauti- 
ful, every plant and tree growing with rank luxuriance. 

Scrambling over chasms and clambering down steep 
rock sides, amongst spiked tangled vines, we reached 
the main channel of the river at the foot of the last 
formidable fall, one of forty feet in height. Behind it 
was a cave most difficult of approach; in order to 
reach it we had to cling to a slightly sloping wall 
of rock, above a dark seething pool of turbulent 
water, with footing on a ledge not six inches in width. 

A few of the Kaieteur swallows were clinging to 
the cave’s walls, and their nests of clay and green 


- 


378 THE UPPER MAZARUNI. Cnap. XV. 


moss, containing pure white eggs, were built on the 
ledges and in crannies of the rock. 

Keeling satisfied that we should have to abandon all 
idea of taking our boat up by water, but would have 
to convey it over the portage, on Venezuelan territory, 
we left the river, and striking off for the path returned 
to camp. This set of falls, called Aruwai by the 
Indians, occupies a distance of two miles of river. 

We commenced our formidable and difficult task 
of making a portage on the 7th of October, and did 
not finish it until midday on the 15th. 

Whilst we were busily engaged upon this under- 
taking, two Indians passed through the portage on 
their way up river, having left their woodskin at the 
foot of the falls, intending to proceed in another which 
they had hidden at the upper end of the portage on 
their journey down. They told the interpreter that 
we would meet another great set of falls farther on 
which we could not possibly pass, so I got them to 
promise to bring woodskins to its head for us to 
proceed in, and they continued on their way. 

We were two days in getting the stores and boat 
across, and then had the satisfaction of launching our 
boat on the river above, after having accomplished a 
most difficult feat. We had dragged a large heavy 
boat of 28 feet in length, by means of a block and 
tackle, up a steep rocky hill at an angle of 56°, upon 
a system of skids lashed together like a huge erooked 
ladder, amongst great blocks of rock to a height of 
165 feet; and then along a skidded pathway, over 
uneven rocky ground for a distance of a mile and 
seven-eighths, rising on the way to a height of 275 feet 


Curar. XV. HUMMING BIRDS FEEDING. ` 379 


above the spot from which we hauled ‘it out of the 
water. ; 

In order to expedite the carriage of stores up the 
hill we made a strong sledge, carrying over 200 lb. 
weight at a time, which we hauled up by block and 
tackle. As its runners were soaped to reduce the 
friction, it went by the name of the “greased light- 
ning.” Each time it reached the top it was unloaded 
and turned round, when it ran down by its own 
weight, being checked by a rope behind. Many an 
Indian must since have gazed upon it, as it lies 
decaying at the foot of the portage, and wondered 
what possible purpose it could have served. 

At Aruwai I watched, with much interest, a pair 
of humming birds feeding on small insécts, which 
were flying slowly across the river in immense 
numbers. Every now and then they would dart 
out from a tree, poise themselves in the air in front 
of an insect, seize and swallow it, and so on with 
another and another, returning now and then to the 
tree to rest. I caught one of these insects and found 
it to be a sort of diminutive May fly (Ephemera), of 
about one-eighth of an inch in length. 

One afternoon our camp at the foot of Aruwai was 
visited by yackaman ants, one column of which, as 
ill luck would have it, marched directly under the 
awning in which our hammocks were slung. They 
kept pouring through in a band three or four inches 
wide from about 4 p.m. until we went to sleep at 
9 p.m. When we turned in we had to be careful not 
to tread even near them, and when once in our 
hammocks dared not get out again in the dark. 


380 THE UPPER MAZARUNI. Cuar. XV. 


Early in the evening, thinking to divert the column, 
we threw a handful of powder on them, and igniting it 
with a live coal, sprung our mine, blowing up thousands 
of the enemy. This had no effect on those coming 
behind, for they marched on as before, after the tem- 
porary panic in their ranks had subsided. Next 
morning, at daybreak, the column had passed on, but 
now and then a weak tired-out straggler was seen 
wending its weary way along the path its comrades 
had traversed. Here and there I saw bodies of some 
which had died of exhaustion, or whose span of life 
had ended on the march. 

At midday, on the 17th of October, we continued 
our journey along the wide smooth river, the valley of 
which was bounded as before by high precipices a 
mile or two back from either side, but with no flank- 
ing hills, for we were then on a level with their tops. 
Next day we came to the foot of the next set of great 
falls called Sericoeng, and packing up a few days’ 
provisions started on the Indian path leading past 
them. Ascending a long mountain spur, we reached 
a level table-land, 1350 feet above the sea, and 830 
feet above the level of our starting point at the foot 
of the falls. Traversing this for the remainder of the 
day at no great distance from the edge of the river’s 
valley, from which proceeded the thunder of great 
falls, we camped in the forest. 

On the following morning we came out on the river, 
at a spot where it formed a wide pool, at the foot of a 
sloping cataract called Siparimer, lined by an exten- 
sive sand-beach, dotted in places with low alder-like 
trees. The greater portion of this beach was covered 


Cuap. XV. SERICOENG FALLS. 381 


with dry drift logs of various sizes, packed closely 
together, a most unusual sight in this country, where 
most of the forest trees are heavy, and sink to the 
bottom of the rivers. 

We had not been seated under the shade of a tree 
for more than ten minutes, before three Indians ap- 
peared in sight coming towards us. One of them 
proved to be the man who passed us at Aruwai, who, 
faithful to his promise, had come to meet us. He 
told the interpreter that he could only obtain two 
small woodskins for our use, but after some per- 
suasion, promised to go back and collect more, a 
proceeding that would detain him a few days. 

I had my men employed in the interval in bringing 
up stores from the boat to our camp, a distance of 
about ten miles. 

Mr. Bagot, the interpreter, and myself remained at 
the sand-beach, where we had huilt a shed and 
thatched it with the large leaves of a parasitic plant, 
after the manner followed by Indians in this part of 
the country where suitable palm leaves are scarce. 
These leaves are split at their base with a knife, and 
hung on poles tied across the roof, one leaf over- 
lapping the other, and thus rendering the whole im- 
pervious to rain. Whilst there we made an excursion 
down the river’s edge to the great fall of Sericoeng, a 
few miles off, where the whole of the water of the 
Mazaruni was precipitated over three steps, the last 
being more than a hundred feet in height, into a 
large almost circular basin bounded by high cliffs. 
‘On the precipice edge on the north side we cut down 
some trees in order to see the face of the fall, thereby 


382 THE UPPER MAZARUNI. CHarp. XV. 


disclosing to view the singular isolated box-shaped 
mountain of Ayangike with its perpendicular sides 
of pinkish rock. From this spot it resembled the 
ruins of a castle with ivy clinging to its walls. Singu- 
larly enough, its position was on a forest-covered 
glacis above the hills to the south immediately over 
the fall, so that it added greatly to the beauty of the 
scene. Ayangike is a regular landmark in this 
district, being visible from near the head of Aruwai, 
from this spot, and from our camp at Siparimer. 

In three and a half days’ time the Indians returned 
with two woodskins, stating that they could not get 
any more, though they had been up the Carimang river 
in search of them. Finding that it was impossible 
to get the Indians to bring more than two woodskins, 
I determined to take three men with me and go on to 
the main source of the river, sending back the other 
men and boats in charge of Mr. Bagot, to await our 
arrival on the Merumé river, a branch of the lower 
Mazaruni, whither I would find my way by an Indian 
path across from the upper Mazaruni. Mr. Bagot 
accompanied me to the top of Siparimer cataract, 
where we loaded our woodskins, and when he saw 
how deep they were in the water, he expressed his 
satisfaction at not having to go in one. I bid him 
adieu, and proceeded up river, reaching a village 
landing called Mokayparu, in the afternoon, where, to 
my astonishment, I saw two large and seven small 
woodskins. 

On questioning the Indians about them they said 
that one of the big ones was for us, the other had 
been brought down river after they left, and. the 


PP ume TI 


Crap. XV. TRICKED BY INDIANS. 383 


small ones were considered of no use to us. It then 
became apparent to me that the Indians had been 
deceiving us in the matter of woodskins, in order 
to prevent the whole of my party from coming into 
their territory, owing to their having been told by 
some Arecunas that we were on a slave hunting 
expedition. | 

We left Mokayparu in three woodskins, and pro- 
ceeded to the landing of the village of Cawayek, 
where we found a lot of Indians encamped. They 
were in two parties, one consisting of the village 
people, and the other of two men and their wives, 
who had come down river, as they afterwards told the 
interpreter, to offer their services as guides; but see- 
ing us already provided, they arranged with our guide 
to take us over from his custody on arriving at their 
village landing. 

The trees lining the river’s edge in this part were 
chiefly of a species of mora, while amongst them 
were a few representatives of the Mora excelsa. 
This is the only portion of the colony in which I have 
seen the Mora excelsa growing at such an elevation. 

The seeds of the first-mentioned mora have a pink 
inside, and are used by the Indians as an article of 
food, after being boiled, then soaked in cold water 
for a day or two, grated, and mixed with cassava 
meal. They make the cassava cakes look coarse and 
dark-coloured, but give them a pleasant, sweetish taste. 

Our course from Sericoeng falls was southerly for a 
couple of days along the river, which lay smooth and 
broad in a wide, tree-clothed valley, flowing at a 
height of over 1200 feet above the sea, and bounded 


384 THE UPPER MAZARUNI. Crap. XV. 


by high sandstone precipices not far off on either 
hand, which disappeared upon the valley widening 
between the mouths of the Caramang and Cako 
rivers. 

On our second day’s journey in canoes we came to 
the end of the Carabung path, which Mr. Sawkins and 
I traversed in November, 1868; and on our third day 
passed the landing at Apiopai, where, on the same 
occasion, we spent a weary time trying to collect 
provisions. 

Upon this elevated portion of the river the nights 
were chilly, and having neither an awning with me 
nor a rug to wrap in, I was frequently awoke by the 
cold. 

After passing the Cukuie, we arrived at the landing 
of the village of our travelling companions, where the 
Indians from Cawayek and Mokayparu formally re- 
signed their posts, were paid off, and started on their 
homeward way ; while the new guides, two in number, 
assumed office in their stead. One of these men could 
speak a little English, and had worked on the Demerara 
wood-cutting grants. He wore a shirt and trousers 
during the day when it was hot, but took them off at 
night when it was cold. 

From the Cako our course, though winding, was in 
an easterly direction, and we passed through a tract 
of country from which the forest had been stripped 
by fire, many years ago. Ferns and coarse grasses 
covered the soil and rocks, and a few blackened tree 
stumps still remained standing. Amongst the shrubs 
which flourished there was one with polished dark- 
green leaves and beautiful wax-like flowers, resem- 


Crap, XV, CAVE TIGERS. 885 


bling single roses in form and size. Their centres 
were yellow, and their petals white, tipped with car- 
mine. A delicately scented ground orchid, with ex- 
. ceedingly long flowers of a yellow colour dotted with 
brown, was very common. 

In passing the Carowuring, a river coming in on 
the north, the guide informed us that when high it is 
navigable for canoes for half a day’s journey up, to 
the foot of a high fall, at which there is a large sand- 
beach, marked with mysterious footprints resembling 
those made by the human foot. The sand also is 
thrown up as if children had been playing there. If 
the Indians who visit the spot trample down these 
heaps, and go away for a short time, on their return 
they find them there again as before. The Indians 
believe that wild men live near the spot, but have 
never yet succeeded in seeing them. 

Along this part of the river are many deep pools, 
where the banks are formed of horizontal beds of sand- 
stone, ledges of which run out into the river. Under 
water, in three of these pools, our guide said the huge, 
black, short-tailed Wailah tigers live. He said that, 
coming down the river early one morning, he saw two 
of these creatures come out of a side creek, swim a 
short distance, and dive under a ledge of sandstone. 

Can it be that in the bank at the place there is 
a cave, reached by passing under the water, in which 
the tigers live ? 

Many Indians see them frequently sleeping on the 
rocks in mid-river. He also said that some years ago 
a man and his family were fishing in Tembe creek, 
near one of these places, when a black tiger rushed out 

f 20 


386 THE UPPER MAZARUNI. Cuar. XV. 


upon them. They attacked it with cutlasses, and 
drove it off for a time; but it followed them to their 
house, and there sprang upon a man, whom it wounded 
severely, when, as before, they all set upon it with . 
cutlasses and drove it away. 

We arrived at a village called Ackar, where there 
were some fine-looking Indians, amongst whom was a 
singularly handsome woman, and a strong, well-built 
man, with long curly hair, a fine specimen of an 
Indian athlete. We there learned of the existence of 
a great fall, called Chichi, which we should have to 
pass in order to continue our journey to the source 
of the Mazaruni. 

At a place farther on, where we entered a gorge in 
the mountains with high sandstone precipices on either 
side, our guide pointed out a cave in which he said the 
Salies or Guacharo birds (Steatornis caripensis) live. 
Wishing to see them, we ascended the slope to it, 
arriving at a great vertical rent in the rocks, some 
ten feet wide and fifty feet high. 

The Indians halloed and threw stones up amongst 
the ledges of rock in the recess of the cave, causing 
the birds to utter a strange squeaking cry, but without 
starting any from their hiding places. Upon the un- 
even floor were numbers of nuts of the Turu palm 
(Enocarpus batawa), which had been swallowed by 
these birds, and disgorged after the thin pulpy cover- 
ing had been digested. 

The Indians had a bush-rope, made fast on the cliff 
above, let down through a hole in the roof, which they 
used for climbing up to dislodge the young salies from 
their nests on the ledges. They eat these young birds 
and make oil from their fat. 


Cuar. XY. DISTANT GREAT WATERFALL. 387 


Some cocks of the rock had their nests on the ledges 
near the entrance of the cave, rudely constructed of 
clay. At Ackar the Indians had two tame birds of 
this kind, which they had caught with strong snares 
placed on the ground at their dancing places. 

From the cave’s mouth, at a height of 1882 feet 
above the sea, we had a grand view of the north- - 
eastern side of Roraima and its surrounding moun- 
tains, some thirty miles distant. Upon the side of 
Roraima I saw an enormously high waterfall, looking 
like a white thread against the dark-blue mountain. 

The Indians said that it was the head of a branch 
of the Cotinga river ; but I afterwards learned from an 
intelligent Arecuna that it was the head of the Caroni 
river, a branch of the Orinooko. It is probably the 
highest fall in the world, and at the same time of con- 
siderable width. The vertical portion of Roraima is 
2000 feet in height, over the upper half of which 
it fell like a plumb-line, and then descended with a 
slight slope outwards. The remaining 3000 feet to 
the valley below slopes at an angle of 45°, and 
being tree-covered, the rest of the fall is hidden by 
foliage. 

I would have visited this fall had not stern duty led 
me in another direction. 

We went back to Ackar village to engage people to 
come up to Chichi fall to assist in carrying our wood- 
skins over, and also went inland to a village, called 
Cowaeny, near Mount Camooda, where I got some 
more Indians to come with us. The chief man of 
Cowaeng had fourteen children, which constituted an 
extraordinary large family for an Indian, the usual 
number seldom exceeding four. This is owing, I sup- 

2c 2 


388 THE UPPER MAZARUNI. Cuar. XV. 


pose, to many children dying when young, from their 
parents lugging them about on long journeys. 

All Indians seem much attached to their children, 
and the mothers always carry them on their hips as 
soon as they are able to hold on. Previously to that 
they carry them in a wide endless woven band, passed 
across one shoulder and the opposite hip. 

Of the old man’s family, four daughters were grown 
up, three being fiue, good-looking unmarried girls, 
while the fourth was ugly and married. I have often 
noticed that Indian men, as a rule, choose ugly women 
for their wives ; and it appears to me that their ideas 
of beauty consist in plainness of features. 

In the valley below Chichi fall every curiously 
formed rock had its Indian name, and besides the 
usual cognomens of the “ Paiworie corial,” “ Cassava 
cake,” &c., there was one called the “ Woodpecker’s 
mother,” and another the “ Fish club.” 

We arrived near the portage of Chichi (so called 
from a large green grasshopper which frequents the 
neighbourhood) at midday on the 3rd of November. 
I walked over the rocks to the fall, while the Indians 
went to clear the steep path up the mountain side of 
overhanging branches, in order to allow the canoes to 
be carried up. ‘Traversing the huge flat rocks that 
almost filled the bed of the river, up an inclined plane 
for a distance of about half a mile, I came to the foot 
of Chichi, which I found to be about one hundred feet 
in height and quite perpendicular. Its width at the 
time was only forty yards, owing to the dryness of the 
season. We spent a day and a half at Chichi, during 
which time the Indians carried our two large wood- 
skins across the portage. 


Cuap. XV. A RAINY NIGHT. ` 389 


The Indians had erected a small banaboo on the 
bank’s top, a little way in the forest, whilst my men 
and I hung our bammocks to poles stuck in the sand 
near the water’s edge. On the second night of our 
stay, about midnight, I was aroused from a sound 
sleep by rain pouring down upon me in torrents. 
Jumping out on the wet sand, in a semi-bewildered 
condition, I felt on the ground for my shoes, but in 
the intense darkness only procured one. As my 
hammock was rapidly getting wet, I struggled at and 
untied the rope of one end; then I got desperate, felt 
for and found my other shoe, and dashed at the other 
end of the hammock, where I found Ben good- 
naturedly untying it for me. Putting it in my tin 
canister, I turned and flew up the bank to the Indian 
banaboo, which I reached after nearly being decapi- 
tated by lianes across the pathway, and there found 
seven Indians and my three men under the little 
shelter it afforded. Three of the Indians hung in 
their small red hammocks suspended against the roof, 
like so many bats, whilst the rest of us crouched 
round a small fire which we kept going in order to 
dry and warm us, all being in a thoroughly soaked 
condition. In this way we spent the rest of the 
dreary night, until the long-wished-for daylight came, 
listening to the patter of the rain on the trees and 

und. 

By 8 a.m. the rain had ceased, and we soon after 
started along the pathway, three Indians carrying the 
third woodskin, the remainder carrying stores. 

We ascended the mountain side on the south, 
coming out on a bare rocky savanna, and walked in 
an east-south-east direction up a gradual ascent to a 


390 THE UPPER MAZARUNI. Cuar. XV 


ridge, from which the view of the surrounding savanna 
mountains was very fine. Many curious flowering 
shrubs and plants dotted the surface of the country 
near the path. 

After three and three-quarter hours’ quick walking 
we reached the Mazaruni river, where its black water 
flowed smoothly along at a height of 2084 feet 
above the level of the sea. Paying the Indians for 
their services, we adjourned to the friendly shelter of 
a projecting cliff of red sandstone, where we lashed 
poles together to support our hammocks, and spent a 
comfortable night. One of the two Indians who had 
come down to meet me at Cawayek—a fat, chubby, 
jovial fellow—left me to return with the Ackar 
Indians, on his homeward voyage, while his companion 
remained and eventually went with me to George- 
town. 

Our object being to find the Haiacker river in order 
to procure a guide, we proceeded up stream, and on 
coming to a large muddy branch on the right hand, 
ascended it. Meeting with some Indians, we learned 
that we were on the Haiacker, and they accompanied 
us to their landing a short distance up a little branch. 

The Haiacker was bordered by forest, but its 
branch ran through savanna, whilst the path on to the 
village lay mostly through open country. From a 
rising ground in the savanna, the huge mountain of 
Ayangcanna could be seen in the distance to the east- 
south-east, forming a most singular picture. The 
word Ayangcanna rendered into English means “ Lice 
searchers.” It has got this disagreeable name from a 
row of huge pointed rocks on its crest which are 
sharply defined against the sky, and to the Indian eye 


Cuar, XV. AYANGOANNA MOUNTAIN. 391 


resemble a row of women seated one behind the other 
searching each other’s heads for vermin, a custom very 
prevalent amongst all Guiana tribes. The upper five 
hundred feet or so of the mountain is within the region 
of fleecy white clouds which, in passing, completely 
enveloped the top. 

I sent my men to the village some distance off, and 
they returned, stating that on their approach all its 
occupants fled, except one old woman, who told them 
the men had gone to another village. 

Whilst I was making a sketch of Ayangcanna in 
the afternoon fifteen Indians came up to me, and then 
accompanied me to camp, where they had a long talk 
with the interpreter. They told him they had heard 
of our coming for the purpose of enslaving them, but 
knew it to be untrue, for a man from the Potaro river, 
who was then amongst them, had seen me on that 
river and had given a favourable account of us. They 
belonged to the Partamona tribe, and the men were 
an exceedingly free and easy lot, without any timidity 
about them. One of them agreed to go with me as 
guide to the farthest point-it is possible to reach in 
woodskins on the Mazaruni; others promised on my 
return to guide me to the Orindouie fall on the Ireng 
river. 

We. descended the Haiacker and continued our 
journey up the Mazaruni for two days, arriving on the 
afternoon of the 9th of November at the utmost limit 
of our journey, where the river was only twelve yards 
wide and a few inches deep, at the foot of a fall which 
is never passed by Indians. During the forenoon of 
that day we passed a part of the river where large 
Mora trees had fallen across it, so that in places we 


392 THE UPPER MAZARUNI. Cuar. XV. 


passed with difficulty over some and under others. 
We spent the night below the fall, near the path end 
leading to the Karnang, where there were some leaky 
old banaboos, in which we had to take shelter from the 
rain, and sit up the livelong night. Owing to the 
torrents of rain that fell that night, the water in the 
river rose seven feet, so that on the following morning 
we descended on the flood, and passed over the 
numerous obstructions met with on our way up. 

The only fish to be procured, in this part of the 
Mazaruni, was a short, small, stubby creature having 
a spot on each side, being probably of the same 
family as the sun fish or lukenanie. The Indians 
procured some by bringing the woodskin to a stop 
close to a bank, where rocks or sunken logs were 
numerous, and then whistling softly, upon which these 
fish swam slowly out from their hiding places, as 
if to inquire into the cause of the sound, and were 
shot with the arrow or taken with a baited hook. 

On our way we passed a deep pool, where there 
was an eddy, in which the guide informed us there 
lived a “water child” covered with long hair. A 
woodskin with Indians was passing the spot one day, 
when the water child came to the surface, caught 
hold of, and upset the canoe. One of its occupants 
sank with it, and, being seized by the child, never 
came up to the surface again. 

The first night of our return journey we gained the 
shelter of a small shed we had built on an open sand- 
beach on our way up, when we were visited by a 
storm of thunder, lightning, and rain. The wind 
blew the rain through and through our shed, putting 
out our fires and making us wet and miserable. The 


Cuap. XV. JOURNEY TO THE IRENG. 393 


lightning was most vivid, being of a violet colour, and 
rendering everything for the instant as plain as in the 
day. 

Next day we ascended the Haiacker again, getting 
back to our old camp, where we placed our stores 
in packs, and crossing a tract of open country, the 
surface of which was strewn with most singular look- 
ing flowers, grasses, ground orchids, &c., many of 
which I had never seen before, reached the village 
of Haiacker. This was prettily situated on a spur 
of a greenstone mountain facing south, commanding 
a fine view of the mountains forming the watershed 
between the branches of the Mazaruni on the north, 
and the rivers which flow into the Amazon valley. 
With my men and four Indians I started on my 
journey to the Ireng river, and descending the wooded 
mountain, passed another village, coming out on a 
savanna. Crossing it, we entered the forest again, 
and had no more open country for the rest of our 
journey. We passed through two more villages, and 
came to a single house in a new clearing, called 
Enawarike, where to my disgust we found four young 
Indians, dressed in cucurit palm-leaves and paint, 
dancing and singing round two corials of casserie, 
in the centre of the house. I learned that they had 
come as a deputation to invite the people to a 
“grand paiworie,” and were observing the ceremony 
usual on such occasions. Knowing how pertinacious 
these fellows are when they once begin to dance, I 
felt sure that they would keep at it all might, but 
before hammock time came I was glad to see the 
dancing cease. 

The rain prevented my sleeping outside, so I had to 


394 THE UPPER MAZARUNI. Cuar. XV. 


put up with the heat, smoke, and flying cockroaches 
within, for the night. The short rainy season had by 
this time thoroughly set in, and we could not look 
forward to any really fine weather during the rest of 
our journey. 

Next day we crossed the Haiacker river, at the foot 
of the watershed, where it came tumbling down the 
mountain side at a steep angle, and passing up 4 
steep ascent we reached the mountain top early in the 
afternoon. As we passed along amongst great blocks 
of greenstone in the deep shade of the forest, at a 
height of 3000 feet, a great black cload completely 
enveloped us, from which for a time torrents of rain fell. 

We then descended the other side of the watershed, 
and saw the head of the Ireng, where it was scarcely 
two feet in width. Building a leaf shed for the night, 
we changed our soaking clothes, and made ourselves 
as comfortable as circumstances would pérmit. The 
ground under my bare feet felt icy cold, and the wood 
gathered for the fire was so soaked with rain that it 
would only smoulder, without giving out heat. 

The walking next day was exceedingly rough, over 
hills and hollows, and across numerous branch streams 
of the Ireng. In one stream our Indians caught some 
crabs, little fish, and light-coloured frogs, all of which 
they roasted and ate. We crossed a large branch of 
the Ireng, called Cakoparu, and in the afternoon came 
upon the Ireng itself for the second time, where it was 
of considerable size. Striking off from its eastern side, 
we ascended a steep hill to an open, sandy, level 
place, in which a village, called Waipahyaracapu, was 
situated. One of our Indians, who had brought his 
gun with him, on reaching the first house suddenly fired 


Cuar. XV. REACH ORINDOUIE. 395 


it off across the doorway—rather a startling mode of 
salutation. 7 

The evening was so clear and bright that from the 
village the top of Ayangcanna could just be seen over 
the flat-topped mountains to the north. 

Whilst waiting for stars to come to the meridian, in 
order to obtain observations for latitude, I saw upon 
the ground a most curious worm-like insect, of about 
an inch in length, which had a phosphorescent light in 
its head, and two rows of similar lights down its back. 
When at rest it extinguished its body lights, but 
when touched it made them flash out with great 
brilliancy, like so many minute shining emeralds. 

Numbers of purple- throated cotingas (Cotinga 
cayenna) were to be seen feeding on the berries of 
shrubs about the houses, and on the seeds of the silver- 
bally tree in the adjacent bush. 

I went down the Ireng in a woodskin to the head 
of Orindouie fall, which I had visited on ascending 
that river in 1871. I was surprised to see that a 
village had sprung up on the east bank, close to the 
head of the fall, at which were gathered some fifty 
people, who had come from villages below to meet me. 
Amongst these I recognized several old acquaintances 
of my former visit, one being the head man of the 
village below the fall. 

At a village on the way back to Waipabyaracapu I 
ohserved a male papaw tree, bearing fruit, which on 
examination was found to be in every way similar to 
that on the female tree. 

We left Waipahyaracapu, and commenced our return 
journey to Haiacker, in a steady fall of rain, which 
continued all the first day and in showers on the 


896 THE UPPER MAZARUNL Cuar. XV. 


second. At noon on the third day we got back to 
Haiacker village from which we had set out, and there 
remained for the night. 

One house had been given up to us, where during the 
afternoon the people of the place and a lot of Indians 
from another village pestered me with their company. 
Squatting on the floor, all round the edge of the house, 
they talked and watched my every movement with 
curiosity. The interpreter, after a great deal of 
trouble, got them to leave me in peace for a time, but 
no sooner did we turn into our hammocks for the night 
than a lot, chiefly men from a neighbouring village, 
came back, headed by the chief of the place, and 
squatting down again, commenced a long conversation 
with the interpreter. They told him that a story was 
circulating amongst the surrounding villages, that I 
had come up to ascertain the number and whereabouts 
of the inhabitants of this part, so that the people of the 
coast could send up a strong party to capture and 
carry them into slavery. They therefore formed a 
deputation from those villages to inquire if there was 
any truth in the story. They all seemed considerably 
depressed, and listened attentively to every word of 
my reply, as translated to them by the interpreter. 
After about an hour’s palaver I was able to do away 
with all their apprehensions, and show them how 
foolish such an idea was. Then filing out of the 
house, in cheerful frames of mind, they left us to onr 
rest. 

The fears induced by tales handed down to them by 
their forefathers, of the abhorrent and evil practice 
of enslaving Indians carried on by the Dutch and 


Cuar. XV. FEARS OF INDIANS. 397 


Brazilians in former days, will never be completely 
eradicated from their minds. 

Leaving the village we proceeded to our canoes on 
the Haiacker, accompanied by nine Indians, and 
descended that river to the Mazaruni; then down the 
latter river to a landing on the north bank at the head 
of the Chichi falls. Carrying the three woodskins for 
about one mile across a savanna, we launched them in 
a branch river, called Ow-weang, and paddling one 
day’s journey up it in a general north-easterly direc- 
tion, came to the landing of the path leading through 
to Merumé. 

On giving the Indians some rice to cook that 
evening, the men told them to wash it first by dipping 
the earthen pot in the water, but to this they de- 
murred, saying that if they placed their pot in the 
water the rain would fall more heavily. 

Making packs of our small stock of food, instru- 
ments, &c., we started on our journey across to the 
Lower Mazaruni at 9 a.m. on the 23rd of November. 
The path was wide, and apparently much used, but 
at the same time extremely rough, fallen logs and 
tree-roots crossing it in all directions. It was all 
through forest, up a steep rise at first, and then along 
a gradual slope, intersected by deep gullies and small 
streams. At one stream on the way the Indians 
caught numbers of small shrimps, which they devoured 
uncooked. During the afternoon we crossed the head 
of the Ow-weang, and at 1.40 p.m. the head of the 
Mazaruni itself, running east at a height of 2315 feet 
above the sea. From thence, until we reached the 
edge of Merumé mountain at 3 P.M., the walking was 


398 THE UPPER MAZARUNI. Cuar. XV. 


dreadfully rough, being up and down gullies and 
across hills and streams. From the brink of the great 
escarpment of Merumé, at a height of 2353 feet above 
the sea, the great level tree-clothed plain, through 
which the Lower Mazaruni winds, lay spread out 
before us, which had to be gained before we could 
reach our boats. After resting a short time we com- 
menced to descend, amongst great sandstone rocks 
and over slippery tree-roots, until we came to a 
perpendicular wall of rock, where a rude ladder of 
poles, with its rounds lashed to it by bush-ropes, had 
been made by Indians, down which we had to make 
our way. We passed down two of these ladders, and 
descended about a thousand feet, when late in the 
afternoon we came to a camping place at the foot of 
an overhanging precipice, where some slight shelter 
could be obtained for the night. There we rigged up 
poles by sticking them in cracks and lashing them 
together, and tied our hammocks to them for the 
night. This was hardly accomplished before a heavy 
rain and thunder storm broke over us, which lasted 
until dusk. 

Nearly all the members of the party had one or 
more falls in coming down the mountain side. The 
guide came a cropper in front of me, at which I 
laughed ; and five minutes afterwards, when I per- 
formed a similar feat, spinning down a smooth rocky 
slope for five or six yards on the broad of my back, 
he returned the compliment heartily. One Indian, 
who carried our only large tin cooking saucepan 
lashed to his pack, fell so often that when he delivered 
it up in camp it was so battered and pierced with 
holes that it was no longer of any use. The only 


Cuar. XV. TERMINATION OF JOURNEY. 399 


water we could procure was caught in calabashes from 
a drip over a rocky ledge, and a bath was obtained 
partly from the water thus caught and partly by 
standing in the rain. 

The Indians begged my men not to roast salt fish 
on the embers, fearing thereby to arouse the ire of a 
large eagle and Camoodie snake, which they said 
lived on the mountain side, and would show their dis- 
pleasure by causing more rain to fall. 

Next day we continued our descent of the mountain 
over a less precipitous path, and then down a long 
slope, while rain fell in torrents. We came to a 
branch of the Merumé river, called Warongabong, at 
9 A.M., and, in crossing this swollen stream on a 
slippery log, I was unfortunate enough to slip off 
into the water, but grasping the log saved myself. 
Walking some three miles along the level plain at 
the mountain’s foot, 260 feet above the sea level, we 
came to the path end on the Merumé river in a most 
sodden, bedraggled condition, and there found a camp 
that had been formed by my men from the boats. We 
had not been there long before three of the men came 
up stream in a woodskin, from whom I learned that the 
boats were at the foot of the fall some miles down, 
and that three men at a time had been up at this 
place in readiness to take us down when we should 
arrive. Going with them, we reached the boats late 
in the afternoon, and were heartily welcomed by all. 
There Mr. Bagot and myself exchanged mutual con- 
gratulations on meeting in safety after our perilous 
journeys. 

Paying off the Haiacker Indians, we descended the 
Merumé next day, and on the following morning 


400 THE UPPER MAZARUNI. Cuap. XY. 


reached Sororieng landing. When we were at that 
place on our way up an accident occurred to a party 
of Indians, late one evening, by a gun, placed care 
lessly in the rafters of a hut, falling and going off 
amongst them, wounding one man in the thigh, 
another in the finger, and a woman in the back. The 
first-mentioned individual was very severely injured, 
and we found him lying in his hammock in a fainting 
condition. He had three shots in the right thigh and 
knee, one in the left knee, and a wound in his left 
side. We administered laudanum and brandy, ap- 
plying Friar’s balsam on lint to the shot-holes. The 
other two were wounded very slightly, the man having 
one shou in his finger, and the woman a slight graze 
in the back. We now learned from the Sororieng 
people that the wounded man had got quite well, and 
had gone to his home on the Cukuie river. 

At Sororieng I purchased a tame Labba tiger 
(Felis pardalis) from the Indians, and took it on 
board one of the boats. Though ill-tempered at first, 
it soon became acquainted with us, and was, until 
its death, some six months afterwards, a mast 
amusing pet. 

Running pleasantly down the Mazaruni, we reached 
the Penal Settlement on the 2nd day of December, 
and from that place went on to Wakenaam stelling, 
at the mouth of the Essequebo, where we joined the 
coast steamer, and by it arrived safely in Georgetown 
on the 4th; thus bringing to a close the last of my 
journeys to the interior of British Guiana.

BOOKS. 8 


BRITISH MANUFACTURING INDUSTRIES—continucd, 


From the ‘ATHENZUM,’ April 8th, 1876. 


This series of small books is professedly undertaken for the purpose of 
bringing “into one focus the leading features and the present position of the 
most important industries of the kingdom.” The idea of publishing, in the 
same form of volume, a number of essays on British industries —they are not 
al] manufacturing—which should be from the pens of men whose positions are 
a guarantee for the correctness of the descriptions given and of the facts 
detailed, is certainly a good one. A large number of people desire to possess a 
general acquaintance with those industrial operations, which have, for a long 
period, distinguished this country ; but they have not the time, or the oppor- 
tunity, or, perhaps the industry, for hunting out the details of them, which 
are probably scattered through numerous books and journals. For this class 
these books appear to us to be exceedingly well adapted. We have now before 
us six volumes, each with an average of 187 pages; they contain about 
twenty-seven essays, by eighteen different authors, all of whom are more or 
less intimately associated with the subjects upon which they have employed 
their pens. When we state that we find amongst them Profs. Warington 
Smyth, Hull, Archer, Barff, and Church, Dr. Dresser, and the Messrs, Patterson, 
J. Arthur Phillips, Galletly, Arnoux, and other equally well-known names, we 
have certainly said enough to recommend those essays to the attention of all 
who desire to know something of the industries of which they treat. These 
volumes are not intended to take the place of Handbooks; it is not their 
purpose te impart technical instruction; they are designed to convey, to those 
who desire it, a general knowledge of the principles and of the more striking 
points of the practice of the workshops. The subjects are sufficiently varied : 
Mining, Quarrying, Metallurgy, Fictile and Textile Manufactures, Woodwork 
and Furniture, and Chemical Arts are comprehended in the books now published, 
The articles are not all equally good; but although a few of them appear to 
have been thrown off ‘hastily, showing a want of method, and, in some few 
cases, a want of thought, yet, as each authof has been selected for his special 
knowledge, every one of them contains trustworthy information. We could 
have desired that the statistics had been completed up to a more recent date 
than they are, and there are a few other points which we should advise the 
editor to look carefully after when new editions are required; but, on the 
whole, the impression left after a careful examination ef each of the subjects 
dealt with is most favourable.
